### [1] [Seeing What the Vehicle Sees: Video-Augmented Virtual Reality for Physical Autonomous Vehicles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13224)
*Md Tanjemul Islam,Mohammad Shafin,Md Rafiul Kabir*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents a video-augmented VR framework linking a physical ROS 2 autonomous robot vehicle to a Unity 6 app on a Meta Quest 3S headset, achieving low state-update latency (29.63 ms), 2.28% route-progress error, and reliable decision notifications across 20 closed-loop navigation trials.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Passengers in autonomous vehicles often remain uncertain about what the vehicle perceives and why it acts as it does. While VR provides a safe, repeatable medium for conveying this information, most passenger-facing VR studies rely on fully simulated vehicles or pre-scripted scenarios, so the motion and perception presented do not originate from a physically operating autonomous system.

Method: A video-augmented VR framework couples a physical ROS 2 autonomous robot vehicle to a Unity 6 application deployed on a Meta Quest 3S headset. Vehicle state and live onboard camera stream are transmitted over two independent communication channels, letting the virtual vehicle mirror the physical robot's motion while the passenger views the first-person camera feed and navigation decisions through an in-vehicle dashboard interface.

Result: Over 20 repeated closed-loop navigation trials, the system achieved a mean state-update latency of 29.63 ms, a mean relative route-progress error of 2.28%, and video delivery at 10.006 fps with 0.25% frame loss. All monitored navigation decisions were correctly reflected in the VR interface, with no missed or incorrect notifications.

Conclusion: The framework can support temporally synchronized, semantically consistent, and accurate route-progress representation for immersive observation of physical autonomous-vehicle behavior.

Abstract: Autonomous vehicles are expected to improve road safety and efficiency, but passengers often remain uncertain about what the vehicle perceives and why it acts as it does. Virtual reality (VR) offers a safe and repeatable medium for presenting this information, yet most passenger-facing VR studies rely on fully simulated vehicles or pre-scripted scenarios, so the motion and perception shown to the user do not originate from a physically operating autonomous system. This paper presents a video-augmented VR framework that couples a physical ROS 2 autonomous robot vehicle to a Unity 6 application deployed on a Meta Quest 3S headset. The vehicle state and live onboard camera stream are transmitted over two independent communication channels, allowing the virtual vehicle to mirror the physical robot's motion while the passenger simultaneously views the vehicle's first-person camera feed and its navigation decisions through an in-vehicle dashboard interface. We evaluate the framework over 20 repeated closed-loop navigation trials. The system achieves a mean state-update latency of 29.63 ms, a mean relative route-progress error of 2.28% between the physical and virtual vehicles, and video delivery at 10.006 frames per second with 0.25% frame loss. All monitored navigation decisions were correctly reflected in the VR interface with no missed or incorrect notifications. The results indicate that the framework can support temporally synchronized, semantically consistent, and accurate route-progress representation for immersive observation of physical autonomous-vehicle behavior.

</details>


### [2] [Harnessing human expertise for high-precision robotic assembly in industrialized construction: A sample-efficient installer-in-the-loop interactive reinforcement learning framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13234)
*Zekai Jin,Huiguang Wang,Xiaoning Sun,Yi Shao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes an installer-in-the-loop interactive RL framework that learns precision robotic assembly of prefabricated window units from teleoperated demonstrations, sparse binary takeovers, and terminal rewards, achieving 100% autonomous seating under a 2 mm-clearance stress test with only 12–15 min of human supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Industrialized construction demands millimeter-scale precision in robotic assembly of modular components, where the bottleneck is not just mechanical clearance but converting tacit installer expertise into data-efficient autonomy under sparse acceptance feedback, contact variability, and tight tolerances.

Method: An installer-in-the-loop interactive RL framework that acquires expertise via offline teleoperated demonstrations, sparse event-driven binary takeovers at contact-failure boundaries, and acceptance-aligned terminal rewards, logged under a unified schema for traceable offline-to-online adaptation. A temporally abstract action-sequence policy based on Q-chunking with Flow Q-Learning captures multimodal recovery maneuvers under sparse terminal rewards, while a non-updating warm-start phase stabilizes the offline-to-online transition. Evaluated in MuJoCo across suction acquisition through clearance-limited seating, under structured staging and end-to-end randomized placement.

Result: In a stress-test regime with 2 mm per-side clearance, bounded pose perturbations, and friction randomization, the pipeline attains 100% autonomous seating with 12–15 min of cumulative installer supervision over 3.0 h of online training, reaching the 95% success milestone in approximately 0.5 h and 1.5 h in the two experiments. The paper also reports wall-clock adaptation time, cumulative takeover minutes, intervention-rate decay, and stage-wise failure attribution for supervision budgeting. Ablations isolate the complementary contributions of temporal abstraction, installer intervention, and warm-start value calibration.

Conclusion: The framework enables data-efficient, precision-critical robotic assembly by combining temporal abstraction, sparse human intervention, and warm-start value calibration, with ablations confirming each component's complementary contribution and the reported metrics supporting principled supervision budgeting.

Abstract: Industrialized construction imposes stringent precision requirements on robotic assembly of modular components such as prefabricated window units. In tolerance-critical operations, the central bottleneck is not only mechanical clearance but also converting tacit installer expertise into data-efficient autonomy under sparse acceptance feedback, contact variability, and millimeter-scale constraints. We present an installer-in-the-loop interactive reinforcement learning framework that acquires expertise through offline teleoperated demonstrations, sparse event-driven binary takeovers at contact-failure boundaries, and acceptance-aligned terminal rewards, logged under a unified schema for traceable offline-to-online adaptation. A temporally abstract action-sequence policy built on Q-chunking with Flow Q-Learning captures multimodal recovery maneuvers under sparse terminal rewards, while a non-updating warm-start phase stabilizes the offline-to-online transition. The framework is evaluated in MuJoCo across the workflow from suction acquisition through clearance-limited seating, under structured staging and end-to-end randomized placement. Within a defined stress-test regime with 2 mm per-side clearance, bounded pose perturbations, and friction randomization, the pipeline attains 100\% autonomous seating with 12--15 min of cumulative installer supervision over 3.0 h of online training, and reaches the 95\% success milestone in approximately 0.5 h and 1.5 h in the two experiments. We also report wall-clock adaptation time, cumulative takeover minutes, intervention-rate decay, and stage-wise failure attribution to inform supervision budgeting. Ablations isolate the complementary contributions of temporal abstraction, installer intervention, and warm-start value calibration.

</details>


### [3] [Learning Manipulation-Sufficient Representations via Outcome Bottlenecks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13235)
*Md Selim Sarowar,Sungho Kim*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A policy-free, action-conditioned stochastic bottleneck learns outcome-preserving representations for bandwidth-limited networked manipulation, outperforming reconstructed geometry on grasp success and supporting conformal filtering, viewpoint selection, and test-time adaptation via a 512-byte interface.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Networked manipulation endpoints couple perception to actuation over compute- and bandwidth-limited links but typically exchange dense geometric states optimized for fidelity rather than for downstream action outcomes. This mismatch motivates a representation that preserves the outcome distribution of every admissible action while being rate-efficient.

Method: A stochastic representation is learned with a policy-free, action-conditioned outcome bottleneck: marginal outcome log-loss acts as the distortion term and a KL term regularizes rate. The construction is grounded in the minimal statistic preserving the outcome distribution across all admissible actions, and the finite model is evaluated as a rate-regularized mixture predictor. A shared encoder and outcome head serve grasp selection, singleton conformal filtering, active viewpoint selection, and latent test-time adaptation. A finite-probe theorem links the local level-set tangent space to the null space of an outcome Jacobian.

Result: The synthetic oracle confirms the theorem, and on scanned objects an analytic surrogate matches measured simulator invariances within 0.56°. Over 11,979 simulated grasps on 13 objects, a reconstructed-geometry wrench score reaches 0.542 AUC against lift success and drops below chance on curved objects, whereas the proposed representation reaches 0.876. At 25% commitment, executed-grasp success is 0.503 versus 0.984. The 512-byte interface is 288× smaller than one RGB-D frame and runs at 16 ms per CPU decision. Synthetic points track tested conformal levels; scanned-object all-pair coverage is reported as a clustered diagnostic. On unseen objects, within-scene AUC falls to 0.569, and a full-feedback update raises mean pairwise coverage from 0.728 to 0.883.

Conclusion: Outcome-conditioned, rate-regularized stochastic representations outperform fidelity-optimized geometric states for action-relevant tasks under tight bandwidth, while supporting multiple downstream functions and efficient adaptation, though generalization to unseen objects remains limited.

Abstract: Networked manipulation endpoints couple perception to actuation across compute- and bandwidth-limited links, yet commonly exchange dense geometric states optimized for fidelity rather than action outcomes. A stochastic representation is learned with a policy-free, action-conditioned outcome bottleneck: marginal outcome log-loss supplies distortion and a KL term regularizes rate. The construction is motivated by the minimal statistic that preserves the outcome distribution of every admissible action, while the implemented finite model is evaluated as a rate-regularized mixture predictor. The same encoder and outcome head support grasp selection, singleton conformal filtering, active viewpoint selection, and latent test-time adaptation. A finite-probe theorem identifies the local level-set tangent space with the null space of an outcome Jacobian. The synthetic oracle verifies this result; on scanned objects, an analytic surrogate agrees with measured simulator invariances within \(0.56^\circ\). Across 11,979 simulated grasps on 13 objects, a reconstructed-geometry wrench score attains 0.542 AUC against lift success and falls below chance on curved objects, while our representation attains 0.876. At 25\% commitment, executed-grasp success is 0.503 versus 0.984. The 512-byte interface is \(288\times\) smaller than one RGB-D frame and runs at 16\,ms per CPU decision. Independent synthetic points track the tested conformal levels; scanned-object all-pair coverage is reported as a clustered empirical diagnostic. On unseen objects, within-scene AUC falls to 0.569, and a full-feedback update raises empirical mean pairwise coverage from 0.728 to 0.883.

</details>


### [4] [Self-Evolving AI for Humanoids: Mechanisms, Safety, and Evaluation of Post-Deployment Self-Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13236)
*Loc X. Nguyen,Avi Deb Raha,Huy Q. Le,Eui-Nam Huh,Dusit Niyato,Choong Seon Hong*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This survey examines how self-evolution differs for physically embodied agents, defining self-evolution for humanoids via a state tuple (policy, perception, memory, workflow, body) updated by an evolution operator in a slow outer loop, organizing the literature into four mechanisms of increasing autonomy, and treating safety and uncertainty as core design dimensions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Humanoid robots are increasingly central to embodied AI, yet most deployed systems remain static: a policy is trained offline for a fixed objective and then frozen, while tasks, environments, and robot bodies drift over time. Self-evolving agents address this by improving from post-deployment experience, but existing studies focus on disembodied software agents, leaving unclear how self-evolution changes when the agent has a physical body.

Method: The survey first defines self-evolution for humanoids, representing a deployed robot as a state tuple (policy, perception, memory, workflow, body) updated by an evolution operator in a slow outer loop with a lifelong objective. It then organizes the literature into four complementary mechanisms of self-evolution ordered by increasing autonomy: self-learning, self-adaptation, self-optimization, and self-generation. Because humanoid changes can introduce physical hazards, safety and uncertainty are treated as key design dimensions of the evolution operator, with admissible evolution formulated as a constraint enforced by a world-model verification gate inside a human-oversight envelope.

Result: The survey presents that evaluation should track a robot's evolving trajectory rather than a fixed checkpoint, and identifies the lack of a benchmark designed specifically for self-evolving humanoids.

Conclusion: The paper outlines open challenges spanning AI algorithms, on-board systems, and governance for self-evolving humanoids.

Abstract: Humanoid robots are becoming an important part of embodied artificial intelligence, driven by advances in reinforcement learning for locomotion, world models for prediction, and vision-language-action models for general control. However, most of these systems remain static after deployment. A policy is trained offline for a fixed objective and then frozen, even though the tasks, environments, and robot bodies keep drifting over time. An emerging paradigm of self-evolving agents aims to address this problem by allowing systems to improve from their own post-deployment experience. Since most existing studies focus on disembodied software agents, this survey examines how self-evolution changes when an agent has a physical body. We first define self-evolution for humanoids and represent a deployed robot using a state tuple that includes its policy, perception, memory, workflow, and body. This state is updated by an evolution operator in a slow outer loop with a lifelong objective. We then organize the literature into four complementary mechanisms of self-evolution, presented in increasing order of autonomy: self-learning, self-adaptation, self-optimization, and self-generation. Since changes to a humanoid can introduce physical hazards, we treat safety and uncertainty as key design dimensions of the evolution operator, and further formulate admissible evolution as a constraint enforced by a world-model verification gate within a human-oversight envelope. Finally, we present that evaluation should track the robot's evolving trajectory rather than a fixed checkpoint, and we identify the lack of a benchmark designed specifically for self-evolving humanoids. Moreover, we outline open challenges spanning AI algorithms, on-board systems, and governance.

</details>


### [5] [GzDRL: Reproducible and Scalable Deep Reinforcement Learning with Gazebo](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13243)
*Amal Dev Haridevan,Junjie Kang,Jinjun Shan*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: GzDRL is a single-process, middleware-free reinforcement learning framework for Gazebo that enables deterministic, high-throughput, reproducible robotics experimentation with validated sim-to-real transfer on a physical quadrotor.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional middleware-based RL-Gazebo integrations suffer from nondeterminism and irreproducibility, limiting scalable and reproducible robotics experimentation.

Method: Introduces a systematic, middleware-free environment-stepping mechanism that directly synchronizes agent actions and physics updates, enabling deterministic, high-throughput data collection, efficient vectorization, and reproducible training and evaluation.

Result: Benchmarks show the highest workstation throughput among evaluated frameworks, competitive performance with GPU-accelerated simulators on laptop hardware, precise agent-environment synchronization, multi-agent scalability, and experiment-level reproducibility. Sim-to-real transfer is validated by deploying learned policies directly onto a physical quadrotor without fine-tuning.

Conclusion: GzDRL is established as an accessible and reproducible platform for advancing RL in robotics and automation.

Abstract: We present GzDRL, a novel single-process reinforcement learning (RL) framework for Gazebo that overcomes longstanding bottlenecks in scalable, reproducible robotics experimentation. Unlike conventional middleware-based RL-Gazebo integrations that suffer from nondeterminism and irreproducibility, GzDRL introduces a systematic, middleware-free environment-stepping mechanism that directly synchronizes agent actions and physics updates. This design enables deterministic, high-throughput data collection, efficient vectorization, and reproducible RL training and evaluation. Comprehensive benchmarks demonstrate that GzDRL achieves the highest workstation throughput among the evaluated frameworks while remaining competitive with GPU-accelerated simulators on laptop hardware, and maintains precise agent-environment synchronization, multi-agent scalability, and experiment-level reproducibility. We further validate sim-to-real transfer by deploying learned policies directly onto a physical quadrotor, without fine-tuning. Our results establish GzDRL as an accessible and reproducible platform for advancing RL in robotics and automation.

</details>


### [6] [Physical Kernel: Structured Visual Latents for Dark Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13244)
*Jinting Hang,Hong Li,Zhenhui Cai,Zhihao Zhao,Jian He*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper studies "dark manipulation": performing contact-rich manipulation skills from a single brief visual write (z0 = Enc(rgb)) followed by policy and open-loop dynamics operating without further pixels (dark_f), and characterizes when this works versus per-step lighting/re-encoding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional pixel-based policies require continuous vision, which is costly and fragile to occlusion and appearance shifts. The authors ask whether a single encoded latent can sustain a full contact-rich skill chain, isolating the role of the initial write versus ongoing visual feedback.

Method: On ManiSkill StackCube (n=160; seeds 0/1000), a lit Write encodes z0, after which a policy pi(z) and open-loop dynamics f(z,a) complete the five-rung chain (near_A -> grasped -> lifted -> on_B -> stacked). Baselines include per-step lit_reenc, freeze/encode_black, a shared Write->HOLD protocol (n=40), occlusion and camera-aligned GT contact-neighbor masks, action shuffling, write-time vs HOLD-time appearance shifts, Write-length Tw ablations, blur/JPEG perturbations, a dedicated pi_write (n=80; vision-budget), Dreamer-style/pixel nulls, and privileged state-RSSM MPC with 9D dark observations.

Result: dark_f reaches 68.1% stacked vs 35.6% for per-step lit_reenc and 0% for freeze/encode_black. Under Write->HOLD, occlusion and camera-aligned GT contact-neighbor masks drop lit lift from 43% to 0% while dark_f holds 82.5%. Action shuffling inflates dynamics MSE ~9.4x. Write-time appearance shifts break encoding (night: 0% stacked), but identical shifts during HOLD leave dark_f lift unchanged. Tw is flat once the stop phase is reached, while earlier stops and write-time blur/JPEG sharply reduce stacked. pi_write reaches 35% vision-budget stacked (n=80); matched Dreamer-style/pixel nulls without privileged geom stay at 0%. Privileged state-RSSM MPC reaches ~35% stacked with 9D dark observations (a stronger-observation null).

Conclusion: A single well-timed visual write can support contact-rich skills without ongoing pixels, and dark_f is robust to HOLD-time occlusion and appearance shifts that break lit re-encoding. Performance hinges on the write quality and timing, and gains from privileged-observation MPC are not a matched visual baseline.

Abstract: We study dark manipulation: after a brief lit Write encodes z0 = Enc(rgb), a policy pi(z) and open-loop dynamics f(z,a) complete contact-rich skills without further pixels (dark_f). On ManiSkill StackCube (n=160; seed packs 0/1000), dark_f attains 68.1% stacked on the five-rung chain (near_A -> grasped -> lifted -> on_B -> stacked), compared with 35.6% for per-step lit_reenc and 0% for freeze/encode_black. On a shared Write->HOLD protocol (n=40), occlusion and camera-aligned GT contact-neighbor masks drive lit lift from 43% to 0%, while dark_f holds 82.5%; shuffling actions inflates dynamics MSE by ~9.4x; write-time appearance shifts break encoding (night: 0% stacked), yet the same shifts during HOLD leave dark_f lift unchanged; Write length Tw is flat once the stop phase is reached, while earlier stops and write-time blur/JPEG sharply cut stacked. A dedicated pi_write reaches 35% vision-budget stacked (n=80); matched Dreamer-style/pixel nulls without privileged geom stay at 0%. Privileged state-RSSM MPC reaches ~35% stacked with 9D dark observations -- a stronger-observation null, not a matched visual baseline.

</details>


### [7] [Conflict-Predictive Variable Horizons in Multi-Drone Distributed Model Predictive Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13270)
*Linda Mümken,Michael Schwung,Stefan Lier,Andreas Schwung*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A conflict-predictive variable prediction horizon is proposed for distributed MPC multi-drone collision avoidance. Each drone locally extrapolates neighbor flight lines, computes times to conflict in closed form using narrowing confidence funnels, and selects the smallest horizon covering the farthest predicted conflict. Recursive feasibility and asymptotic stability are proven down to a computable minimum bound, and simulations show reduced solver cost versus long fixed horizons while preserving separation, unlike short fixed horizons.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In distributed MPC for multi-drone collision avoidance, a fixed prediction horizon imposes a trade-off: short horizons are cheap per step but react late to approaching neighbors, whereas long horizons anticipate conflicts but incur superlinearly growing per-step cost. This motivates a horizon that adapts to actual conflict risk.

Method: Each drone locally sets a conflict-predictive variable horizon without modifying the underlying distributed MPC. From a short history of observed positions, it extrapolates neighbors' flight lines and tests each against its own using confidence funnels that narrow with prediction range, yielding times to conflict in closed form. The horizon is the smallest admissible value whose planning window covers the farthest predicted conflict, collapsing to the minimum in clear airspace. Stability guarantees are established for a linear model; a cascaded inner loop reduces quadrotor translational dynamics to a perturbed double integrator, carrying guarantees to the linearized quadrotor model and, as practical stability, to the full nonlinear one.

Result: Provided the minimum horizon meets a single computable feasibility bound, recursive feasibility and asymptotic stability are proven for every horizon the policy can select. On dense antipodal-swap benchmarks, the variable horizon lowers both per-step solver cost and total computation well below a long fixed horizon, and maintains separation in every run, which a short fixed horizon of comparable per-step cost fails to do.

Conclusion: A locally computed conflict-predictive variable horizon enables efficient, provably stable distributed MPC collision avoidance, achieving long-horizon safety at short-horizon cost, with guarantees extending from linear to linearized and (practically) nonlinear quadrotor models.

Abstract: In distributed model predictive control for multi-drone collision avoidance, a fixed prediction horizon forces a compromise: a short horizon is inexpensive but reacts late to approaching neighbors, whereas a long one anticipates conflicts at a per-step cost that grows superlinearly with its length. We propose a conflict-predictive variable horizon that each drone sets locally, leaving the distributed model predictive control itself unchanged. From a short history of observed positions, a drone extrapolates the flight lines of its neighbors, tests each against its own using confidence funnels that narrow with prediction range, and obtains each time to conflict in closed form. The horizon is then the smallest admissible value whose planning window covers the farthest predicted conflict. It collapses to its minimum in clear airspace and grows only when a conflict lies ahead. Provided this minimum meets a single computable feasibility bound, we prove that recursive feasibility and asymptotic stability are preserved for every horizon the policy can select. These guarantees hold for a linear model, and a cascaded inner loop reduces each quadrotor's translational dynamics to a perturbed double integrator, so they carry over to the linearized quadrotor model and, as practical stability, to the full nonlinear one. In simulation on dense antipodal-swap benchmarks, the variable horizon reduces both per-step solver cost and total computation well below those of a long fixed horizon, and it maintains separation in every run, which a short fixed horizon of comparable per-step cost does not.

</details>


### [8] [Constraint-Grounded Reinforcement Learning for Variable Impedance Control in Contact-Rich Robotic Insertion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13516)
*Lin He,Min Deng*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: CG-RL is a variable impedance framework that adapts controller gains online using a force-limit-conditioned RL policy with a projection layer keeping gains admissible, enabling a single policy to operate under different force limits without manual retuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In robotic insertion under uncertain contact, the axial force limit and appropriate controller gain vary across tasks, so a single fixed gain is unlikely to suit different conditions, forcing conventional impedance controllers to rely on manual retuning.

Method: Conditioned on the force limit and contact feedback, the policy outputs a residual motion, an insertion rate, and a requested gain. The controller projects this gain into the admissible range without exposing the range itself to the policy, separating range knowledge from policy learning.

Result: On simulated oblique insertion across five training seeds, CG-RL achieves 85.8±7.7% (mean ± SD) success without violating the force limit while keeping the applied gain admissible, versus 50.1% for a fixed midpoint-gain baseline. The policy continuously adapts insertion rate to the specified force limit and generalizes to more permissive limits above the training range, whereas the same actor without force-limit input does not.

Conclusion: The applied gain is guaranteed to remain within the admissible range, while force-limit satisfaction is validated empirically rather than guaranteed formally.

Abstract: In robotic insertion under uncertain contact, the axial force limit and the appropriate controller gain vary across tasks. As a result, a single fixed gain is unlikely to remain suitable across different task conditions, making conventional impedance controllers reliant on manual retuning. To eliminate manual retuning, we propose Constraint-Grounded Reinforcement Learning (CG-RL), a variable impedance framework for online gain adaptation. Conditioned on the force limit and contact feedback, the policy outputs a residual motion, an insertion rate, and a requested gain. The controller projects this gain into the admissible range without exposing the range itself to the policy. This separation allows a single policy to operate under different force limits without retraining or manual retuning. We evaluate CG-RL on simulated oblique insertion across five training seeds. CG-RL achieves an $85.8\pm7.7\%$ (mean $\pm$ SD) success rate of insertions without violating the force limit, while keeping the applied gain within the admissible range. As a comparison, a fixed-gain baseline using the midpoint gain achieves a success rate of $50.1\%$. The policy adapts its insertion rate continuously to the specified force limit and further generalizes to more permissive force limits above the training range. In contrast, the same actor without force-limit input does not exhibit this adaptation. The applied gain is guaranteed to remain within the admissible range, while force-limit satisfaction is validated empirically rather than guaranteed formally.

</details>


### [9] [From Vision to Harvest: Benchmarking Vision-Language Models for Multi-Arm Robotic Fruit Harvesting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13606)
*Vrishan Inukollu,Adyan Zaman,Anvi Kudaraya,Carlos Lazcano,Yuankai Zhu,Stavros Vougioukas,Xiaofan Yu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper introduces the first benchmark for evaluating pretrained VLMs on zero-shot multi-arm fruit harvesting planning using real orchard images, comparing a VLM-based pipeline against a traditional perception-and-planning pipeline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-arm robotic harvesting can boost efficiency and reduce manual labor, but deployment is hard: the system must generalize across environments and coordinate multiple arms in a shared workspace. Existing methods need extensive target-environment data collection or rely on simplifying assumptions that degrade planning quality.

Method: The authors build a benchmark with real-world apple and citrus orchard images. They compare a VLM-based planning pipeline—where a pretrained VLM directly generates harvesting sequences and waypoints for each arm, with a lightweight trajectory verifier checking for collisions—against a traditional perception-and-planning pipeline.

Result: Frontier VLMs can generate effective multi-arm harvesting plans zero-shot. However, practical deployment remains limited by accurate 3D waypoint generation and collision-aware coordination.

Conclusion: The study highlights both the promise and current limitations of pretrained VLMs for multi-arm robotic harvesting, establishing a benchmark and identifying 3D waypoint accuracy and collision-aware coordination as key bottlenecks.

Abstract: Multi-arm robotic harvesting offers a promising path to improve harvesting efficiency and reduce reliance on manual labor. However, practical deployment remains challenging because the system must generalize across diverse environments while efficiently coordinating multiple arms in a shared workspace. Existing methods often require substantial data collection in target environments or rely on simplifying assumptions that limit planning quality. In this work, we introduce the first comprehensive benchmark for evaluating pretrained Vision-Language Models (VLMs) on zero-shot multi-arm fruit harvesting planning. Our benchmark uses real-world apple and citrus orchard images and compares a VLM-based planning pipeline with a traditional perception-and-planning pipeline. The VLM pipeline directly generates harvesting sequences and waypoints for each arm, while a lightweight trajectory verifier checks for collisions. Our results show that frontier VLMs can generate effective multi-arm harvesting plans zero-shot, but a practical deployment remains limited by accurate 3D waypoint generation and collision-aware coordination. These results highlight both the promise and current limitations of pretrained VLMs for multi-arm robotic harvesting.

</details>


### [10] [Compositional Shift Algebra: Extrapolating Mixed Robot Shifts Without Mixed Finetuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13651)
*Jinting Hang,Zhenhui Cai*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Compositional Shift Algebra (CSA) learns single-factor observation, policy, and dynamics shift operators on a modular robot stack from exact-reset probes, then composes them to extrapolate to held-out mixed shifts without mixed-shift finetuning, achieving near-oracle compositional generalization on manipulation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robot deployments rarely change one mechanism at a time; cameras, action interfaces, and physical dynamics often shift together. Existing adaptation recipes either finetune a new model for every mix of shifts or try to select which module to update, both of which scale poorly to combinatorial shift combinations.

Method: CSA operates on a modular stack z=E(o), a=g(z,u), z'=f(z,a). It fits single-factor observation, policy, and dynamics operators from exact-reset probes, then extrapolates held-out mixed shifts by composing these operators, avoiding mixed-shift finetuning. Evaluations include residual CSA, an RGB-D vision-in-the-loop variant, a delay commutator stress test (ordered policy×delay), and an L1 vision controller without privileged cues.

Result: On ManiSkill StackCube, residual CSA matches an oracle mixed inverse (success 1.0 over 10 seeds) and beats best-single/zero-shot/parameter-average baselines by ~67 pp; RGB-D composition stays near oracle. On PickCube, residual CSA reaches compose 1.0 vs. 0.33 non-compositional (n=10), and the L1 vision controller retains compose 0.95 vs. 0.00. Main-track upgrades freeze PushCube (+33 pp), PegInsertion joint8/pose7 EE (+67 pp each), and thin BC under frozen CSA (+67 pp); deeper BC, fair adapt baselines, and vision-localized BC still need compose (+67/+67/+56 pp). Delay favors ordered/few-shot deploy. Intervention-Gated Adaptation is reported as a negative control.

Conclusion: Operator composition on a modular stack enables compositional generalization to held-out mixed shifts without mixed-shift finetuning, substantially outperforming non-compositional and single-shift adaptation baselines across manipulation tasks, while ordered composition matters under delay and Intervention-Gated Adaptation fails as a control.

Abstract: Robot deployments rarely change one mechanism at a time: cameras, action interfaces, and physical dynamics often shift together. Prior adaptation recipes either finetune a new model for every mix or attempt to select which module to update. We instead learn shift operators on a modular stack z{=}E(o), a{=}g(z,u), z'{=}f(z,a) and compose them. Compositional Shift Algebra (CSA) fits single-factor observation, policy, and dynamics operators from exact-reset probes, then extrapolates held-out mixed shifts by operator composition---without mixed-shift finetuning. On ManiSkill StackCube, residual CSA matches an oracle mixed inverse on held-out mixes (success 1.0 over 10 seeds) while beating best-single / zero-shot / parameter-average baselines by { approx}67 pp. RGB-D vision-in-the-loop composition remains near oracle and far above non-compositional arms; a delay commutator stress shows ordered necessity for policy timesdelay. On a second task (PickCube), residual CSA again reaches compose 1.0 vs. 0.33 non-compositional (n{=}10), and an L1 vision controller without privileged cube/goal poses or grasp flags in the control loop retains compose 0.95 vs. 0.00. Main-track upgrades freeze PushCube (+33 pp), PegInsertion joint8 / pose7 EE (+67 pp each), and thin BC under frozen CSA (+67 pp); deeper BC and fair adapt baselines still need compose (+67 pp each), vision-localized BC needs compose (+56 pp), and delay favors ordered/few-shot deploy. We report Intervention-Gated Adaptation as a negative control.

</details>


### [11] [Does Online Gravity Estimation Matter? Revisiting a Silent Design Split in LiDAR-Inertial Odometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13675)
*Jie Xu,Ziyi Jin,Kangjin Yu,Can Jiang,Hongjun Huang,Tongxing Jin,Hongkun Luo,Zhongpu Xia*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This study empirically compares online versus fixed gravity-bias estimation in LiDAR-inertial odometry systems (FAST-LIO2 and LIO-SAM), finding that keeping gravity and accelerometer bias online is generally more robust, with a gravity-direction factor only conditionally beneficial.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LIO systems differ in whether they continue estimating gravity after initialization, but there is a lack of clear empirical evidence on the practical trade-offs of fixing versus maintaining online gravity, particularly under challenging conditions like LiDAR outages and varying trajectories.

Method: The authors compared four gravity-bias state configurations across both FAST-LIO2 and LIO-SAM, evaluated a separate gravity-direction factor, tested 12 dataset sequences with FAST-LIO2 and 4 with LIO-SAM, examined multi-second LiDAR outages versus reduced range/FOV, performed a history-matched 23D-to-21D switch, and conducted 5-s outage and dynamic-start tests.

Result: Fixing gravity under continuous LiDAR correction yielded mean paired changes in vertical and 3D position errors with 90% confidence intervals within ±2%; LIO-SAM showed no consistent benefit from online gravity; multi-second LiDAR outages revealed trajectory-dependent costs of fixing gravity; the direction factor improved Hall05 but worsened TUHH results; dynamic-start tests revealed fixed-bias failures at particular starting phases.

Conclusion: The authors recommend keeping gravity and accelerometer bias online for robustness, and using a gravity-direction factor only after verifying vertical and 3D accuracy gains under the intended operating conditions.

Abstract: LiDAR-inertial odometry (LIO) systems differ in whether they continue estimating gravity after initialization. We compare four gravity-bias state configurations in each of FAST-LIO2 and LIO-SAM, then separately test a gravity-direction factor. Across 12 dataset sequences evaluated with FAST-LIO2, fixing gravity under continuous LiDAR correction produces mean paired changes in vertical and 3D position errors with 90% confidence intervals within $\pm 2\%$. Tests on 4 sequences with LIO-SAM likewise show no consistent benefit from online gravity. Multi-second LiDAR outages, unlike reduced range or field of view, reveal trajectory-dependent costs of fixing gravity. A history-matched 23D-to-21D switch places the repeatable 3D error increase after LiDAR updates resume. Under 5-s outages, a direction factor from the same IMU used for preintegration improves accuracy on Hall05 but worsens both errors with online gravity on TUHH. Dynamic-start tests also show fixed-bias failures at particular starting phases. We recommend keeping gravity and accelerometer bias online for robustness; use a direction factor only after verifying vertical and 3D accuracy gains under the intended operating conditions.

</details>


### [12] [How to Better Train VLAs: Lessons Learned From the REAL-I Challenge at ICRA 2026](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13679)
*Jiaming Wang,Jizhuo Chen,Diwen Liu,Wang Song,Qiang Wang,Jie Ren,Chao Fu,Dingkun Zhu,Minchi Ruan,Hongtong Li,Yuhua Jiang,Zhiwei Xue,Yongping Pan,Harold Soh*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The first REAL-I Challenge at ICRA 2026 studied how robot policies can learn effectively under a fixed demonstration budget, using simulation, real-robot evaluation, and an on-site final on a shared dual-arm humanoid. Comparing three teams' systems reveals key practices: adapting to deployment while retaining priors, curating demonstrations at proper temporal scale, and suppressing inactive-component errors. Offline action-prediction metrics proved poor predictors of closed-loop success.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how robot policies can learn more effectively when the demonstration budget is fixed.

Method: A challenge design combining simulation, real-robot evaluation, and an on-site final on a shared dual-arm humanoid platform; description of tasks, data and deployment interfaces; and comparative analysis of systems from NUS-CLEAR, RCL-Lab, and Deeptouch.ai, which combined pretrained vision-language-action models with task-specific imitation policies using differing strategies for data curation, staged adaptation, checkpoint selection, and action-space design.

Result: The teams' systems demonstrated the importance of adapting to the deployment environment while retaining prior capabilities, treating demonstration quality at an appropriate temporal scale, and suppressing errors in inactive robot components; additionally, offline action-prediction metrics were found to be limited in forecasting closed-loop success.

Conclusion: These observations motivate a view of fixed-data robot learning that integrates data, adaptation, evaluation, and deployment.

Abstract: How can robot policies learn more effectively from a fixed demonstration budget? The first Real-world Embodied AI Learning (REAL-I) Challenge at ICRA 2026 examined this question through simulation, real-robot evaluation, and an on-site final on a shared dual-arm humanoid platform. We describe the challenge tasks, data and deployment interfaces, and competition results, then compare the approaches contributed by NUS-CLEAR, RCL-Lab, and Deeptouch.ai. Their systems combined pretrained vision-language-action models and task-specific imitation policies with different strategies for data curation, staged adaptation, checkpoint selection, and action-space design. The team reports highlight the importance of adapting to the deployment environment while retaining prior capabilities, treating demonstration quality at an appropriate temporal scale, and suppressing errors in inactive robot components. They also expose the limitations of offline action-prediction metrics for forecasting closed-loop success. These observations motivate a view of fixed-data robot learning that integrates data, adaptation, evaluation, and deployment.

</details>


### [13] [GROOVE: Geometry-Guided Reduction of Operational-Space Jerk in VLA Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13695)
*Sangho Yun,Minsoo Kim,Minwoo Cho,Hwanjo Yu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: GROOVE is an online regulator that reduces jerk in chunked VLA policies by searching directional correction regions around the raw EEF path using QPs, without retraining or extra VLA inference, achieving lower jerk while preserving task success.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chunked VLA policies execute multiple commands per query, but jerk within chunks and at replanning boundaries causes oscillatory motion and sharp actuator transients, which is undesirable for smooth, safe execution.

Method: GROOVE performs online optimization on the new chunk, using delivered commands as boundary conditions to reduce boundary and within-chunk jerk while bounding cumulative translation and local axis-angle deviation. It uses quadratic programs to generate a cube reference and thirteen directional candidates, then selects the one with lowest command-space jerk under a reference-relative deviation cap. It requires no retraining or additional VLA inference.

Result: On a held-out LIBERO benchmark, GROOVE reduces translational and rotational EEF jerk by 33.02% and 43.42% respectively, with 95.75% task success vs 93.75% for raw execution. Across 50 matched UR5e pairs with measured timing, it reduces translational and rotational TCP jerk by 16.39% and 19.49% and joint current slew by 29.09%.

Conclusion: GROOVE effectively smooths chunked VLA policy execution, achieving the largest jerk reductions among evaluated methods while maintaining or improving task success, and generalizing to real hardware with reduced joint current slew.

Abstract: Chunked vision language action (VLA) policies execute several commands per query, but jerk within chunks and across replanning boundaries can induce oscillatory motion and sharp actuator transients. We present GROOVE, an online regulator that searches directional correction regions around the raw three dimensional end effector (EEF) path, without retraining or additional VLA inference. It optimizes the new chunk using delivered commands as boundary conditions, reducing boundary and within chunk jerk while bounding cumulative translation and local axis angle deviation from the raw plan after every command. Using quadratic programs (QPs), GROOVE generates a cube reference and thirteen directional candidates, then selects the one with the lowest command space jerk under a reference relative deviation cap. On a held out LIBERO benchmark, GROOVE achieves the largest reductions among the evaluated methods, reducing translational and rotational EEF jerk by 33.02% and 43.42%, respectively, with task success of 95.75% versus 93.75% for raw execution. Across 50 matched UR5e pairs with measured execution timing, it reduces translational and rotational tool center point (TCP) jerk by 16.39% and 19.49% and joint current slew by 29.09%.

</details>


### [14] [Decentralized Multi-Robot Task Allocation Under Degraded Communication: A Benchmark of Performance, Reliability, and Computation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13711)
*James Lott,Vahraz Honary*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper benchmarks six decentralized Multi-Robot Task Allocation (MRTA) allocators (CBAA, ACBBA, PI, HIPC, DMCHBA, DGA) in a Collaborative Visit scenario, characterizing tradeoffs across travel objectives, communication robustness, reliability, computation, and scale.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Selecting a decentralized MRTA method for embedded deployment on autonomous platforms requires evaluating more than route performance alone; a multi-dimensional characterization of tradeoffs is needed.

Method: 500 paired ten-target instances across 25 ideal and degraded communication conditions (Bernoulli loss, Gilbert-Elliott loss, Rayleigh fading), plus campaigns on pre-allocation, execution-integrated computation, and sensitivity to grid size, robot density, and target load.

Result: Across 24 impaired conditions, DGA and DMCHBA had the lowest mean MinMax travel (24.49 and 24.78 steps); HIPC led mean MinSum (66.95) followed by DGA (67.22), both topping in every impaired condition. DMCHBA had lowest publication intensity (2.08 pub/team step). In pre-allocation, HIPC and DMCHBA stayed viable/stable; ACBBA, PI, and DGA lost stability or viability as communication degraded. Median full-protocol computation ranged from 4.88 ms (DMCHBA) to 1.346 s (DGA). DGA led MinSum at three of four target loads; HIPC led at 50 targets.

Conclusion: The results identify distinct allocator operating regions defined by route objective, communication behavior, reliability, and computational constraints, guiding deployment-appropriate selection.

Abstract: Selecting a decentralized Multi-Robot Task Allocation (MRTA) method for embedded deployment on autonomous platforms requires considering more than route performance alone. We benchmark six decentralized MRTA allocators (CBAA, ACBBA, PI, HIPC, DMCHBA, and DGA) in the Collaborative Visit (CV) scenario to characterize tradeoffs among MinMax and MinSum travel, communication robustness and demand, allocation reliability, computational burden, and scale sensitivity. The core study uses 500 paired ten-target instances across 25 ideal and degraded communication conditions spanning Bernoulli loss, Gilbert--Elliott loss, and Rayleigh fading, with additional campaigns examining pre-allocation, execution-integrated computation, and sensitivity to grid size, robot density, and target load. Across the 24 impaired core conditions, DGA and DMCHBA achieved the lowest mean MinMax travel at 24.49 and 24.78 steps, respectively. HIPC narrowly led mean MinSum travel at 66.95 steps, followed by DGA at 67.22, with both methods occupying the top two in every impaired condition. DMCHBA had the lowest publication intensity at 2.08 publications per team step. In ten-target pre-allocation, HIPC and DMCHBA remained viable and stable in every tested condition, while ACBBA, PI, and DGA lost stability or viability as communication degraded. Under ideal delivery, median full-protocol computation $\Cterm$ in the primary ten-target comparison ranged from 4.88 ms for DMCHBA to 1.346 s for DGA. Static route quality preserved DGA and DMCHBA as the leading MinMax methods, while DGA led MinSum at three of four target loads and HIPC led at 50 targets. Static and execution-integrated computation rankings diverged as task load increased. The results identify distinct allocator operating regions across route objective, communication behavior, reliability, and computational constraints.

</details>


### [15] [Learning In-Hand Object Reaching to General 6D Poses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13761)
*Junxiao Lin,Tianyue Wu,Jie Yin,Jia Pan,Kaifeng Zhang,Weiming Zhi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: POISE is a sim-to-real RL framework enabling multi-fingered hands to move a grasped object to palm-relative 6D target poses (position + orientation) without regrasping.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In-hand manipulation boosts efficiency by avoiding repeated grasp acquisition and large arm motions, but most learning-based methods only handle reorientation, continuous rotation, or translation. Many real tasks require joint control of object position and orientation, i.e., full 6D pose reaching.

Method: The authors formulate in-hand 6D object pose reaching from an existing grasp and propose POISE (Palm-relative Object reaching In SE(3)), a sim-to-real reinforcement learning framework. POISE combines diverse stable-grasp initialization, goal- and geometry-conditioned control, an adaptive 6D goal curriculum, and a compact reward scheme for pose reaching and grasp preservation.

Result: In simulation, diverse initialization raises held-out-grasp success from 40.1% to 51.5% and post-drop recovery from 33.8% to 72.9%; the curriculum raises full-range success from 6.2% to 59.5%. On hardware, the grasp-maintenance reward improves three-target sequence success from 20% to 80%. Real-world experiments show POISE reaches successive 6D targets without manual reset across multiple object geometries and wrist orientations, and recovers from external disturbances.

Conclusion: POISE enables robust real-world in-hand 6D pose reaching and disturbance recovery. Code will be released to support further dexterous manipulation research.

Abstract: In-hand manipulation allows multi-fingered dexterous hands to reconfigure grasped objects without releasing and regrasping them. This improves manipulation efficiency by reducing repeated grasp acquisition and large arm motions. However, most learning-based methods focus on reorientation, continuous rotation, or translation, whereas many tasks require joint control of object position and orientation. We formulate this capability as in-hand 6D object pose reaching: starting from an existing grasp, coordinated finger motions move the object to a palm-relative target pose. We present POISE (Palm-relative Object reaching In SE(3)), a sim-to-real reinforcement learning framework for this task. POISE combines diverse stable-grasp initialization, goal- and geometry-conditioned control, an adaptive 6D goal curriculum, and a compact reward scheme for pose reaching and grasp preservation. In simulation, diverse initialization raises held-out-grasp success from 40.1% to 51.5% and post-drop recovery from 33.8% to 72.9%; the curriculum raises full-range success from 6.2% to 59.5%. On hardware, the grasp-maintenance reward improves three-target sequence success from 20% to 80%. In real-world experiments, POISE reaches successive 6D targets without manual reset across multiple object geometries and wrist orientations, and recovers from external disturbances. To support further research in dexterous manipulation, we will release our code at https://junxiaolin.github.io/poise-website/.

</details>


### [16] [Force-Aware Reinforcement Learning with Hybrid Sensorless Force Estimation for Wheeled-Legged Loco-Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13779)
*Xuanqi Zeng,Jiaming Wang,Tianlin Zhang,Lingwei Zhang,Botian Xu,Weipeng Xia,Zhongyu Li,Yun-Hui Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A force-aware RL framework with hybrid sensorless force estimation enables wheeled-legged loco-manipulation to regulate contact forces without an end-effector F/T sensor, combining model-based momentum/contact-wrench estimation with temporal residual learning and a mode-conditioned whole-body policy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Force-controlled loco-manipulation requires whole-body coordination of locomotion and arm motion under floating-base dynamics and changing support contacts, which is difficult. The problem is exacerbated when end-effector force/torque sensing is unavailable for control.

Method: The method estimates end-effector force by combining generalized momentum observation, contact-constrained wrench projection, and temporal residual learning: model-based components extract the physically structured whole-body disturbance, and a residual network compensates motion-dependent bias. The estimate is fed as an explicit observation into a mode-conditioned whole-body policy with an axis-wise force/position selector, supporting free-space motion, pure force regulation, and hybrid force/position control within a single controller.

Result: Simulation results show improved sensorless force estimation accuracy and force-control performance. Hardware experiments validate the controller through quantitative valve-rotation and hybrid wiping evaluations, plus force-guided door opening and zero-force human-guided motion on a real wheeled-legged platform.

Conclusion: The proposed force-aware RL approach with hybrid sensorless force estimation enables effective force-controlled loco-manipulation on wheeled-legged robots without relying on an end-effector force/torque sensor for control, demonstrating both estimation and control benefits in simulation and hardware.

Abstract: Force-controlled loco-manipulation requires a whole-body policy to coordinate locomotion and arm motion while regulating end-effector interaction forces. This is challenging under floating-base dynamics and changing support contacts, particularly when end-effector force/torque sensing is unavailable for control. This paper presents a force-aware reinforcement learning approach with hybrid sensorless force estimation for wheeled-legged loco-manipulation. The proposed method provides a structured estimate of the end-effector force as an explicit policy observation, enabling force-guided contact behavior without using an end-effector force/torque sensor for control. The force estimate is obtained by combining generalized momentum observation, contact-constrained wrench projection, and temporal residual learning: the model-based components extract the physically structured part of the whole-body disturbance, while the residual network compensates the remaining motion-dependent bias. The estimated force is integrated into a mode-conditioned whole-body policy with an axis-wise force/position selector, allowing free-space motion, pure force regulation, and hybrid force/position control within one controller. Simulation results demonstrate improved sensorless force estimation and force-control performance. Hardware experiments further validate the proposed controller through quantitative valve-rotation and hybrid wiping evaluations, together with force-guided door opening and zero-force human-guided motion on a real wheeled-legged platform.

</details>


### [17] [LePlanner: An Iterative Amortized Controller For World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13845)
*Saksham Bansal,Om Naphade,Chayan Aggarwal,Vrishin M*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: LePlanner is an amortized iterative controller that learns to construct and refine latent action sequences via a frozen world-model predictor, matching search-based planners at a fraction of the compute (10x fewer predictor evaluations, 3-49x lower latency).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Planning in learned latent world models typically relies on either search-based methods (CEM, MPPI, iCEM) that need many predictor rollouts and incur high per-decision compute/latency, or policy-based methods that amortize inference into a single pass but degrade on contact-rich, multimodal demonstration distributions. The authors seek a controller that combines search-quality performance with amortized inference efficiency.

Method: LePlanner is an amortized iterative controller that learns to construct and refine latent action sequences through a frozen world-model predictor. It is trained with an arrival-and-hold objective that encourages reaching the goal at the earliest feasible horizon and staying there, mitigating horizon-reset procrastination (repeated receding-horizon replanning deferring goal arrival). An additional action-Gaussian loss keeps generated actions near the offline dataset's support.

Result: Across navigation, contact-rich manipulation, and continuous-control environments, LePlanner matches or exceeds search-based planners with an order of magnitude fewer predictor evaluations and 3-49x lower wall-clock time per decision. Success rates: 98% on PushT, 100% on Reacher, 100% on TwoRooms, and 92% on OGBench Cube.

Conclusion: Much of the structure discovered through online search can be amortized into a lightweight iterative policy, enabling fast, horizon-aware, nonlinear physical control without online optimization.

Abstract: World models trained with joint-embedding predictive architectures learn compact, structured latent representations from physical interaction, yet planning in these latent spaces typically relies on one of two costly approaches. Search-based planners such as CEM, MPPI, and iCEM optimize action sequences through many predictor rollouts, achieving strong performance at the cost of high per-decision compute and latency. Policy-based methods amortize inference into a single forward pass but can degrade on contact-rich tasks where the demonstration distribution is multimodal. We propose LePlanner, an amortized iterative controller that learns to construct and refine latent action sequences through a frozen world-model predictor. LePlanner is trained with an arrival-and-hold objective that encourages the controller to reach the goal at the earliest feasible horizon and remain there. This addresses horizon-reset procrastination, a failure mode in which repeated receding-horizon replanning continually postpones goal arrival. An additional action-Gaussian loss keeps generated actions near the support of the offline dataset. Across navigation, contact-rich manipulation, and continuous-control environments, LePlanner matches or exceeds search-based planners while requiring an order of magnitude fewer predictor evaluations and 3-49x lower wall-clock time per decision. It achieves success rates of 98% on PushT, 100% on Reacher, 100% on TwoRooms, and 92% on the OGBench Cube task. These results show that much of the structure discovered through online search can be amortized into a lightweight iterative policy, enabling fast, horizon-aware, nonlinear physical control without online optimization.

</details>


### [18] [What Makes an Efficient VLA? Navigating Action-Head Design, Scaling, and Latency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13984)
*Luoyang Sun,Guoyang Xia,Fengfa Li,Lei Ren,Xinyu Cui,Haifeng Zhang,Fangxiang Feng,Kaike Zhang,Kun Zhan,Yan Xie,Jun Wang,Cheng Deng*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A controlled study of Vision-Language-Action models shows that action-head initialization (copying the language backbone's last transformer layers) is the dominant lever for performance, while architectural, loss, and inference-budget choices matter only when the head is misaligned; scaling the aligned action head gives the best returns but saturates near current VLA sizes, yielding EffVLA, a compact model matching top open-source VLAs on LIBERO at lower latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLA models combine a pretrained vision encoder, a language backbone, and an action head, but the relative contribution of each component has not been established under controlled, latency-paired conditions. The paper aims to disentangle which design choices actually drive performance by fixing backbone families and training pipeline while systematically varying action-head design and scale.

Method: The authors fix the backbone families (SigLIP2 and Qwen2.5) and the training pipeline, then sweep action-head design and module scale, pairing each configuration with measured on-device latency. They analyze initialization (copying the last transformer layers of the language backbone into the head), decoder architecture, loss (flow matching), inference budget, and module scaling, supported by representational evidence on attention to object nouns and weight-space proximity to the backbone.

Result: Three findings: (1) action-head performance is governed primarily by initialization rather than decoder architecture, loss, or inference budget—copying the language backbone's last transformer layers is the single largest, latency-free lever and helps at every module scale; alignment also explains the other axes, since flow matching and a heavier decoder pay off only while misaligned and reverse once aligned, and extra inference passes give no measurable benefit. (2) Capacity pays only after alignment, with the aligned action head the highest-return module to scale. (3) Returns diminish sharply near the size current π-series VLAs already use. The resulting EffVLA matches the strongest open-source VLAs on standard LIBERO, leads on most LIBERO-Plus perturbation axes at lower latency, and transfers to a real SO-ARM101 arm with the recipe unchanged.

Conclusion: Alignment via initialization is the key organizing account for the measurements, though it is offered as the best explanation rather than a demonstrated cause, with a named control proposed to settle it. Capacity pays only after alignment, and its returns saturate near current VLA scales, indicating further growth buys little in-domain accuracy for its latency. These principles specify EffVLA, a compact and efficient VLA that is competitive with larger open-source models and transfers to real hardware without recipe changes.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models combine a pretrained vision encoder, a language backbone, and an action head, but their relative contribution has not been established under controlled, latency-paired conditions. We fix the backbone families (SigLIP2 and Qwen2.5) and the training pipeline, sweep action-head design and module scale, and pair each configuration with measured on-device latency. The study yields three findings. First, action-head performance is governed primarily by initialization rather than decoder architecture, loss, or inference budget: copying the last transformer layers of the language backbone into the head is the single largest lever, at no latency cost, and the only axis that helps at every module scale. Alignment also explains the other axes: flow matching and a heavier decoder pay off only while the head is misaligned and reverse once it is aligned, and extra inference passes give no measurable benefit; expressiveness appears to substitute for missing alignment. We read this as representation transfer: the aligned head keeps attending to the instruction's object nouns and stays close to the backbone in weight space rather than relearning to act from scratch. Because we reach alignment only through initialization, we offer this as the account that best organizes the measurements, not a demonstrated cause, and name the control that would settle it. Second, capacity pays only after alignment: the aligned action head is the highest-return module to scale. Third, those returns diminish sharply near the size today's $π$-series VLAs already use, so further growth buys little in-domain accuracy for its latency. These specify EffVLA, a compact model matching the strongest open-source VLAs on standard LIBERO, leading on most LIBERO-Plus perturbation axes at lower latency, and transferring to a real SO-ARM101 arm with the recipe unchanged.

</details>


### [19] [Vision-Force Admittance Learning for Peg Insertion into a Movable Hole](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14133)
*Yuzhong Chen,Yongqing Liang,Yunzhi Xu,Irving Fang,Chase Kidder,Hui-ping Wang,Raihan Haque,Yubiao Zhang,Chen Feng*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes VFAL, a Vision-Force Admittance Learning framework that fuses asynchronous visual feedback with a high-frequency force-based model using visual pose as a regularization term, enabling millimeter-precision peg-in-the-hole insertion in dynamic environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Precise manipulation in dynamic environments—caused by a mobile robot base or a target with unknown motion—remains a major robotics challenge. Dynamic motion introduces substantial uncertainty that fundamentally conflicts with the tight precision required by tasks like peg-in-the-hole.

Method: VFAL fuses asynchronous visual feedback with a high-frequency force-based model, using visual pose estimations (obtained via state-of-the-art vision foundation models) as a regularization term. It adapts insertion strategies online to dynamic motion, and incorporates failure recovery mechanisms for robustness.

Result: Real-world experiments demonstrate high success rates and strong adaptability across various pegs and dynamic environments while maintaining millimeter-level precision.

Conclusion: The proposed VFAL framework effectively achieves precise dynamic manipulation by combining low-frequency visual pose regularization with high-frequency force-based admittance control, offering a robust solution for peg-in-the-hole tasks under dynamic uncertainty.

Abstract: Precise manipulation in dynamic environments, whether induced by a mobile robot base or a target with unknown motion, remains a major challenge in robotics. Manipulation in dynamic environments introduces substantial uncertainty, which fundamentally conflicts with the tight precision requirement of precise tasks such as peg-in-the-hole. We propose a Vision-Force Admittance Learning (VFAL) framework that fuses asynchronous visual feedback with a high-frequency force-based model, using visual pose estimations as a regularization term. VFAL adapts insertion strategies online to dynamic motion while maintaining millimeter-level precision. To obtain robust, low-frequency pose information, we employ state-of-the-art vision foundation models for visual pose estimation. Additionally, we incorporate failure recovery mechanisms to enhance overall robustness. We validate our approach in real-world experiments, demonstrating high success rates and strong adaptability to various pegs and dynamic environments.

</details>


### [20] [When Faster VLA Deployment Changes Closed-Loop Behavior: Task Success-Latency Analysis of SmolVLA Across PyTorch and ONNX Variants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14146)
*Rafiqul Islam*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Evaluating smolvla_libero on RTX 2060: ONNX Runtime CUDA EP halves p99 latency but collapses LIBERO Spatial success unless language context width is restored to 24–32 tokens.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLA deployment via inference optimization can reduce latency but may alter closed-loop task behavior; the paper questions whether latency gains from ONNX export/quantization preserve task success, and whether artifact claims (FP16/INT8) reflect actual operator-level precision.

Method: Benchmark HuggingFaceVLA/smolvla_libero on RTX 2060 (6 GB) in LIBERO Spatial and Object (MuJoCo 3.3.2, LeRobot 0.6.1, seed 42), comparing PyTorch+AMP vs ONNX Runtime CUDA EP. Main eval 100 episodes/suite; paired rollout 300 episodes/suite. Includes a graph audit of the ONNX artifacts and a static language-width ablation (16/24/32 tokens).

Result: PyTorch+AMP: 70.0%/88.0% Spatial/Object at 1181 ms p99. Requested-FP16/INT8 ONNX: tether-inspect p99 601 ms/532 ms, Spatial 41.0%/40.0%, Object 89.0%. Graph audit reveals byte-identical FP32 graphs (no operator-level INT8). Language-width ablation: 41.0%/75.0%/71.0% Spatial at 16/24/32 tokens; Object and uniform-bench latency ~stable. Width-24 ONNX Spatial matches PyTorch+AMP at ~half latency (Wilson intervals overlap; chi-squared p=0.53).

Conclusion: Context width is an important contributor to the PyTorch-vs-ONNX Spatial gap but does not explain all differences. Deployment evaluation should jointly report latency, artifact inspection, interface constraints, and closed-loop success.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) deployment can reduce inference latency while changing closed-loop task behavior. We evaluate HuggingFaceVLA/smolvla_libero on an RTX 2060 (6 GB) in LIBERO Spatial and Object (MuJoCo 3.3.2, LeRobot 0.6.1, seed 42), comparing PyTorch+AMP with ONNX Runtime CUDA Execution Provider (CUDA EP). The main evaluation uses 100 episodes/suite; a paired rollout uses 300 episodes/suite. PyTorch+AMP reaches 70.0%/88.0% Spatial/Object success at 1181 ms p99. Requested-FP16 and requested-INT8 ONNX reduce tether-inspect p99 to 601 ms and 532 ms, while Spatial success falls to 41.0% and 40.0% and Object remains at 89.0%. A graph audit shows those artifacts are byte-identical FP32 graphs, so the requested-INT8 row is not operator-level INT8 quantization. A static language-width ablation (16/24/32 tokens) yields Spatial success of 41.0%, 75.0%, and 71.0%; widths 24 and 32 recover much of the Spatial drop while Object success and uniform-bench latency stay approximately stable. Width-24 ONNX Spatial success is comparable to the PyTorch+AMP baseline at roughly half the latency (Wilson intervals overlap; two-proportion chi-squared p=0.53). Context width is an important contributor in this stack; it does not account for every PyTorch-vs-ONNX difference. Deployment evaluation should jointly report latency, artifact inspection, interface constraints, and closed-loop success. Code: https://github.com/rafiqul713/smolvla-libero-onnx.

</details>


### [21] [GIFT: Glove-Inferred Force Transfer: Force-Aware Human-to-Robot Skill Transfer from a Wearable Sensing Glove to a Robot Hand Without Tactile Sensors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14173)
*Tzah Sarusi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: GIFT transfers human fingertip force to a robot hand in newtons without shared tactile hardware, using a sensing glove on the human side and actuator-current-residual force estimation on the robot side.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior human-to-robot skill transfer from sensing gloves required shared hardware (the same glove on both sides, or learned alignment between two tactile sensors), limiting generality and requiring tactile hardware on the robot.

Method: A wearable glove records finger flexion, calibrated fingertip force, and wrist orientation; a head-mounted camera records the demonstration (no robot present). The policy operates in glove space, predicting finger-position targets. At deployment, the robot estimates force from actuator-current residuals relative to a free-space baseline, mapped to newtons via calibration. Two calibrated adapters bridge the domains: a retargeting decoder (five glove channels to seven robot actuators) and a force estimator; any position-controlled hand reporting motor current can serve as the platform.

Result: On a cup grasp-and-hold task, two action-chunking policies trained on identical demonstrations (one retaining fingertip-force inputs, one with them zeroed) both succeeded in all 25 rollouts in a 50-rollout evaluation. Median hold-phase grip-force estimate was 53% lower with force inputs: 1.20 N vs 2.55 N (one-sided Mann-Whitney U, p<0.0001). In an observation ablation, a vision-only policy achieved 0/15 grasps; hand-command state enabled grasping, and force inputs determined hold force.

Conclusion: A force channel measured on the human hand transfers to a robot hand without tactile hardware, through a retargeting map from five glove channels to seven robot actuators, with no shared sensor between human and robot.

Abstract: Human-to-robot skill transfer from sensing gloves has so far relied on shared hardware: the same tactile glove worn by the demonstrator and the robot, or a learned alignment between two tactile sensors. We present GIFT (Glove-Inferred Force Transfer), a pipeline in which the interface between human and robot is a physical unit rather than a shared sensor: fingertip force is measured in newtons on the human side and estimated in newtons on the robot side. A wearable glove records finger flexion, calibrated fingertip force, and wrist orientation, while a head-mounted camera records the demonstration; no robot is present. At deployment, the robot estimates force from actuator-current residuals relative to a free-space baseline, through a calibrated mapping to newtons, so any position-controlled hand that reports motor current can serve as the deployment platform. The policy uses a glove-space state and predicts finger-position targets; the robot enters only through two calibrated adapters, a retargeting decoder and a force estimator. We evaluate GIFT on a cup grasp-and-hold task with two action-chunking policies trained on the same demonstrations, with fingertip-force inputs retained in one and zeroed in the other. In a 50-rollout evaluation with sample size and metrics fixed before scoring, both policies succeeded in all 25 rollouts. The median of the per-rollout hold-phase grip-force estimates was 53% lower with force inputs: 1.20 N versus 2.55 N (one-sided Mann-Whitney U, p<0.0001). In an observation ablation, a vision-only policy achieved 0/15 grasps, policies given hand-command state acquired the grasp, and the force inputs determined how hard the policy held. A force channel measured on the human hand thus transfers to a robot hand with no tactile hardware, through a retargeting map from five glove channels to seven robot actuators, with no sensor shared between the two.

</details>


### [22] [DreamSat-Bench: Development and Initial Testing of a Testbed for AI-Based Pose Estimation from 3D Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14183)
*Alex Posadas-Nava,August Berne,Giovanni Lavezzi,Kareena Shah,Alejandro Carrasco,Josiane Uwumukiza,Giacomo Battaglia,Paolo Panicucci,Minduli C. Wijayatunga,Victor Rodriguez-Fernandez,Richard Linares*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DreamSat-Bench is a modular software- and hardware-in-the-loop testbed that benchmarks AI-based relative navigation for rendezvous and proximity operations, integrating MuJoCo, Isaac Lab, and LeRobot, and is validated on a vision pipeline combining DreamSat 3D reconstruction with FoundationPose 6-DoF tracking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous relative navigation for unprepared space assets lacking prior geometric models requires reliable simulation-to-reality transfer, yet no unified benchmarking platform exists to rigorously evaluate AI-based navigation pipelines across both digital and physical domains.

Method: The platform unifies MuJoCo, Isaac Lab, and LeRobot into a single software- and hardware-in-the-loop robotic pipeline using robotic arms to trace 3D trajectories, with customizable orbital environments and lighting. An end-to-end vision-based pipeline pairs DreamSat (generative single-view 3D reconstruction) with FoundationPose (zero-shot 6-DoF tracking), evaluated on fixed-point station-keeping and fly-around segments via parametric studies.

Result: Parametric studies quantify the impacts of reconstruction latency, mesh resolution, orbital range, and illumination geometry on pose estimation accuracy. A preliminary hardware-in-the-loop campaign qualitatively validates physical deployment, identifying target symmetry and accumulated tracking drift as critical factors for robust navigation.

Conclusion: DreamSat-Bench provides a rigorous framework for maturing autonomous navigation with unprepared space assets in the absence of prior geometric models.

Abstract: This paper presents the development and initial testing of DreamSat-Bench, a modular rendezvous and proximity operation testbed designed to benchmark AI-based relative navigation techniques. By integrating a software- and hardware-in-the-loop robotic pipeline, the platform enables a seamless transition from digital simulation to physical reality. DreamSat-Bench unifies state-of-the-art robotic learning tools such as MuJoCo, Isaac Lab, and LeRobot into a single benchmarking platform, utilizing robotic arms to trace 3D trajectories. The platform allows for extensive customization of orbital environments and lighting to evaluate the simulation-to-reality gap. We demonstrate the testbed's utility by evaluating an end-to-end vision-based navigation pipeline that pairs DreamSat, a generative AI framework for single-view 3D reconstruction, with FoundationPose for zero-shot 6-DoF tracking of unseen spacecraft. Initial testing explores mission-representative orbital segments, including fixed-point station-keeping and fly-around characterization. Through a series of parametric studies, we quantify the impacts of reconstruction latency, mesh resolution, orbital range, and illumination geometry on pose estimation accuracy. Finally, a preliminary hardware-in-the-loop campaign qualitatively validates the physical deployment of the pipeline, identifying target symmetry and accumulated tracking drift as critical factors for robust navigation. DreamSat-Bench provides a rigorous framework for maturing autonomous navigation with unprepared space assets in the absence of prior geometric models.

</details>


### [23] [Novel Ex-vivo Calf Brain Model with Integrated Sub-Skull Force Sensors to Access Simulated Neurosurgical Procedures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14198)
*Hamad Binhammad,Matheus Ballestero,Mohammed Babgi,Seana Shaka,Nima Hemati,Rothaina Saeedi,Aiden Mazidi,Bianca Giglio,Rukun Dou,Houssem-Eddine Gueziri,Amir Hooshiar,Rolando F. Del Maestro*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A portable ex-vivo force-sensing platform using fresh calf brain in a 3D-printed skull with a 6-DOF force/torque sensor accurately quantifies tool-tissue forces during simulated neurosurgery (R = 0.9997, RMSE < 0.005 N, detection down to 9.8 mN).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
