Motivation: Surgical tissue manipulation requires precision, yet the magnitudes of tool-tissue interaction forces under realistic conditions are rarely quantified, leaving a gap in objective skill training and performance assessment.

Method: A portable ex-vivo platform was proposed and validated: fresh calf brain tissue (biological surrogate for brain parenchyma) was placed in a 3D-printed human skull model fitted with a 6 degree-of-freedom force/torque sensor and real-time data acquisition. Five validation protocols were used: ground-truth static accuracy and linearity with calibrated weights (0.5-50 g), minimum detectable force, spatial consistency across anatomical regions, effect of surgical draping, and long-duration stability.

Result: Measured forces agreed excellently with reference loads (R = 0.9997), RMSE < 0.005 N, mean relative error under 2%. Low-magnitude forces down to 1 g (9.8 mN) were reliably detected; surgical drapes caused no meaningful signal distortion; prolonged recordings showed minimal drift.

Conclusion: The framework provides objective, high-fidelity force quantification for skill training and performance assessment using fresh calf brain tissue, and may serve as a foundation for force-based evaluation in other surgical procedures. Future work will integrate clinically used instruments for greater realism and progress toward clinical trials assessing usability, educational impact, and translational relevance.

Abstract: Surgical tissue manipulation demands precision; however, tool-tissue manipulation force magnitudes under realistic conditions are rarely quantified. To address this gap, we proposed and validated a portable ex-vivo force-sensing platform that measures tool-tissue interaction forces across the skull-brain interface during simulated neurosurgery. The system involves fresh calf brain tissue, used as a biological surrogate for brain parenchyma, placed in a 3D-printed human skull model equipped with a 6 degree-of-freedom force/torque sensor and a real-time data acquisition system. Five validation protocols assessed the accuracy and dynamic fidelity of the platform against ground-truth measurement, static accuracy and linearity using calibrated weights (0.5-50 g), minimum detectable force, spatial consistency across different anatomical regions, effect of surgical draping, and long-duration stability. Across protocols, measured forces showed excellent agreement with reference loads (correlation R = 0.9997), with RMSE < 0.005 N and mean relative error under 2%. The platform reliably detected low-magnitude forces down to 1 g (9.8 mN), while surgical drapes introduced no meaningful signal distortion and prolonged recordings exhibited minimal drift. Overall, the proposed framework provides objective, high-fidelity force quantification for skill training and performance assessment using fresh calf brain tissue and may serve as a foundation for force-based evaluation across other surgical procedures. Future work will integrate clinically used surgical instruments to increase procedural realism and will progress toward clinical trials to evaluate usability, educational impact, and translational relevance in practice-adjacent settings.

</details>


### [24] [ITA-LaCAM: A Complete and Scalable TAPF Solver via Assignment-Aware Configuration-Space Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14208)
*Yimin Tang,Han Zhang,Shao-Hung Chan,Junsoo Kim,Erdem Bıyık,Sven Koenig,Jingkai Chen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ITA-LaCAM is a complete, scalable Combined Target Assignment and Path Finding (TAPF) solver that embeds an incrementally repaired agent-to-target matching in each joint-configuration node, achieving 100% solve rate on 9,760 benchmark instances.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: TAPF requires both assigning targets to agents and planning collision-free paths. Existing solvers either enumerate the combinatorial assignment space explicitly or lack completeness/scalability, so a method is needed that adaptively reassigns targets while preserving completeness and scaling to many agents.

Method: ITA-LaCAM extends LaCAM and ITA-CBS. Each joint-configuration node carries an agent-to-target matching. When a successor is generated, the matching is incrementally repaired only for the agents that moved, and these targets guide PIBT successor generation. This avoids explicit enumeration of the combinatorial assignment space.

Result: On 9,760 instances across eight maps with 5--200 agents, ITA-LaCAM solved 100% of instances versus 95.6% for IR-TAPF with DBS-Hungarian. It found an initial solution faster in 84.0% of comparisons and achieved a lower sum of costs in 65.0% of instances solved by both.

Conclusion: Incrementally repairing matchings within LaCAM's node structure enables complete, scalable TAPF solving with superior success rate, solution speed, and cost quality over a strong baseline, without enumerating the assignment space.

Abstract: Combined Target Assignment and Path Finding (TAPF) requires assigning targets for agents while simultaneously planning collision-free paths. We present ITA-LaCAM, a complete and scalable TAPF solver inspired by LaCAM and ITA-CBS. In ITA-LaCAM, each joint-configuration node carries an agent-to-target matching. When a successor is generated, ITA-LaCAM incrementally repairs the matching for the agents that moved and uses the targets to guide PIBT successor generation. This design enables adaptive reassignment without explicitly enumerating the combinatorial assignment space, while preserving LaCAM's completeness and scalability. Across 9,760 benchmark instances on eight maps with 5--200 agents, ITA-LaCAM solved 100% of the instances, compared with 95.6% for IR-TAPF configured with DBS-Hungarian. ITA-LaCAM found an initial solution faster in 84.0% of the comparisons and achieved a lower sum of costs in 65.0% of the instances solved by both methods.

</details>


### [25] [Task-Specified Active Metrological Inspection with Measurement-Steered VLA Manipulation and Deterministic Evidence Gating](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14219)
*Zhiling Chen,Jingzhan Ge,Ruimin Chen,Matthew P. Castanier,David Gorsich,Farhad Imani*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: FRAME is a hierarchical dual-arm framework that converts inspection instructions and structured specifications into traceable conformance evidence for high-mix low-volume manufacturing, combining learned manipulation with calibrated laser profilometry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-mix low-volume manufacturing demands inspection systems that adapt to changing parts, specifications, and work orders without repeated task-specific programming. Existing inspection automation typically assumes predefined sensing sequences, and general-purpose robot agents optimize task completion rather than the completeness and validity of metrological evidence.

Method: The authors formulate task-specified active metrological inspection and propose FRAME, a hierarchical dual-arm framework. It coordinates learned manipulation with calibrated laser profilometry: a task manager grounds and schedules requirements; active surface correspondence verifies physical-to-specification localization; and evidence memory tracks measurement provenance, admissibility, and coverage. Learned components may propose inspection targets and physical access actions, but deterministic datum-grounded measurement, admissibility checks, coverage auditing, and conformance evaluation gate PASS decisions.

Result: Physical experiments show that FRAME achieves higher end-to-end inspection reliability, fewer false accepts, and shorter task completion time.

Conclusion: By separating learned proposal from deterministic verification, FRAME prevents incomplete or unverified evidence from authorizing PASS, enabling adaptive, traceable inspection in HMLV settings.

Abstract: High-mix low-volume (HMLV) manufacturing requires inspection systems to adapt to changing parts, specifications, and work orders without repeated task-specific programming. Existing inspection automation typically assumes predefined sensing sequences, while general purpose robot agents optimize task completion rather than the completeness and validity of metrological evidence. We formulate task-specified active metrological inspection and propose From Requirements to Admissible Metrological Evidence (FRAME), a hierarchical dual-arm framework that converts an inspection instruction and structured specification into traceable conformance evidence. FRAME coordinates learned manipulation with calibrated laser profilometry: a task manager grounds and schedules requirements, active surface correspondence verifies physical-to-specification localization, and evidence memory tracks measurement provenance, admissibility, and coverage. Learned components may propose inspection targets and physical access actions, but deterministic datum-grounded measurement, admissibility checks, coverage auditing, and conformance evaluation prevent incomplete or unverified evidence from authorizing PASS. A series of physical experiments shows that FRAME achieves higher end-to-end inspection reliability, fewer false accepts, and shorter task completion time.

</details>


### [26] [VGFM: Expressive Robot Policies via Dense Value Guidance in Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14261)
*Prajwal Koirala,Mark Campbell*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: VGFM is an offline RL framework that adds dense value guidance to a flow-matching policy without backpropagation through time or extra architectural overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Expressive multimodal generative policies are attractive for complex robotic control, but policy improvement with multi-step generative actors is hard. Value-based objectives along generative trajectories typically require BPTT, auxiliary architectures, or distillation losses, adding substantial training complexity.

Method: VGFM parameterizes the policy as a conditional flow-matching model in action (x-prediction) space, so every intermediate flow step yields a valid action directly evaluable by a standard offline RL critic. Value guidance is applied at randomly sampled flow times without differentiating through the whole generative trajectory, and inference-time flexibility is preserved by varying the flow ODE discretization without retraining.

Result: On OGBench robotic locomotion and manipulation tasks, VGFM achieves strong performance across a wide range of tasks under rigorous evaluation protocols, with minimal hyperparameter tuning.

Conclusion: VGFM offers a simple, scalable, and effective approach for expressive policy learning in long-horizon, goal-oriented robotic control.

Abstract: Recent robot learning paradigms increasingly rely on large offline datasets of robotic interactions to train control policies. Expressive generative models enable rich and multimodal action representations, expanding the capability of this paradigm for complex robotic control. However, policy improvement with multi-step generative actors remains challenging. In offline reinforcement learning (RL), incorporating value-based objectives along generative trajectories often introduces substantial training complexity, including backpropagation through time (BPTT), auxiliary architectures, or distillation losses. We propose Value-Guided Flow Matching (VGFM), a scalable offline RL framework that enables dense value-guided shaping within a flow-based policy while avoiding BPTT and additional algorithmic overhead. VGFM parameterizes the policy as a conditional flow-matching model in action (x-prediction) space, ensuring that each intermediate flow step produces a valid robot action that can be directly evaluated by a standard offline RL critic. This design allows value guidance to be applied at randomly sampled flow times without differentiating through the entire generative trajectory, while preserving inference-time flexibility by varying the discretization of the underlying flow ODE without retraining. Evaluated on robotic locomotion and manipulation tasks in OGBench, VGFM achieves strong performance across a wide range of tasks under rigorous evaluation protocols. With minimal hyperparameter tuning, these results demonstrate that VGFM provides a simple, scalable, and effective approach for expressive policy learning in long-horizon, goal-oriented robotic control.

</details>


### [27] [Learning Communication-Conditioned Generative Policies for Decentralized Multi-Agent Collision Avoidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14268)
*Prajwal Koirala,Mark Campbell*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A decentralized, communication-conditioned generative framework for multi-agent collision avoidance uses flow-matching policies trained on privileged offline demonstrations to produce short-horizon action sequences, enabling coordinated interaction without centralized planning and graceful degradation under communication dropouts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-agent collision avoidance demands coordinated behavior under partial observability, yet centralized planning is costly and explicit communication signals are seldom available in demonstrations. The paper aims to learn decentralized policies that can flexibly trade off independent versus coordinated behavior at test time, relying only on local observations and learned latent messages.

Method: The approach trains a flow-matching generative policy on privileged offline demonstrations that have access to global state but no explicit communication signals. Agents learn to exchange and aggregate latent messages encoding interaction-relevant intent. At inference, unconditioned generation yields independent behavior while communication-conditioned generation enables coordination, combined with a receding-horizon scheme for efficient single-step inference of short-horizon action sequences.

Result: Extensive simulations show near-expert collision avoidance performance, strong generalization to denser, unseen multi-agent scenarios, graceful degradation under communication dropouts, and successful zero-shot transfer to real-robot experiments.

Conclusion: Decentralized communication-conditioned flow-matching policies learned from privileged offline data can achieve near-expert, generalizable, and robust multi-agent collision avoidance, running fully decentralized at execution time and transferring zero-shot to real robots.

Abstract: In this work, we propose a decentralized communication-conditioned generative framework for multi-agent collision avoidance. Agents generate short-horizon action sequences using a flow-matching policy trained from privileged offline demonstrations with access to global state. The demonstrations do not include explicit communication signals; instead, agents learn to exchange and aggregate latent messages that encode interaction-relevant intent under partial observability. This formulation supports flexible inference at test time, where unconditioned generation corresponds to independent behavior and communication-conditioned generation enables coordinated interaction without centralized planning. The resulting policies operate in a fully decentralized manner at execution time, relying only on local observations and learned messages. Combined with a receding-horizon inference scheme, the proposed approach enables efficient single-step inference of short-horizon action sequences and degrades gracefully under communication dropouts. Extensive simulation results demonstrate near-expert collision avoidance performance and strong generalization to denser, unseen multi-agent scenarios, along with zero-shot transfer to real-robot experiments.

</details>


### [28] [VLBiMan++: Expanding the Generalization Boundary of Vision-Language Anchored One-Shot Bimanual Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14310)
*Huayi Zhou,Wei Gao,Yiyang Han,Kui Jia,Hui Huang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: VLBiMan++ is an extended one-shot bimanual manipulation framework that systematically expands generalization across five dimensions—tasks, objects, scenes, embodiments, and deployment conditions—from a single human demonstration, using task-aware decomposition, vision-language grounded geometric adaptation, object-state-aware adaptation, and lightweight trajectory optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generalizable bimanual manipulation demands a reusable task prior that persists across diverse tasks, objects, scenes, embodiments, and execution conditions, eliminating the prohibitive cost of large-scale teleoperated demonstrations and policy retraining. Prior one-shot bimanual methods demonstrated only isolated transferability rather than a systematic, scalable generalization framework.

Method: Starting from a single human demonstration, VLBiMan++ performs task-aware decomposition to extract reusable and adaptable skill components, and applies vision-language grounded geometric adaptation to transfer these skills to novel configurations without retraining. It further introduces object-state-aware adaptation and lightweight trajectory optimization to handle changes beyond simple rigid 6-DoF pose variations while preserving reliable bimanual coordination. Generalization is extended along five dimensions: task, object, scene, embodiment, and deployment.

Result: Extensive real-world experiments show VLBiMan++ maintains strong task success and adaptation capability across increasingly challenging settings, including diverse and long-horizon skill compositions, unseen object categories with varying geometries and articulated or deformable objects, cluttered, occluded, and dynamically interfered scenes, heterogeneous dual-arm platforms, and prolonged closed-loop execution under repeated external perturbations.

Conclusion: VLBiMan++ advances one-shot bimanual manipulation from demonstrating isolated transferability toward a more systematic and scalable framework for generalization across tasks, objects, scenes, embodiments, and long-term deployment conditions.

Abstract: Generalizable bimanual robotic manipulation requires a reusable task prior that can persist across increasingly diverse tasks, objects, scenes, embodiments, and execution conditions, thus avoiding the prohibitive cost of large-scale teleoperated demonstrations and policy retraining. In this work, we present VLBiMan++, an extended framework that expands the generalization boundary of vision-language anchored one-shot bimanual manipulation. Starting from a single human demonstration, VLBiMan++ performs task-aware decomposition to identify reusable and adaptable skill components, and employs vision-language grounded geometric adaptation to transfer these skills to novel configurations without retraining. Building on this foundation, we systematically extend generalization along five dimensions: task generalization through diverse and long-horizon skill compositions; object generalization across unseen categories, varying geometries, and more complex articulated or deformable objects; scene generalization under clutter, occlusion, and dynamic interference; embodiment generalization across heterogeneous dual-arm robotic platforms; and deployment generalization through prolonged closed-loop execution under repeated external perturbations. To support this broader scope, we further introduce object-state-aware adaptation and lightweight trajectory optimization mechanisms that accommodate changes beyond simple rigid 6-DoF pose variations while preserving reliable bimanual coordination. Extensive real-world experiments demonstrate that VLBiMan++ maintains strong task success and adaptation capability across these increasingly challenging settings. Overall, VLBiMan++ advances one-shot bimanual manipulation from demonstrating isolated transferability toward a more systematic and scalable framework for generalization across tasks, objects, scenes, embodiments, and long-term deployment conditions.

</details>


### [29] [BIG-CBF: Behavior-Imagination-Guided Control Barrier Function with Shared Uncertainty for Mobile Robot Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14343)
*Shibo Li,Zhongcheng Wang,Jiahe Cao,Jianhua Yang,Ke Wu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: BIG-CBF is a two-rate navigation architecture that separates low-rate maneuver selection from high-rate safety filtering, using behavior imagination over a short horizon with shared uncertainty to improve CBF-based navigation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Minimum-intervention CBF filters lack task-level maneuver awareness and can select unproductive avoidance directions when multiple maneuvers are locally viable, causing safe but stalled behavior in geometrically ambiguous environments. Planning-execution mismatch also degrades consistency between maneuver selection and safety-critical execution.

Method: BIG-CBF imagines and evaluates six closed-loop feedback behaviors over a short horizon using analytic CBF compatibility plus a lightweight objective for task progress, freezing, smoothness, and switching. Imagination and execution layers share consistent uncertainty sources for relative-motion delay, obstacle prediction, zero-order-hold motion, and command-execution residuals, with a hard CBF as final safety authority.

Result: In a 3,600-episode benchmark across nine scenarios, BIG-CBF achieves 99.78% overall task success while substantially reducing downstream CBF intervention. On a physical omnidirectional robot with onboard Jetson Orin Nano, it completes all 15 evaluation runs without contact. Matched hardware comparisons against the non-shared variant show lower CBF intervention energy and activation frequency.

Conclusion: Behavior-imagination-guided maneuver selection with shared uncertainty improves task-level navigation performance while preserving hard safety guarantees, reducing CBF interventions and improving consistency between maneuver selection and safety-critical execution.

Abstract: Control barrier functions (CBFs) provide a mathematically grounded framework for enforcing local collision-avoidance constraints in autonomous mobile robots, commonly through optimization-based safety filters. However, a minimum-intervention CBF filter lacks task-level maneuver awareness and may fail to select a productive avoidance direction when multiple distinct maneuvers are locally viable, leading to safe but stalled behavior in geometrically ambiguous environments. This paper presents BIG-CBF, Behavior-Imagination-Guided Control Barrier Function with shared uncertainty, a two-rate navigation architecture that separates low-rate maneuver selection from high-rate safety filtering. Over a short horizon, six closed-loop feedback behaviors are imagined and evaluated using analytic CBF compatibility together with a lightweight objective accounting for task progress, freezing, smoothness, and switching. To reduce planning-execution mismatch, the imagination and execution layers share consistent uncertainty sources for relative-motion delay, obstacle prediction, zero-order-hold motion, and command-execution residuals, while a hard CBF remains the final safety authority. In a 3,600-episode comparative benchmark across nine scenarios, BIG-CBF achieves the highest overall task success rate of 99.78% while substantially reducing downstream CBF intervention. On a physical omnidirectional robot with onboard Jetson Orin Nano computation, BIG-CBF completes all 15 evaluation runs without a recorded contact event. Matched hardware comparisons against the non-shared variant further show lower CBF intervention energy and activation frequency, supporting improved consistency between maneuver selection and safety-critical execution.

</details>


### [30] [Orientation Control of Soft Robots via Adiabatic Spectral Submanifolds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14376)
*Aron Karakai,Roshan S. Kaundinya,Mike Yan Michelis,Robert Katzschmann,George Haller*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper extends adiabatic spectral submanifold (aSSM) model reduction to larger observable datasets and improves aSSM-based model predictive control, achieving over 60% reduction in tracking error for a pressure-actuated soft arm in high-fidelity simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Soft robots need accurate position and orientation control for safety-critical interactions, but model predictive control requires a model of infinite-dimensional nonlinear dynamics that is both accurate and computationally cheap, which is hard to obtain.

Method: The authors extend recent data-driven aSSM model-reduction methods to identify aSSMs from enlarged observable datasets, and upgrade existing aSSM-MPC schemes accordingly.

Result: Evaluated on a high-fidelity finite-element simulation of a pressure-actuated soft arm, the controller reduces position and orientation tracking error by more than 60% compared to existing data-driven baselines.

Conclusion: The enlarged-observable aSSM identification and upgraded aSSM-MPC yield substantially improved tracking performance for soft robot control.

Abstract: Soft robots are commonly sought for safety-critical interactions in delicate environments, where accurate position and orientation control is imperative. Model predictive control (MPC) offers a solution, but it requires a model of the robot's infinite-dimensional nonlinear dynamics that is at once accurate and computationally cheap. Recent theory on adiabatic spectral submanifolds (aSSMs) and their applications to soft robots provide data-driven model-reduction methods to construct such models. Here, we extend these methods to identify aSSMs from enlarged observable datasets and upgrade the currently available aSSM-MPC schemes. Evaluated on a high-fidelity finite-element simulation of a pressure-actuated soft arm, our controller reduces position and orientation tracking error by more than 60% compared to existing data-driven baselines.

</details>


### [31] [Learning-Based Dynamic Obstacle Avoidance for a UAV Using Only Three Range Sensors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14426)
*Mohammad Reza Ranjbar Divkoti,A. Pedro Aguiar*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A DRL+PPO framework with a behavior grid map enables a yaw-only fixed-altitude UAV to navigate unknown dynamic environments using only three directional range sensors, achieving real-time obstacle avoidance and target reaching.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: UAVs operating at fixed altitude under extreme partial observability must avoid static and dynamic obstacles in real time, but yaw-only control imposes nonholonomic constraints similar to fixed-wing platforms. Dense sensing (LiDAR, vision) is costly, so reliable navigation from sparse, low-cost measurements is needed.

Method: Co-design of state representation and control policy: a behavior grid map aggregates sparse measurements from three low-cost directional range sensors into a structured local representation, combined with Deep Reinforcement Learning using Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) for stable continuous-control policy learning.

Result: Simulations across varying environment sizes and obstacle densities show higher success rates than PPO variants and MPC (94% vs. 79–90% in small-scale high-congestion scenarios; 83% vs. 62–71% in large-scale high-congestion scenarios) while maintaining real-time performance. Real-world tests in four scenarios confirm practical feasibility with consistent target reaching and no collisions.

Conclusion: Combining a behavior grid map state representation with PPO yields an effective, real-time kinodynamic online motion planner for yaw-only UAVs in unknown dynamic environments, validated in both simulation and real-world experiments.

Abstract: We present a learning-based approach to kinodynamic online motion planning for an Unmanned Aerial Vehicle (UAV) operating at a fixed altitude in unknown dynamic environments, where real-time avoidance of both static and dynamic obstacles must be achieved under conditions of extreme partial observability. The UAV is controlled with a single degree of freedom (yaw only), resulting in constrained, nonholonomic motion similar to fixed-wing platforms.
  The proposed framework integrates a behavior grid map representation with Deep Reinforcement Learning (DRL), using Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) for stable policy learning in continuous control. The key idea is the co-design of a state representation and control policy that enables reliable navigation using only three low-cost directional range sensors, without reliance on dense sensing modalities such as LiDAR or vision-based systems. The behavior grid map dynamically aggregates sparse measurements into a structured local representation that supports real-time decision-making for obstacle avoidance and target reaching. Extensive simulations across environments of varying sizes and obstacle densities demonstrate that the proposed standard and enhanced methods achieve higher success rates than PPO variants and Model Predictive Control (MPC) (94\% vs. 79--90\% in small-scale high-congestion scenarios, and 83\% vs. 62--71\% in large-scale high-congestion scenarios), while maintaining real-time performance. Real-world experiments across four scenarios further confirm practical feasibility, with consistent target-reaching behaviour and no collisions under the tested conditions.

</details>


### [32] [EMoG: Emotion-Modulated Gait Generation for Expressive Humanoid Locomotion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14432)
*Yi Lu,Tianhao Jiang,Honglong Tian,Yumeng Zhang,Qingrui Zhao,Zhengtao Wang,Xiao-Xiao Long,Qiu Shen,Xun Cao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: EMoG is an emotion-modulated gait generation framework that enables expressive yet command-consistent humanoid locomotion, using a continuously adjustable emotional-style code, a lightweight MLP trajectory generator, and a unified RL tracking policy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing humanoid locomotion systems prioritize stability and task execution; integrating expressiveness with explicit locomotion control remains challenging. There is a need for parameterized emotional-style walking for human-robot interaction.

Method: EMoG uses an emotional-style code with continuously adjustable intensity. Conditioned on this code and physical commands, a lightweight MLP generates expressive, command-consistent periodic gait trajectories in real time, tracked by a unified reinforcement learning policy. A large-scale emotion-annotated gait dataset from professional performers and an automated pipeline extract physically consistent periodic gait cycles. An LLM-based parser converts free-form language into emotional style and motion parameters for interactive control.

Result: Experiments demonstrate continuous gait-style modulation with perceptible expressive cues while maintaining command tracking.

Conclusion: EMoG provides a practical approach to parameterized emotional-style walking for human-robot interaction.

Abstract: Existing humanoid locomotion systems primarily focus on stability and task execution, while integrating expressiveness with explicit locomotion control remains challenging. We propose EMoG, an emotion-modulated gait generation framework for expressive humanoid locomotion. EMoG introduces an emotional-style code with continuously adjustable intensity. Conditioned on this code and physical commands, a lightweight MLP generates expressive, command-consistent periodic gait trajectories in real time, which are tracked by a unified reinforcement learning policy for physical execution. To support training, we collect a large-scale emotion-annotated gait dataset from professional performers and develop an automated pipeline to extract physically consistent periodic gait cycles. EMoG also integrates an LLM-based parser that converts free-form language into emotional style and motion parameters for interactive control. Experiments demonstrate continuous gait-style modulation with perceptible expressive cues while maintaining command tracking. EMoG provides a practical approach to parameterized emotional-style walking for human-robot interaction.

</details>


### [33] [EcoBoat: Design and Experimental Validation of an Autonomous Body-Board Boat For Cleaning Water Bodies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14450)
*M. Aman Ansari,Saifullah Khan,Rahul Kulkarni,PB Sujit*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: EcoBoat is a low-cost autonomous surface vehicle that collects floating debris in indoor and outdoor water bodies using minimal sensing (ultrasonic indoors, GPS outdoors) and a Tesla-valve-inspired collection basket.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cleaning water bodies such as pools and lakes typically requires significant manual effort or costly, sensor-intensive robotic systems. There is a need for an affordable, versatile alternative for floating debris collection.

Method: The authors built EcoBoat on a modified hull. Indoor operation uses ultrasonic sensors for boundary and obstacle detection, combining random-walk motion with boundary-following behavior. Outdoor operation uses GPS-based geofencing with a random-walk coverage strategy. A Tesla-valve-inspired collection basket enables debris intake during forward motion while preventing escape during turning or braking. The design was refined iteratively through field experiments in pools and lakes.

Result: Field experiments in pools and lakes validated the design and drove iterative refinement. The system demonstrated effective, versatile debris collection across diverse water bodies with minimalist sensing and computation.

Conclusion: A minimalist sensing and computation approach can achieve effective, versatile floating debris collection across diverse water bodies, offering a low-cost alternative to manual effort or sensor-intensive robotic systems.

Abstract: Cleaning water bodies such as swimming pools and lakes typically demands significant manual effort or reliance on costly, sensor-intensive robotic systems. This paper presents EcoBoat, a low-cost autonomous surface vehicle built on a modified hull, designed to collect floating debris in both indoor and outdoor water bodies. For indoor environments, EcoBoat uses ultrasonic sensors to detect boundaries and obstacles, combining random-walk motion with boundary-following behavior. For outdoor environments, it relies on GPS-based geofencing paired with a random-walk strategy for area coverage. A key design feature is a Tesla-valve-inspired collection basket that allows debris intake during forward motion while preventing its escape during turning or braking. Field experiments in pools and lakes validated the design through iterative refinement, demonstrating that a minimalist sensing and computation approach can achieve effective, versatile debris collection across diverse water bodies.

</details>


### [34] [Fault Diagnosis for Underwater Vehicles using Moving Horizon Estimation and Gaussian Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14539)
*Fotis Panetsos,Kostas J. Kyriakopoulos*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A model-based fault detection and diagnosis framework for underwater vehicles with actuator faults that separates unmodeled dynamics from fault effects using MHE and Gaussian Processes, validated experimentally in a water tank.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fault diagnosis in underwater vehicles is challenged by unmodeled dynamics, which can be mistaken for faults. The work aims to explicitly account for these unmodeled effects to improve robustness of fault detection, isolation, and magnitude estimation.

Method: A Moving Horizon Estimator (MHE) estimates a lumped disturbance capturing both unmodeled and fault effects. Gaussian Processes (GPs) approximate the unmodeled dynamics, providing mean and uncertainty predictions across operating conditions. Online, the residual between MHE disturbance estimate and GP prediction is evaluated via a Generalized Likelihood Ratio Test.

Result: The methodology was experimentally validated in a laboratory water tank, showing reliable diagnostic performance under both open-loop and closed-loop control.

Conclusion: Incorporating GP-based predictions within the diagnostic framework yields robustness to unmodeled dynamics, enabling effective fault detection and isolation and accurate quantitative fault magnitude estimation.

Abstract: This work proposes a model-based fault detection and diagnosis framework for underwater vehicles subject to actuator faults that explicitly accounts for the presence of unmodeled dynamics. To this end, a Moving Horizon Estimator (MHE) is developed to estimate the lumped disturbance, capturing both unmodeled and fault effects. Gaussian Processes (GPs) are employed to approximate the unmodeled dynamics, providing predictions of the corresponding mean and uncertainty across diverse operating conditions. During online operation, the residual between the MHE lumped disturbance estimate and the GP prediction is evaluated using a Generalized Likelihood Ratio Test. By incorporating GP-based predictions within the diagnostic framework, robustness to unmodeled dynamics is achieved, enabling effective fault detection and isolation as well as accurate quantitative estimation of fault magnitude. The proposed methodology is experimentally validated in a laboratory water tank, demonstrating reliable diagnostic performance under both open-loop and closed-loop control.

</details>


### [35] [NavPatch: Evidence-Guided Object-Level Costmap Correction with Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14543)
*Shiji Sun,Xingyu Tao,Hao Wang,Ling Wang,Zhengyi Chen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: NavPatch is an object-level correction layer that uses a vision-language model to apply ADD, REMOVE, or EXTEND corrections to navigation-relevant objects, improving real-robot navigation success from 70.0% to 86.0%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Geometric maps used by mobile robots for obstacle avoidance and path planning do not always represent obstacles in a way that matches how objects should affect navigation: low-lying cables may be missed, flexible curtains may create spurious blockage, and traffic cones may require an exclusion region larger than their observed footprint.

Method: NavPatch performs periodic scene understanding with a vision-language model to assign ADD, REMOVE, or EXTEND to navigation-relevant object categories. Open-vocabulary grounding localizes object instances, while LiDAR and RGB-D observations provide 3D support. Observation quality filtering and cross-frame maintenance determine when each correction patch is committed, replaced, or revoked.

Result: In 50 real robot trials across five layouts, NavPatch achieves an 86.0% overall success rate. An ablation with four configurations and 200 total runs shows NavPatch improves success rate from 70.0% to 86.0% and reduces the false commit rate from 68.4% to 40.7% compared with updates based only on the current observation.

Conclusion: An object-level correction layer driven by vision-language scene understanding and cross-frame maintenance can bridge the mismatch between geometric obstacle representation and navigation-relevant object semantics, yielding more reliable real-robot navigation.

Abstract: Mobile robots typically rely on geometric maps for obstacle avoidance and path planning, but the resulting obstacle representation does not always match how an object should affect navigation. A low lying cable may be missed, a flexible curtain may create spurious blockage, and a traffic cone may require an exclusion region larger than its observed footprint. We present NavPatch, an object level correction layer that assigns ADD, REMOVE, or EXTEND to navigation relevant object categories through periodic scene understanding with a vision-language model. Open vocabulary grounding localizes object instances, and LiDAR and RGB-D observations provide 3D support. Observation quality filtering and cross frame maintenance determine when each correction patch is committed, replaced, or revoked. In 50 real robot trials across five layouts, NavPatch achieves an overall success rate of 86.0%. An ablation study of four configurations with 200 runs in total shows that NavPatch improves the success rate from 70.0% to 86.0% and reduces the false commit rate from 68.4% to 40.7% compared with updates based only on the current observation.

</details>


### [36] [GLAM: Training a latent world model over global spatiotemporal memory for active exploration and navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14561)
*I-Tak Ieong,Ruizhi Feng,Zhaoyang Lu,Yifei Cao,Jiayao Zhao,Leon Li,Senhua Zhu,Wenbo Ding*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: GLAM is a goal-conditioned latent world model over global spatiotemporal memory that jointly predicts future map representations and robot-centric waypoint latents in a shared space, enabling GLAM NAV to improve success rate and SPL over a reproduced BSC-Nav baseline on a controlled HM3D-ObjectNav subset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Embodied agents for active exploration and semantic navigation must build memory from partial observations, predict how observed spatial memory will evolve to support future motion, and convert that prediction into actionable plans. This requires a model that links memory evolution with navigation intent.

Method: GLAM is a goal-conditioned latent world model trained over global spatiotemporal memory. Given historical map tokens, a navigation goal, and the current robot pose, it jointly predicts future map representations and robot-centric waypoint latents in a shared representation space. It follows a JEPA-like latent prediction paradigm, operating on map-level latent tokens rather than RGB reconstruction, and uses a pretrained waypoint encoder-decoder to supervise and decode navigation plans within GLAM NAV. Training data are collected by replaying ObjectNav expert trajectories in Habitat over HM3D v0.2 scene assets and slicing them into multi-timescale prediction samples.

Result: On a controlled HM3D-ObjectNav subset reproduction setting, GLAM NAV improves over a reproduced BSC-Nav baseline in both success rate and success weighted by path length (SPL).

Conclusion: Predicting future map representations jointly with waypoint latents in a shared latent space provides an effective basis for goal-conditioned semantic navigation, outperforming the reproduced BSC-Nav baseline on the evaluated HM3D-ObjectNav subset.

Abstract: Active exploration and semantic navigation require an embodied agent to build memory from partial observations, predict how the evolution of observed spatial memory may support future motion, and convert that prediction into actionable plans. We present GLAM, a goal-conditioned latent world model trained over global spatiotemporal memory, and GLAM NAV, the complete navigation system built around it. Given historical map tokens, a navigation goal, and the current robot pose, GLAM jointly predicts future map representations and robot-centric waypoint latents, allowing future spatial context and navigation intent to be inferred in a shared representation space. The model follows a JEPA-like latent prediction paradigm, operates directly on map-level latent tokens rather than RGB reconstruction, and uses a pretrained waypoint encoder-decoder to supervise and decode navigation plans within GLAM NAV. Training data are collected by replaying ObjectNav expert trajectories in Habitat over HM3D v0.2 scene assets and slicing them into multi-timescale prediction samples. On a controlled HM3D-ObjectNav subset reproduction setting, GLAM NAV improves over a reproduced BSC-Nav baseline in both success rate and success weighted by path length.

</details>


### [37] [Learning Multi-Agent Task Assignment and Navigation in the Factory: from Simulation to Real Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14567)
*Abdalwhab Bakheet Mohamed Abdalwhab,Giovanni Beltrame,David St-Onge*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: FMAPPO fuses 2D LiDAR with task state for decentralized multi-robot multi-machine tending, validated via a sim-to-real ROS2 pipeline on physical robots and showing large gains over MAPPO and SMAPPO.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deploying multi-agent RL (MARL) on physical multi-robot systems in industrial environments remains challenging; the paper targets real-world applicability of decentralized MARL for multi-robot multi-machine tending.

Method: FMAPPO (Feature-fusion Multi-Agent Proximal Policy Optimization) fuses 2D LiDAR measurements with task-specific state information for safe decentralized multi-robot task assignment and navigation. A full sim-to-real pipeline uses high-fidelity robotic simulation and ROS2, deployed on physical mobile-manipulator platforms (arms disabled). Sensitivity to command update frequency is also studied.

Result: In simulation FMAPPO outperformed baselines with large effect size: +106%/+21% parts delivery and +48%/+11% parts collection over MAPPO/SMAPPO; +31 and +10 pp machine utilization; -18%/-15% collisions; +14 and +6 pp safety score. Real-world experiments confirmed decentralized policies coordinated multiple robots servicing multiple machines safely under real sensing and control constraints.

Conclusion: Feature-fused decentralized MARL is practically deployable for industrial multi-robot multi-machine tending, delivering safer and more efficient coordination, with command update frequency identified as a key deployment consideration.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) has shown considerable promise for robotic decision-making, yet deploying multi-agent RL (MARL) on physical multi-robot systems in industrial environments remains challenging. This paper investigates the real-world applicability of decentralized MARL for multi-robot multi-machine tending. We propose Feature-fusion Multi-Agent Proximal Policy Optimization (FMAPPO), which fuses 2D LiDAR measurements with task-specific state information to enable safe decentralized multi-robot task assignment and navigation. A complete simulation-to-reality pipeline was developed using high-fidelity robotic simulation and ROS2 and deployed on physical mobile-manipulator platforms operating under realistic real-world conditions, with the robotic arms disabled during the experiments. We further investigate the sensitivity of the learned policy to command update frequency, an important consideration for real-world deployment. Comparative evaluation in simulation demonstrated that FMAPPO significantly outperformed state-of-the-art baselines with a large effect size, achieving improvements of 106\% and 21\% in parts delivery and 48\% and 11\% in parts collection over MAPPO and SMAPPO, respectively. FMAPPO also increased machine utilization by 31 and 10 percentage points, respectively, while reducing collisions by 18\% and 15\% and increasing the safety score by 14 and 6 percentage points compared with MAPPO and SMAPPO, respectively. Furthermore, real-world experiments demonstrated that the learned decentralized policies can coordinate multiple robots to service multiple machines while maintaining safe operation under real-world sensing and control constraints. Videos of the real-world experiment are available online https://anonymouspapers123.github.io/FMAPPO/.

</details>


### [38] [Robotic Servo Tracking of Moving Targets with Dynamic Imitation Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14589)
*Yazhe Luo,Sipu Ruan,Yifei Li,Diansheng Chen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a servo tracking method based on imitation trajectory constraints to enable explicit intermediate trajectory constraints in robot visual servoing for moving targets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing tracking methods map visual residuals to control velocities for fast responses, but they impose weak constraints on the intermediate motion process, leading to trajectory discontinuity, oscillation, or conservative behaviors. Imposing explicit trajectory constraints in robot visual servoing remains challenging.

Method: The authors formulate and analyze a dynamic model describing a robot approaching a moving target for convergence. They introduce a time-scalable deformation mechanism and a trajectory modulation with shape and amplitude components to generate trajectories in real time, from which tracking points are adaptively selected to form dynamic constraints. Robot velocity is then computed from target pose differentials or tracked key features to follow the constrained trajectory.

Result: Simulation and real-world experiments show the proposed method achieves dynamic obstacle avoidance and high-precision convergence compared with several state-of-the-art methods in complex environments.

Conclusion: The proposed imitation-trajectory-constraint servo tracking method enables constrained tracking of moving targets, providing explicit intermediate trajectory constraints and outperforming existing approaches in complex environments.

Abstract: Imposing explicit trajectory constraints in robot visual servoing remains challenging. Existing tracking methods achieve fast responses by mapping visual residuals to control velocities, but they have weak constraints on the intermediate motion process, which lead to trajectory discontinuity, oscillation, or conservative behaviors. To enable constrained tracking for moving targets, this paper proposes a servo tracking method based on imitation trajectory constraints. A dynamic model describing the robot approaching a moving target is formulated and analyzed for convergence. A time-scalable deformation mechanism and a trajectory modulation incorporating shape and amplitude components are introduced to generate a series of trajectories in real time, from which tracking points are adaptively determined to form dynamic constraints. The robot velocity is then computed from target pose differentials or tracked key features to follow the constrained trajectory. Simulation and real-world experiments demonstrate that the proposed method can achieve dynamic obstacle avoidance and high-precision convergence compared with several state-of-the-art methods in complex environments.

</details>


### [39] [REVOLVE: An Automated Closed-Loop Framework for Evolving Robot Manipulation with Minimal Human Intervention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14633)
*Hanyu Liu,Qian Li,Yizhu Ding,Jiayi Wen,Keqiang Ren,Yunsheng Ma,Tao Jian,Zhihua Wang,Zhuofan Yu,Xinran Li,Zhigong Song*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: REVOLVE is an automated closed-loop framework that integrates data collection, policy training/deployment, failure recovery, and continual learning to evolve robot manipulation policies with minimal human intervention.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Data-driven robot manipulation policies have improved task execution and generalization, but real-world deployment still depends heavily on humans for failure assessment, correction, and environment reset. Moreover, models often fail to continually learn from failures and corrective experience.

Method: Built on a unified software platform, REVOLVE integrates data collection, policy training and deployment, failure recovery, and continual learning into a single closed-loop workflow. It features two components: (1) Automated Reset and Collection (ARC), which automatically resets the environment and intervenes to correct policy failures; and (2) Dual-Loop Evolution (DLE), which feeds real-world interaction and failure-correction data back into policy learning to improve both the manipulation policy and the agent, using an external mismatch memory to refine agent judgments.

Result: Across four real-world manipulation tasks, after five iterations REVOLVE improved average policy success rate by 18.5% and agent judgment accuracy by 8.5%, while reducing human effort in data collection and deployment testing by 94.4% and 95.1%, respectively.

Conclusion: REVOLVE transforms real-world deployment into a closed-loop learning process that continually accumulates and uses execution experience, enabling continual evolution of both the policy and supervisory model with substantially less human intervention.

Abstract: Recent advances in data-driven robot manipulation policies have substantially improved task execution and generalization. However, real-world deployment still relies heavily on humans for failure assessment, correction, and environment reset, while models often fail to continually learn from failures and corrective experience. We present REVOLVE (Robot Evolving via Orchestrated Loops, Verification, and Experience), an automated closed-loop framework for evolving robot manipulation with minimal human intervention. Built on a unified software platform, REVOLVE integrates data collection, policy training and deployment, failure recovery, and continual learning into a single closed-loop workflow. Its Automated Reset and Collection (ARC) architecture automatically resets the environment and intervenes to correct policy failures. Dual-Loop Evolution (DLE) continually improves the manipulation policy and agent by feeding real-world interaction and failure--correction data back into policy learning and using an external mismatch memory to refine agent judgments. Experiments across four real-world manipulation tasks show that, after five iterations, REVOLVE improves average policy success rate by 18.5% and agent judgment accuracy by 8.5%, while reducing human effort in data collection and deployment testing by 94.4% and 95.1%, respectively. These results demonstrate that REVOLVE transforms real-world deployment into a closed-loop learning process that continually accumulates and uses execution experience, enabling continual evolution of both the policy and supervisory model with substantially less human intervention.

</details>


### [40] [Skill Composition for Legged Robot Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14647)
*Daniel Gigliotti,Flavio Maiorana,Fabio Patrizi,Luca Iocchi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper argues that composing independently trained sub-policies should be a research problem in its own right, since reliable transitions between specialized controllers are the fragile bottleneck that determines whether separate skills become a usable, extensible repertoire.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Specialized controllers are easy to train, converge reliably, and can be validated in isolation, whereas a single end-to-end policy covering everything is not. The weakness lies in the transition between skills. Treating composition as an implementation detail leaves this fragility unaddressed and prevents skills from forming a shareable repertoire.

Method: The paper proposes treating the composition of independent sub-policies as a first-class research problem. It advocates enabling safe control handover between specialized policies at any moment, so that the decision of what to do next can be delegated to a component of a different nature (a planner, automaton, or symbolic controller) whose behavior is inspectable in advance.

Result: No empirical results are reported; the abstract presents a conceptual position rather than experimental findings.

Conclusion: If control can be passed safely between specialized policies, policies would only ever have to act, and what the robot can be trusted to do would become verifiable, turning separate skills into a validated, extensible repertoire.

Abstract: Robots, and humanoid robots in particular, are increasingly competent at individual behaviors, each obtained by training a specialized controller. A specialized skill is quick to train, converges reliably because the problem it faces is narrow, and can be validated on its own, none of which is true of a single end-to-end policy asked to cover everything. What remains fragile is the transition between them. We argue that the composition of independent sub-policies deserves to be treated as a research problem in its own right, rather than as an implementation detail left to whatever mechanism happens to be at hand. Reliable composition is what turns a collection of separate skills into a repertoire that can be used, extended and shared. More fundamentally, if control can be passed between specialized policies safely, and at any moment, the choice of what the robot should do next can be delegated to a component of an entirely different nature, such as a planner, an automaton or a symbolic controller, whose behavior can be inspected in advance. The policies would then only ever have to act, and what the robot can be trusted to do would become verifiable.

</details>


### [41] [A Novel Robot-Assisted Learning Pedagogy for Children with ASD](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14710)
*Laura Boccanfuso,Erin Barney,Marilena Mademtzi,Claire Foster,Quan Wang,Colette Torres,Lisa Chen,Brian Scassellati,Pamela Ventola,Frederick Shic*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A novel robot-assisted autism intervention using the learning-by-teaching pedagogy is evaluated, comparing a robot versus a human confederate as the tutee. Children with ASD showed better performance and higher engagement in the robot condition, and engagement in the robot condition contributed to improved performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior robot-assisted autism interventions typically deploy robots as teachers, clinical assistants, or more-abled peers, relying on trained practitioners, and rarely integrate validated classroom teaching pedagogies—despite most children with ASD attending mainstream schools and spending 80% or more of their time in general classrooms. This gap motivates incorporating the learning-by-teaching pedagogy into robot-assisted social skills interventions.

Method: The authors introduce a novel teaching methodology for advancing social skills in school-aged children with ASD, grounded in the learning-by-teaching pedagogy. They evaluate intervention effectiveness by comparing two conditions—a robot tutee versus a human confederate tutee—on a set of social skills tasks, measuring performance and engagement across participants and scenario orders.

Result: 80% of participants performed better in the robot condition (mean performance: robot=63%, confederate=37%), irrespective of scenario order. 90% of participants were significantly more engaged in the robot condition (mean engagement: robot=61%, confederate=32%). The engagement effect did not extend to the confederate condition, and analyses indicate that overall engagement in the robot condition contributed to improved performance.

Conclusion: Robots employed in a learning-by-teaching context may help enhance engagement and improve performance on a simple social skills task for children with ASD, supporting the value of integrating validated classroom pedagogies into robot-assisted autism interventions.

Abstract: Interaction paradigms used in robot-assisted autism intervention have historically employed robots as teachers, clinical assistants, or more-abled peers to promote a variety of social skills. These modalities often leverage the expertise of trained practitioners to ensure that child-robot interactions are productive or clinically grounded to yield positive therapeutic benefits for children across the autism spectrum. Yet, despite the fact that the majority of children with autism spectrum disorder (ASD) attend mainstream schools and spend 80% or more of their time in the general classroom [27], there is a paucity of research incorporating validated classroom teaching pedagogies into robot-assisted autism interventions. In this work, we introduce a novel teaching methodology for advancing social skills in school-aged children with ASD. We evaluate the effectiveness of a novel robot-assisted autism intervention which incorporates the learning-by-teaching pedagogy and explores the comparative benefits of employing a robot versus a human confederate for improved performance on a set of social skills tasks. Results show that 80% of study participants performed better in the robot condition (mean performance in the robot condition=63%, mean performance in the confederate condition=37%), irrespective of the scenario order. Further, 90% of all participants were significantly more engaged in the robot condition (mean engagement: robot=61%, confederate=32%) and, while the effect did not result in the confederate condition, analyses indicate that overall engagement in the robot condition contributed to improved performance. These results suggest that robots employed in a learning-by-teaching context may help enhance engagement and improve performance on a simple social skills task for children with ASD.

</details>


### [42] [Beyond Dead Reckoning: A Point of View on Camera--DAS--GNSS Continuity in Road Tunnels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14748)
*Lyes Saad Saoud,Hesham A. Rakha,Mona Jaber,Moussa Ayyash*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This Point of View reframes road tunnel localization as infrastructure-assisted cross-modal track continuity rather than dead-reckoning from the last satellite fix. It proposes a fused architecture combining portal GNSS anchors, DAS motion evidence, camera identity/lane anchors, onboard sensing, and edge fusion with integrity monitoring and guarded reacquisition, and lays out a falsifiable research and deployment agenda.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Connected and automated vehicles need continuous, attributable, integrity-bounded positioning, yet road tunnels remove satellite visibility. Treating tunnel localization as extrapolation from the last trusted satellite fix is inadequate for these requirements, motivating a shift toward infrastructure-assisted continuity.

Method: A conceptual/position approach: a trusted portal satellite solution supplies the global anchor; distributed acoustic sensing (DAS) provides continuous motion evidence; cameras supply sparse identity and lane anchors; onboard sensing captures short-term dynamics; and edge computing handles association, fusion, integrity monitoring, and guarded reacquisition. Ground truth is reserved for offline calibration and validation only.

Result: As a Point of View, the paper does not report experimental results; instead it derives an architectural framework and a falsifiable research and deployment agenda for moving from synchronized multimodal evidence to validated, integrity-aware tunnel positioning continuity.

Conclusion: Tunnel localization should be reconceived as infrastructure-assisted cross-modal track continuity. Effective solutions require integrating portal GNSS, DAS, cameras, onboard sensing, and edge fusion with integrity monitoring, with ground truth confined to offline calibration/validation.

Abstract: Road tunnels remove satellite visibility where connected and automated vehicles still require continuous, attributable, and integrity-bounded positioning. This Point of View argues that tunnel localization should be treated as infrastructure-assisted cross-modal track continuity, not as extrapolation from the last trusted satellite fix. A trusted portal satellite solution provides the global anchor, distributed acoustic sensing (DAS) continuous motion evidence, cameras sparse identity and lane anchors, onboard sensing short-term dynamics, and edge computing association, fusion, integrity monitoring, and guarded reacquisition. Ground truth is reserved for offline calibration and validation. The article develops a falsifiable research and deployment agenda for progressing from synchronized multimodal evidence to validated, integrity-aware tunnel positioning continuity.

</details>


### [43] [An Adaptive Fixed-Time Line-of-Sight Guidance Scheme for 3D Path Following of Underwater Vehicles: Theory and Experiment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14756)
*Hanzhi Yang,Jalil Chavez-Galaviz,Nina Mahmoudian*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a fixed-time adaptive line-of-sight (LOS) guidance framework for 3D path tracking of autonomous underwater vehicles (AUVs), achieving convergence within a preset time independent of initial conditions and enabling guidance-level robustness without modifying the underlying vehicle controller.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reliable path tracking is essential for AUVs in dynamic, uncertain marine environments, but traditional LOS guidance suffers from asymptotic convergence, causing slow disturbance recovery and unpredictable tracking. Existing robust control methods require modifying the vehicle's underlying controller, which limits deployment on commercial AUV platforms.

Method: The framework integrates fixed-time stability theory with LOS guidance to guarantee convergence within a preset time independent of initial conditions. A fixed-time adaptive estimator rapidly compensates for time-varying sideslip disturbances induced by ocean currents, and a time-varying look-ahead mechanism improves tracking on curved paths. Lyapunov analysis establishes fixed-time stability, and the method is validated via numerical simulations and physical experiments.

Result: Simulations showed the time-varying look-ahead variant reduced cross-track and vertical-track RMSE by 69.37% and 67.46% on curved paths. Field experiments with an Iver 3 AUV showed the fixed-time guidance reduced average tracking error by 56.35% (straight path) and 27.59% (curved path) versus conventional adaptive LOS guidance. Superior accuracy, convergence speed, and anti-interference capability were demonstrated.

Conclusion: The proposed framework offers a practical guidance-level solution for reliable autonomous navigation of AUVs in complex marine environments, delivering improved tracking accuracy, faster convergence, and strong disturbance rejection without altering the underlying vehicle controller.

Abstract: Reliable path tracking is crucial for autonomous underwater vehicles (AUVs) operating in dynamic and uncertain marine environments. However, traditional line-of-sight (LOS) guidance methods rely on asymptotic convergence, resulting in slow disturbance recovery and unpredictable tracking performance. Existing robust control methods typically require modifications to the underlying vehicle controller, limiting their practical application on commercial AUV platforms. This paper proposes a robust fixed-time adaptive LOS guidance framework for 3D path tracking for AUVs. By combining fixed-time stability theory with LOS guidance, this method guarantees path tracking convergence within a preset time range, with the convergence time independent of initial conditions. Furthermore, a fixed-time adaptive estimator is developed to rapidly compensate for time-varying sideslip disturbances caused by ocean currents. A time-varying look-ahead mechanism is also introduced to improve tracking performance on curved paths. Lyapunov analysis proves the fixed-time stability of the proposed framework, and numerical simulations and physical experiments demonstrate that, compared to state-of-the-art adaptive LOS methods, this framework exhibits superior tracking accuracy, convergence speed, and anti-interference capability. In simulation, the time-varying look-ahead variant reduced cross-track and vertical-track RMSE by 69.37\% and 67.46\%, respectively, during curved-path tracking. In field experiments with an Iver 3 AUV, the proposed fixed-time guidance reduced average tracking error by 56.35\% in straight-path evaluation and 27.59\% in curved-path evaluation compared with conventional adaptive LOS guidance.The proposed method provides a practical guidance-level solution for achieving reliable autonomous navigation of AUVs in complex marine environments.

</details>


### [44] [A Personalized Dynamic Balance Evaluation Paradigm for Hip Exoskeleton-Assisted Walking under Unexpected Ground Perturbations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14765)
*Yun Chen,Oluwasegun T. Akinniyi,Qiang Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A participant-specific composite balance cost fusing seven biomechanical sub-metrics, combined with an empirical-Bayes hierarchical model, selects high-probability assistance conditions and reduces candidate-set size in perturbation-based hip-exoskeleton personalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Personalizing hip-exoskeleton assistance for recovery from unexpected gait perturbations is difficult because balance is multidimensional and human-in-the-loop experiments are small-sample and noisy.

Method: A participant-specific composite balance cost integrates seven biomechanical sub-metrics covering margin of stability, center-of-mass dynamics, and whole-body angular momentum. Sub-metrics are converted into direction-aligned, dimensionless cost features, and nonnegative fusion weights are learned on the simplex. An empirical-Bayes hierarchical model then estimates each condition's posterior probability of being best, P(best), and a high-probability candidate set of size K_0.8. Three participants walked at 1.1 m/s under unilateral belt-slip perturbations across 46 hip-assistance conditions, with B = 4 repeats per condition.

Result: With the full budget, the learned-composite selector concentrated 80% of posterior probability within 1-5 of 46 conditions, versus 2-12 for equal-weight fusion and 4-37 for PCA fusion. Selected-condition trials showed lower observed composite costs than no-torque trials, with nominal p < 0.05 for P2 and P3. Leave-one-repeat-out refits gave positive mean held-out rank correlations for all participants and moderate stability of learned weights and candidate sets.

Conclusion: These proof-of-concept results support participant-specific composite balance evaluation for candidate selection in perturbation-based human-in-the-loop experiments, potentially shortening personalization and limiting participants' exposure to repeated perturbations.

Abstract: Hip exoskeletons may improve recovery from unexpected gait perturbations, yet personalizing assistance remains difficult because balance is multidimensional and human-in-the-loop experiments are small-sample and noisy. We present a participant-specific composite balance cost that integrates seven biomechanical sub-metrics spanning margin of stability, center-of-mass dynamics, and whole-body angular momentum. The sub-metrics are converted to direction-aligned, dimensionless cost features, and nonnegative fusion weights are learned on the simplex. Coupled with an empirical-Bayes hierarchical model, the learned-composite selector estimates each tested condition's posterior probability of being best, P(best), and a high-probability candidate set with size $K_{0.8}$. The framework was evaluated with three participants walking at 1.1 m/s during unilateral belt-slip perturbations across 46 hip-assistance conditions. In the full-budget analysis (B = 4 repeats per condition), the selector concentrated 80% of the posterior probability within 1 to 5 of 46 conditions, compared with 2 to 12 for equal-weight fusion and 4 to 37 for principal component analysis fusion. This smaller candidate set could shorten personalization experiments and limit participants' exposure to repeated perturbations in future studies. Selected-condition trials showed lower observed composite costs than no-torque trials, with nominal p < 0.05 for P2 and P3. Leave-one-repeat-out refits yielded positive mean held-out rank correlations for all participants and moderate stability of the learned weights and candidate sets. These proof-of-concept results support participant-specific composite balance evaluation for candidate selection in perturbation-based human-in-the-loop experiments.

</details>


### [45] [Language-Guided Representation Learning for Robust Cross-Sensor Material Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14783)
*Mashood M. Mohsan,Muhayy Ud Din,Binzhao Xu,Ahmad Abubakar,Irfan Hussain*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes a language-guided distillation framework that aligns sensor-specific tactile images with language embeddings to learn sensor-robust, hardware-agnostic tactile representations, achieving strong few-shot and cross-sensor transfer performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robots require touch for safe, reliable manipulation since properties like softness, texture, and contact stability are hard to infer from vision alone. However, vision-based tactile sensors produce varied observations of the same material due to differences in optics, elastomer properties, and illumination, causing poor generalization when trained on single or multiple sensors. A sensor-agnostic supervisory signal is thus needed.

Method: The authors propose a language-guided distillation framework. Language encodes high-level semantic touch properties (e.g., rough, soft, slippery) that remain invariant across sensing hardware, serving as a natural sensor-agnostic supervisory signal. They construct a 39K-sample touch-language dataset with human-annotated material labels and train a tactile encoder to align sensor-specific tactile images with language embeddings in a shared semantic space.

Result: The approach is evaluated for few-shot learning and cross-sensor transfer and benchmarked on six existing tactile datasets. It achieves 95% accuracy in the 100-shot setting, improves cross-sensor transfer by an average of 13.3% accuracy, and yields up to 19% accuracy gains across six existing tactile datasets.

Conclusion: Language-guided distillation enables scalable and hardware-agnostic tactile representation learning, overcoming sensor variability. Code and dataset are available at https://mashood3624.github.io/Language_Tactile/.

Abstract: Robots need touch to manipulate objects safely and reliably, as many properties, such as softness, texture, and contact stability, are hard to infer from vision alone. However, vision-based tactile sensors yield different observations of the same material due to variations in optics, elastomer properties, and illumination, leading to poor generalization when trained on a single or multiple sensors. We propose a language-guided distillation framework for learning sensor-robust tactile representations. Language encodes high-level semantic properties of touch (e.g., rough, soft, slippery) that remain invariant across sensing hardware, providing a natural sensor-agnostic supervisory signal. We construct a 39K-sample touch-language dataset with human-annotated material labels and train a tactile encoder to align sensor-specific tactile images with language embeddings in a shared semantic space. We evaluate our approach for few-shot learning and cross-sensor transfer and benchmark it on six existing tactile datasets. Our method achieves 95% accuracy in the 100-shot setting, improves cross-sensor transfer by an average of 13.3% accuracy, and yields up to 19% accuracy gains across six existing tactile datasets. These results demonstrate that language-guided distillation enables scalable and hardware-agnostic tactile representation learning. Code and dataset are available at https://mashood3624.github.io/Language_Tactile/

</details>


### [46] [Belief-Adaptive Online Autonomy for Quadrotor UAV Navigation under GNSS Degradation in Urban Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14806)
*Deepak Kumar Panda,Weisi Guo*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a belief-adaptive online autonomy framework for quadrotors in GNSS-degraded urban airspaces, augmenting an EKF with explicit GNSS trust modelling, second-order belief adaptation, and latency-aware out-of-sequence measurement handling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reliable online autonomy is critical for quadrotor operation in urban airspaces, where GNSS measurements suffer from multipath, blockage, and latency, introducing non-stationary, temporally correlated errors that degrade conventional GNSS-IMU fusion.

Method: GNSS trust is represented as a latent belief state that modulates measurement weighting and multipath bias uncertainty, updated online via EKF consistency signals. A second-order online belief adaptation scheme enables proactive, stable sensor trust adaptation without prior environmental knowledge or offline training, combined with latency-aware out-of-sequence measurement handling.

Result: Evaluation in simulated urban air mobility scenarios with correlated multipath, stochastic latency, and obstacle constraints shows improved belief convergence, smoother trajectories, and reduced estimation and tracking errors versus naive, adaptive, and first-order baselines.

Conclusion: The framework preserves the classical GNSS-IMU fusion structure and can be integrated directly into existing flight control pipelines, supporting robust online autonomy in GNSS-degraded environments.

Abstract: Reliable online autonomy is critical for quadrotor operation in urban airspaces, where global navigation satellite systems (GNSS) measurements suffer from multipath, blockage, and latency issues, introducing non-stationary, temporally correlated errors that degrade conventional GNSS-IMU fusion. This paper presents a belief-adaptive online autonomy framework that augments an extended Kalman filter (EKF) with explicit GNSS trust modelling, second-order online belief adaptation, and latency-aware out-of-sequence measurement handling. GNSS trust is represented as a latent belief state that modulates measurement weighting and multipath bias uncertainty, and is updated online using EKF consistency signals. Unlike reactive covariance tuning, the proposed approach enables proactive and stable sensor trust adaptation without prior environmental knowledge or offline training. Evaluation in simulated urban air mobility scenarios with correlated multipath, stochastic latency, and obstacle constraints demonstrates improved belief convergence, smoother trajectories, and reduced estimation and tracking errors compared to naive, adaptive, and first-order baselines. The framework preserves classical GNSS-IMU fusion structure and can be integrated directly into existing flight control pipelines, supporting robust online autonomy in GNSS degraded environments.

</details>


### [47] [Primitive-Informed Sampling-Based MPC for Multi-Fingered Dexterous Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14868)
*Emek Barış Küçüktabak,Karankumar Patel,Jinda Cui,Zhaodong Yang,Kazuhiro Sasabuchi,Jun Takamatsu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A primitive-informed sampling-based MPC framework for multi-fingered dexterous manipulation achieves reliable in-hand rotation on a physical Allegro hand by biasing sampling with low-dimensional coordinated finger motion primitives plus joint-level residual optimization and rollout constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sampling-based MPC avoids gradients through complex contact dynamics, but direct exploration of the high-dimensional joint space is inefficient and performance depends strongly on the sampling distribution. The paper aims to overcome this inefficiency for multi-fingered dexterous manipulation.

Method: The framework biases sampling using low-dimensional manipulation primitives encoding coordinated finger motions, while simultaneously optimizing joint-level residuals to adapt these motions to the current hand-object configuration. Task-related rollout constraints reject infeasible trajectories during forward simulation, improving effective use of the sampling budget. Evaluation uses a physical Allegro hand with a synchronized MuJoCo digital twin.
