Result: Ablations show both the primitive and residual are necessary for reliable continuous in-hand rotation; increasing the sampling budget alone does not recover this coordination; rollout constraints substantially improve success rate. A primitive extracted from a single object remains effective across object sizes and under model mismatch. The framework supports grasping, object reorientation, and coordinated arm-hand reach-grasp-transport, using primitives extracted from a simulation-trained policy and human hand-motion data.

Conclusion: Primitive-informed sampling with joint-level residuals and rollout constraints enables efficient, generalizable dexterous manipulation on real hardware, extending beyond in-hand rotation to grasping, reorientation, and arm-hand coordination.

Abstract: We present a primitive-informed sampling-based model predictive control (MPC) framework for multi-fingered dexterous manipulation. Sampling-based MPC avoids the need for gradients through complex contact dynamics, but direct exploration of the high-dimensional joint space is inefficient and makes performance strongly dependent on the sampling distribution. Our framework biases sampling using low-dimensional manipulation primitives that encode coordinated finger motions, while simultaneously optimizing joint-level residuals to adapt these motions to the current hand-object configuration. Task-related rollout constraints reject infeasible trajectories during forward simulation, improving the effective use of the sampling budget. We evaluate the approach on a physical Allegro hand using a synchronized MuJoCo digital twin. Ablations show that both the primitive and residual are necessary for reliable continuous in-hand rotation, that increasing the sampling budget alone does not recover this coordination, and that rollout constraints substantially improve success rate. A primitive extracted from a single object remains effective across object sizes and under model mismatch, and the framework further supports grasping, object reorientation, and coordinated arm-hand reach-grasp-transport, using primitives extracted from both a simulation-trained policy and human hand-motion data.

</details>


### [48] [Real-World Reinforcement Learning with MPC Scaffolding for Dexterous Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14878)
*Emek Barış Küçüktabak,Karankumar Patel,Zhaodong Yang,Jinda Cui,Kazuhiro Sasabuchi,Jun Takamatsu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes using sampling-based MPC as scaffolding for real-world dexterous RL, providing structured prior experience and task guidance without human demonstrations. On a 16-DoF Allegro hand for in-hand rotation, it achieves 100% policy-only success after 7 minutes of online RL, 5x faster than MPC after 20 minutes, and 1000 consecutive rotations without a drop.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world RL could enable dexterous manipulation policies that adapt directly from physical interaction, but learning is hindered by inefficient early exploration and costly failures.

Method: A sampling-based MPC framework scaffolds real-world dexterous RL. A small set of MPC trajectories populates an offline replay buffer and pretrains actor and critic. During online learning, MPC intermittently guides data collection while an off-policy Soft Actor-Critic learner trains from both prior MPC experience and new physical interaction, with control gradually transitioning to the learned policy. No human demonstrations or corrective actions are used.

Result: On continuous in-hand rotation with a 16-DoF Allegro hand, the method reaches 100% success in policy-only evaluation (5/5 trials) after 7 minutes of online RL, following initialization with 20 MPC trajectories collected in 12 minutes. Online training incurs about three object drops on average. After 20 minutes the policy exceeds 5x the MPC rotation speed and completes 1000 consecutive rotations over 110+ minutes without a drop. Ablations confirm complementary benefits of MPC-based pretraining, retained MPC experience, and online MPC guidance. Rapid adaptation to different object geometries and goal-conditioned reorientation are also demonstrated.

Conclusion: The framework enables efficient, low-intervention, real-world dexterous RL, achieving fast learning, high robustness, and superior performance over the MPC controller while requiring minimal hardware data and very few failures.

Abstract: Real-world reinforcement learning (RL) offers a promising route to dexterous manipulation policies that can adapt directly from physical interaction, but learning is hindered by inefficient early exploration and costly failures. We propose a framework that uses sampling-based model predictive control (MPC) as scaffolding for real-world dexterous RL, providing structured prior experience and task-directed guidance during learning without human demonstrations or corrective actions. A small set of MPC trajectories is first used to populate an offline replay buffer and to pretrain the actor and critic. During online learning, MPC intermittently guides data collection while an off-policy Soft Actor-Critic learner trains from both prior MPC experience and newly collected physical interaction, with control gradually transitioning to the learned policy. On continuous in-hand rotation with a 16-DoF Allegro hand, the method reaches 100\% success in policy-only evaluation (5/5 trials) after 7 minutes of online RL, following initialization with 20 MPC trajectories collected on hardware in 12 minutes. Online training incurs about three object drops on average. After 20 minutes of online learning, the policy achieves more than five times the rotation speed of the MPC controller. It completes 1000 consecutive rotations over more than 110 minutes without a drop. Ablations show complementary benefits from MPC-based pretraining, retained MPC experience, and online MPC guidance. We further demonstrate rapid adaptation to different object geometries and successful goal-conditioned reorientation, showing that the framework enables efficient, low-intervention, real-world dexterous RL.

</details>


### [49] [HydroMap: Probabilistic Water Surface Elevation Mapping for Semantic Scene Representation in Inland Waterways](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14903)
*Zhongbi Luo,Yunjia Wang,Herman Bruyninckx,Peter Slaets*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: HydroMap reconstructs water surface elevation from stereo observations and fuses it with LiDAR structural maps into a unified 2.5D semantic representation for inland waterway navigation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous surface vehicles in inland waterways need persistent representations of both surrounding structures and the water surface. LiDAR-based SLAM produces sparse or missing water returns, so the operational water surface is absent from reconstructed scenes.

Method: An odometry-decoupled framework that reconstructs water surface elevation from stereo observations and integrates it with the structural map. Per-frame water points form joint cell observations with propagated stereo and pose uncertainty, fused into a persistent probabilistic elevation map. Semantic map conversion then combines elevation and structural geometry into a unified 2.5D representation of water, boundaries, structures, and overhead regions.

Result: On the Pohang Canal and Leuven Vaart datasets, elevation RMSE stays below 5 cm relative to LiDAR references in the same map frame. Elevation and semantic maps are published at 2 Hz and 1 Hz respectively.

Conclusion: HydroMap complements LiDAR maps with a persistent water surface representation, supporting downstream navigation in inland waterways.

Abstract: Autonomous surface vehicles operating in inland waterways require a persistent representation of both surrounding structures and the water surface. LiDAR-based simultaneous localization and mapping often produces sparse or missing water returns, leaving this operational surface absent from the reconstructed scene. We propose HydroMap, an odometry-decoupled framework that reconstructs water surface elevation from stereo observations and integrates it with the structural map. Per-frame water points form joint cell observations with propagated stereo and pose uncertainty, and successive observations are fused into a persistent probabilistic elevation map. Semantic map conversion then combines the elevation map with structural geometry in a unified 2.5D representation of water, boundaries, structures, and overhead regions. On the Pohang Canal and Leuven Vaart datasets, the elevation RMSE remains below 5 cm relative to LiDAR references expressed in the same map frame. The elevation and semantic maps are published at 2 Hz and 1 Hz, respectively. HydroMap thereby complements LiDAR maps with a persistent representation of the water surface for downstream navigation in inland waterways.

</details>


### [50] [Exact Feasibility Certification and Optimal Responsibility Allocation for Multi-Robot CBF Safety Filters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14935)
*Chandan Kumar Sah,Jishnu Keshavan*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper introduces an exact feasibility certificate for multi-robot Control Barrier Function (CBF) safety filters with heterogeneous control-affine dynamics, decomposing feasibility into safety-constraint demand versus actuator supply, and proposes an optimal shared-constraint allocation that reduces infeasibility and safety violations in simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-robot CBF safety filters can become infeasible, but a failed quadratic program (QP) provides no diagnosis of why the conflict occurred or how to resolve it. There is a need for a principled way to certify feasibility, attribute infeasibility to specific agents/interactions, and decide whether gain tuning or increased actuation can help.

Method: Develops an exact feasibility certificate for multi-agent CBF filters under heterogeneous control-affine dynamics and convex input sets. The certificate quantifies a feasibility reserve by separating the demand from safety constraints from the available actuator supply. This decomposition reveals when CBF gain tuning or increased actuation can or cannot resolve infeasibility, and identifies the responsible agents and interactions. Also proposes an algorithm for optimally allocating shared safety constraints by maximizing the worst local feasibility margin, formulated as a linear program for polyhedral input sets.

Result: In 320 paired closed-loop simulations, the proposed allocation reduced infeasible control steps from ~50% to 6.2%, and reduced safety-violating runs from 118/160 to 24/160. Across 52 infeasibility events, the certificate identified an interaction whose relaxation restored feasibility in 94% of cases.

Conclusion: The feasibility certificate and constraint-allocation method provide interpretable diagnosis of multi-robot CBF infeasibility and substantially improve closed-loop feasibility and safety, with strong performance in identifying resolvable conflicts.

Abstract: Multi-robot Control Barrier Function (CBF) safety filters can become infeasible, but a failed quadratic program (QP) does not indicate why the conflict occurred or how to resolve it. To address this, we develop an exact feasibility certificate for multi-agent CBF filters with heterogeneous control-affine dynamics and convex input sets. The certificate quantifies a feasibility reserve by separating the demand imposed by safety constraints from the available actuator supply. This decomposition shows when CBF gain tuning or increased actuation can and cannot resolve infeasibility, and identifies the agents and interactions responsible for the conflict. We further propose an algorithm to optimally allocate shared safety constraints by maximizing the worst local feasibility margin, yielding a linear program for polyhedral input sets. In $320$ paired closed-loop simulations, the proposed allocation reduces infeasible control steps from roughly $50\%$ to $6.2\%$, and reduces safety-violating runs from $118/160$ to $24/160$. In addition, across $52$ infeasibility events, the certificate identifies an interaction whose relaxation restores feasibility in $94\%$ of cases.

</details>


### [51] [Comparing Trajectories from Positions Alone: Curvature-Based Time Alignment and Drift Error Metric](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14936)
*Effie Daum,Daniele De Martini,Claire Dune,François Pomerleau*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a standardized trajectory-evaluation protocol for SLAM and localization, combining a curvature-signal-based temporal alignment method with a distance-normalized error metric, while explicitly handling synchronization, sampling alignment, and extrinsic calibration via sensitivity analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In field robotics, obtaining independent large-scale reference trajectories more accurate than the estimates being evaluated remains an open challenge. Evaluation widely relies on Absolute Trajectory Error (ATE) and Relative Pose Error (RPE), computed with automated tools, that rest on assumptions and evaluation parameters rarely made explicit. When unreported, these errors can be misleading and hinder fair comparisons.

Method: The protocol combines a novel temporal alignment method based on curvature signals with an error metric normalized by travelled distance. It explicitly accounts for temporal synchronization, sampling alignment, and extrinsic calibration, quantifying their influence through a sensitivity analysis.

Result: The approach yields more rigorous, reproducible, and standardized trajectory evaluation, with sensitivity analysis quantifying the influence of synchronization, sampling alignment, and extrinsic calibration.

Conclusion: The proposed protocol contributes to more rigorous, reproducible, and standardized trajectory evaluation for state estimation, localization, and SLAM.

Abstract: In field robotics, acquiring independent large-scale reference trajectories more accurate than the evaluated estimates remains an open challenge. The domain is widely reliant on Absolute Trajectory Error (ATE) and Relative Pose Error (RPE), computed with automated tools, that rest on assumptions and evaluation parameters rarely made explicit. When unreported, the errors can be misleading and hinder fair comparisons. This paper introduces a trajectory-evaluation protocol for standardized and reliable accuracy assessment in state estimation, localization, and Simultaneous Localization And Mapping (SLAM). The approach combines a novel temporal alignment method based on curvature signals with an error metric normalized by travelled distance. We explicitly account for temporal synchronization, sampling alignment, and extrinsic calibration, quantifying their influence through a sensitivity analysis. The proposed protocol contributes to more rigorous, reproducible, and standardized trajectory evaluation.

</details>


### [52] [Two-Stage Personalized Gait Phase Estimation in Stroke Survivors During Exoskeleton-Assisted Walking: An Offline Feasibility Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14984)
*Hyungseok Ryu,Pilwon Hur*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A two-stage sequential adaptation framework (distillation-regularized personalization + conditional low-rank adaptation) personalized healthy-pre-trained gait phase estimators to stroke survivors using a thigh-mounted IMU, cutting phase RMSE by up to 84.2% and achieving 2.90% gait-cycle error with the Transformer while meeting embedded real-time constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Gait phase estimators pre-trained on healthy gait degrade for stroke survivors due to pathological, participant-specific gait patterns; personalized estimation with functional IMU alignment is needed for exoskeleton-assisted walking, while respecting embedded real-time computation limits.

Method: Thigh-mounted IMU signals with heel force-sensitive resistor reference labels. Two-stage sequential adaptation: Stage 1 distillation-regularized participant-specific model; Stage 2 conditional refinement via low-rank adaptation. LSTM, TCN, and Transformer evaluated on five stroke survivors with a powered knee exoskeleton using leave-one-subject-out hyperparameter selection and sequential test-then-adapt Stage 2 replay; policy-specific and alignment ablations plus concurrent embedded timing tests.

Result: Stage 1+2 reduced mean participant-wise phase RMSE by 84.2% (LSTM), 77.0% (TCN), and 60.7% (Transformer) vs. non-adapted baselines. Transformer achieved the lowest final error (2.90 ± 1.13% of gait cycle) and heel-strike timing error (23.7 ± 4.5 ms). Every-cycle updates generally yielded lowest or near-lowest error; conditional updating lowered update frequency with small accuracy differences. Alignment caused model-dependent phase-error changes while preserving/improving heel-strike detection and reducing timing error for LSTM and Transformer. Embedded tests: TCN and Transformer sustained 100-Hz inference in Stage 2 without deadline misses; LSTM missed the 10-ms deadline in 6.6% of inferences. All updates finished within 0.8 s.

Conclusion: The proposed functional-alignment, two-stage sequential adaptation framework is feasible offline and computationally viable for embedded deployment, supporting personalized gait phase estimation for exoskeleton-assisted walking in stroke survivors.

Abstract: This study evaluated personalized gait phase estimation for stroke survivors using functional inertial measurement unit (IMU) alignment and two-stage sequential adaptation of models pre-trained on healthy gait. The estimator used signals from a thigh-mounted IMU. Heel force-sensitive resistor measurements provided reference phase labels for offline adaptation and evaluation. Stage 1 established a distillation-regularized participant-specific model, and Stage 2 performed conditional refinement using low-rank adaptation. Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM), Temporal Convolutional Network (TCN), and Transformer models were evaluated in five stroke survivors walking with a powered knee exoskeleton using leave-one-subject-out hyperparameter selection and sequential test-then-adapt Stage 2 replay. Relative to the non-adapted baselines, Stage 1+2 reduced the mean participant-wise phase root mean square error by 84.2%, 77.0%, and 60.7%, respectively. The Transformer achieved the lowest final error (2.90 +- 1.13$% of the gait cycle) and heel-strike timing error (23.7 +- 4.5ms). Policy-specific ablations showed that every-cycle updates generally produced the lowest or near-lowest error, whereas conditional updating reduced the update frequency with small accuracy differences. After personalization, alignment produced model-dependent changes in phase error while preserving or improving heel-strike detection and reducing heel-strike timing error for the LSTM and Transformer. Concurrent embedded tests showed that the TCN and Transformer maintained 100-Hz inference during Stage 2 updates without deadline misses, whereas the LSTM missed the 10-ms deadline in 6.6% of inferences. All updates completed within 0.8s. These results support the offline feasibility and embedded computational timing of the proposed framework for exoskeleton-assisted walking.

</details>


### [53] [From Learned-Mode AV-Traffic Pairing to Planner Decisions: A Marginal-Preserving Study on Argoverse 2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14997)
*Jingyu Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A marginal-preserving product control removes AV-traffic pairing in joint motion forecasts while holding actor-level marginals fixed; it shifts a fraction of planner decisions but leaves actor-level forecast metrics identical, and matching analysis cannot isolate recorded-outcome effects from conditioned-concentration changes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Joint motion forecasts pair each autonomous-vehicle future with surrounding traffic, yet actor-level metrics cannot reveal whether this pairing structure actually matters to a downstream planner. The paper asks whether the learned AV-traffic pairing influences planner decisions independently of actor-level forecast quality.

Method: A marginal-preserving product control intervening on learned modes: it removes AV-traffic pairing while retaining the fixed constant-velocity pair and holding trajectories, actor-level marginals before planner conditioning, candidates, cost terms and weights, and fallback fixed. Evaluation spans twelve runs on 1,400 held-out Argoverse 2 scenarios, with route-level offline selection changes, control-minus-joint recorded-trajectory regret, crossed and seed-t intervals, concentration matching, and pairing-strength and temperature sweeps.

Result: At τ=4 m, the intervention changes 3.0% of route-level offline selections; control-minus-joint recorded-trajectory regret is −0.026 and −0.118 at the two training sizes, with crossed and seed-t intervals spanning zero. At τ=1 m, relative costs change in 87.9% of route evaluations and route-level selections in 8.1%. Before concentration matching, descriptive outcome estimates favor the control; most of this gap disappears along an approximate concentration-matching path, leaving contrasts of +0.112 and −0.047 with both crossed intervals spanning zero. Actor-level forecast metrics remain identical, and decision contrast grows with pairing removal and sharper conditioning.

Conclusion: The intervention changes planner decisions even though actor-level metrics remain unchanged, indicating that joint AV-traffic pairing is decision-relevant. However, the matching analysis cannot separate any recorded-outcome effect of learned-mode AV-traffic pairing from the accompanying change in conditioned concentration.

Abstract: Joint motion forecasts pair each autonomous-vehicle (AV) future with surrounding traffic, but actor-level metrics do not show whether that structure matters to a planner. We study this question with a marginal-preserving product control that removes AV-traffic pairing among the learned modes while retaining the fixed constant-velocity pair and holding trajectories, actor-level marginals before planner conditioning, candidates, the cost terms and weights, and fallback fixed. The intervention also changes candidate-conditioned concentration. Across twelve runs on 1,400 held-out Argoverse 2 scenarios, the intervention changes 3.0% of route-level offline selections at $τ=4$ m. Control-minus-joint recorded-trajectory regret is $-0.026$ and $-0.118$ at the two training sizes; crossed and seed-$t$ intervals span zero. At $τ=1$ m, relative costs change in 87.9% of route evaluations and route-level offline selections in 8.1%. Before concentration matching, descriptive outcome estimates favor the control. Most of this gap disappears along an approximate concentration-matching path; the remaining contrasts are $+0.112$ and $-0.047$, and both crossed intervals span zero. Actor-level forecast metrics remain identical. Pairing-strength and temperature sweeps show that the decision contrast grows with pairing removal and sharper conditioning. The intervention changes planner decisions even though actor-level metrics remain unchanged. The matching analysis, however, cannot separate any recorded-outcome effect of learned-mode AV-traffic pairing from the accompanying change in conditioned concentration.

</details>


### [54] [IMPACT-VLA: Interaction-aware Multimodal Propagation Attribution via Counterfactual Trajectories for Vision-Language-Action Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15005)
*Jinwoong Kim,Sangjin Park*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: IMPACT-VLA attributes multimodal input contributions in VLA robot policies via phase-modality blocks and closed-loop counterfactual re-execution, revealing phase-dependent and conditionally coupled modality roles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: It is unclear at which execution stages each modality (vision, proprioception, language) contributes to task success and how input interventions propagate through subsequent states, observations, and actions. Existing attribution methods measure local sensitivity or temporally aggregated importance, failing to capture phase-dependent contributions and cross-phase dependencies.

Method: IMPACT-VLA constructs behavioral phases from action transitions in a successful reference rollout, aligns them with policy query boundaries, and defines phase-modality blocks as attribution units. It performs closed-loop counterfactual re-execution to quantify each block's contribution to final task success, then analyzes cross-phase non-additive interactions and trajectory propagation while distinguishing behavioral from functional recovery.

Result: Across 30 LIBERO manipulation tasks with OpenVLA-OFT, dominant-modality transitions occurred in 25 tasks (83.3%). Closed-loop attribution identified task-critical information more faithfully than Static Action Perturbation. Later-block marginal gains for negatively interacting pairs increased by ~3.3x under early-phase input replacement, and functional recovery occurred without behavioral recovery.

Conclusion: The work reveals when multimodal inputs support task success and how their contributions become conditionally coupled during closed-loop execution.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) policies perform robot manipulation tasks using multimodal inputs such as visual observations, proprioceptive states, and language instructions. However, it remains unclear at which execution stages each modality contributes to final task success and how input interventions propagate through subsequent states, observations, and actions. Existing attribution approaches primarily measure local sensitivity or temporally aggregated importance, limiting their ability to capture phase-dependent contributions and cross-phase dependencies. We propose Interaction-aware Multimodal Propagation Attribution via Counterfactual Trajectories for Vision-Language-Action Policies (IMPACT-VLA). IMPACT-VLA constructs behavioral phases from action transitions in a successful reference rollout, aligns them with policy query boundaries, and defines phase-modality blocks as attribution units. It then performs closed-loop counterfactual re-execution to quantify each block's contribution to final task success. We further analyze cross-phase non-additive interactions and trajectory propagation while distinguishing behavioral from functional recovery. Across 30 LIBERO robot manipulation tasks using OpenVLA-OFT, dominant-modality transitions occurred in 25 tasks (83.3%), and closed-loop attribution identified task-critical information more faithfully than Static Action Perturbation. Later-block marginal gains for negatively interacting pairs increased by approximately 3.3x under early-phase input replacement, while functional recovery could occur without behavioral recovery. These results reveal when multimodal inputs support task success and how their contributions become conditionally coupled during closed-loop execution.

</details>


### [55] [Atomic Motion Coordinate for Language-Steerable and Force-Responsive Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15012)
*Jiaqi Zhai,Jingkai Zhao,Chen Yang,Siyuan Ma,Yutian Zhang,Liwen Yang,Qinglian Wu,Weiqi Fan,Yifei Wang,Yi Zheng,Chenxi Gu,Dong Wei,Wei Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper introduces Atomic Motion Coordinate (AMC), a geometry-grounded coordinate representation for steerable and force-responsive VLA manipulation, enabling language instructions to control end-effector motion rather than being dominated by visual priors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses whether changing only the language instruction can redirect a VLA policy's end effector, or whether the visually driven motion prior dominates. It aims to make manipulation steerable via language and responsive to contact forces.

Method: AMC assigns each arm thirteen signed translation, rotation, and hold atoms grounded from text and forward kinematics with vision withheld. The coordinate is injected into every action-expert block via weighted codebook alignment. Contact history modulates the coordinate through a bounded spherical residual recomputed from a fixed nominal latent to regenerate only the unexecuted horizon suffix.

Result: Across 7,520 offline horizon interventions, opposite-atom separation reaches 92.5/83.1% (single/dual) vs. 39.1/24.0% for LA4VLA-style. Over 50 real-robot trials per task, AMC raises OOD fruit progress from 60.5% to 87.8%; force adaptation raises Plug/Vase from 59.0/71.5% to 78.5/75.2%.

Conclusion: The results indicate that language instructions can effectively steer a VLA policy when actions are grounded in a geometry-based atomic motion coordinate rather than dominated by visual motion priors, and that the same coordinate supports force-responsive adaptation.

Abstract: Can changing only the language instruction redirect a VLA policy's end effector, or does the visually driven motion prior dominate? We present Atomic Motion Coordinate, a geometry-grounded coordinate for steerable and force-responsive manipulation. Each arm owns thirteen signed translation, rotation, and hold atoms grounded from text and forward kinematics with vision withheld, and the coordinate is injected into every action-expert block via weighted codebook alignment. Contact history modulates the same coordinate through a bounded spherical residual that is recomputed from a fixed nominal latent to regenerate only the unexecuted horizon suffix. Across 7,520 offline horizon interventions, opposite-atom separation reaches 92.5/83.1% (single/dual) versus 39.1/24.0% for LA4VLA-style. Across 50 real-robot trials per task, AMC raises OOD fruit progress from 60.5% to 87.8%; force adaptation raises Plug/Vase from 59.0/71.5% to 78.5/75.2%.

</details>


### [56] [Steering Generative Robot Policies with Lexicographic Preferences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15014)
*Yixuan Jia,Jonathan P. How*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A frozen diffusion/flow-matching robot policy can be steered at inference—via dynamic-barrier guidance plus cascade sample selection—to satisfy lexicographically ordered deployment objectives without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pretrained generative policies generalize well, but deployment introduces requirements and preferences not seen in training. These are often prioritized in a lexicographic order (e.g., feasibility constraints first, then user preferences) that varies per deployment, which standard weighted-sum conditioning cannot express.

Method: Two sampler modifications on a frozen policy: (1) dynamic-barrier guidance on sampled trajectories constrains lower-priority updates so higher-priority costs do not increase to first order; (2) a cascade filter selects the executed sample by successively filtering candidates at each priority level. Policy weights are unchanged.

Result: On a navigation benchmark, improved success, traversability, and preference compliance over the frozen policy, and substantially better compliance than tuned weighted-sum baselines. Transfers to a flow-matching manipulation policy on LIBERO, improving compliance without reducing task success. In a controlled manipulation study, the dynamic barrier matches best achievable performance over a much wider parameter range than a fixed weight.

Conclusion: Inference-time, weight-free steering can enforce lexicographically ordered deployment objectives for both diffusion and flow-matching robot policies, offering a more robust alternative to weighted-sum tuning.

Abstract: Pretrained generative robot policies can produce effective behaviors across diverse environments, but deployment can lead to requirements and preferences that may not have been represented during training. Furthermore, at deployment, an operator, user, or application may assign these requirements and preferences a priority order that can vary across deployments. For example, embodiment-specific feasibility constraints may need to be satisfied first, while user-specific preferences guide behavior among the feasible options. We show that a frozen generative robot policy---based on either diffusion or flow matching---can be steered at inference time to respect such lexicographically ordered deployment objectives.
  To achieve this, we introduce two modifications to the sampler. First, we apply dynamic-barrier guidance to sampled trajectories, constraining lower-priority updates so that higher-priority costs do not increase (up to first order). Second, we select the executed sample using a cascade that successively filters candidate samples according to each priority level. The policy weights remain unchanged.
  On a navigation benchmark, we demonstrate that our method improves success, traversability, and preference compliance over the frozen policy, and achieves substantially better compliance than tuned weighted-sum baselines. The same method transfers to a flow-matching manipulation policy on LIBERO, where it improves compliance without reducing task success. A controlled manipulation study further shows that, in settings where a fixed weight can match the desired ordering, the dynamic barrier reaches comparable best performance over a substantially wider range of parameter settings.

</details>


### [57] [Task-Distribution-Aware Counterweight Synthesis and Constrained Co-Design for Serial Manipulators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15082)
*Mohammad Abbadi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes a task-distribution-aware passive counterweight synthesis framework where the operating distribution ρ(q) enters the design explicitly, deriving closed-form optimal counterweight moment p* and rating feasible coverage gains via a three-link manipulator case study.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Passive counterweights are simple gravity compensators, but a counterweight selected from a single pose is generally not optimal for the configurations and tasks a manipulator actually executes. Thus a design method accounting for the true operating distribution is needed.

Method: For counterweight moment p=m_c r_c with gravity torque -gpφ(q), the weighted mean-square residual gravity torque has closed-form minimizer p*=E_ρ[τ_gφ]/(gE_ρ[φ^2]). For affine payload gravity torque the optimum is affine: p*(m_p,ρ)=p_0*(ρ)+m_p K_p(ρ). For fixed static moment, counterweight inertia is I_c=p r_c and mass m_c=p/r_c, making mass-radius selection underdetermined without physical constraints. A recovered three-link manipulator serves as case study, with nondominated fronts and a rated-torque-referenced all-joint screen plus a lumped point-mass trajectory study.

Result: At r_c=0.20 m, zero-payload equivalent optima are 0.672 kg (uniform joint-space), 0.683 kg (approximately uniform task-space), 0.713 kg (representative pick-and-place family), and 0.952 kg (high-gravity-biased distribution)—over 40% change solely from the operating distribution. Nondominated fronts show preferred mass-radius pairs depend on declared engineering bounds. The rated-torque-referenced screen raises zero-payload feasible task-space coverage from 78.1% (no compensation) to 93.7% (uniform-distribution design). The trajectory study gives a provisional crossover from no counterweight at very aggressive motion to stronger compensation as motion slows.

Conclusion: Designing passive counterweights around the actual task distribution materially changes the optimal solution and feasible workspace, and actuator/dynamic findings are engineering consequence studies requiring physical validation.

Abstract: Passive counterweights are simple gravity compensators, but a counterweight selected from a single pose is not generally optimal for the configurations and tasks a manipulator actually executes. This paper develops a task-distribution-aware synthesis framework in which the operating distribution $ρ(q)$ enters the design explicitly. For a counterweight moment $p=m_c r_c$ with gravity torque $-gpφ(q)$, the weighted mean-square residual gravity torque has the closed-form minimizer $p^*=E_ρ[τ_gφ]/(gE_ρ[φ^2])$. If payload gravity torque is affine in payload mass, the optimum is also affine: $p^*(m_p,ρ)=p_0^*(ρ)+m_pK_p(ρ)$. For fixed static moment, added counterweight inertia is $I_c=pr_c$ while mass is $m_c=p/r_c$, so mass-radius selection is underdetermined unless physical constraints are specified. A recovered three-link manipulator is used as a case study. At $r_c=0.20$ m, zero-payload equivalent optima are 0.672 kg for uniform joint-space operation, 0.683 kg for approximately uniform task-space operation, 0.713 kg for a representative pick-and-place family, and 0.952 kg for a high-gravity-biased distribution, a change of more than 40% caused solely by the operating distribution. Nondominated fronts show that preferred mass-radius pairs depend on declared engineering bounds. A rated-torque-referenced all-joint screen increases zero-payload feasible task-space coverage from 78.1% without compensation to 93.7% for the uniform-distribution design. A lumped point-mass trajectory study gives a provisional crossover from no counterweight at very aggressive motion to stronger compensation as motion slows. These actuator and dynamic results are engineering consequence studies rather than physical validation.

</details>


### [58] [Legislating World-Model-Based Planning with Legal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15113)
*Dylan Waldner,Yiannis Kantaros,Guido Governatori,Risto Miikkulainen,Amir Banifatemi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper formalizes two "isomorphism gaps" (grounding and ontological) that hinder aligning legal norms with robot control, and introduces a Defeasible Deontic Logic planning stack that enforces ex ante legal governance on a simulated robot arm, showing higher normative abidance, efficient auditable reasoning, and adaptability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As robotic systems become more general, legal norms are needed to integrate them into society. Aligning legal source texts with their encodings poses an isomorphism problem, exacerbated by perception error grounding false atoms and by one legal conclusion admitting many faithful translations into planning constraints.

Method: The paper extends the isomorphism problem of aligning legal source texts with their encodings and introduces a legal planning stack using Defeasible Deontic Logic (DDL) to constrain a motion planner. The stack leverages learned world models for planning and legal context, enabling ex ante governance that intervenes before an illegal action is executed. It was deployed on a simulated robot arm pushing a cube across a 3x3 grid.

Result: (1) The legislated agent abided substantially more often than the non-legislated one, with perception-uncertainty modeling lifting abidance further; (2) legal reasoning ran efficiently at runtime with auditable verdicts; (3) the stack adapted to exogenous signals and endogenous rule changes; (4) world model and probe error corrupted the factual input for the DDL reasoner (grounding isomorphism gap); and (5) a single law admitted several faithful metric interpretations yielding drastically different abidance (ontological isomorphism gap).

Conclusion: Ex ante legislation functions as intended. Closing the grounding and ontological isomorphism gaps via a standardized mapping from law to runtime constraints and improved fact grounding from perception will yield robust laws that align robot behavior with society's norms.

Abstract: As robotic systems grow more general, legal norms are needed to integrate them into society. This paper extends the isomorphism problem of aligning legal source texts with their encodings, and measures two key challenges to robot normative control: (1) the \textit{grounding isomorphism gap}, where perception error grounds false atoms for legal reasoning, and (2) the \textit{ontological isomorphism gap}, where one legal conclusion admits many faithful translations into planning constraints. The paper introduces a legal planning stack that employs Defeasible Deontic Logic (DDL) to constrain a motion planner. The stack leverages learned world models to plan and to provide legal context, enabling \textit{ex ante} governance that intervenes before an illegal action is executed. It was deployed on a simulated robot arm pushing a cube across a $3\times3$ grid. The findings were (1) the legislated agent abided substantially more often than the non-legislated one, and modeling perception uncertainty lifted abidance even further, (2) the legal reasoning ran efficiently at runtime and its verdicts were auditable, and (3) the stack adapted to exogenous signals and endogenous rule changes. Both gaps were measured: (4) world model and probe error corrupted the factual input for the DDL reasoner, and (5) a single law admitted several faithful metric interpretations yielding drastically different abidance. Thus, \textit{ex ante} legislation functions as intended, and closing these gaps with a standardized mapping from the law to runtime constraints and improved fact grounding from perception will yield robust laws that align robot behavior with society's norms.

</details>


### [59] [C$^2$Nav: Compare Before You Commit for Zero-Shot Vision-and-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15142)
*Runtian Zheng,Congpeng Zhang,Ying Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: C2Nav is a training-free zero-shot VLN-CE framework that keeps geometry on the robot side and asks the VLM only to compare controller-constructed alternatives, achieving up to 44.0% SR and 29.0% SPL on OpenNav R2R-CE 100.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing zero-shot VLN-CE systems ask VLMs for cardinal outputs (waypoints, pixels, headings, progress values, absolute arrival decisions), which couples generative responses to geometric magnitude or forces irreversible commitments. The authors explore whether a comparative decision interface, where thresholds and execution stay on the physical side, is a better model-robot interface.

Method: C2Nav is a training-free framework with three faculties. Seeing performs ordinal Gaze Election over physically vetted candidate views. Remembering maintains a compact route sketch and compares adjacent instruction-leg hypotheses. Arriving combines a hesitation ladder, look-back comparison, and revocable walk-back for stopping. The VLM only compares alternatives; geometry, thresholds, action magnitude, and execution remain on the robot side.

Result: On OpenNav R2R-CE 100, C2Nav with Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct reaches 41.0% OSR, 31.0% SR, 16.7% SPL; with GPT-5.5 it reaches 54.0% OSR, 44.0% SR, 29.0% SPL. Ablations reduce SR to 14.0% (no Seeing), 25.0% (no Remembering), 29.0% (no Arriving). Role inversions replacing comparative with cardinal/absolute questions reduce SR to 12.0%, 28.0%, and 21.0% in spatial, transition, and terminal slots.

Conclusion: A constrained comparative decision interface and stronger VLM reasoning are complementary rather than interchangeable: gains from the interface design and from model capability stack, and both ablations and role inversions confirm the necessity of each faculty and the comparative answer form.

Abstract: Zero-shot vision-and-language navigation in continuous environments (VLN-CE) increasingly places foundation vision-language models (VLMs) inside the navigation loop. Existing systems commonly request cardinal outputs such as waypoints, pixels, headings, progress values, or absolute arrival decisions, coupling a generative response to geometric magnitude or an irreversible commitment. We study a complementary model-robot interface: the VLM compares controller-constructed alternatives, while geometry, thresholds, action magnitude, and execution remain on the physical side. We instantiate this idea in C2Nav, a training-free framework with three coordinated faculties. Seeing performs ordinal Gaze Election over physically vetted candidate views; Remembering maintains a compact route sketch and compares adjacent instruction-leg hypotheses; and Arriving combines a hesitation ladder, look-back comparison, and revocable walk-back for reliable stopping. On the public OpenNav R2R-CE 100 protocol, C2Nav with Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct obtains 41.0% OSR, 31.0% SR, and 16.7% SPL, while the same interface with the standard GPT-5.5 model reaches 54.0% OSR, 44.0% SR, and 29.0% SPL. Whole-faculty ablations reduce SR to 14.0% without Seeing, 25.0% without Remembering, and 29.0% without Arriving. Matched role inversions that replace only the comparative answer form with cardinal/absolute questions reduce SR to 12.0%, 28.0%, and 21.0% in the spatial, transition, and terminal slots, respectively. The results indicate that a constrained decision interface and stronger VLM reasoning are complementary rather than interchangeable.

</details>


### [60] [LieSpline-DP: Lie-Group B-Spline Diffusion Policy for Smooth Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15162)
*Erxuan Xie,Bang Liu,Pingyun Nie,Xingkai Liu,Zhuang Fu,Bo Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: LieSpline-DP is a Lie-group B-spline diffusion policy that generates end-effector trajectories directly on SE(3) and enforces C² continuity across action chunks via shared boundary control poses, improving smoothness and success rates over standard Diffusion Policy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion Policy is effective for robotic manipulation via learning from demonstration but produces discontinuous, non-smooth trajectories. Existing spline-based action representations improve intra-chunk smoothness but do not guarantee cross-chunk C² continuity and ignore the group structure of SE(3).

Method: The authors propose LieSpline-DP, a Lie-group B-spline diffusion policy that generates end-effector trajectories directly on the SE(3) manifold and couples consecutive action plans by sharing their boundary control poses, thereby ensuring C² continuity across the entire planned trajectory.

Result: Across three real-robot tasks, LieSpline-DP yields lower trajectory jerk and higher task success rates than the DP baseline. Gains are especially large for liquids and flexible objects: 100% success on both pouring and bucket hooking versus 10% and 30% for the DP baseline.

Conclusion: Encoding SE(3) geometry and cross-chunk C² continuity into a B-spline diffusion policy substantially improves motion smoothness and manipulation reliability, particularly for contact-rich tasks involving liquids and deformable/flexible objects.

Abstract: Diffusion Policy (DP) is a powerful Learning from Demonstration (LfD) method for robotic manipulation, yet it suffers from discontinuous and non-smooth trajectories. Spline-based action representations promote smooth motion within individual action chunks, but existing spline-based methods neither guarantee cross-chunk $C^2$ continuity nor account for the group structure of $\mathrm{SE}(3)$. We therefore propose LieSpline-DP, a Lie-group B-spline diffusion policy that generates end-effector trajectories directly on $\mathrm{SE}(3)$ and couples consecutive plans by sharing their boundary control poses, ensuring $C^2$ continuity throughout the entire planned trajectory. Across three real-robot tasks, LieSpline-DP produces lower trajectory jerk and higher task success rates than the DP baseline. The gains are particularly pronounced in real-world tasks involving liquids and flexible objects: in our real-robot experiments, LieSpline-DP achieved a 100% success rate on both pouring and bucket hooking, whereas the DP baseline achieved only 10% and 30%, respectively.

</details>


### [61] [PredTac: Learning Contact-Rich Manipulation with Predicted Touch](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15198)
*Weijia Fan,Daqiang Guo*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: PredTac learns to predict tactile states from vision and robot states, using the predicted touch as an interface for policy learning, achieving near measured-touch performance on contact-rich tasks without physical tactile sensors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Physical tactile sensors impose hardware, calibration, synchronization, and maintenance costs that complicate policy learning and deployment, so the authors seek an alternative source of contact information that avoids these burdens.

Method: A tactile predictor is trained with tactile supervision to infer tactile states from causal visual observations and robot states; the predicted touch is then used as an explicit interface for downstream policy training and execution without measured tactile input. Evaluated on three contact-rich manipulation tasks in simulation and on a real robot, including goal-offset evaluations and fixed-policy interventions probing sensitivity to predicted contact content.

Result: In simulation goal-offset evaluations, predicted-touch policies reach 27.0%, 52.0%, and 44.7% success on USB, Barbed-spike, and Valve, improving over the visual baseline by 8.0-13.7 percentage points. On the real robot, predicted-touch ACT achieves 70.0%, 50.0%, and 90.0% on USB insertion, Barbed extraction, and Valve rotation (mean 70.0%), approaching measured-touch ACT (72.2%) and far exceeding visual ACT (21.1%). Fixed-policy interventions show sensitivity to spatial structure, with spatial rearrangement at fixed value distributions reducing Valve success by 10.7 percentage points.

Conclusion: Predicted touch can supply useful contact information for contact-rich manipulation, approaching measured-touch performance while eliminating the need for tactile sensing as a policy input.

Abstract: Contact-rich manipulation benefits from tactile feedback, yet physical tactile sensors introduce hardware, calibration, synchronization, and maintenance costs that complicate policy learning and deployment. We formulate predicted touch as an alternative to measured tactile input and present PredTac, a framework that learns to infer tactile states from causal visual observations and robot states and uses the predicted touch as an explicit interface for policy learning and execution. A tactile predictor is first trained with tactile supervision and then used to provide contact information without requiring measured tactile input during downstream policy training or execution. We evaluate PredTac across three contact-rich manipulation tasks in simulation and on a real robot, and further examine how policy performance depends on the predicted contact content. In simulation goal-offset evaluations, predicted-touch policies achieve 27.0%, 52.0%, and 44.7% success on USB, Barbed-spike, and Valve, respectively, improving over the visual baseline by 8.0-13.7 percentage points. On the real robot, predicted-touch ACT achieves 70.0%, 50.0%, and 90.0% success on USB insertion, Barbed extraction, and Valve rotation, respectively, with a three-task mean of 70.0%, approaching measured-touch ACT at 72.2% and substantially outperforming visual ACT at 21.1%. Fixed-policy interventions further show that performance is sensitive to the spatial structure of predicted contact, with spatial rearrangement at fixed value distributions reducing Valve success by 10.7 percentage points. These results demonstrate that predicted touch can provide useful contact information for contact-rich manipulation without requiring tactile sensing as a policy input.

</details>


### [62] [X-WBC: A Cross-Embodiment Foundation Model for Humanoid Whole-Body Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15213)
*Juntong Zhang,Chun Gu,Li Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: X-WBC is a cross-embodiment foundation framework for humanoid whole-body control that decouples shared human motion semantics from robot-specific execution, using aligned human-centered command tokens, a causal Transformer backbone, and lightweight per-robot modules. Joint training across nine simulated and four real robots improves tracking and enables consistent control across command sources.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scaling humanoid whole-body control toward general-purpose deployment needs large human motion corpora and training experience shared across robot bodies, but existing methods train one policy per robot, isolating motion experience across embodiments.

Method: X-WBC separates relatively shared human motion semantics from embodiment-specific physical execution. Human-centered command tokens align full human motion, robot reference motion, and sparse VR observations. A causal Transformer learns reusable temporal structure from mixed multi-robot rollouts, while lightweight robot-specific modules map the shared representation to each robot's proprioception and action space.

Result: Across nine simulated embodiments, external motions, and four real robots, joint training improves tracking, the aligned representation supports consistent control across command sources, and the learned policy remains competitive beyond the training corpus.

Conclusion: Heterogeneous humanoids can serve as joint data sources, establishing cross-embodiment joint training as a practical route toward whole-body control foundation models.

Abstract: Scaling humanoid whole-body control toward general-purpose deployment requires large human motion corpora and training experience shared across robot bodies. Existing methods usually train one policy per robot, leaving motion experience isolated across embodiments. We introduce X-WBC, a cross-embodiment foundation framework that separates relatively shared human motion semantics from embodiment-specific physical execution. Human-centered command tokens align full human motion, robot reference motion, and sparse VR observations. A causal Transformer learns reusable temporal structure from mixed multi-robot rollouts, while lightweight robot-specific modules map the shared representation to each robot's proprioception and action space. Across nine simulated embodiments, external motions, and four real robots, experiments show that joint training improves tracking, the aligned representation supports consistent control across command sources, and the learned policy remains competitive beyond the training corpus. These results support heterogeneous humanoids as joint data sources and establish cross-embodiment joint training as a practical route toward whole-body control foundation models.

</details>


### [63] [A Vision Based Framework Integrating Attention and Action Cues for Interpretable Cognitive Workload Assessment in Human Robot Collaborative Assembly](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15232)
*Junyan Xionga,Naiyi Feng,Xingke Xia,Qihang Fan,Suchang Chen,Daqiang Guo*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A vision-based attention–action framework (HRC-CWL) uses RGB-D observations, robot states, and task-area calibration to continuously and interpretably assess operator workload in human-robot collaborative assembly without body-worn sensors, validated against subjective ratings and ECG signals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human-robot collaboration in industrial assembly demands that operators continuously coordinate manual tasks with real-time task information and robot behaviors, causing fluctuating workload. Conventional workload assessment relies on body-worn physiological sensors, which complicates practical deployment.

Method: The framework combines RGB-D observations with robot states and calibrated task-related areas to build a temporally confirmed representation of operator behavior. This representation identifies where task demand concentrates and explains how it develops when attention and action diverge, task context changes, or the operator hesitates. It was evaluated in a three-level collaborative gearbox assembly experiment with ten participants, using subjective ratings and synchronized physiological signals as independent references.

Result: Raw NASA-TLX ratings confirmed increasing perceived workload across conditions, with significant effects on overall workload and its mental and temporal dimensions. The vision-derived HRC-CWL output was significantly associated with ECG-derived features in seven of nine participants with complete correlation data. Synchronized interaction episodes showed temporal correspondence between detected hesitation and physiological activity. Real-time deployment confirmed the framework operates without requiring operators to wear additional sensors.

Conclusion: HRC-CWL is an interpretable behavioral proxy for cognitive ergonomics analysis and adaptive robot assistance, rather than a direct psychophysiological measure of workload.

Abstract: The introduction of human-robot collaboration (HRC) in industrial assembly operations is revolutionizing the manufacturing landscape. In this evolving environment, operators are required to seamlessly coordinate their manual tasks with real-time task information and robotic behaviors. These demands fluctuate during operation, yet conventional workload assessments depend on body-worn physiological sensors that complicate practical deployment. Here, we present a vision-based attention--action framework for continuous and interpretable workload-related assessment in HRC assembly. The framework combines RGB-D observations with robot states and calibrated task-related areas to construct a temporally confirmed representation of operator behavior. This representation identifies where task demand is concentrated and explains how it develops when attention and action diverge, the task context changes, or the operator hesitates. We evaluated the framework in a three-level collaborative gearbox assembly experiment with ten participants, using subjective ratings and synchronized physiological signals as independent references. Raw NASA-TLX ratings confirmed increasing perceived workload across conditions, with significant effects on overall workload and its mental and temporal dimensions. The vision-derived HRC-CWL output was significantly associated with ECG-derived features in seven of nine participants with complete correlation data. Synchronized interaction episodes further showed temporal correspondence between detected hesitation and physiological activity. Real-time deployment demonstrated that the framework can operate without requiring operators to wear additional sensors. These findings support HRC-CWL as an interpretable behavioral proxy for cognitive ergonomics analysis and adaptive robot assistance, rather than a direct psychophysiological measure of workload.

</details>


### [64] [Distributed Safe Cooperative Vector Field for Trajectory Curvature Constrained Multi-Robot Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15266)
*Zhouru Xiao,Tao Teng,Weijia Yao,Hui Zhang,Xiangke Wang,Yaonan Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a distributed safe cooperative vector field approach for multi-robot path-following that respects trajectory curvature constraints, using an adaptive collision avoidance vector field requiring only one virtual variable per neighbor, validated by simulations and real experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Practical multi-robot systems have limited turning capabilities, so trajectory curvature constraints are inherent. Ignoring these constraints can cause task failure and path divergence, motivating a cooperative method that guarantees physically feasible motion and collision avoidance.

Method: A distributed safe cooperative vector field composed of two parts: a cooperative vector field for cooperative motion, and a safety-oriented collision avoidance vector field with an adaptively adjustable reactive boundary that accommodates robots' kinematic curvature constraints. Each robot needs only a single virtual variable from each neighboring robot to achieve cooperative motion and both obstacle and inter-robot collision avoidance.

Result: Effectiveness is validated through simulations and real-world experiments on an actual multi-robot platform.

Conclusion: The approach enables distributed, safe, cooperative multi-robot path following under trajectory curvature constraints, ensuring physically feasible collision avoidance while requiring minimal information exchange between robots.

Abstract: Trajectory curvature constraints are inherent in practical multi-robot systems due to the limited turning capabilities of the robots. Without properly accounting for these constraints, robots may fail to accomplish assigned tasks, and their trajectories may diverge from the intended paths. This paper proposes a distributed safe cooperative vector field approach for multi-robot systems subject to trajectory curvature constraints. The proposed approach is composed of a cooperative vector field and a safety-oriented collision avoidance vector field, aiming to address the problems of cooperative motion and safe collision avoidance in multi-robot path-following tasks. A safety-oriented collision avoidance vector field with adaptively adjustable reactive boundary is developed to accommodate the kinematic curvature constraints of robots, thereby ensuring the physical feasibility of collision avoidance maneuvers. The proposed vector field requires only a single virtual variable from each neighboring robot to achieve cooperative motion and ensure both obstacle avoidance and inter-robot collision avoidance. The effectiveness of the proposed approach is validated through both simulations and real-world experiments on an actual multi-robot platform.

</details>


### [65] [Low Clearance Hinge Joint Mechanism Based on 3D Printing on Sheet Fabrication Methodology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15276)
*Jaehyung Jang,Euibin Shin,Allison M. Okamura,Jee-Hwan Ryu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A low-clearance hinge joint for 3D-printed-on-sheet fabrication is proposed, achieving 0.1 mm clearance and verified via a 3-DOF delta robot.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional origami-inspired hinge mechanisms require adhesives during assembly, complicating fabrication and limiting scalability to tens-of-centimeters-scale robots. A hinge design is needed that eliminates adhesives while maintaining low clearance.

Method: Three hinge joint types are compared, and one supporting low-clearance design across various facet thicknesses is selected. Twisting angle and bending force are analyzed, a 0.1 mm clearance is implemented, and torsional resistance is experimentally measured to quantify torque from plastic deformation and clearance.

Result: Torque from plastic deformation suffices to constrain undesired hinge degrees of freedom, while torque for twisting due to clearance is minimal. Applied to a 3-DOF delta robot manipulator, the joint enables precise motion with low clearance.

Conclusion: The proposed 3D-printed-on-sheet hinge joint simplifies fabrication without adhesives and provides low-clearance, precise actuation suitable for tens-of-centimeters-scale robots.

Abstract: This paper presents a low-clearance hinge joint mechanism based on the 3D printing on sheet fabrication method. This approach simplifies the fabrication of hinge mechanisms and overcomes limitations of conventional origami manufacturing by eliminating the need for adhesives commonly used during assembly, making it suitable for robots at the tens-of-centimeters scale. The advantages and disadvantages of three types of hinge joint mechanisms are compared, and a hinge joint that can be designed with low clearance for various facet thicknesses is selected. Based on the selected hinge joint, the twisting angle and bending force are analyzed, leading to the implementation of a clearance of 0.1 mm. Torsional resistance is experimentally evaluated to measure the torque required for twisting caused by plastic deformation and clearance. The results show that the torque associated with plastic deformation is sufficient to constrain the undesired degrees of freedom of the hinge joint, while the torque required for twisting due to clearance is minimal. Based on the analyzed data, the proposed hinge joint mechanism is applied to a 3-degree-of-freedom delta robot manipulator, demonstrating precise motion with low clearance.

</details>


### [66] [Planning in the Backbone: DiffAdapterVLA for Native Continuous Trajectory Generation with Driving VLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15322)
*Changxin Lu,Xiaoliang Meng,Yu Wu,Rui Huang,Honglin Li,Tao Chen,Kaixuan Zhou,Yadong Shao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DiffAdapterVLA injects explicit trajectory tokens into a VLM's late layers, enabling trajectory state to co-evolve with driving conditions at different depths—"Planning in the Backbone"—achieving high-quality closed-loop planning on NAVSIM with low latency and few trainable parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pretrained driving VLMs encode rich driving priors (visual, route, language, driving context), but their representation objectives remain decoupled from continuous driving planning. Existing methods generate trajectories only after the VLM forms a final condition, keeping depth-wise condition computation outside the stepwise formation of trajectory state.

Method: DiffAdapterVLA injects explicit trajectory tokens into selected VLM late layers so trajectory state participates in backbone forward computation and co-evolves with driving conditions across depths. Lightweight layer-wise DiffAdapters organize this into recursive trajectory refinement, and asymmetric joint attention preserves directed guidance from the condition stream to trajectory planning. Only lightweight trajectory modules are trained, adapting existing driving priors into continuous planning without an independent trajectory planner.

Result: NAVSIM results show high-quality closed-loop planning with low end-to-end latency and few trainable parameters, demonstrating that jointly evolving trajectory state and depth-wise driving conditions in VLM late-layer computation effectively realizes continuous trajectory planning.

Conclusion: Embedding planning directly within existing backbone computation, rather than via a separate trajectory planner, allows lightweight modular adaptation to convert pretrained driving priors into efficient continuous planning capability.

Abstract: Pretrained driving vision-language models (VLMs) integrate visual, route, language, and driving context into rich driving priors, yet their representation objectives remain separated from continuous driving planning. Existing methods typically begin trajectory generation only after the VLM has formed a final condition, leaving depth-wise condition computation outside the stepwise formation of trajectory state. We introduce DiffAdapterVLA, which realizes Planning in the Backbone: it injects explicit trajectory tokens into selected VLM late layers, bringing trajectory state into backbone forward computation, where it co-evolves with driving conditions at different depths. Lightweight layer-wise DiffAdapters organize this computation into recursive trajectory refinement, while asymmetric joint attention preserves directed guidance from the condition stream to trajectory planning. By placing planning within existing backbone computation rather than relying on an independent trajectory planner, DiffAdapterVLA adapts only lightweight trajectory modules to turn existing driving priors into efficient continuous planning capability. NAVSIM results show that it achieves high-quality closed-loop planning with low end-to-end latency using few trainable parameters, and demonstrate that jointly evolving trajectory state and depth-wise driving conditions in VLM late-layer computation effectively realizes continuous trajectory planning.

</details>


### [67] [Assistance Torque Estimation via Dynamics-Aware Optimization for Lower-Limb Exoskeleton in Complex Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15352)
*Xiao-Yin Liu,Guotao Li,Weiqun Wang,Zeng-Guang Hou*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes a dynamic-model-based assistance torque estimation method that directly generates motor-assist torque via optimization, then trains a data-driven network for real-time prediction, reducing metabolic rate, heart rate, and muscle activation in exoskeleton assistance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ground-truth human joint torque estimation depends on motion capture systems, which have limited outdoor usability and high deployment costs. Moreover, directly scaling ground-truth joint torques to derive motor torque commands is not necessarily optimal.

Method: Inspired by human motion generation, an assistance torque estimation method based on the dynamic model is proposed. From an optimization perspective, it directly generates motor-assist torque and lowers data acquisition cost. A data-driven assistance torque prediction network is then trained for accurate real-time prediction in complex outdoor environments.

Result: The optimized (estimated) assistance torque shows better phase consistency with gait trajectories and better alignment with task characteristics. Compared with the Zero torque condition, the predicted torque reduces metabolic rate by 11.8%-17.7%, heart rate by 8.9%-14.3%, and peak muscle activation levels by 28.2%-54.0%.

Conclusion: The proposed approach offers a new perspective for low-cost adaptive exoskeleton assistance.

Abstract: Ground-truth human joint torque estimation relies on motion capture systems, which suffer from limited outdoor usability and significant deployment expenses. Furthermore, direct scaling of ground-truth joint torques to obtain motor torque commands is not necessarily the optimal strategy. To address the aforementioned limitations, inspired by the human motion generation process, this paper proposes a novel assistance torque estimation method based on the dynamic model. From an optimization perspective, the proposed method directly generates motor-assist torque and lowers the cost of data acquisition. Then, a data-driven assistance torque prediction network is trained to enable accurate real-time prediction under complex outdoor environments. Experimental results demonstrate that optimized (estimated) assistance torque exhibits better phase consistency with gait trajectories and better alignment with task characteristics. Relative to the Zero torque condition, the predicted torque can decrease metabolic rate by 11.8%-17.7%, heart rate by 8.9%-14.3%, and peak muscle activation levels by 28.2%-54.0%, respectively. This provides a new perspective for low-cost adaptive exoskeleton assistance.

</details>


### [68] [Understanding User Preferences of a Slope-Aware Variable-Admittance Filter for a Robot Guide Dog](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15362)
*Federico Esposito,Mario Selvaggio,Aaron Link,Fabio Ruggiero*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This letter studies how slope-aware variable-admittance filter parameters affect user preference in force-based interaction with a robotic guide dog for the visually impaired.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Force-based interaction with a robotic guide dog requires tuning of admittance parameters, yet how these parameters should adapt with terrain slope to match user preferences is unclear.

Method: A quadruped robot with a sensor-free rigid handle combines path following, momentum-based interaction-wrench estimation, and a variable-admittance filter whose stiffness and damping are adapted online from slope information from the robot's depth camera. Policies are evaluated via high-fidelity simulations and a human-subject study with blindfolded sighted participants, using a Taguchi L9 design of experiments.

Result: Preliminary main-effect results suggest that increasing stiffness uphill and decreasing it downhill improves both objective and subjective metrics, whereas damping shows no significant main effect.

Conclusion: Slope-dependent stiffness adaptation is beneficial for user preference in robotic guide dog interaction, while damping tuning appears non-critical.

Abstract: This letter investigates how the parameters of a slope-aware variable-admittance filter influence user preferences in force-based interaction with a robotic guide dog for visually impaired individuals. The proposed system consists of a quadruped robot equipped with a sensor-free rigid handle for physical guidance. The framework combines path following, momentum-based interaction-wrench estimation, and a variable-admittance filter whose stiffness and damping are adapted online from slope information extracted by the robot's depth camera. The adaptation policies are evaluated through high-fidelity simulations and a human-subject study involving blindfolded sighted participants. Multiple strategies are compared using a Taguchi L9 design of experiments. Preliminary main-effect results suggest that increasing stiffness uphill and decreasing it downhill improves both objective and subjective metrics, whereas damping shows no significant main effect.

</details>


### [69] [From Prediction to Decision: World-Model-Guided Action Selection for Continuous Pile Excavation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15382)
*Ailing Zhang,Fan Gao,Song Zhang,Kawa Leong,Ziyu Wu,Yafei Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: WAM is a World-Action Model for autonomous wheel-loader excavation that proposes scoop candidates, filters inadmissible ones, predicts signed terrain change and loaded volume, executes the highest-predicted-load candidate, and replans in a closed loop. It cuts mean scoops by 17.1% over matched diffusion proposals, preserves 32/32 completion, beats a soft actor-critic baseline (32/32 vs 29/32), and runs the full ROS2/TensorRT loop in 72.4 ms on a Jetson AGX Orin.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Wheel-loader excavation is a sequential decision problem where each scoop alters the terrain available to later actions. A practical world model must therefore predict action consequences accurately, rank candidates in real time, and operate within the closed loop of a full-size machine.

Method: The World-Action Model proposes multiple scoops, rejects geometrically inadmissible candidates, jointly predicts signed terrain change and loaded volume, executes the candidate with the largest predicted load, and replans from the newly observed terrain. The authors compare input representations, spatial support, and five architectures to identify an accurate and efficient physics-structured predictor, and evaluate the interface on event-disjoint full-size-loader data.

Result: On 32 geometry-disjoint MinSlope test episodes, adding world-model ranking to matched diffusion proposals reduces mean scoop count from 651.8 to 540.6 (17.1%), preserves 32/32 completion, and improves every paired episode. In complete-system comparison, WAM completes 32/32 episodes versus 29/32 for an independently trained soft actor-critic policy. The ROS2/TensorRT implementation processes five candidates in 72.4 ms on a Jetson AGX Orin.

Conclusion: Simulation results establish decision-level gains, while physical experiments demonstrate real-world closed-loop feasibility of the complete perception-proposal-prediction-selection-execution loop for autonomous excavation.

Abstract: Wheel-loader excavation is a sequential decision problem in which every scoop changes the terrain available to subsequent actions. A practical world model must predict action consequences accurately, rank candidates in real time, and operate inside the closed loop of a full-size machine. We present the World-Action Model (WAM), which proposes multiple scoops, rejects geometrically inadmissible candidates, jointly predicts signed terrain change and loaded volume, executes the candidate with the largest predicted load, and replans from the newly observed terrain. On 32 geometry-disjoint MinSlope test episodes, adding world-model ranking to matched diffusion proposals reduces the mean scoop count from 651.8 to 540.6 (17.1%), preserves 32/32 completion, and improves every paired episode. In a complete-system comparison, WAM completes 32/32 episodes versus 29/32 for an independently trained soft actor-critic policy. Comparisons of input representations, spatial support, and five architectures identify an accurate and efficient physics-structured predictor. We further evaluate the interface on event-disjoint full-size-loader data and deploy the complete perception-proposal-prediction-selection-execution loop for autonomous excavation. The ROS2/TensorRT implementation processes five candidates in 72.4 ms on a Jetson AGX Orin. The simulation results establish decision-level gains, while the physical experiments demonstrate real-world closed-loop feasibility.

</details>


### [70] [Dynamics-Informed Reinforcement Learning for Agile and Energy-Efficient Locomotion of a Monopedal Hopping Quadcopter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15399)
*Ruigang Chen,Qi Zhang,Zhicheng Zhong,Zhuorui Yun,Yizhar Or,Mingyi Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes a Dynamics-Informed RL framework for a monopedal hopping quadcopter that embeds a target Specific Energy into the reward to constrain optimization to a physically viable energy manifold, achieving stable high-speed hopping with 82% and 73% energy reductions over baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Aerial-legged robots combine agility and efficiency, but controlling high-speed hopping under complex hybrid dynamics is challenging. RL is promising yet prone to energy-inefficient "reward hacking".

Method: Embed a target Specific Energy into the reward to constrain optimization to a physically viable energy manifold. Reward phase-consistent behavior to encourage bio-inspired stance-phase impulse, and penalize electro-mechanical power waste so motors generate efficient impulse, enabling energy injection strictly during spring restitution without heuristic state machines. Validated in MuJoCo simulations.

Result: MuJoCo simulations validate robust height regulation and forward velocity tracking up to 2.0 m/s despite severe attitude-contact coupling. The approach yields a highly agile hopping gait, reducing energy consumption by 82% and 73% compared to hovering and inefficiency baselines, respectively.

Conclusion: The Dynamics-Informed RL framework enables efficient, stable, and agile hopping control for a monopedal hopping quadcopter by embedding physical energy constraints into the reward, avoiding reward hacking and heuristic state machines.

Abstract: Although aerial-legged robots offer combined agility and efficiency, controlling high-speed hopping under complex hybrid dynamics is challenging. Reinforcement Learning (RL) is promising but prone to energy-inefficient "reward hacking". We propose a Dynamics-Informed RL framework for a monopedal hopping quadcopter. By embedding a target Specific Energy into the reward, we constrain the optimization to a physically viable energy manifold, ensuring stable hopping behaviour. By rewarding the phase-consistent behavior, it can encourage bio-inspired stance-phase impulse. Furthermore, penalizing the electro-mechanical power waste induces the motors generate an efficient impulse. This enables the policy to inject energy strictly during spring restitution without heuristic state machines. MuJoCo simulations validate robust height regulation and forward velocity tracking up to 2.0 m/s despite severe attitude-contact coupling. Ultimately, our approach yields a highly agile hopping gait, reducing energy consumption by 82% and 73% compared to hovering baselines and inefficiency baseline, respectively.

</details>
