### [71] [Learning to Exploit Passive Dynamics for Energy-Efficient Target Hopping of a Spring-Legged Quadcopter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15447)
*Ruigang Chen,Qi Zhang,Zhicheng Zhong,Zhuorui Yun,Yizhar Or,Mingyi Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A direct estimated-state-to-motor PPO policy enables a monopedal quadcopter to hop over complex terrain more efficiently than a tuned PID stack, cutting measured electrical power by 30.7% and mean normalized thrust by 49.8% while preserving repeatable height-controlled hopping.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Monopedal quadcopters combine aerial thrust with spring-loaded hopping for locomotion over complex terrain, but heuristic PID tuning limits coordination between active thrust and passive contact dynamics.

Method: A direct estimated-state-to-motor Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) policy commands all four motors without an explicit hopping state machine or low-level attitude PID. Its reward combines Energy-Manifold Shaping for mass-normalized vertical-energy tracking and apex-state anchoring with Efficiency Shaping, which uses a history-aware power estimator to penalize general power use, impose an additional airborne-power cost, and penalize airborne near-stationarity.

Result: In representative hardware runs, the PPO-based control stack reduced cycle-averaged measured electrical power by 30.7% and mean total normalized thrust by 49.8% relative to the tuned PID-based stack, while retaining repeatable commanded-height hopping and more concentrated landings.

Conclusion: The observations are consistent with improved use of passive dynamics and reduced measured electrical demand, indicating that direct state-to-motor PPO control can outperform heuristic PID tuning for hopping monopedal quadcopters.

Abstract: Combining aerial thrust with spring-loaded hopping makes monopedal quadcopters promising for locomotion over complex terrain, but heuristic proportional-integral-derivative (PID) tuning limits coordination between active thrust and passive contact dynamics. We present a direct estimated-state-to-motor Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) policy that commands four motors without an explicit hopping state machine or low-level attitude PID. Its reward combines Energy-Manifold Shaping for mass-normalized vertical-energy tracking and apex-state anchoring with Efficiency Shaping, which uses a history-aware power estimator to penalize general power use, impose an additional airborne-power cost, and penalize airborne near-stationarity. In representative hardware runs, the PPO-based control stack reduced cycle-averaged measured electrical power by 30.7% and mean total normalized thrust by 49.8% relative to the tuned PID-based control stack, while retaining repeatable commanded-height hopping and more concentrated landings. These observations are consistent with improved use of passive dynamics and reduced measured electrical demand.

</details>


### [72] [InterSocialBench: Benchmarking Human and LLM Preferences for Companion-Robot Social Behavior](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15455)
*Yaodan Xu,Boyang Guo,Yuqing Gu,Qingxin Zhang,Yiwen Deng,Meng Liu,Lintian Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: InterSocialBench is a new benchmark of 210 domestic scenarios and 18 behaviors that pairs 100 human participants' judgments with 23,520 LLM responses across 16 personality conditions, showing that models under-diversify and that individual preference cannot be collapsed into a single consensus label.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Companion robots encounter everyday situations where multiple behaviors are feasible but different people prefer different responses. Existing evaluation typically collapses social behavior selection into a single consensus label, ignoring individual differences.

Method: InterSocialBench comprises 210 domestic scenarios and 18 high-level behaviors. Each human annotation includes a preferred action plus explicitly appropriate and inappropriate candidates. A structured construction pipeline covers behavioral alternatives, competing situational cues, and relevant history/future tasks. Evaluation separates preferred-choice agreement from explicit rejection, using scenario-grouped splits for trainable predictors; simple frequency and persona-voting baselines are provided, and seven LLMs are tested under 16 personality conditions.

Result: Model and human behavior distributions differ, and the diversity gap persists after matching response counts: 4.68 distinct choices per scenario for humans vs. 2.06--3.46 for models. Human scenario-level plurality agreement is 51.5%, indicating substantial disagreement rather than a universal prediction ceiling.

Conclusion: InterSocialBench enables evaluating social behavior selection without replacing individual judgments with a single consensus label, highlighting the importance of capturing human diversity in companion-robot behavior prediction.

Abstract: Companion robots face everyday situations in which several feasible behaviors may be appropriate, yet different people prefer different responses. We introduce InterSocialBench, a benchmark of 210 domestic scenarios and 18 high-level behaviors, pairing judgments from 100 human participants with 23,520 responses from seven large language models under 16 personality conditions. Each human annotation preserves a preferred action alongside explicitly appropriate and inappropriate candidates. A structured construction pipeline covers behavioral alternatives, competing situational cues, and relevant history and future tasks. Evaluation distinguishes preferred-choice agreement from explicit rejection, using scenario-grouped splits for trainable predictors. Simple frequency and persona-voting baselines illustrate these objectives. Across the tested prompts, model and human behavior distributions differ, and the diversity gap remains after matching response counts: humans exhibit 4.68 distinct choices per scenario, compared with 2.06--3.46 for the models. Human scenario-level plurality agreement is 51.5%, describing disagreement rather than a universal prediction ceiling. InterSocialBench supports evaluating social behavior selection without replacing individual judgments with a single consensus label.

</details>


### [73] [StereoPatch: Patch-Aligned RGB-Depth Fusion for Spatial Perception in Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15509)
*Yanan Zhou,Zhaoyan Qian,James Zhao,Weiming Zhi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: StereoPatch is a patch-aligned RGB-depth representation that binds registered metric geometry to the RGB patches used for action prediction via asymmetric cross-attention on a shared 2-D patch grid, improving closed-loop success across six real-robot tasks and three simulation suites.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Visuomotor policies predicting actions from RGB alone can map geometrically distinct but visually similar states to similar inputs, since pretrained RGB features lack metric geometry. Naively adding depth forces the policy to learn RGB-depth correspondence from the same limited demonstrations used for control, and treating depth as an independent modality does not resolve control-relevant geometric ambiguity.

Method: StereoPatch builds a patch-aligned RGB-depth representation on a shared 2-D patch grid. Asymmetric cross-attention injects depth information into the corresponding RGB features before action decoding, yielding StereoPatch Tokens that condition general visuomotor policies without altering their underlying learning objectives.

Result: Across six real-robot tasks, StereoPatch achieves higher closed-loop success than appearance-only, geometry-only, raw RGB-D, and late-fusion baselines. Additional experiments across three simulation suites evaluate compatibility across visuomotor policy architectures, spatial generalization, and operating limits.

Conclusion: Resolving control-relevant geometric ambiguity benefits from aligning depth directly with the visual features used for action prediction, rather than supplying depth as an independent modality.

Abstract: Recent advances in robot imitation learning have produced visuomotor policies that predict actions directly from visual observations. Yet visually similar scenes can require different actions as target position, object height, or contact geometry changes. Pretrained RGB features may map these geometrically distinct states to similar policy inputs, while simply adding depth requires the policy to learn RGB-depth correspondence from the same limited demonstrations used to learn control. We introduce StereoPatch, a patch-aligned RGB-depth representation that binds registered metric geometry directly to the RGB patches used for action prediction. On a shared 2-D patch grid, asymmetric cross-attention incorporates depth information into the corresponding RGB features before action decoding. The resulting StereoPatch Tokens provide a geometry-aware visual representation that can condition general visuomotor policies without changing their underlying learning objectives. Across six real-robot tasks, StereoPatch achieves higher closed-loop success than appearance-only, geometry-only, raw RGB-D, and late-fusion baselines. Additional experiments across three simulation suites evaluate compatibility across visuomotor policy architectures, spatial generalization, and operating limits. Results suggest that resolving control-relevant geometric ambiguity benefits from aligning depth directly with the visual features used for action prediction, rather than supplying it as an independent modality. Project page: https://aus.bot/research/stereopatch/.

</details>


### [74] [DIDO: Distilling Interaction-Centric Dynamics into One-Step Denoising for World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15570)
*Jing Lyu,Shuanghao Bai,Runze Xiao,Zhenyu Liao,Wenxing Tan,Zihan Tang,Ruochuan Shi,Cheng Peng,Yuheng Ji,Yihao Wang,Badong Chen,Pengwei Wang,Zhongyuan Wang,Xiaoguang Zhao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DIDO distills a multi-step video-generation world action model into a single denoising step using distribution matching distillation plus interaction-centric representation guidance, achieving high success rates on LIBERO, LIBERO-Plus, and RoboTwin with reduced latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: World Action Models (WAMs) predict future visual dynamics via video generation for robotic manipulation, but iterative denoising adds latency incompatible with closed-loop control. The authors observe that visual content converges at different rates: static background structure forms early, while the gripper, manipulated object, and their interaction dynamics remain blurry after the first step and only emerge through subsequent denoising. Thus, naively truncating a multi-step model to one step keeps scene structure but discards the interaction-centric dynamics most critical for manipulation.

Method: DIDO distills the converged dynamics of a multi-step video model into a single denoising step. It combines distribution matching distillation with interaction-centric representation guidance. It explicitly models the gripper, manipulated object, and their interaction via supervised bounding-box visual reasoning tokens, and aligns the target object's representations across multiple model layers with features from a pretrained DINOv3 encoder.

Result: DIDO achieves an average success rate of 99.0% on LIBERO, 76.6% on LIBERO-Plus, and 92.0% on RoboTwin, and demonstrates effective transfer to long-horizon and generalization tasks in real-world robotic manipulation.

Conclusion: Interaction-centric guidance enables the distilled single-step model to preserve both the relevant entities and their future dynamics while substantially reducing inference latency, validating single-step distillation as a viable approach for low-latency closed-loop robotic manipulation.

Abstract: World Action Models (WAMs) use video generation models to predict future visual dynamics for robotic manipulation, but iterative denoising introduces additional latency for closed-loop control. We empirically find that visual content converges at different rates during denoising. Static background structure forms early, whereas the gripper and manipulated object remain blurry after the first step, with their interaction dynamics emerging only through subsequent denoising. Consequently, naively truncating a multi-step video model to one step preserves scene structure but loses the interaction-centric dynamics most critical for manipulation. To address this issue, we propose DIDO, which distills the converged dynamics of a multi-step video model into a single denoising step. DIDO combines distribution matching distillation with interaction-centric representation guidance. Beyond compressing multi-step generation into one forward pass, DIDO explicitly models the gripper, manipulated object, and their interaction using supervised bounding-box visual reasoning tokens. Additionally, DIDO aligns the target object's representations across multiple model layers with features from a pretrained DINOv3 encoder. This interaction-centric guidance helps the distilled model preserve both the relevant entities and their future dynamics in a single step, while substantially reducing inference latency. DIDO achieves an average success rate of 99.0\% on LIBERO, 76.6\% on LIBERO-Plus, and 92.0\% on RoboTwin, while also demonstrating effective transfer to long-horizon and generalization tasks in real-world robotic manipulation.

</details>


### [75] [An Information-Space Perspective to Scene Graph Sufficiency for Robotic Task Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15587)
*Başak Sakçak,Francesco Verdoja*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper formalizes what makes a scene graph sufficient for planning by modeling planning over scene graphs in an information-spaces framework, defining scene graph transition systems, and introducing derived scene graphs via information mappings that produce quotient transition systems. Sufficiency requires a deterministic quotient and a well-posed task over derived traces, guaranteeing feasibility on the maximal system.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Planning in complex environments needs task specifications grounded in representations capturing objects, relations, and affordances. Scene graphs meet this need, but their size in large environments impedes efficient planning. Prior work explored task-aware pruning and hierarchical abstractions, yet a general, task-centric formalization of what constitutes a sufficient scene graph for planning remained open.

Method: The paper formalizes planning over scene graphs within an information-spaces framework, defining scene graph transition systems and action semantics for navigation and manipulation. It introduces derived scene graphs via information mappings that merge and prune nodes, inducing quotient transition systems augmented with motion primitives to capture higher-level actions over merged graph nodes. Sufficiency is characterized by two conditions: the information mapping yields a deterministic quotient, and the task is well-posed over derived traces.

Result: Sufficiency is characterized by (i) a deterministic quotient induced by the information mapping and (ii) a task well-posed over derived traces, ensuring plans found on the derived model are feasible on the maximal system. The framework is illustrated on a task in an example environment, showing both sufficient and insufficient reduced scene graphs.

Conclusion: The work provides a general, task-centric formalization of scene graph sufficiency for planning, linking reduced/derived scene graphs to feasible plans on the full maximal system and demonstrating the conditions via illustrative examples.

Abstract: Planning in complex environments requires task specifications grounded in representations that capture objects, relations, and affordances; scene graphs meet this need, but their size in large environments hinders efficient planning. While task-aware pruning and hierarchical abstractions have been explored, a general, task-centric formalization of what constitutes a sufficient scene graph for planning remains open. This paper provides such a formalization by modeling planning over scene graphs within an information-spaces framework through the definition of scene graph transition systems and relevant action semantics for navigation and manipulation. We then introduce derived scene graphs via information mappings that merge and prune nodes and induce quotient transition systems augmented with motion primitives to capture higher-level actions over merged graph nodes. Sufficiency is characterized by two conditions: (i) the information mapping yields a deterministic quotient, and (ii) the task is well-posed over derived traces, ensuring plans found on the derived model are feasible on the maximal system. We illustrate the framework using a task over an example environment, showing both sufficient and insufficient reduced scene graphs.

</details>


### [76] [Flow-Matched Motion Priors: Online Optimal-Transport Rewards for Imitation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15631)
*Yilin Zou,Chenghua Liu,Chenglong Wu,Fanghua Jiang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: FMP replaces AMP's discriminator with an entropic-OT coupling plus a flow-matching-trained neural potential, yielding a single scalar motion-prior reward that generalizes better and produces stable G1 walking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adversarial Motion Priors reward learning via a discriminator becomes uninformative when policy and expert supports are far apart; the naive optimal-transport (OT) alternative averages matched expert successors into a barycentric target, and averaging across gait phases weakens the target's joint motion.

Method: Train an online scalar reward from paths connecting current rollout histories to an expert motion bank. Entropic OT supplies the coupling between rollout and expert trajectories. Before each policy update, a neural potential is trained with flow matching along rollout-to-expert paths, combined with endpoint-gradient supervision and relative-value calibration. The actor observes only physical observations and receives a scalar reward, as in AMP.

Result: Controlled reward-model experiments show substantially better generalization beyond the fitting rollout than value-only or endpoint-only fitting. On Unitree G1, matched 50-million-transition experiments yield stable forward walking at 0.727 m/s from demonstration resets and 0.338 m/s from a fixed default pose; in the fixed-pose condition FMP incurs 129 falls versus 243 for the endpoint-only control. Against a static score-gradient teacher, dynamic FM reduces score-increment error at interpolation fractions 0.25 and 0.50 while using 29% less offline fitting time.

Conclusion: Reformulating motion-prior rewards as flow-matched scalar potentials coupled by entropic OT avoids the pitfalls of adversarial and barycentric objectives, improving out-of-rollout generalization and locomotion stability at lower fitting cost.

Abstract: Learning a motion prior requires a reward that guides a policy from its current behavior toward demonstrated motion. Adversarial Motion Priors (AMP) provide such a reward with a discriminator. However, adversarial objectives can become uninformative when policy and expert supports are far apart. A naive use of optimal transport (OT) averages matched expert successors into a barycentric target. Averaging across gait phases can weaken the target's joint motion. We introduce Flow-Matched Motion Priors (FMP), an online scalar reward learned from paths connecting current rollout histories to an expert motion bank. Entropic OT supplies the coupling. Before each policy update, we train a neural potential with flow matching (FM) along the rollout-to-expert paths, endpoint-gradient supervision, and relative-value calibration. The actor receives only physical observations and the reward remains a scalar, as in AMP. Controlled reward-model experiments show substantially better generalization beyond the fitting rollout than value-only or endpoint-only fitting. On Unitree G1, matched 50-million-transition experiments compare FMP with AMP, a barycentric OT reward, and nested ablations under demonstration and fixed-pose initialization. FMP produces stable forward walking at 0.727 m/s from demonstration resets and 0.338 m/s from a fixed default pose. In the fixed-pose condition, it incurs 129 falls versus 243 for the endpoint-only control. Against a static score-gradient teacher, dynamic FM reduces score-increment error at interpolation fractions 0.25 and 0.50 while using 29% less offline fitting time.

</details>


### [77] [Tracking the Ground: Online Lidar Identification of Robot-Induced Soil Deformation in Agricultural Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15667)
*Tom Montagnon,Johann Laconte,Benoit Thuilot,Wonjae Cho,Roland Lenain*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a framework to quantify and online-estimate traffic-induced soil deformation from lidar observations, using a reduced-order parametric model that yields an observable, physically interpretable soil-state representation, as a basis for soil-aware robotic field operations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agriculture faces efficiency and sustainability challenges, and preserving soil health is critical because vehicle-soil interactions degrade soil structure and cause unwanted surface deformation. Although soil-aware robotics requires explicitly accounting for how vehicle traffic deforms the ground, soil state is typically not treated as a variable, leaving a gap in quantifying and monitoring traffic-induced deformation.

Method: A framework is proposed to quantify traffic-induced soil deformation and estimate its evolution online from lidar observations. It relies on a reduced-order parametric model that represents soil behavior through physically interpretable parameters, yielding a continuously updated and observable representation of soil state.

Result: Experiments conducted under different soil conditions demonstrate the approach's ability to capture the deformation induced by the robot.

Conclusion: By making soil response measurable and interpretable during operation, the framework establishes a basis for soil-aware robotic operation, where the estimated state can be exploited to adapt robotic behaviors to reduce soil degradation.

Abstract: Agriculture faces many challenges, and robotic systems can play an important role in addressing them by improving the efficiency and sustainability of field operations. Among these challenges, preserving soil health is a critical concern, as vehicle-soil interactions can degrade the soil structure and produce unwanted surface deformation. A key step toward soil-aware robotics is to explicitly account for how vehicle traffic deforms the ground, yet soil state is typically not treated as a variable. We address this gap by proposing a framework to quantify traffic-induced soil deformation and estimate its evolution online from lidar observations. The method relies on a reduced-order parametric model that represents the soil behavior via physically interpretable parameters, yielding a continuously updated and observable representation of soil state. Experiments conducted in different soil conditions demonstrate the ability of the approach to capture deformation induced by the robot. By making soil response measurable and interpretable during operation, the proposed framework establishes a basis for soil-aware robotic operation, in which the estimated state can be exploited to adapt robotic behaviors in order to reduce soil degradation.

</details>


### [78] [Volumetric Harmonic Field Navigation for Quadrotors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15680)
*Shuxiu Jia,Amartya Mukherjee,Yating Yuan,Jun Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper couples a precomputed volumetric harmonic potential field with a constrained predictive planner for quadrotor navigation, querying the field at predicted positions rather than extracting a reference path, and demonstrates it physically on a Crazyflie.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quadrotor navigation in cluttered 3-D environments requires global guidance while local motion must respect collision and motion limits. Harmonic potentials offer dense global guidance via a boundary value problem, but coupling a volumetric harmonic field to constrained physical quadrotor motion has remained an open experimental problem.

Method: A precomputed volumetric harmonic field is coupled with a constrained predictive planner. Instead of extracting a global reference path, the planner queries the harmonic field directly at predicted positions to generate local constrained motion.

Result: In structured 3-D tests, harmonic guidance achieves larger minimum clearance and lower RMS jerk than matched Dijkstra guidance at the cost of longer paths; the same pattern holds when both methods use the same passage. Long maze tests cover routes far beyond one prediction horizon, and Crazyflie trials validate physical execution.

Conclusion: This is, to the authors' knowledge, the first physical quadrotor demonstration of volumetric harmonic field navigation, showing that globally constructed harmonic guidance can directly support local constrained motion generation on a physical quadrotor.

Abstract: Quadrotor navigation in cluttered 3-D environments requires global guidance while local motion remains subject to collision and motion limits. Harmonic potentials provide dense guidance from a global boundary value problem, but coupling a volumetric harmonic field to constrained physical quadrotor motion remains an open experimental problem. We couple a precomputed volumetric harmonic field with a constrained predictive planner that queries the field at predicted positions instead of extracting a global reference path. In Structured 3-D tests, harmonic guidance yields larger minimum clearance and lower RMS jerk than matched Dijkstra guidance, at the cost of longer paths; the same pattern remains when both methods use the same passage. Long maze tests span routes far beyond one prediction horizon, and Crazyflie trials validate physical execution. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first physical quadrotor demonstration of volumetric harmonic field navigation. The results show that globally constructed harmonic guidance can directly support local constrained motion generation on a physical quadrotor.

</details>


### [79] [Continuous Manifold-Decomposed Impedance Retargeting for Contact-Rich Imitation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15716)
*Jiahao Liu,Kento Kawaharazuka,Tasuku Makabe,Kei Okada*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: CMDIR extends MDIR to convert fixed-impedance demonstrations into continuous variable-impedance controllers, introducing a continuous Task-Manifold Impedance Representation (TMIR) usable as structured imitation-learning supervision; a Quality-to-Fast compiler speeds up controller solving, with real-contact trials showing improved retention and reduced force/pose deviations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fixed-impedance demonstrations limit retargeting and imitation learning; the work aims to obtain continuous variable-impedance controllers that also provide structured supervision for imitation learning.

Method: CMDIR builds on Manifold-Decomposed Impedance Retargeting (MDIR). Continuous TMIR pairs an evolving task frame with controller instructions. Demo-relative Compromise dynamics retain moving-basis transport and control/physical metric mismatch, yielding displacement, reaction-impulse, and perturbation-sensitivity criteria. Quality-to-Fast automatically compiles a solver structure from development paths within a predefined finite space, re-instantiates it per demonstration, and certifies candidates via multi-resolution evaluation. FastMPO is the resulting fast solver.

Result: Over 225 retargeted-controller trials across three real contact tasks, full CMDIR improves mean task-proxy retention and reduces mean pose deviation, force fluctuation, and peak force versus discrete MDIR. FastMPO achieves 5.8–9.4x speedup over C-MPO with comparable closed-loop outcomes. Downstream experiments show the complete TMIR supervision interface is learnable; lower force fluctuation and peak force appear in successful executions, though completion reliability remains uneven across tasks and environments.

Conclusion: CMDIR successfully generalizes MDIR to continuous variable-impedance retargeting and offers a learnable TMIR supervision interface; the Quality-to-Fast/FastMPO solver yields large speedups with comparable outcomes, while completion reliability still varies across tasks and environments.

Abstract: CMDIR extends Manifold-Decomposed Impedance Retargeting (MDIR) to transform fixed-impedance demonstrations into continuous variable-impedance controllers, which can also serve as structured supervision for imitation learning. Continuous Task-Manifold Impedance Representation (TMIR) pairs an evolving task frame with controller instructions. Demo-relative Compromise dynamics retain moving-basis transport and control/physical metric mismatch, yielding displacement, reaction-impulse, and perturbation-sensitivity criteria. Quality-to-Fast automatically compiles a solver structure from development paths within a predefined finite space, re-instantiates that structure for each demonstration, and certifies the resulting candidate by multi-resolution evaluation. Across 225 retargeted-controller trials in three real contact tasks, full CMDIR improves mean task-proxy retention and reduces mean pose deviation, force fluctuation, and peak force relative to discrete MDIR. FastMPO achieves a $5.8$--$9.4\times$ speedup over C-MPO with comparable closed-loop outcomes. Downstream experiments demonstrate learnability of the complete TMIR supervision interface; lower force fluctuation and peak force are observed among successful executions, while completion reliability remains uneven across tasks and environments.

</details>


### [80] [Bench2Dex: Benchmarking Visuo-Tactile Bimanual Dexterous Manipulation Across Dexterous Hands](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15726)
*Zhenjie Yang,Yideng Zhang,Dongjie Zhang,Chenyu Jiang,Xianshuai Liu,Yufeng Li,Zuhao Ge,Xingyu Jiao,Zheng Zhang,Kaiyu He,He Wang,Yuwen Zhong,Yi Deng,Muyun Jiang,Xianliang Huang,Haisheng Su,Donghang Zhang,Jian Zhang,Xue Yang,Hongyang Li,Zuxuan Wu,Yu-Gang Jiang,Xiaosong Jia,Junchi Yan*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Bench2Dex is a simulation benchmark for visuo-tactile bimanual manipulation across 12 dexterous hands, featuring a shared tactile interface, 26 tasks, ~1.3K teleoperated demonstrations, and an invariance/equivariance robustness evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tactile sensing captures contact information hard to infer from vision, but dexterous-hand tactile hardware lacks a common design. Hands differ in finger structure, contact surfaces, and sensor layouts, and simulated tactile signals diverge from real sensor measurements. This heterogeneity makes studying visuo-tactile manipulation across diverse hands in a consistent experimental setting difficult.

Method: Adapt existing robot models with a shared simulated tactile interface that converts local contact geometry into image-like tactile observations, giving a consistent observation format across hand morphologies without replicating any specific physical sensor. Include 26 bimanual tasks (tool use, articulated-object interaction, multi-stage manipulation) with ~1.3K human-teleoperated demonstrations, synchronized visual, tactile, proprioceptive, action, and object-state observations, and executable task metrics. Robustness is organized into invariance and equivariance axes over seven perturbation types.

Result: Evaluated ACT, Diffusion Policy, pi0.5, and GR00T N1.5 on Bench2Dex, reporting performance and failure modes.

Conclusion: Bench2Dex provides a shared platform for studying visuo-tactile learning across dexterous hands. It does not claim simulated tactile observations replace real tactile sensing, but offers a common setting for algorithm development while tactile hardware and simulation models evolve.

Abstract: Tactile sensing provides contact information that can be difficult to infer from vision alone, but tactile hardware for dexterous hands has not converged to a common design. Dexterous hands differ in finger structure, contact surfaces, and sensor layouts, while simulated tactile signals still differ from measurements produced by physical sensors. These factors make it difficult to study visuo-tactile manipulation across diverse dexterous hands within a consistent experimental setting. We present Bench2Dex, a simulation benchmark for visuo-tactile bimanual manipulation across 12 dexterous hands. We adapt existing robot models with a shared simulated tactile interface that converts local contact geometry into image-like tactile observations. The interface provides a consistent observation format across different hand morphologies without attempting to reproduce the output of a specific physical tactile sensor. Bench2Dex includes 26 bimanual manipulation tasks that involve tool use, articulated-object interaction, and multi-stage manipulation, together with about 1.3K human-teleoperated demonstrations. The benchmark provides synchronized visual, tactile, proprioceptive, action, and object-state observations, together with executable task metrics. For robustness, we group seven perturbation types into invariance axis, where the correct action does not change, and equivariance axis, where the correct action changes together with the perturbation. We evaluate ACT, Diffusion Policy, pi0.5, and GR00T N1.5 on Bench2Dex and report their performance and failure modes. Bench2Dex is meant as a platform for studying visuo-tactile learning across dexterous hands. It does not assume that simulated tactile observations can replace real tactile sensing; it offers a shared setting for algorithm development while tactile hardware and simulation models are still evolving.

</details>


### [81] [JEPLO: Joint-Embedding Predictive Learning for LiDAR-Based Legged Locomotion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15770)
*Qihao Yuan,Yixuan Qiu,Ziyu Cao,Ming Cao,Kailai Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: JEPLO is a single-stage, map-free learning framework for LiDAR-based perceptive legged locomotion, using a proprio-exteroceptive JEPA world model and a concurrent teacher-student pipeline to achieve robust sim-to-real transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LiDAR is under-explored relative to RGB-D sensing for perceptive legged locomotion, and existing LiDAR-based approaches typically depend on explicit mapping, which is computationally heavy and brittle under degraded perception.

Method: Introduces a proprio-exteroceptive JEPA (PE-JEPA) world model that learns predictive egocentric terrain representations from onboard observations including raw LiDAR scans, plus a concurrent JEPA-teacher-student (CJTS) pipeline that trains a locomotion policy informed by JEPA latent representations in simulation via deep reinforcement learning with a simple reward formulation.

Result: Achieves successful sim-to-real transfer enabling omnidirectional traversal of diverse terrains such as long staircases and high boxes with lightweight onboard computation; demonstrates greater robustness than existing perceptive locomotion frameworks, especially under occlusion, sparsity, and noise.

Conclusion: JEPLO provides a mapping-free, computationally efficient route to robust LiDAR-based perceptive legged locomotion, with analysis validating its ability to retain task-relevant information under challenging perception conditions; implementation, datasets, and hardware designs are open-sourced.

Abstract: Light detection and ranging (LiDAR) remains less explored than RGB-D sensing for perceptive legged locomotion, and existing LiDAR-based approaches often rely on explicit mapping. We present JEPLO (Joint-Embedding Predictive learning for legged LOcomotion), a single-stage learning framework for mapping-free, LiDAR-based perceptive locomotion for legged robots. We introduce a proprio-exteroceptive JEPA (PE-JEPA) world model to learn predictive egocentric terrain representations from onboard observations, including raw LiDAR scans. A concurrent JEPA-teacher-student (CJTS) pipeline is further proposed to train a locomotion policy informed by JEPA latent representations in simulation using deep reinforcement learning with a simple reward formulation. The framework achieves successful sim-to-real transfer, enabling omnidirectional traversal of diverse terrains, including long staircases and high boxes, with lightweight onboard computation. Evaluations demonstrate greater robustness than existing perceptive locomotion frameworks, particularly under degraded perception caused by occlusion, sparsity and noise. Further analysis validates JEPLO's ability to retain task-relevant information under these challenging conditions. We open-source our implementation, experimental datasets, and hardware setup designs https://github.com/ASIG-X/JEPLO.

</details>


### [82] [Uncertainty-Guided Sparse Refinement for Action Chunking Transformer Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15840)
*Chenyang Wang,Yuntian Wang,Xiaoxiong Yang,Dingde Jiang,Siao Liu,Yang Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: UGR is a sparse refinement framework for chunk-based visuomotor policies that estimates per-step temporal uncertainty from coarse hidden states and applies residual correction only to the most uncertain timesteps via a binary mask, achieving state-of-the-art results on RoboTwin tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon robot manipulation with chunk-based visuomotor policies is limited because failures are dominated by prediction errors at a few critical timesteps, not uniform errors across the whole action chunk. This makes uniform refinement inefficient and insufficiently targeted.

Method: UGR follows a coarse-to-refine design: it (1) predicts a full action chunk, (2) estimates per-step temporal uncertainty from the coarse hidden states, and (3) applies residual correction only to the most uncertain timesteps selected by a binary mask. The uncertainty branch is decoupled from the coarse action predictor to cleanly attribute refinement gains to uncertainty-guided correction rather than extra predictor capacity.

Result: On five dual-arm manipulation tasks from the RoboTwin benchmark, UGR achieves the best success rate on four tasks, improves over the ACT baseline by up to 13% absolute, and outperforms both full-chunk and position-agnostic block refinement in ablations.

Conclusion: Sparse, uncertainty-guided refinement is an effective and targeted remedy for the critical-timestep failure bottleneck in chunk-based visuomotor policies, outperforming uniform or position-agnostic refinement strategies.

Abstract: Learning chunk-based visuomotor policies for long-horizon robot manipulation remains challenging. Recent action-chunking methods have shown promising performance by predicting temporally extended action sequences. However, their failures are often dominated by prediction errors at a small number of critical timesteps rather than uniformly poor predictions across the entire action chunk, making uniform refinement inefficient and insufficiently targeted. To address this bottleneck, we propose Uncertainty-Guided Refinement (UGR), a sparse refinement framework for chunk-based visuomotor policies. Specifically, UGR follows a coarse-to-refine design: it first predicts a full action chunk, estimates per-step temporal uncertainty from the coarse hidden states, and applies residual correction only to the most uncertain timesteps selected by a binary mask. The uncertainty branch is decoupled from the coarse action predictor, enabling clean attribution of the refinement gains to uncertainty-guided correction rather than additional predictor capacity. Extensive experiments on five dual-arm manipulation tasks from the RoboTwin benchmark show that UGR achieves the best success rate on four tasks, improves over the ACT baseline by up to 13% absolute, and outperforms both full-chunk and position-agnostic block refinement in ablation studies.

</details>


### [83] [DuctAM: A Duct-Assisted Quadrotor-Based Aerial Manipulator Enabling High-Force Push-and-Pull Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15861)
*Yi Wang,Rui Jin,Xinhang Xu,Haotian Jin,Ruiyang Liu,Yizhuo Yang,Lihua Xie*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DuctAM is a compact aerial manipulation platform adding two ducted fans along the quadrotor's interaction axis, with an attitude-force decoupled controller that enables sustained horizontal push-and-pull forces while keeping stable flight.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Uncrewed Aerial Manipulators (UAMs) extend UAVs from perception to physical interaction, but push-and-pull operations require sustained horizontal forces while maintaining stable flight—a capability conventional quadrotors lack, as horizontal force typically demands large attitude tilts.

Method: The authors integrate two ducted fans along the quadrotor's interaction axis into a compact platform and design an attitude-force decoupled control scheme that produces controllable horizontal forces without large attitude changes. Validation includes figure-eight trajectory tracking in both quad and duct modes, force-measurement experiments, and representative push-and-pull tasks (cart pushing, door closing, drawer opening).

Result: Figure-eight trajectory tracking confirms stable flight and accurate motion control in both quad and duct modes. Force-measurement experiments quantify the decoupled longitudinal force capability, and push-and-pull tasks verify practical effectiveness. DuctAM achieves significantly improved horizontal interaction force capability versus conventional UAVs while maintaining stable flight.

Conclusion: DuctAM demonstrates that ducted-fan augmentation combined with attitude-force decoupling control is an effective approach for compact aerial manipulation, enabling practical sustained push-and-pull interactions without sacrificing flight stability.

Abstract: Uncrewed Aerial Manipulators (UAMs) extend the capabilities of Uncrewed Aerial Vehicles (UAVs) from perception to physical interaction. Among various aerial interactions, push-and-pull operations are fundamental manipulation primitives that require sustained horizontal forces while maintaining stable flight. In this paper, we propose DuctAM, a compact aerial manipulation platform that enhances horizontal force capability for push-and-pull interactions using two ducted fans integrated along the quadrotor interaction axis. An attitude-force decoupled control scheme enables controllable horizontal forces without requiring large attitude changes. Extensive real-world experiments are conducted to validate the DuctAM. Figure-eight trajectory tracking experiments demonstrate stable flight and accurate motion control in both quad and duct modes. Force-measurement experiments quantify the decoupled longitudinal force capability of DuctAM. Finally, representative push-and-pull interaction tasks, including cart pushing, door closing, and drawer opening, verify the practical effectiveness of DuctAM. The results show that DuctAM achieves significantly improved horizontal interaction force capability while maintaining stable flight compared with conventional UAVs.

</details>


### [84] [WLA$^3$: World Latent Action Modeling for Semantics, Dynamics, and Kinematics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15870)
*Peidong Liu,Zhiyuan Xiang,Mingyang Li,Wenhao Li,Jiale Zhang,Jiahao Sun,Jiawei Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: WLA$^3$ is a generalist policy framework built on World Latent Action representations learned from multimodal world-state transitions; it unifies semantics, dynamics, and kinematics and improves real-robot success and scalability over prior baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scaling generalist policy models with heterogeneous data is bottlenecked by a lack of unified, low-noise action supervision. Human egocentric videos are abundant but rarely carry high-quality hand-action labels, so a common, cross-source supervision signal is needed.

Method: A World Latent Action Model (WLAM) encodes how multimodal world states change over a local interval, mapping synchronized camera views and embodiment-state changes into a compact local latent action plus a richer transition feature. Robustness is enforced via reconstruction from partial modalities and consistency across overlapping windows. WLA$^3$ reuses these representations: latent actions for action-sensitive physical-dynamics modeling, segment-level features to supervise a VLM through a Semantic Latent Aggregate (SLA), and an action expert that jointly predicts latent actions and embodiment-specific robot controls. Human videos supply scalable transition supervision; robot trajectories ground the shared representation in executable controls.

Result: On LARYBench, the 32D latent action achieves 67.89% average classification accuracy. WLA$^3$ reaches 81.9% average success on six real-robot tasks versus 66.2% for $π_{0.5}$. Performance improves as mid-training data scales, and human videos enable human-to-robot transfer.

Conclusion: World-transition-based latent actions provide unified, low-noise supervision that lets generalist policies scale across heterogeneous and unlabeled human video data, yielding stronger real-robot performance and effective human-to-robot transfer.

Abstract: Scaling generalist policy models with heterogeneous data is limited by the lack of unified, low-noise action supervision. Human egocentric videos are abundant, but only a small fraction comes with high-quality hand-action labels. Observed world transitions offer a common source of action-related supervision across data sources. We introduce WLA$^3$ (World Latent Action Modeling for Semantics, Dynamics, and Kinematics), a unified generalist policy model framework built around representations learned by a World Latent Action Model (WLAM). WLAM first learns how multimodal world states change over a local interval, encoding synchronized camera views and available embodiment-state changes into a compact local latent action and a richer transition feature. Reconstruction from partial modalities and consistency across overlapping windows encourage robust transition representations. WLA$^3$ reuses them across semantics, dynamics, and kinematics: local latent actions support action-sensitive physical-dynamics modeling, segment-level features directly supervise the VLM through a Semantic Latent Aggregate (SLA), and an action expert jointly predicts latent actions together with embodiment-specific robot controls. Human videos provide scalable transition supervision, while robot trajectories ground the shared representation in executable native controls. On LARYBench, the final 32D latent action reaches 67.89\% average classification accuracy. WLA$^3$ achieves 81.9% average success across six real-robot tasks versus 66.2% for $π_{0.5}$. Performance improves as generalist policy model mid-training data scales, and human videos support human-to-robot transfer. Project page can be found at https://wla-3.github.io/.

</details>


### [85] [Goal-Oriented Communications for Physical AI: Design and Testbed](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15895)
*Shutong Chen,Wenkai Zhang,Adnan Aijaz,Miao Guo,Yansha Deng*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The work builds an end-to-end goal-oriented communication (GoC) testbed for Physical AI, implementing three semantic-representation-based GoC frameworks (3D bounding boxes, 2D scene graphs, 3D scene graphs) on a robot arm + 5G + edge setup, showing up to 52.6% lower task completion time and 45% higher task success probability versus raw-image transmission.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Physical AI needs frequently updated, latency-sensitive video streams to perceive and interact with the physical world, imposing strict latency requirements and high data volumes that existing 5G networks cannot support. GoC is a promising solution by transmitting only task-relevant semantic representations, but existing GoC frameworks were mainly evaluated in simulation and never validated in a practical Physical AI deployment.

Method: The authors develop an end-to-end GoC testbed for Physical AI, connecting a PiPER robot arm with an RGB-D camera and a 5G modem to an NVIDIA Jetson AGX Orin edge server over a 5G OpenAirInterface network. They propose and implement three GoC frameworks transmitting 3D bounding boxes, 2D scene graphs, and 3D scene graphs as semantic representations. These share common functional modules for closed-loop Physical AI: semantic extraction, full-stack 5G transmission, language model inference, digital twin validation, and robotic control.

Result: Extensive testbed experiments show the GoC frameworks reduce task completion time by up to 52.6% and improve task success probability by up to 45% compared with the traditional framework that periodically transmits raw image data.

Conclusion: The results validate the practical effectiveness of the proposed GoC framework and pave the way for efficient and reliable Physical AI applications over future 6G networks.

Abstract: Physical AI relies on frequently-updated, latency-sensitive video stream to perceive, reason, and interact with the physical world, resulting in strict latency requirements with much higher data volumes that existing 5G networks cannot support. Goal-oriented communication (GoC) offers as a promising approach to solve this challenge by transmitting only task-relevant semantic representations. However, existing GoC frameworks were mainly evaluated in the simulations while their effectiveness has never been validated in a practical deployment of physical AI application. In this work, we develop an end-to-end GoC testbed for Physical AI, which connects a PiPER robot arm equipped with an RGB-D camera and a 5G modem to an NVIDIA Jetson AGX Orin edge server through a 5G OpenAirInterface network. We propose and implement three GoC frameworks that transmit 3D bounding boxes, 2D scene graphs, and 3D scene graphs, as three types of semantic representations, respectively. They share the common functional modules designed for closed-loop Physical AI applications, including semantic extraction, full stack 5G transmission, language model inference, digital twin validation, and robotic control. Extensive experiments on our testbed show that our GoC frameworks reduce the task completion time by up to 52.6% and improve task success probability by up to 45%, compared to the traditional framework that periodically transmits the raw image data. These results validate the practical effectiveness of our GoC framework and pave the way for efficient and reliable Physical AI applications over future 6G networks. Project website: https://sites.google.com/view/goc-physical-ai-testbed.

</details>


### [86] [SlipSense: Multimodal Tactile Learning for Low-Latency and Generalized Slip Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15910)
*Tong Jian,Aditya Thurvas Senthil Kumar,Xinyi Li,Ziling Chen,Tianyu Dai,Ali Sengul,Matteo Grimaldi,Wenjie Lu,Saleh Nabi,Tao Yu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SlipSense, a multimodal tactile slip-detection framework using a piezoresistive array (32×32, 240 Hz) and a 3-axis MEMS accelerometer (8 kHz), fuses modalities via cross-modal attention with causal temporal prediction, achieving 96.7% Macro F1, <1.6% FPR, 76% of slips detected within 23.1 ms, and zero-shot cross-platform transfer from UMI to a Tesollo hand.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Slip detection is essential for dexterous manipulation, but existing systems typically lack precise characterization of detection latency and cross-platform generalization, limiting reliable deployment.

Method: Builds on TacV5, a compact sensor pairing a 32×32 piezoresistive array at 240 Hz (spatial pressure distribution) with a 3-axis MEMS accelerometer at 8 kHz (friction-induced vibration). The framework applies modality-specific encoding, intra-sensor fusion, and cross-modal attention with causal temporal prediction at 240 Hz, trained on a dataset of 1.4 million frames across 37 objects.

Result: The two modalities prove complementary. SlipSense reaches 96.7% Macro F1 with false-positive rate below 1.6%, detecting 76% of slip events within 23.1 ms. Trained solely on UMI data, it generalizes zero-shot to a Tesollo dexterous hand, transferring across unseen objects, distinct sensor units, and robotic platforms without retraining.

Conclusion: Multimodal fusion of tactile pressure and vibration enables low-latency, low-false-positive slip detection with strong zero-shot cross-platform generalization, supporting practical dexterous manipulation.

Abstract: Slip detection is fundamental to dexterous manipulation, yet existing systems often lack precise characterization of detection latency and cross-platform generalization. We present SlipSense, a multimodal tactile slip-detection framework built on TacV5, a compact sensor integrating a $32 \times 32$ piezoresistive array operating at 240 Hz and a 3-axis MEMS accelerometer operating at 8 kHz. The piezoresistive array captures spatial pressure distributions, while the accelerometer captures friction-induced vibrations, providing complementary slip cues. The framework performs modality-specific encoding, intra-sensor fusion, and cross-modal attention with causal temporal prediction at 240 Hz. Experiments on a dataset of 1.4 million frames spanning 37 objects demonstrate the complementarity of the two modalities. SlipSense achieves 96.7% Macro F1 with a false-positive rate below 1.6%, detecting 76% of slip events within 23.1 ms. When trained solely on UMI data, SlipSense generalizes zero-shot to a Tesollo dexterous hand, transferring across unseen objects, distinct sensor units, and robotic platforms without retraining.

</details>


### [87] [Touch2Trace: Tactile-Driven Imitation Learning for Dexterous Cable Tracing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15921)
*Matteo Grimaldi,David Klee,Ziling Chen,Tong Jian,Wonju Lee,Wenjie Lu,Tao Yu,Saleh Nabi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Touch2Trace is a tactile-driven imitation-learning system for dexterous cable tracing, providing the first systematic real-world study of how encoder pretraining, control rate, temporal context, and spatial resolution affect policy performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dexterous manipulation of deformable objects requires continuous fingertip-level regulation of pressure, friction, and incipient slip. Cable tracing—feeding a cable through the hand via repeated pinch-and-curl motions of thumb and index finger—is among the most challenging such tasks, and tactile feedback is critical yet underexplored.

Method: Touch2Trace combines a tactile encoder pretrained via self-supervised learning on a custom 32 x 32 piezoresistive sensor (TacV5) with a lightweight transformer policy trained by behavior cloning on teleoperated demonstrations, deployed at 60 Hz on a Tesollo DG-5F hand. The study systematically characterizes encoder pretraining, control rate, temporal context, and spatial resolution.

Result: Tactile feedback without vision or explicit cable-state estimation substantially improves tracing over a proprioception-only baseline: mean distance improves from 0.2 cm to 20.1 cm and success rate from 0% to 93%, with zero-shot transfer to unseen cables and routing conditions.

Conclusion: The results quantify the influence of key parameters in tactile-driven systems, offering a practical learning recipe for reliable dexterous deformable object manipulation.

Abstract: Dexterous manipulation of deformable objects demands continuous fingertip-level regulation of pressure, friction, and incipient slip. We study one of the most challenging cases: dexterous cable tracing, feeding a cable through the hand with repeated pinch-and-curl motions of the thumb and index finger. We introduce Touch2Trace, a tactile-driven imitation-learning system for this task, and provide, to our knowledge, the first systematic real-world characterization of how encoder pretraining, control rate, temporal context, and spatial resolution each shape policy performance. The winning learning recipe combines a tactile encoder pretrained for a custom 32 x 32 piezoresistive sensor (TacV5) via self-supervised learning with a lightweight transformer policy trained on teleoperated demonstrations via behavior cloning, deployed at 60 Hz on a Tesollo DG-5F hand. Tactile feedback without vision or explicit cable-state estimation significantly improves tracing performance versus a proprioception-only baseline: from 0.2 cm to 20.1 cm mean distance and 0% to 93% success rate, with zero-shot transfer to unseen cables and routing conditions. The results quantify the influence of key parameters in tactile-driven systems for reliable dexterous deformable object manipulation.

</details>


### [88] [Beyond Single-Axis Testing: Paired Evaluation of Compound Robustness in Vision-Language-Action Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15940)
*Hiroki Sawada,Shunichi Kasahara*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Compound robustness of vision-language-action policies cannot be inferred from aggregate single-axis success rates, because emergent failures and compensated successes largely cancel in aggregate; matched per-instance evaluation is required.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLA policies are usually evaluated one perturbation at a time, but real deployment involves multiple simultaneous shifts. It is unclear whether compound robustness composes from single-axis measurements.

Method: Introduce LIBERO-CTRL, a six-axis benchmark pairing each initial state across single-axis conditions with a matched simultaneous condition, enabling identification of emergent failures (all single-axis succeed, simultaneous fails) and compensated successes (some single-axis fail, simultaneous succeeds).

Result: Opposing transitions largely cancel in aggregate: even when the difference between transition rates is statistically indistinguishable from zero, up to 29.0% of matched initial states change outcome; across six policies and three severity levels, changes reach 34.5% in the most affected condition. Relative prevalence varies by policy and severity, while transition rates remain similar under independent re-evaluation of stochastic policies.

Conclusion: Compound robustness cannot be characterized from aggregate single-axis success rates alone; matched per-instance evaluation is needed to reveal how joint perturbations alter behavior.

Abstract: Vision-language-action policies are typically evaluated one perturbation at a time, providing a useful diagnosis of their sensitivity to individual distribution shifts. Real-world deployment, however, may involve several shifts simultaneously, and it remains unclear how these individual robustness measurements compose. We ask whether compound robustness can be inferred from single-axis evaluations. We introduce LIBERO-CTRL, a six-axis benchmark that pairs each initial state across single-axis conditions and a matched simultaneous condition. This design reveals two opposing outcome changes that aggregate success rates cannot distinguish: emergent failures, where all single-axis rollouts succeed but the simultaneous rollout fails, and compensated successes, where at least one single-axis rollout fails but the simultaneous rollout succeeds. Because one transition decreases compound success while the other increases it, they can cancel, making aggregate compound performance appear consistent with single-axis measurements even when individual outcomes differ substantially. These opposing transitions can largely cancel in aggregate: even when the difference between the two transition rates is not statistically distinguishable from zero, as many as 29.0% of matched initial states still change outcome. Across six policies and three severity levels, such outcome changes reach 34.5% in the most affected condition. The relative prevalence of the two transitions varies across policies and severities, while the transition rates remain similar under independent re-evaluation of stochastic policies. Compound robustness therefore cannot be characterized from aggregate single-axis success rates alone; matched per-instance evaluation is needed to reveal how joint perturbations alter behavior.

</details>


### [89] [MessyMem: Learning-from-Doing Memory for Mobile Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15976)
*Anuva Banwasi,William Muckelroy,Priya Sundaresan,Linfeng Zhao,Jeannette Bohg,Cherie Ho*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MessyMem is a persistent memory system for mobile manipulators that builds a spatially grounded 3D scene graph augmented with interaction-derived knowledge and visual observations, enabling robots to reuse experience across tasks and achieving 80.0% task progress in a 25-task simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mobile manipulators operating across many rooms and repeated visits should improve with experience, yet current systems treat each task as new. Compact scene representations omit interaction-derived knowledge, raw video histories are hard to query, and VLM planners reason only at inference time without persistently updating robot knowledge.

Method: MessyMem maintains a spatially grounded 3D scene graph of objects and locations, augments it with properties and outcomes learned through interaction, and links visual observations for fine-grained recall, enabling persistent knowledge accumulation and reuse across future tasks.

Result: In a continuous 25-task simulation spanning over 3 hours, MessyMem achieved 80.0% task progress, outperforming the strongest ablation by 14.8 percentage points and the strongest external baseline by 28.9 points, while retrieving task-relevant evidence from thousands of stored keyframes and over an hour into the past. It was also evaluated on a real mobile manipulator.

Conclusion: MessyMem demonstrates that persistent, interaction-grounded memory with linked visual observations enables mobile manipulators to effectively learn from experience and reuse knowledge across extended task sequences, substantially outperforming ablated and external baselines in both simulation and real-world settings.

Abstract: Mobile manipulators deployed across many rooms and visits should improve with experience: after discovering that a cabinet is locked or finding an object in a drawer, the robot should reuse that knowledge rather than start each task from scratch. Yet today's robots often treat each task as new: compact scene representations omit interaction-derived knowledge, raw video histories are difficult to query, and VLM planners reason at inference time without persistently updating what the robot knows. We present MessyMem, a persistent memory system that enables mobile manipulators to learn from experience and reuse that knowledge across future tasks. It maintains a spatially grounded 3D scene graph of objects and locations, augments it with properties and outcomes learned through interaction, and links visual observations for fine-grained recall. We evaluate MessyMem in simulation and on a real mobile manipulator. In a continuous 25-task simulation spanning over 3 hours, MessyMem achieves 80.0% task progress, outperforming the strongest ablation by 14.8 percentage points and the strongest external baseline by 28.9 points, while retrieving task-relevant evidence from thousands of stored keyframes and over an hour into the past.

</details>


### [90] [ResSafe: Learning Safety Filtering with Residual Reinforcement Learning for Humanoids](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15988)
*Gechen Qu,Tong Zhang,Bike Zhang,Yen-Jen Wang,Koushil Sreenath,Claire Tomlin,Jason Jangho Choi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes residual reinforcement learning as an implicit safety-filtering mechanism for safe humanoid control, decoupling task performance (nominal policy) from safety (residual policy).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safe control of humanoid robots is challenging due to high-dimensional dynamics, contact-rich interactions, and sensitivity to disturbances. While reinforcement learning enables effective locomotion and motion tracking, learned policies can still produce unsafe actions causing instability or falls. A single nominal policy cannot simultaneously balance performance, safety, and robustness, and tuning multiple competing reward terms within one policy training is difficult.

Method: The authors decouple performance and safety: a nominal policy focuses solely on task performance, while a residual policy learns safety corrections. This residual learning formulation serves as an implicit safety filter, avoiding careful tuning of multiple competing reward terms within a single policy training.

Result: The decoupling yields a better performance-safety Pareto trade-off, and the residual policy is shown to act as an implicit safety filter.

Conclusion: Residual reinforcement learning offers an effective implicit safety-filtering mechanism for humanoid control, improving the performance-safety trade-off without requiring joint tuning of competing reward terms.

Abstract: Safe control of humanoid robots remains challenging due to their high-dimensional dynamics, contact-rich interactions, and sensitivity to disturbances. Although reinforcement learning has enabled effective locomotion and motion tracking, learned policies can still generate unsafe actions that lead to instability or falls. In this work, we propose residual reinforcement learning as an implicit safety-filtering mechanism for safe humanoid control. Instead of relying on a single nominal policy to simultaneously balance performance, safety, and robustness, we decouple performance and safety. The nominal policy focuses solely on task performance, while a residual policy learns safety corrections. This decoupling leads to a better performance--safety Pareto trade-off and avoids the need for careful tuning of multiple competing reward terms within a single policy training. We show that the residual policy can act as an implicit safety filter.

</details>


### [91] [IMM-based Multiple Object Tracking using a State Prediction Neural Network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13307)
*Chan-Bin Lim,Dong-Hee Paek,Seung-Hyun Kong*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes PR-IMM, a tracking method that integrates a transformer-based radar Doppler displacement predictor as an additional mode within an IMM framework alongside CV, CA, and CT models, achieving substantial gains in position accuracy and identity-consistency metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Object tracking is critical for autonomous vehicles to avoid obstacles and plan routes. Radar is attractive because it maintains detection performance in adverse weather and measures relative velocity via the Doppler effect. However, physics-based motion models (e.g., CV, CA, CT) offer stability and interpretability but poorly represent nonlinear object motion, which limits tracking accuracy.

Method: PR-IMM employs a transformer-based Prediction model (PR) that uses radar Doppler measurements to predict object displacement. The PR model is integrated into the IMM as an additional mode alongside the CV, CA, and CT motion models. The prior positions from all modes are dynamically combined according to their mode probabilities.

Result: PR-IMM reduces position-estimation error by 57.3% over the IMM and by 16.5% over the PR alone. It also reduces ID switches by 25.3% and improves IDF1 by 9.6% over the IMM.

Conclusion: The proposed PR-IMM improves nonlinear object-motion representation while preserving the stability and interpretability of physics-based motion models, and experimentally outperforms both the baseline IMM and the standalone PR model in position accuracy and tracking consistency.

Abstract: Object tracking is essential for autonomous vehicles to avoid obstacles and plan routes. Radar maintains detection performance even in adverse weather and can measure relative velocity through the Doppler effect, making it well suited for object tracking. In this paper, we propose a data-driven state PRedictor-based Interacting Multiple Model tracking method (PR-IMM) that improves nonlinear object-motion representation while preserving the stability and interpretability of physics-based motion models. The proposed method employs a transformer-based PRediction model (PR) that incorporates radar Doppler measurements to predict object displacement. The PR model is integrated into the IMM as a mode alongside the CV, CA, and CT motion models, and their prior positions are dynamically combined according to the mode probabilities. Experimental results show that PR-IMM reduces position-estimation error by 57.3% over the IMM and by 16.5% over the PR, while reducing ID switches by 25.3% and improving IDF1 by 9.6% over the IMM.

</details>


### [92] [Bridging Thought and Action: Taming Long-Horizon Instability in Open-Source LLM Agents with a MetaTool-Enhanced ROS Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13335)
*Kazi Abrar Mahmud,Nilotpaul Kundu Dhurubo,Tamal Kirttonia,Sabbir Hossain Ujjal,Mohammad Ariful Haque*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes a MetaTool-enhanced ROS-Agent architecture that forces open-source LLMs to generate a structured pseudo-code plan before action execution, improving reliability and efficiency of agentic robotic systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Open-source LLMs deployed in agentic robotic frameworks often suffer from unstable long-horizon reasoning and inefficient action execution, undermining task reliability in human-robot interaction.

Method: An enhanced ROS-Agent architecture introduces the MetaTool, an intermediate mechanism that induces the LLM to produce a pseudo-code plan of intended tool invocations. This plan is stored in the ROS-Agent's scratchpad and persists throughout execution, explicitly separating planning from execution. The system is validated on a custom mobile robotic platform with multimodal perception and motion control.

Result: Real-world interactive task experiments show improved task completion and contextual consistency, with up to ~24% gains on complex tasks versus the baseline framework.

Conclusion: Enforcing structured planning prior to execution via MetaTool reduces execution loops, improves deterministic behavior, and enhances reliability and efficiency of agentic robotic systems using open-source LLMs.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have enabled more natural human-robot interaction, but open-source models often exhibit unstable long-horizon reasoning and inefficient action execution when deployed in agentic robotic frameworks. This paper presents an enhanced ROS-Agent based architecture that improves task reliability and execution efficiency for agentic robotic systems using open-source LLMs. The proposed system introduces a novel intermediate mechanism, termed the MetaTool, which enforces structured planning prior to action execution. Given a natural-language command, the MetaTool induces the LLM to generate a pseudo-code plan of intended tool invocations, which is stored in the ROS-Agent's scratchpad and persists throughout execution. By explicitly separating planning from execution, the proposed approach reduces execution loops and improves deterministic behavior. The architecture is validated on a custom mobile robotic platform with multimodal perception and motion control capabilities. Experimental results on real-world interactive tasks demonstrate improved task completion and contextual consistency, with up to ~24% gains on complex tasks compared to the baseline framework.

</details>


### [93] [Chance-Constrained Belief-Space Maneuver Planning for Autonomous Collision Avoidance Under Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13428)
*Grace Ra Kim,Duncan Eddy,Mykel J. Kochenderfer*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This work frames conjunction mitigation as a belief-space planning problem, using chance-constrained belief-space MCTS to decide whether to wait for better tracking or maneuver before TCA, evaluated on 96 scenarios from eight historical NASA conjunctions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Growing conjunction frequency in low Earth orbit pressures operators to decide not only whether an encounter needs mitigation but whether enough information exists to justify committing to a maneuver, motivating an explicit information-action tradeoff.

Method: Conjunctions between a maneuverable spacecraft and an unmaneuverable secondary are modeled as a belief-space planning problem. Uncertain orbital states are represented as Gaussian beliefs, and a chance-constrained belief-space Monte Carlo tree search reasons over possible future tracking updates before TCA. A terminal chance constraint bounds the probability of reaching TCA above a prescribed collision-risk threshold.

Result: Evaluated on eight historical conjunctions from NASA's Conjunction Assessment Risk Analysis dataset, expanded to 96 scenarios by varying secondary-object measurement quality and tracking cadence. The planner reaches TCA without maneuvering in ~40% of episodes with no terminal collision-risk violations. Fixed-time rule-based policies resolve more encounters without maneuvering when intervention is deferred closer to TCA, but incur more terminal risk violations. Non-maneuver reach fraction ranges from 76% under accurate, frequent measurements to ~18%-20% under the poorest tracking conditions.

Conclusion: Tracking quality and frequency are not merely inputs to collision-risk estimation; they can determine when intervention becomes necessary, supporting the belief-space planning formulation as a principled way to trade information gathering against maneuver commitment.

Abstract: Increasing conjunction frequency in low Earth orbit places growing pressure on spacecraft operators to determine not only whether an encounter requires mitigation, but whether sufficient information is available to commit to a maneuver. This work formulates this information-action tradeoff as a belief-space planning problem for conjunctions between a maneuverable spacecraft and an unmaneuverable secondary object. The planner represents the uncertain orbital states as Gaussian beliefs and uses a chance-constrained belief-space Monte Carlo tree search framework to reason over possible future tracking updates before time of closest approach (TCA). A terminal chance constraint limits the probability of reaching TCA above a prescribed collision-risk threshold, allowing the planner to wait for informative tracking while intervening when deferral becomes too risky. We evaluate the approach on eight historical conjunctions from NASA's Conjunction Assessment Risk Analysis dataset. By varying the secondary-object measurement quality and tracking cadence, we generate a total of 96 distinct evaluation scenarios. Across the evaluated conditions, the planner reaches TCA without maneuvering in approximately 40% of episodes while maintaining no terminal collision-risk violations. In contrast, fixed-time rule-based maneuver policies resolve more encounters without maneuvering when intervention is deferred closer to TCA, but at the expense of increasing terminal risk violations. The fraction of episodes reaching TCA without maneuvering depends strongly on tracking quality and measurement cadence, ranging from 76% under accurate, frequent measurements to approximately 18%-20% under the poorest tracking conditions. These results show that tracking quality and frequency are not only inputs to collision-risk estimation: they can determine when intervention becomes necessary.

</details>


### [94] [Rethinking the Implications of Human Feedback for Preference Learning in Human-Robot Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13982)
*Qiping Zhang,Kate Candon,Debasmita Ghose,Marynel Vázquez*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper introduces IMPLIED, a method that learns to infer and revise implication labels from human feedback instead of relying on fixed rules, improving reward model learning and robot behavior adaptation in human-robot interaction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The standard approach to learning reward models from human feedback uses fixed implication rules to derive accepted/rejected labels for unchosen actions. However, a user study shows that human-provided implication labels often diverge from these fixed rules, and using human labels significantly improves reward learning. This mismatch motivates a more flexible, learned approach to implication modeling.

Method: IMPLIED is an implication modeling method that treats fixed-rule implications as an initial guide while learning to infer and revise accepted and rejected action labels over time. It is built on the Preference Learning from Implicit and Explicit Feedback (PIE) algorithm and evaluated against fixed-rule approaches and LLM baselines, tested on recorded human-robot interaction trajectories and a physical robot pizza-making study.

Result: IMPLIED predicts human implications more accurately than the fixed-rule approach and LLM baselines, approaching the performance of a human-label oracle. It reduces preference-estimation error and produces robot actions that are more often rational with respect to a combined reward (true preference reward plus task-specific reward) compared to baselines.

Conclusion: By learning to reason about the implications of human feedback, IMPLIED enables more faithful and efficient robot behavior adaptation during human-robot collaboration.

Abstract: In Human-Robot Interaction, the standard approach to learn a reward model that represents human preferences for robot behavior consists of three steps. First, the robot collects limited direct evidence from human feedback (e.g., positive or negative binary feedback). Then, the robot utilizes the direct evidence to derive accepted or rejected labels to feasible but unchosen actions using fixed implication rules. Finally, the robot updates the reward model with both the direct and derived evidence. Unfortunately, the fixed rule can hinder preference learning: in a user study with two collaborative simulation environments, human-provided implication labels often differed from the standard fixed rule, and using the human labels substantially improved reward learning with the Preference Learning from Implicit and Explicit Feedback (PIE) algorithm. Consequently, we propose IMPLIED, an implication modeling method that treats fixed-rule implications as an initial guide while learning to infer and revise accepted and rejected action labels over time. Across evaluations on recorded human-robot interaction trajectories and a physical robot pizza-making study, IMPLIED predicts human implications more accurately than the fixed rule approach and LLM baselines, approaching the performance of a human-label oracle.
