In turn, IMPLIED reduces preference-estimation error and leads to robot actions that are more often rational with respect to a combined reward (which includes the true preference reward and a task-specific reward) compared to baselines. By learning to reason about the implications of human feedback, this work enables more faithful and efficient robot behavior adaptation during human-robot collaboration.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [95] [ZGCM-1: A Fully Open and Extremely Efficient Foundation Model for Math and Agentic Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13356)
*Jiyan He,Guang Liang,Hao Liu,Haoxiang Guan,Jinbo Sun,Junyi Guo,Wenjun Feng,Yantai Xie,Yifei Shen,Bin Shao,Chuyang Wei,Kai Chen,Kexin Zhou,Minghang Zhu,Shuxin Zheng,Tie-Yan Liu,Taine Zhao,Wenhui Zhu,Xueyin Xu,Xiaoqing Zhang,Yatao Li,Yuxuan Ren*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ZGCM-1 is a fully open 7B dense foundation model trained from scratch with extreme data, system, and algorithmic efficiency, using a 256K context and coupling internal thinking with external tool use.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Compact models cannot passively memorize the open web, so the work aims to overcome parametric capacity limits by coupling deliberate internal thinking with active external tool use.

Method: Architecture and system co-design (interleaved gated sliding-window and full attention, stable FP8 Muon optimizer), progressive curriculum and MDP mid-training (context scaling across 16K, 64K, 256K; reformulating interaction traces as Markov Decision Processes), and an AI-native R&amp;D workflow with agent swarms for cluster operations, data curation, and diagnostic evaluation.

Result: ZGCM-1-7B is competitive across the 7B model family on general benchmarks and remains competitive with frontier models orders of magnitude larger (e.g., Qwen3-235B-A22B and GLM-5.1) on challenging mathematical reasoning and agentic search suites; the pre-training design offers ~4.2x efficiency improvement in 16K pre-training time-to-loss.

Conclusion: The work distills eight actionable empirical findings across architectural scaling, SFT quality pruning, long-context generalization, and agentic co-training dynamics, and open-sources model weights, intermediate checkpoints, training code, per-stage data and recipes, and W&amp;B logs.

Abstract: In this work, we present ZGCM-1, a fully open 7B dense foundation model trained from scratch with extreme data, system, and algorithmic efficiency. ZGCM-1 is founded on a core premise: compact models cannot passively memorize the open web, but can overcome parametric capacity limits by coupling deliberate internal thinking with active external tool use. To support this paradigm across a 256K context, we develop an end-to-end, high-efficiency open training recipe: Architecture & System Co-design: interleaved gated sliding-window and full attention, and a stable FP8 Muon optimizer; Progressive Curriculum & MDP Mid-Training: context scaling across 16K, 64K, and 256K, and the reformulation of interaction traces into Markov Decision Processes. Furthermore, we establish an AI-native R&D workflow where agent swarms autonomously manage cluster operations, data curation, and rapid diagnostic evaluation. Extensive evaluations show that ZGCM-1-7B is competitive across 7B model family on general benchmarks. On several challenging mathematical reasoning and agentic search suites, it remains competitive with frontier models orders of magnitude larger, such as Qwen3-235B-A22B and GLM-5.1. We also show that our pre-training design offers a ~4.2x efficiency improvement in 16K pre-training time-to-loss. Across the full development lifecycle, we distill eight actionable empirical findings-spanning architectural scaling, SFT quality pruning, long-context generalization, and agentic co-training dynamics. To facilitate community research, we open-source model weights from the pre-training, mid-training, and post-training stages, intermediate checkpoints, training code, per-stage data and data recipes, and W&B logs.

</details>


### [96] [Converge Then Diversify: Decoupling Convergence and Diversity in Multi-Objective Bayesian Optimisation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13396)
*Chao Jiang,Yueling Huang,Miqing Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a converge-then-diversify (CTD) approach for multi-objective Bayesian optimisation that decouples convergence and diversity into two sequential stages, yielding strong performance under tight evaluation budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MOBO methods attempt to achieve convergence (closeness to the Pareto front) and diversity (spread across the front) simultaneously throughout the search. This joint approach works well when sufficient search budgets are available, but it is difficult to design carefully and can fail under very tight budgets, where there are not enough generated solutions to approach the entire Pareto front at once.

Method: The paper introduces a converge-then-diversify (CTD) approach that decouples convergence and diversity into two stages. In the first stage, CTD focuses on convergence, quickly driving the search toward a single point on the Pareto front. In the second stage, CTD focuses on diversity, spreading solutions across the front. Two simple instantiations of CTD are presented using widely adopted acquisition functions.

Result: Across all 446 pairwise comparisons, CTD statistically outperforms state-of-the-art methods in 72.9% of cases, performs equivalently in 21.1%, and is statistically worse in only 6.1%. The advantage is particularly evident in settings with very tight evaluation budgets or in high-dimensional problems.

Conclusion: Decoupling convergence and diversity into two sequential stages is an effective strategy for MOBO, especially under tight evaluation budgets and in high-dimensional settings, where it consistently surpasses existing state-of-the-art methods.

Abstract: Multi-objective Bayesian optimisation (MOBO) is a sample-efficient approach for optimising expensive black-box functions with multiple objectives. In MOBO, the goal is to adequately approximate the Pareto front; that is, to obtain a high-quality solution set with 1) good convergence (closeness to the Pareto front) and 2) good diversity (spread across the Pareto front). Existing MOBO methods typically aim to accomplish these two tasks simultaneously, i.e., driving the search towards the Pareto front while maintaining a diverse set of nondominated solutions, such that the solutions, ideally, can gradually approach the entire front. When sufficient search budgets are available, this approach is effective. However, considering both convergence and diversity throughout the search is not easy and requires careful design. Under very tight budgets, there may not be enough solutions generated to be able to simultaneously approach the entire Pareto front. To address this issue, this paper proposes a \textit{converge-then-diversify} (CTD) approach that decouples convergence and diversity into two stages. In the first stage, CTD focuses on convergence, aiming to quickly drive the search toward a single point on the Pareto front. In the second stage, CTD focuses on diversity, aiming to spread solutions across the front. We present two simple instantiations of CTD by using widely adopted acquisition functions in the area. Experimental results show that, across all 446 pairwise comparisons, CTD statistically outperforms state-of-the-art methods in 72.9\% of the cases, performs equivalently in 21.1\%, and is statistically worse in only 6.1\%, with the advantage being particularly evident in settings with very tight evaluation budgets or in high-dimensional problems.

</details>


### [97] [Generalized Agent Iteration: One Formal Framework for Iterative Policy Improvement and Recursive Self-Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13406)
*Hongyao Tang,Yi Ma,Pengyi Li,Yifu Yuan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Generalized Agent Iteration (GAI), a formal framework that unifies iterative policy improvement and recursive self-improvement (RSI) as two cases of a single learning paradigm, distinguished by two dials: whether the improving mechanism lies inside the agent, and whether the evaluation standard is externally grounded.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RSI is claimed at many scales, yet no single formal framework describes these emerging instances. Its classical counterpart, iterative policy improvement, is well captured by generalized policy iteration (GPI) with understood theoretical properties—but only when the update principle and evaluation base lie outside the agent. A unifying account is needed to characterize, compare, and design RSI systems.

Method: The authors introduce GAI, defining the agent as a configuration of modifiable components within a system and modeling learning as a cycle of agent evaluation and agent improvement. Two pivotal dials distinguish instances: (1) whether the improving mechanism is part of the agent (delineating GPI from RSI), and (2) whether the standard the agent is measured against is grounded outside it (determining polarity as anchored, goal drift, or fully self-referential). Existing systems are placed on these two axes.

Result: The framework positions existing systems on two axes—internal vs. external improving mechanism, and internally vs. externally grounded evaluation standard—and renders the defects of RSI statable one condition at a time.

Conclusion: This is presented as a first step toward a formal characterization of RSI that rests on the classical account, makes existing systems comparable, and offers a principled basis for analyzing and designing new ones.

Abstract: When we speak of recursive self-improvement (RSI), are we speaking of a phenomenon, a mechanism, or a prospect? Towards autonomous and evolving intelligence, RSI is being claimed at many scales, while no single framework that formally describes these emerging instances exists. Its counterpart in the classical realm, iterative policy improvement, is characterized by generalized policy iteration (GPI), a framework of broad applicability with well-understood theoretical properties, but only where the update principle and the evaluation base lie outside the agent. In this paper, we propose Generalized Agent Iteration (GAI), a formal framework that describes iterative policy improvement and RSI as two cases of a single learning paradigm. GAI defines the agent as a configuration of modifiable components within a system and models the learning process as a cycle of agent evaluation and agent improvement. Two pivotal dials then distinguish the instances: whether the improving mechanism is part of the agent and whether the standard it is measured against is grounded outside it. The former dial delineates the boundary between GPI and RSI, and the latter determines a system's polarity as anchored, goal drift, or fully self-referential. Moreover, we use these coordinates to place existing systems on the same two axes and make the defects of recursive self-improvement statable one condition at a time. We see this paper as a first step toward exploring a formal characterization of RSI that rests on the classical account, makes existing systems comparable, and provides a principled basis for analyzing and designing new ones.

</details>


### [98] [Vibe Patenting: Evaluating LLM Judges for Professional Patent-Drafting Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13422)
*Toshiaki Koike-Akino,Vlad Blaykhman,Ye Wang,Jing Liu,Gene V. Vinokur*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces "Vibe Patenting," an end-to-end patent-drafting testbed, to study whether LLM judges are reliable evaluators and optimization signals for complex professional work. Judge-guided iterative revision consistently improves judge-assessed draft quality and lets a weaker agent approach a stronger one, but validation against a professional patent attorney shows only metric-dependent agreement and systematic calibration gaps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM judges are increasingly deployed to evaluate and improve AI-generated outputs, but their reliability for complex professional work remains unclear. The authors aim to test both the utility and the limitations of LLM judges as evaluators and as optimization signals in a realistic, high-stakes professional workflow.

Method: The authors build Vibe Patenting, an end-to-end patent-drafting testbed for AI-agent evaluation. A separately-invoked LLM judge evaluates generated patent drafts and returns structured feedback used for iterative revision. Experiments span multiple inventions and drafting-agent configurations, comparing judge-guided versus unguided revision, varying model strength and reasoning budgets, and testing domain-specific agentic workflows. The judge is validated against independent evaluation by a professional patent attorney.

Result: Judge-guided revision consistently improves judge-assessed quality, whereas unguided revision tends to saturate. Iterative judge feedback enables a low-reasoning agent to approach the performance of a substantially more expensive high-reasoning agent. Stronger models and increased reasoning generally raise judge-assessed drafting quality, with domain-specific agentic workflows giving further gains. Validation against the attorney shows meaningful but strongly metric-dependent agreement and systematic calibration differences.

Conclusion: LLM judges offer real utility as evaluators and optimization signals in complex professional workflows, but they are not a drop-in substitute for expert human judgment: agreement depends heavily on the metric and calibration is systematically biased. The findings argue for cautious, validation-backed use of LLM judges in professional domains.

Abstract: LLM judges are increasingly used to evaluate and improve AI-generated outputs, yet their reliability for complex professional work remains unclear. We study this problem through Vibe Patenting, an end-to-end patent-drafting testbed for AI-agent evaluation. A separately-invoked LLM judge evaluates generated patent drafts and provides structured feedback for iterative revision. Across multiple inventions and drafting-agent configurations, judge-guided revision consistently improves judge-assessed quality, while unguided revision tends to saturate. Notably, iterative judge feedback enables a low-reasoning agent to approach the performance of a substantially more expensive high-reasoning agent. Stronger models and increased reasoning generally improve judge-assessed drafting quality, while domain-specific agentic workflows provide further gains. We validate the judge against independent evaluation by a professional patent attorney and find meaningful but strongly metric-dependent agreement and systematic calibration differences. These results highlight both the utility and limitations of LLM judges as evaluators and optimization signals for complex professional workflows.

</details>


### [99] [Toward Self-Adaptive Physical AI: Can LLM Agents Manage Long-Horizon Physical Tasks?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13436)
*Varun Kaushik,Yayun Tan,Xiaofan Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This work builds a zero-shot, self-adaptive multi-agent LLM framework for long-term physical tasks, matching RL agents under matched conditions and outperforming them under shifted weather environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Managing long-term physical tasks autonomously requires agents to continuously observe the environment, take consequential actions, and remain effective as conditions change. Existing approaches either demand substantial data and retraining or focus mainly on virtual-world agents, leaving self-adaptive physical agents underexplored.

Method: The authors design a multi-agent framework integrating planning, tool calling, observation, and verification, and evaluate it zero-shot on agricultural tasks against reinforcement learning agents across different weather patterns, including shifted environments.

Result: Zero-shot LLM agents achieve management outcomes comparable to RL agents under the same weather pattern and adapt more effectively than RL when evaluated under a shifted environment.

Conclusion: The findings indicate a promising path toward self-adaptive physical AI agents that handle long-term physical tasks without human intervention or retraining.

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM) agents offer a promising path toward autonomously managing long-term physical tasks without human intervention. However, physical tasks require agents to continuously observe the environment, make consequential actions, and remain effective as the environment changes. Existing approaches either require substantial data and retraining, or primarily focus on agents operating in the virtual world. In this work, we explore the feasibility of building a self-adaptive physical AI agent that manages long-term physical tasks in a zero-shot manner and adapts to environmental changes without human intervention. We design a multi-agent framework that integrates planning, tool calling, observation, and verification, and evaluate it on agricultural tasks against reinforcement learning (RL) agents under different weather patterns. Our results show that zero-shot LLM agents can achieve comparable management outcomes to RL agents under the same weather pattern and adapt more effectively than RL when evaluated under a shifted environment, highlighting a promising path toward self-adaptive physical AI agents.

</details>


### [100] [LabAgent: Customize Any Research Hubs for Scientific Discoveries Using AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13437)
*Lei Liu,Yikun Zhang,Jialin Chen,Wanjia Zhao,Rex Ying,Wengong Jin,Hua Xu,James Zou,Tianyu Liu,Hongyu Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LabAgent is a reproduce-and-discovery harness that captures, executes, and verifies a lab's research methods to preserve and expand institutional knowledge across staff turnover.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scientific research is cumulative, but methods built by predecessors are often lost when lab personnel leave (e.g., student graduation). Effort-intensive methods cannot be replicated or continued, creating a knowledge-continuity gap that limits follow-up investigation.

Method: LabAgent uses two mechanisms: (1) guaranteeing that all skills can be executed and verified, and (2) recording corrective methods and experiences so similar errors can be directly corrected or avoided.

Result: Applied to drug property prediction, biomedical problem analysis, protein variant effect prediction, and statistical genetics, LabAgent ranks first over commercial generalist agents in every domain and accurately reproduces a published figure.

Conclusion: LabAgent can effectively integrate and reasonably expand laboratory knowledge, supporting continuous and inheritable scientific work.

Abstract: Scientific research is a continuous process that emphasizes inheritance. Methods developed by predecessors are often expanded upon by new researchers to explore more novel and in-depth scientific questions. However, the change of lab staff, such as student graduation, leads to a lack of personnel capable of replicating methods. Methods that have been developed with significant effort and resources cannot be continued. To address these limitations, we propose LabAgent, a reproduce and discovery harness tailored for a lab's continuous work. LabAgent employs two mechanisms to guarantee that all skills can be executed and verified and to record the corrective methods and experiences, allowing for direct correction or avoidance of similar errors. We applied LabAgent to drug property prediction, biomedical problem analysis, protein variant effect prediction, and statistical genetics in life science domains. LabAgent ranks first over commercial generalist agents in every domain, and demonstrates accurate reproduction of a published figure. Overall, these results demonstrate that LabAgent can effectively integrate and reasonably expand laboratory knowledge.

</details>


### [101] [Root-Cause Attribution Is a Search Problem: Continual Search for Long-Horizon Agent Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13463)
*Harsh Raj,David Lee,Anas Mahmoud,Renxiong Wang,Razvan-Gabriel Dumitru,Chenguang Wang,Tong Zhao,Yunzhong He,Darvin Yi,Vipul Gupta*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Continual Search is an iterative RCA framework that nudges an LLM judge over successive turns to keep searching for unresolved diagnostic evidence in long execution traces, improving attribution accuracy across benchmarks, with large gains on a new large-scale benchmark MegaRCA-Mix.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diagnosing failures in massive AI-agent execution logs is essential for reliability, but human review is impractical, motivating automated root-cause attribution (RCA). Existing LLM-based RCA methods are inaccurate on long traces because relevant evidence is sparse, distributed across distant actions, and disconnected from the visible failure, making RCA a massive search problem. One-shot LLM judges settle on a plausible diagnosis early and leave critical evidence unexamined.

Method: Continual Search: an iterative framework that, across successive turns, nudges the LLM judge to keep searching for unresolved diagnostic evidence rather than committing to an early diagnosis. Evaluation spans four existing RCA benchmarks, plus a new benchmark, MegaRCA-Mix, comprising 50 human-annotated failure trials over long-horizon, execution-heavy tasks to test RCA at scale.

Result: Across multiple benchmark suites and model families, Continual Search consistently improves attribution performance. On MegaRCA-Mix, it raises GPT-5.5's F1 from 0.349 to 0.498 (over 40% improvement). Within the same model family, lower-tier models can surpass higher-tier counterparts, showing effective search supersedes raw model scale.

Conclusion: Iterative, search-oriented prompting improves LLM-based root-cause attribution on long execution traces more than one-shot judging, and can compensate for smaller model scale. The introduced MegaRCA-Mix provides a challenging testbed for RCA at scale and highlights the value of sustained evidence search.

Abstract: The increasing deployment of AI agents in long-horizon tasks yields massive execution logs. Diagnosing failures within these records is crucial for reliability, as it transforms outcome-level signals into actionable interventions. The sheer scale of the data renders human review impractical, driving the need for automated root-cause attribution (RCA). However, automated RCA methods using LLMs suffer from low diagnostic accuracy, especially as execution traces grow larger. They struggle because relevant information is often sparse, distributed across distant actions, and disconnected from the visible failure, reducing root-cause attribution to a massive search problem. Existing RCA methods typically rely on one-shot LLM judgments to diagnose failures from execution traces. While effective for shorter trajectories, these judges tend to settle on a plausible diagnosis early, leaving critical evidence in longer traces unexamined. We introduce Continual Search, an iterative framework that nudges the judge, over successive turns, to keep searching for unresolved diagnostic evidence. We evaluate Continual Search across four existing RCA benchmarks. Recognizing the lack of massive execution traces in current benchmarks, we introduce MegaRCA-Mix to evaluate RCA at scale. MegaRCA-Mix provides a challenging testbed of 50 human-annotated failure trials spanning long-horizon, execution-heavy tasks. Across multiple benchmark suites and model families, Continual Search consistently improves attribution performance. On MegaRCA-Mix, for example, it improves GPT-5.5's F1 score by more than 40\%, from $0.349$ to $0.498$. More interestingly, within the same model family, lower-tier models can even surpass their higher-tier counterparts, demonstrating that effective search supersedes raw model scale.

</details>


### [102] [OrchSLM: Probing the Dynamics of Small Language Model Orchestration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13470)
*Chengxi Zhang,Yu Yao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces OrchSLM, a routing framework that unifies non-interactive SLM orchestration methods, exposing their design choices as controllable parameters to systematically study how orchestration behavior emerges from task structure, model-pool composition, and consensus.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs rely on cloud-scale infrastructure, causing latency, privacy, connectivity, and cost issues in agentic pipelines. SLMs are a promising alternative for repetitive subtasks, but their limited capacity and context windows constrain long-horizon reasoning and interaction-heavy orchestration like iterative verification and debate. This motivates a complementary non-interactive paradigm.

Method: The authors introduce OrchSLM, a routing framework that unifies existing non-interactive orchestration methods and exposes their underlying design choices as controllable parameters. Heterogeneous SLMs independently generate candidate solutions, and a router orchestrates their cached samples without further model interaction. OrchSLM is used as a systematic probe to analyze orchestration behavior across various knobs.

Result: OrchSLM reveals how orchestration behavior emerges from diverse knobs, including task structure, model-pool composition, and multi-agent consensus.

Conclusion: The framework provides a systematic way to understand and control non-interactive SLM orchestration, offering a complementary paradigm to interaction-heavy strategies for deploying SLMs in agentic workloads.

Abstract: Although large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities, their reliance on cloud-scale infrastructure poses fundamental challenges for deployment in agentic pipelines, including latency, privacy, connectivity, and substantial computational cost. Small language models (SLMs) offer a compelling alternative: recent studies suggest that many repetitive and narrowly scoped subtasks in agentic workloads may be better served by specialized SLMs than by monolithic LLMs. However, the limited capacity and context windows of SLMs can constrain long-horizon reasoning and interaction-heavy orchestration strategies such as iterative verification and debate. This motivates a complementary, non-interactive paradigm in which heterogeneous SLMs independently generate candidate solutions and a router orchestrates their cached samples without further model interaction. To further understand the mechanisms of such orchestration, we introduce OrchSLM, a routing framework that unifies existing non-interactive orchestration methods and exposes their underlying design choices as controllable parameters. Using OrchSLM as a systematic probe, we reveal how orchestration behavior emerges from diverse knobs, including the task structure, model-pool composition, and multi-agent consensus.

</details>


### [103] [Grounded Adjudication of Variations across Extracted TimeLines (GAVEL): Comparing Clinical Timelines Against Their Case Reports](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13475)
*Jack Cummins,Sayantan Kumar,Ketan Tamirisa,Jeremy C. Weiss*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GAVEL, an LLM-judge protocol that compares two clinical timelines against the source case report, issues per-discrepancy verdicts, and thereby enables rank-based evaluation and merging of timelines without assuming a gold-standard reference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical timeline extraction from case reports is normally scored against an expert reference, but such evaluation is constrained by imperfect reference annotations and imprecise event alignment, which limits the reliability of both evaluation and system ranking.

Method: GAVEL compares two timelines together with the case report and, for each difference, returns a discrepancy type, a verdict, and the supporting report passage. The authors evaluated the event matcher, manually reviewed 2,738 findings produced by GPT5.6sol and DeepSeek V3.2, ranked six LLM extractors and two human annotators, and tested GAVEL-guided timeline merging.

Result: True match rates were 60% just below and 48% just above the 0.10 cutoff. Manual review confirmed 89.4% and 88.6% of findings. Across 126 reports, merged timelines were preferred in 77.0% of comparisons (95% CI, 69.8 to 84.1%) and reduced discrepancies attributed to the evaluated timeline from 7.63 to 0.85 per report.

Conclusion: GAVEL supports report-grounded comparison and revision of clinical timelines without treating either timeline as ground truth, offering a reference-free alternative to conventional expert-annotation-based evaluation.

Abstract: Existing pipelines for clinical timeline extraction from case reports are evaluated using an expert reference and are limited by imperfect reference annotations and imprecise event alignment. We developed GAVEL, an LLM judge protocol that compares two timelines with the case report and returns a discrepancy type, verdict, and report passage for each difference. We evaluated the event matcher, reviewed 2,738 findings from GPT5.6sol and DeepSeek V3.2, ranked six LLM extractors and two human annotators, and tested GAVEL guided merging. True match rates were 60% immediately below and 48% immediately above the 0.10 cutoff. Manual review confirmed 89.4% and 88.6% of findings. Across 126 reports, merged timelines were preferred in 77.0% of comparisons (95% CI, 69.8 to 84.1%) and reduced discrepancies attributed to the evaluated timeline from 7.63 to 0.85 per report. GAVEL supports report-based comparison and revision without treating either timeline as ground truth.

</details>


### [104] [Token Efficient Task Execution via Application Behavior Modeling for Web Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13491)
*Alexandru Ianta,Eleni Stroulia*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OdoBot is a novel web-agent architecture that leverages a behavioral model of the underlying application, built from successful task-execution demonstrations, to complete web tasks at a fraction of the token cost of conventional agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI agents' strong task performance is driving heavy investment in agentic infrastructures, but token-processing costs are rising rapidly. Web agents, which automate natural-language-described web tasks by analyzing and interacting with a UI, incur substantial token costs, motivating a more cost-efficient design.

Method: OdoBot constructs a behavioral model of the target application by analyzing successful task-execution demonstrations, and uses this model to complete tasks rather than relying on conventional agent reasoning over the UI.

Result: Across 45 tasks on the Canvas Learning Management System (LMS), OdoBot used 44% and 80% fewer tokens than Agent-E and WebVoyager respectively, while also surpassing WebVoyager in task success rate.

Conclusion: Leveraging a demonstration-derived behavioral model enables web agents to achieve strong task success at substantially lower token cost, offering a more economical alternative to conventional web-agent architectures.

Abstract: The strong performance of AI Agents across an impressive variety of tasks is driving an unprecedented investment in agentic infrastructures, however the cost of processing tokens is fast increasing. Web agents automate the execution of web-application tasks described in natural language, by analyzing the web-application's user interface (UI) and interacting with it. This work introduces OdoBot, a novel web-agent architecture that completes tasks at a fraction of the cost when compared to conventional web agents. This is achieved by leveraging a behavioral model of the underlying application constructed by analyzing successful task-execution demonstrations. Our experiments with 45 tasks on the Canvas Learning Management System (LMS) demonstrate that OdoBot uses 44% and 80% fewer tokens than two state-of-the-art competitor agents (Agent-E and WebVoyager), while also surpassing WebVoyager in terms of task success rate.

</details>


### [105] [From Legal Text to AI-specific Risk Sources: A Systematic Analysis of the EU AI Act's High-Risk Requirements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13535)
*Ronald Schnitzer,Mike Auer,Rumpa Choudhury,Andreas Hapfelmeier,Maximilian Hoeving,Isabelle Painter,Josiane Xavier Parreira,Sonja Zillner*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper systematically classifies EU AI Act Section 2 high-risk requirements, showing most are organizational/process obligations while only a minority target AI-specific risk sources, and derives a consolidated EU AI Act Risk Source List to bridge regulatory obligations and AI risk management taxonomies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The EU AI Act mandates requirements for high-risk AI systems to ensure trustworthy AI, while AI risk management relies on structured risk taxonomies to identify and treat AI-specific risk sources. Since both aim to address AI-induced risks, there is a natural question of whether they align in covered risk sources, yet no clear mapping exists between the risks implicitly addressed by the Act's requirements and established taxonomies, leaving practitioners without a structured basis to align regulatory obligations with risk management practice.

Method: The authors perform a systematic classification of the requirements extracted from EU AI Act Section 2 (Requirements for high-risk AI systems), categorizing them and identifying which ones directly address AI-specific risk sources versus those imposing organizational process and documentation obligations. From the AI risk-related requirements, they derive a consolidated list of distinct AI-specific risk sources.

Result: The classification reveals that only a minority of the requirements directly address AI-specific risk sources, while the majority impose organizational process and documentation obligations. From the AI risk-related requirements, a consolidated list of distinct AI-specific risk sources is derived, resulting in the EU AI Act Risk Source List.

Conclusion: The EU AI Act Risk Source List takes an important step towards bridging the gap between legal obligation and AI risk management practice, providing a structured reference for explicit comparison between existing AI risk taxonomies and the risk sources implicitly addressed by the EU AI Act.

Abstract: The EU AI Act introduces mandatory requirements for high-risk AI systems with the explicit goal of ensuring the development and operation of trustworthy AI. At the same time, AI risk management practices rely on structured risk taxonomies to systematically identify and treat AI-specific risk sources. As both the AI Act and established risk taxonomies aim to address AI-induced risks, a natural question is whether they align in the risk sources they cover. However, no clear mapping exists between the risks implicitly addressed by the Act's high-risk requirements and established taxonomies, leaving practitioners without a structured basis for aligning regulatory obligations with AI risk management practice. This paper presents a systematic classification of the requirements extracted from the EU AI Act Section 2 (Requirements for high-risk AI systems), revealing that only a minority directly address AI-specific risk sources, while the majority impose organizational process and documentation obligations. From the AI risk-related requirements, a consolidated list of distinct AI-specific risk sources is derived. The resulting EU AI Act Risk Source List takes an important step towards bridging the gap between legal obligation and AI risk management practice, providing a structured reference for explicit comparison between existing AI risk taxonomies and the risk sources implicitly addressed by the EU AI Act. Important Note: This is the authors' preprint. The paper was presented at the 4th International Conference on Frontiers of Artificial Intelligence, Ethics, and Multidisciplinary Applications. A link to the conference's official proceedings will be provided upon publication.

</details>


### [106] [Asclepius: An Adaptive Harness for Long-Horizon Clinical Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13543)
*Grace Chang Yuan,Xiaoman Zhang,Sung Eun Kim,Luyang Luo,Pranav Rajpurkar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Current LLM agents are benchmarked on short single-task trajectories, but real deployments run long under contention, exposing a distinct execution gap. Using the Clinical Environment Simulator (CES) as a long-horizon testbed, the authors identify three failure modes—instruction-adherence drift, treatment incompleteness, and a severity-equity gap in timeliness—and propose Asclepius, an adaptive scaffolding with a self-evolving harness, externalized clinical skills library, and three isolated subagents, improving critical-action correctness by 22% (p = 0.024) on held-out batches and up to 25% on the full set.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents are predominantly benchmarked on short, single-task trajectories, whereas real deployments run for hours under contention and surface a different class of failures. Existing evaluations therefore miss long-horizon execution failures, and diagnosing them requires a structured, multi-dimensional testbed rather than short-horizon benchmarks.

Method: The authors use the Clinical Environment Simulator (CES), where an agent manages an entire emergency-department shift under continuous time and resource pressure, to make long-horizon failures measurable in a single rollout via structured, multi-dimensional grading. They attribute the observed execution gap to three long-horizon failure modes, each operationalized as a per-trace counter: instruction-adherence drift, treatment incompleteness, and a severity-equity gap in timeliness. They then introduce Asclepius, an adaptive agent scaffolding comprising a self-evolving harness that rewrites the operating manual between shifts from trace-level feedback, an externalized clinical skills library for high-stakes regimen knowledge, and three isolated subagents that partition per-turn decisions across the patient queue.

Result: On CES, current agents reach the correct diagnosis in most cases yet fail to deliver complete and timely critical actions, revealing an execution gap. On held-out batches never observed during harness evolution, Asclepius improves critical-action correctness by 22% (p = 0.024) over a strong baseline agent framework while preserving diagnostic accuracy, with consistent gains across five LLM judges from three model families. On the full ten-batch set, improvements reach 25% on critical actions and 13% on timeliness.

Conclusion: The three long-horizon failure modes form a coupled bottleneck: decisive reductions appear only when all three Asclepius components act together. This indicates that long-horizon execution failures in LLM agents are multi-factorial and require coordinated intervention rather than isolated fixes.

Abstract: LLM agents are predominantly benchmarked on short, single-task trajectories, yet real deployments run for hours under contention, surfacing a different class of failures. We use the Clinical Environment Simulator (CES), in which an agent manages an entire emergency-department shift under continuous time and resource pressure, as a testbed: long-horizon execution failures manifest measurably in a single rollout under structured, multi-dimensional grading. On CES, current agents reach the correct diagnosis in most cases yet fail to deliver complete and timely critical actions, revealing an execution gap. We attribute this gap to three long-horizon failure modes, each operationalized as a per-trace counter: instruction-adherence drift, treatment incompleteness, and a severity-equity gap in timeliness. We then introduce Asclepius, an adaptive agent scaffolding with a self-evolving harness that rewrites the operating manual between shifts from trace-level feedback, an externalized clinical skills library for high-stakes regimen knowledge, and three isolated subagents that partition per-turn decisions across the patient queue. On held-out batches never observed during harness evolution, Asclepius improves critical-action correctness by 22% (p = 0.024) over a strong baseline agent framework while preserving diagnostic accuracy, with consistent gains across five LLM judges from three model families; on the full ten-batch set, improvements reach 25% on critical actions and 13% on timeliness. The three failure modes form a coupled bottleneck: decisive reductions appear only when all three components act together.

</details>


### [107] [Toward a Decision-Assurance Layer for AI-Assisted Flight Planning in Air Traffic Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13552)
*Alexandre Barreto,Shou Matsumoto,Jorge Valverde-Rebaza,Cleiton Ataide,Paulo Costa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces the AI Trust and Assurance Layer (ATAL), a model-agnostic decision assurance architecture that assesses the reliability of AI-generated flight-planning outputs before they influence operational decisions in Air Traffic Management.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generative AI is being adopted informally in Air Traffic Management for tasks like flight plan generation, trajectory interpretation, and constraint checking, reducing workload and accelerating planning. However, its non-deterministic outputs introduce safety and operational risks in human-in-the-loop settings, creating a need to verify whether AI outputs are reliable enough for operational use.

Method: ATAL combines three evaluation signals: semantic stability under prompt variation, operational consistency of structured outputs, and normative constraint validation against domain rules. These signals are mapped to a Decision Readiness Level (DRL) to inform human operators. The approach was tested through an ATM-inspired experimental study.

Result: The experimental study demonstrates that unsafe, inconsistent, or misleading AI-generated outputs can be identified before they influence flight-plan validation or execution.

Conclusion: ATAL provides a model-agnostic assurance mechanism that supports human oversight of generative AI in aviation. Although demonstrated in ATM, the framework is transferable to other safety-critical decision-support domains operating under regulatory constraints.

Abstract: Generative AI is increasingly being used informally in Air Traffic Management (ATM) for tasks such as flight plan generation, trajectory interpretation, and constraint checking. Although these tools can reduce workload and accelerate planning, their non-deterministic outputs create safety and operational risks in human-in-the-loop settings. This paper proposes the AI Trust and Assurance Layer (ATAL), a model-agnostic decision assurance architecture that evaluates whether AI-generated flight-planning outputs are sufficiently reliable for operational use. ATAL combines semantic stability under prompt variation, operational consistency of structured outputs, and normative constraint validation against domain rules, and maps these signals to a Decision Readiness Level (DRL) for human operators. An ATM-inspired experimental study shows how unsafe, inconsistent, or misleading outputs can be identified before influencing flight-plan validation or execution. Although demonstrated in aviation, the framework is also transferable to other safety-critical decision-support domains that require human oversight under regulatory constraints.

</details>


### [108] [A Hybrid Agentic AI Framework for Intelligent Supply Chain Analytics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13561)
*Xian Yeow Lee,Teppei Inoue,Haiyan Wang,Chetan Gupta*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes an agentic multi-agent system for supply chain analytics where a coordinator agent delegates tasks to specialized agents, achieving 90% accuracy (competitive with a single-agent baseline) while cutting input token usage roughly fourfold, via a modular, prompt-centric, auditable design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Efficiently using supply chain analytics for decision making is difficult for planners: tasks like database querying, KPI analysis, demand forecasting, and performance diagnosis demand heterogeneous expertise across data engineering, operations research, and domain knowledge, creating a gap between business decision-making and technical expertise.

Method: An agentic system with a coordinator agent that interprets user intent and delegates sub-tasks to specialized agents. It supports both exploratory analysis and deterministic workflows, encapsulating domain logic within specialist agents and prompts for a scalable, modular, auditable design with low-cost prompt-centric extension. Evaluation is on a test environment replicating multi-echelon inventory management operations.

Result: The multi-agent design attains 90% accuracy, competitive with a single-agent baseline, while reducing input token usage by roughly fourfold, improving scalability and cost-efficiency. Case studies demonstrate interpretable suboptimality detection and automated forecast optimization.

Conclusion: Agentic architectures can effectively combine open-ended exploratory analysis with deterministic supply chain analytics workflows, offering a practical pathway toward more accessible and extensible decision-support systems.

Abstract: Efficient utilization of supply chain analytics for decision making remains a significant challenge for planners, as critical tasks such as database querying, key performance indicator (KPI) analysis, demand forecasting, and performance diagnosis require heterogeneous expertise spanning data engineering, operations research, and domain knowledge. In this work, we propose an agentic system for supply chain analytics that bridges the gap between business decision-making and technical expertise, where a coordinator agent interprets user intent and delegates sub-tasks to specialized agents. The system supports both exploratory analysis and deterministic workflows, enabling planners to transition between ad hoc questions and structured processes. Domain logic is encapsulated within specialist agents and prompts, yielding a scalable, modular, and auditable design and lowering the cost of functional extension through prompt-centric development. We evaluate the proposed architecture on a test environment that replicates multi-echelon inventory management operations. Results show that our multi-agent design achieves a 90\% accuracy, which is competitive with a single agent baseline while reducing input token usage by roughly fourfold, substantially improving scalability and cost-efficiency. Furthermore, we provide case studies to demonstrate interpretable suboptimality detection and automated forecast optimization, illustrating how agentic architectures can effectively combine open-ended exploratory analysis and deterministic supply chain analytics workflows, and provide a practical pathway toward more accessible and extensible decision-support systems.

</details>


### [109] [Planning or Learning: Reliability and Cost in Multi-Asset Maintenance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13566)
*Xian Yeow Lee,Chandrasekar Venkatraman,Ahmed Farahat*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper empirically compares planning and RL for multi-asset bearing maintenance under identical conditions, finding that planning treats reliability as a hard constraint (zero failures, penalty-insensitive cost), while RL trades failures for lower cost, making the two approaches complementary.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Industrial maintenance involves multiple interacting assets and shared resources, making the reliability-cost trade-off hard to balance within a single decision framework. Although RL has gained attention for maintenance scheduling, direct comparisons with planning methods under identical settings remain scarce.

Method: An empirical comparison of planning versus RL for multi-asset bearing maintenance using run-to-failure data. The study tests both paradigms across a range of failure penalty scenarios, plus lightweight constraint mechanisms (reward shaping and action masking) to push RL toward reliability. All methods share a unified environment, cost model, and evaluation protocol.

Result: A consistent behavioral difference emerges from objective formulation: planning enforces reliability as a hard constraint, yielding zero-failure policies whose total cost is largely insensitive to failure penalty magnitude, whereas RL optimizes expected cost, trading preventive maintenance against occasional failures—achieving lower costs under low penalties but persistent non-zero failures even at high penalties.

Conclusion: Planning suits strict-reliability, short-horizon deployments, while RL suits cost-efficient, failure-tolerant, long-run settings; the two are complementary. The controlled benchmark protocol itself is a reusable template for comparing decision-making approaches in other maintenance contexts.

Abstract: Industrial maintenance systems involve multiple interacting assets and shared resources, making it challenging to balance reliability and operational cost using a single decision framework. While recent work has focused on reinforcement learning (RL) for maintenance scheduling, direct comparisons with planning approaches under identical settings remain limited. In this work, we empirically compare planning and RL for multi-asset bearing maintenance using run-to-failure data. We examine how these methods behave when balancing preventive maintenance against tolerable failures across a range of failure penalty scenarios. We observed a consistent behavioral difference driven by objective formulation. Planning enforces reliability as a hard constraint and produces zero-failure policies whose total cost is largely insensitive to the magnitude of failure penalties. RL agents optimize expected cost and often trade off preventive maintenance against occasional failures as penalties vary, resulting in lower costs under low-penalty regimes but persistent non-zero failures even when penalties are high. We also investigate lightweight constraint mechanisms, including reward shaping and action masking, to encourage RL's reliability. From a practical perspective, planning may be more suitable when strict reliability is required and deployment horizons are short, whereas RL may provide cost-efficient policies when limited failures are acceptable and long-run operational efficiency is prioritized. Overall, this study clarifies the trade-offs between reliability and cost in multi-asset maintenance and suggests that planning and RL are complementary approaches. Beyond these findings, the controlled benchmark protocol itself that unifies environment, cost model, and evaluation across paradigms, offers a reusable template for comparing decision-making approaches in other maintenance settings.

</details>


### [110] [Causal multi-modal AI for personalized chemosensitivity prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13567)
*Dhruva Biswas,Jeroen Berrevoets,Alec McClean,Linus Bao,Jungkyu Park,Ken G. Zeng,Joseph Cappadona,Cerise Tang,Chuwen Liu,Bartosz Machura,Yin Wu,Valerie Speirs,Hatem Soliman,Rohit Bhargava,Sheheryar Kabraji,Thaer Khoury,David Page,Brian Piening,Carlo Bifulco,Claudia Meurs,Pieter Westenend,Sylvie Chabaud,Jerome Lemonnier,Paul H. Cottu,Florence Dalenc,Fabrice Andre,Frederique Madeleine Penault-Llorca,Thomas Bachelot,Frederick Howard,Francisco J. Esteva,Kevin Kalinsky,Lajos Pusztai,Jan Witowski,Krzysztof J. Geras*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A causal multi-modal AI model predicts personalized chemosensitivity in breast cancer from routine pathology and clinical data, outperforming recurrence-score tests and potentially cutting chemotherapy use by 30% without worsening recurrence-free rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chemotherapy benefits only some breast cancer patients, but doctors cannot reliably identify who will benefit. Current guidelines use recurrence scores as a proxy for treatment benefit, which may drive chemotherapy overprescription. There is a need for a tool that directly predicts personalized chemosensitivity rather than relying on indirect proxies.

Method: The authors built a causal multi-modal AI model that predicts personalized chemosensitivity using routinely collected pathology and clinical information. It was developed on a multi-national dataset of 9,141 patients (twelve cohorts, nine countries) and evaluated on 1,994 patients (five cohorts, three countries), generating treatment-specific recurrence probabilities per patient.

Result: The model achieved near-perfect calibration and strong prognostic discrimination at 5- and 10-year horizons. Its chemotherapy benefit predictions were robust and outperformed existing recurrence-score-based tests. Using it to guide therapy could reduce chemotherapy recipients by 30% while maintaining the same recurrence-free rate. Highly chemosensitive tumors showed concordant molecular and morphological proliferation, cell cycle, and replication stress programs. Predictions transferred zero-shot to non-breast cancers.

Conclusion: The causal multi-modal AI approach can support personally tailored therapeutic decisions, reducing chemotherapy overuse while preserving outcomes. Its zero-shot transfer to non-breast cancers suggests it may offer a universal strategy for predicting treatment outcomes across cancer types.

Abstract: Chemotherapy improves survival for some patients with breast cancer, but doctors cannot reliably predict who. Current guidelines rely on recurrence scores as a proxy for treatment benefit, which may contribute to the overprescription of chemotherapy. Here we present a causal multi-modal AI model that predicts personalized chemosensitivity using routinely collected pathology and clinical information. We developed our model on a multi-national dataset of 9,141 patients (twelve cohorts, nine countries) and evaluated it on another 1,994 patients (five cohorts, three countries). The model generated treatment-specific recurrence probabilities for each patient, with near-perfect calibration and strong prognostic discrimination across both 5- and 10-year follow-up horizons. Moreover, its chemotherapy benefit predictions demonstrated robust predictive performance, and out-performed existing recurrence-score-based tests. Compared to the standard of care, using the model to support personally tailored therapeutic decisions could reduce the number of patients receiving chemotherapy by 30% while achieving the same recurrence-free rate. Tumors predicted to be highly chemosensitive displayed concordant molecular and morphological programs of proliferation, cell cycle progression, and replication stress. The model's predictive capabilities transferred zero-shot to non-breast cancers, indicating our causal multi-modal AI approach may provide a universal strategy to predict treatment outcomes across cancer types.

</details>


### [111] [How User-AI Mistreatment Occurs and Matters in Conversational Systems?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13579)
*Fanqi Zeng,Sadid A. Hasan,Chaocheng He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper audits 777K English LMSYS-Chat-1M conversations to characterise user-to-model hostility and coercion, finding that lexicon-based and moderation-based detectors capture weakly overlapping phenomena, with mistreatment aimed at the assistant at ~0.90% after precision adjustment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety research predominantly examines harms generated by models, while hostility, coercion, and adversarial pressure directed by users at models remain under-examined. Understanding these dynamics is necessary to interpret model behaviour, alignment drift, and deployment risks accurately.

Method: The authors audit 777K English LMSYS-Chat-1M conversations using two independent detectors: (1) an eight-category lexicon tagging hostility directed at the model (insults, threats, jailbreak coercion), and (2) the dataset's moderation signal. They cross-validate the detectors, measure overlap, adjust rates for precision, and analyse variation across models, across first-turn vs. post-response turns, and within conversations (linking assistant apologies to next-turn hostility), plus temporal structure.

Result: The two detectors capture different, weakly overlapping phenomena: the lexicon flags insults, threats, and jailbreak coercion at the assistant, while moderation flags are dominated by toxic-content solicitation rather than model-directed hostility. Together they mark ~5% of user turns; precision-adjusting the narrower lexicon-harassment union yields 0.90% mistreatment aimed at the assistant. User hostility varies 13-fold across models, driven largely by audience/attraction effects rather than model behaviour, with first-turn hostility spreading far wider than post-response hostility and >15-fold separation at extremes after deduplicating opening prompts. Assistant apologies are consistently associated with higher odds of next-turn hostility under both detectors, robust to restricting to non-refused prior turns and jailbreak-free conversations, and positive in 20 of 23 models; yet across models, more apologetic models receive less hostility overall. Hostility shows temporal structure: coercive openings front-load the first turn while affective hostility accumulates over a session.

Conclusion: Absolute hostility rates describe arena-style evaluation traffic and should not be generalised as deployment-wide base rates. The study releases the lexicon, the detector cross-validation pipeline, and all derived tables, and highlights the value of distinguishing model-directed hostility from generic toxic-content signals.

Abstract: Safety research often focuses on model-generated harms, but users may also direct hostility, coercion, and adversarial pressure at models. Understanding how and when that occurs is essential for accurately interpreting model behaviour, alignment drift, and real-world deployment risks. In this paper, we audit 777K English LMSYS-Chat-1M conversations with two independent detectors: an eight-category lexicon for hostility directed at the model, and the dataset's moderation signal; and show that they capture different, weakly overlapping phenomena. The lexicon identifies insults, threats, and jailbreak coercion aimed at the assistant, while moderation flags are dominated by toxic-content solicitation rather than hostility at the model. Together, they mark about 5% of user turns; adjusting the narrower lexicon-harassment union for measured precision puts mistreatment aimed at the assistant at 0.90%. These absolute rates describe arena-style evaluation traffic and should not be read as deployment-wide base rates. We find that user hostility varies 13-fold across models, driven largely by who each model attracts rather than by model behaviour: first-turn hostility spreads far wider than post-response hostility, and more than fifteenfold separates the extremes even after deduplicating opening prompts. Within conversations, assistant apologies are consistently associated with higher odds of next-turn hostility under both detectors; the effect survives restricting to non-refused prior turns and to jailbreak-free conversations, and is positive in 20 of 23 models. Yet across models, more apologetic models receive less hostility overall. Finally, hostility also shows temporal structure, with coercive openings front-loading the first turn while affective hostility accumulates over a session. We release the lexicon, the detector cross-validation pipeline, and all derived tables.

</details>


### [112] [FLoKD: Adaptive Knowledge Distillation for Federated Low-Rank LLM over Wireless Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13580)
*Xinlu Zhang,Na Yan,Yang Su,Yansha Deng,Toktam Mahmoodi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FLoKD is an adaptive knowledge-distillation framework for federated LoRA fine-tuning of LLMs over wireless networks that communicates intermediate LoRA activations instead of logits or full parameters, using block importance scoring and dataset selection to cut communication overhead by 50-65%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional LLM fine-tuning relies on centralized data, raising privacy concerns. Federated learning (FL) enables collaborative fine-tuning without sharing raw client data, but its deployment over bandwidth-constrained wireless networks is hindered by the communication overhead of model-parameter transmission. LoRA reduces trainable parameters, yet its communication cost still grows with model scale. Knowledge distillation avoids parameter sharing via output logits, but token-level logits in LLMs incur high communication cost due to sequence length and vocabulary size, and reducing logits weakens supervision and degrades accuracy.

Method: FLoKD communicates intermediate LoRA activations as the distillation signal rather than logits or full parameters. To avoid the cost of transmitting all blocks over the entire public dataset, it introduces a transformer block importance scoring framework that selectively transmits the most informative blocks, along with two dataset selection strategies that discard public samples deviating from the local data distribution and prioritise those most informative for distillation.

Result: Extensive experiments across multiple generative language datasets, including WikiText-103, PTB, and Dialog, show that the proposed framework reduces communication overhead by 50-65% while achieving rapid convergence to competitive perplexity compared to baselines.

Conclusion: FLoKD provides an adaptive knowledge-distillation approach for federated LoRA fine-tuning of LLMs over wireless networks, effectively balancing communication efficiency and model accuracy by exploiting intermediate LoRA activations, block importance scoring, and dataset selection.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong capabilities across a wide range of natural language processing tasks. However, conventional fine-tuning typically relies on centralized data collection, bringing in privacy concerns. Federated learning (FL) enables collaborative LLM fine-tuning without sharing raw client data, but its deployment over bandwidth-constrained wireless networks is hindered by the communication overhead of model-parameter transmission. Although Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) reduces the number of trainable parameters, its communication cost still increases with model scale. Knowledge distillation avoids parameter sharing via output logits, but token-level logits in LLMs incur high communication cost due to sequence length and vocabulary size. Reducing logits lowers the cost but weakens supervision and degrades accuracy. To address these limitations, we propose FLoKD, an adaptive knowledge-distillation framework for federated LoRA fine-tuning of LLMs over wireless networks, which communicates intermediate LoRA activations as the distillation signal rather than logits or full parameters. Since transmitting all blocks over the entire public dataset remains costly, we further propose a transformer block importance scoring framework that selectively transmits the most informative blocks, and two dataset selection strategies that discard public samples deviating from the local data distribution and prioritise those most informative for distillation. Extensive experiments across multiple generative language datasets, including WikiText-103, PTB, and Dialog, demonstrate that our proposed framework reduces communication overhead by 50-65% while achieving rapid convergence to competitive perplexity compared to baselines.

</details>


### [113] [$τ$-Elicitation: Benchmarking multi-turn entity extraction in voice agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13602)
*Soham Ray,Victor Barres*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: τ-Elicitation, a 200-task voice benchmark over 10 entity types, shows voice agents achieve only 0.14–0.41 robust exact success versus perfect text agents; strategy prescription raises Pass³ by 14–31 points at 21–28s cost, identifying strategy selection and error recovery as key bottlenecks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: End-to-end voice benchmarks obscure where spoken entity capture fails, since agents must collect names, addresses, identifiers, dates, and times exactly. There is a need to isolate and diagnose failure modes in exact spoken entity collection.

Method: Introduced τ-Elicitation, a 200-task voice benchmark spanning 10 entity types with controlled difficulty, caller realisms, and three environments. Compared against a matched text agent, evaluated four voice configurations, and tested a scaffold prescribing spelling, read-back, correction, and confirmation.

Result: Matched text agent passed all tasks; four voice configurations achieved robust exact success of only 0.14–0.41. Agents verified more for hard/unfamiliar entities and sometimes incorrect captures, but not for their weakest caller voice; only 24–37% of verified errors were repaired. The scaffold raised robust Pass³ by 14–31 points at a cost of 21–28 seconds per call. Spelling variations and restarts did not detectably affect exact success; mispronunciation increased repair effort.

Conclusion: Strategy selection and successful recovery are the central bottlenecks in exact spoken entity collection, rather than robustness to realisms like spelling variations or restarts.

Abstract: Voice agents often need to collect names, addresses, identifiers, dates, and times exactly, yet end-to-end benchmarks obscure where capture fails. We introduce $τ$-Elicitation, a 200-task voice benchmark spanning 10 entity types, controlled difficulty, caller realisms, and three environments. A matched text agent passes all tasks, but four voice configurations achieve robust exact success from 0.14 to 0.41. Agents increase verification for hard and unfamiliar entities and sometimes for incorrect captures, but not for their weakest caller voice; only 24 to 37 percent of verified errors are repaired. A scaffold that prescribes spelling, read-back, correction, and confirmation raises robust Pass$^3$ by 14 to 31 points, at a cost of 21 to 28 seconds per call. Realisms such as spelling variations and restarts do not detectably affect exact success; mispronunciation increases repair effort. These results identify strategy selection and successful recovery as the central bottlenecks in exact spoken entity collection.

</details>


### [114] [Identity Is More Than Recall: A Benchmark for Persistent Identity in Deployed AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13637)
*Zhenyu Zhao,Roy Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PAI-Bench is a provider-neutral benchmark that separately measures recall, composition, behavioral enactment, resistance, persistence, lineage, and role-conditioned updates of a versioned, update-governed identity contract, using frozen campaigns and judge-independent audits to expose prompt-dependent and startup-cue-dependent identity expression.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Persistent agents require evaluations that distinguish identity facts they can recall from those they express and enact; existing evaluations conflate factual availability, identity expression, and behavioral enactment, and scoring oracles may be entangled with the target process.

Method: PAI-Bench keeps scoring oracles outside the target process and separates seven evaluation dimensions. Two frozen campaigns cover sixteen synthetic profiles, thirty-two probes, and three independently initialized target configurations, yielding 1,536 retained responses. A judge-independent literal audit checks direct-parent identifiers and implicit self-portraits, factorial cues vary explicit field cues, and a startup body-label substitution test is run; a replay of identical factorial responses tests evaluator sensitivity.

Result: Direct-parent identifiers appear in 48/48 atomic responses but in only 1/48 implicit self-portraits. Explicit field cues raise joint presence of three identity identifiers from 0/8 to 7/8 on eight profiles under the same four-sentence instruction. Startup body-label substitution raises full-designation presence from 1/8 to 7/8 while parents remain absent. Replaying identical factorial responses yields a Claude headline mean 12.5 percentage points below Astra's.

Conclusion: The contrasts reveal prompt-dependent component selection and component-specific sensitivity to startup cues in the tested deployments, and demonstrate evaluator sensitivity separately from target behavior. PAI-Bench offers a reproducible protocol for measuring factual availability, identity expression, and behavioral enactment as distinct aspects of identity-contract fidelity, though studies use single target samples per condition with post-hoc audits and follow-ups.

Abstract: Persistent agents need evaluations that distinguish identity facts they can recall from those they express and enact. We introduce PAI-Bench, a provider-neutral benchmark for fidelity to a versioned, update-governed identity contract. It separates recall, composition, behavioral enactment, resistance, persistence, lineage, and role-conditioned updates while keeping scoring oracles outside the target process. Two frozen campaigns cover sixteen synthetic profiles, thirty-two probes, and three independently initialized target configurations, yielding 1,536 retained responses. A judge-independent literal audit finds direct-parent identifiers in 48/48 atomic responses but only 1/48 implicit self-portraits. On eight profiles, explicit field cues increase joint presence of three identity identifiers from 0/8 to 7/8 under the same four-sentence instruction. A separate startup body-label substitution increases full-designation presence from 1/8 to 7/8 while parents remain absent. These contrasts reveal prompt-dependent component selection and component-specific sensitivity to startup cues in the tested deployments. Replaying identical factorial responses also yields a Claude headline mean 12.5 percentage points below Astra's, demonstrating evaluator sensitivity separately from target behavior. The studies use single target samples per condition, with post-hoc audits and follow-ups. PAI-Bench provides a reproducible evaluation protocol for measuring factual availability, identity expression, and behavioral enactment as distinct aspects of identity-contract fidelity.

</details>


### [115] [Safety as a Constraint: Fine-Tuning a LLM Recommender to Explain Itself](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13657)
*Jiashu He,Emma Yanyang Kong,JJ Tan,David Fagnan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The authors fine-tune a recommender LLM to generate personalized, faithful, and non-harmful explanations for its recommendations using two LLM-judge reward models and a novel constrained GRPO, raising the three-criteria PASS rate from 0.649 to 0.956 without degrading recommendation or language abilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional recommender systems predict the next item a user will interact with but not why, and offering personalized evidence for why a user might like an item enhances service quality and genuine user interest. Integrating a frontier-model call into the member-facing pipeline to provide such explanations adds extra cost and latency, motivating a single fine-tuned model instead.

Method: The authors train a recommender LLM to generate personalized explanations based on a user's watching history at a large video streaming service, imposing two requirements: faithfulness to the elements of the shows it links, and strict non-harmfulness to the user. They first train two LLM-judge reward models covering three specific criteria, then propose constrained GRPO to incorporate these different criteria.

Result: On a held-out real-world test set, the fine-tuned model raises the all-three-criteria PASS rate from 0.649 to 0.956 under their own judges and from 0.677 to 0.931 under an independent judge, whereas the frontier generator performs similarly to the untuned recommender baseline. Further experiments show the model's language and recommendation abilities remain unchanged.

Conclusion: An LLM-based recommender can be fine-tuned on other complex tasks without compromising its original recommendation performance, providing insights for further agentic user interfaces powered by a single model.

Abstract: Traditional recommender systems are typically trained to predict what item users will interact with next, but not why. However, offering personalized evidence for why a user might like the predicted item is an important way to enhance the service and to raise the likelihood that the user will be genuinely interested in the recommendation. This service can be delivered by integrating a frontier-model call into the member-facing pipeline, but it will add extra cost and latency. In this paper, we train a recommender LLM to generate personalized explanations for its reccomendation, based on the user's watching history at a large video streaming service. We impose two requirements on the generated explanation: it must be faithful to the elements of the shows it links, and it must be strictly non-harmful to the user. To this end, we first train two LLM-judge reward models covering three specific criteria, and propose constrained GRPO to incorporate these different criteria. On a held-out real-world testing set, our fine-tuned model improves the all-three-criteria PASS rate rises from 0.649 to 0.956 under our own judges and from 0.677 to 0.931 under an independent judge, where as the frontier generator performs similar to the untuned recommender baseline. We conduct further experiments to show that the model's language and recommendation abilities remain unchanged. Based on these results, we conclude that an LLM-based recommender can be fine-tuned on other complex tasks without compromising its original recommendation performance, thus provide insights for further agentic user interface powered by a single model.

</details>


### [116] [Cost Characterization of Vertically Partitioned Federated Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13664)
*Md Saikat Islam Khan Bappy,Oshani Seneviratne*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper formalizes vertical partitioning of federated knowledge graphs as a design space, compares four partitioning strategies across five metrics, and shows the design problem reduces to a trade-off between cross-silo path length and load balance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Knowledge graphs are increasingly distributed across autonomous organizations sharing an entity space but owning disjoint relation subsets (vertical partition). Multi-hop queries may need facts from several silos, so the partitioning strategy critically affects communication, indexing, load balance, and query latency. However, the costs of different partitioning strategies remain insufficiently studied.

Method: The authors formalize vertical partitioning as a design space and compare four strategies: semantic domain grouping, frequency-balanced partitioning, co-occurrence graph-cut partitioning, and random partitioning. They evaluate these using five metrics: communication cost, candidate index size, cross-silo path length, load balance, and end-to-end query latency. Experiments use MetaQA and PathQuestion with a fixed federated KGQA architecture based on TransE embeddings and a frozen BERT encoder, across three silo configurations, keeping the learning model unchanged to isolate partitioning effects.

Result: Three of the five metrics are determined by the graph and the silo count rather than by the partition, reducing the design problem to two conflicting axes: cross-silo path length and load balance. The trade-off between locality and balance holds only where each silo can hold several relations, and weakens as the number of silos increases.

Conclusion: Vertical partitioning design reduces to managing the tension between cross-silo path length and load balance, and this trade-off is only meaningful under moderate silo counts. The study offers practical guidance for deployments constrained by cross-silo reasoning or by silo load.

Abstract: Knowledge graphs are increasingly distributed across autonomous organizations that share an entity space but own disjoint subsets of relations, forming a vertical partition. Answering a multi-hop query may require combining facts from several silos, making the partitioning strategy a key data management decision that affects communication, indexing, load balance, and query latency. However, the costs associated with different partitioning strategies remain insufficiently studied. We formalize vertical partitioning as a design space and compare four strategies: semantic domain grouping, frequency-balanced partitioning, co-occurrence graph-cut partitioning, and random partitioning. We evaluate them using five metrics: communication cost, candidate index size, cross-silo path length, load balance, and end-to-end query latency. Three of the five prove to be determined by the graph and the silo count rather than by the partition, which reduces the design problem to two conflicting axes, cross-silo path length and load balance. Experiments on MetaQA and PathQuestion use a fixed federated knowledge graph question-answering architecture based on TransE embeddings and a frozen BERT encoder across three silo configurations. By keeping the learning model unchanged, we isolate the effect of partitioning and show that the trade-off between locality and balance holds only where each silo can hold several relations, weakening as the number of silos increases. The study provides practical guidance for deployments constrained by cross-silo reasoning or by silo load.

</details>


### [117] [GeoSkill:Experience-Driven Hierarchical Skill Learning with Collaborative Revision forGeospatialAgents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13667)
*Han Luo,Xian Xu,Yinhe Liu,Yanfei Zhong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GeoSkill is an experience-driven hierarchical skill learning framework for geospatial agents that distills historical execution traces into a Planning Skill Bank and a Tool Skill Bank, and revises them via a collaborative Judge-Critic-Refiner mechanism, improving task accuracy and tool reliability on EarthBench and ThinkGeo.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Geospatial agents increasingly handle recurring, evolving analytical tasks rather than isolated workflows, so they must distill prior execution experience into reusable procedural knowledge. Existing memory-augmented paradigms fail to summarize both long-horizon tool-chain orchestration experience and tool-level invocation constraints, and relying solely on LLM self-reflection causes misattribution and unreliable revisions that pollute memory.
