Method: GeoSkill has two components: (i) a Hierarchical Skill Bank (HSB) with a Planning Skill Bank (high-level task-planning experience) and a Tool Skill Bank (tool usage constraints), enabling structured representation and cross-task reuse; (ii) a Collaborative Trace-driven Skill Revision (CTSR) mechanism in which Judge, Critic, and Refiner jointly perform error identification, skill-level defect localization, and targeted modification. Skills are learned and validated from historical executions during development, then the bank is frozen for retrieval-only guidance on unseen tasks during deployment.

Result: Extensive experiments on EarthBench and ThinkGeo show that GeoSkill converts historical execution experience into reusable hierarchical skills, improving both end-to-end task accuracy and tool-execution reliability.

Conclusion: Hierarchical, structured skill distillation combined with collaborative, trace-driven revision effectively turns historical geospatial agent executions into reusable knowledge, yielding more accurate planning and more reliable tool use under a frozen retrieval-only deployment setting.

Abstract: Geospatial agents are increasingly expected to support recurring and evolving analytical tasks rather than execute isolated workflows. In such settings, effective agents must distill prior execution experience into reusable geospatial procedural knowledge to guide future planning and tool use. However, existing memory-augmented paradigms struggle to summarize both long-horizon tool-chain orchestration experience and tool-level invocation constraints in geospatial analysis, while directly relying on LLM self-reflection to update experience often leads to misattribution and unreliable revisions. To address these challenges, we propose GeoSkill, an experience-driven hierarchical skill learning framework for geospatial agents. GeoSkill comprises two core components: (i) a Hierarchical Skill Bank (HSB), consisting of a Planning Skill Bank and a Tool Skill Bank, which respectively distill high-level task-planning experience and tool usage constraints, enabling structured representation and cross-task reuse of historical execution experience; and (ii) a Collaborative Trace-driven Skill Revision (CTSR) mechanism, where Judge, Critic, and Refiner collaboratively perform error identification, skill-level defect localization, and targeted modification, preventing misattributed and unreliable revisions from polluting the skill bank. GeoSkill learns and validates skills from historical executions during development, and freezes the skill bank for retrieval-only guidance on unseen tasks during deployment. Extensive experiments on EarthBench and ThinkGeo demonstrate that GeoSkill effectively transforms historical execution experience into reusable hierarchical skills, improving both end-to-end task accuracy and tool-execution reliability in geospatial tasks.

</details>


### [118] [Enhancing Event Candidate Acquisition for Event Linking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13670)
*Ziyang Zhang,Yinan Liu,Boyi Xue,Yingxuan Huang,Bin Wang,Xiaochun Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MACE is a multi-agent candidate event acquisition method that refines event structure with specialized LLM agents before linking, consistently improving event linking accuracy on two benchmarks without altering the linking model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing event linking methods struggle with candidate event acquisition due to short ambiguous mentions, noisy arguments, and unevenly useful evidence.

Method: MACE employs evidence-specialized LLM agents to acquire time, location, participant, and event-type evidence, exposes intermediate queries to candidate-event lookup tools, and uses a coordinator to revise the evidence set before final candidate construction.

Result: Experiments on two event linking benchmarks show that adding MACE to different event linking models consistently improves accuracy.

Conclusion: MACE improves event linking through better candidate event acquisition without modifying the event linking model.

Abstract: Event linking associates event mentions in text with entries in a knowledge base (KB), or identifies them as out-of-KB events. Although existing methods use different architectures, candidate event acquisition can still be weakened by short ambiguous mentions, noisy arguments, and evidence that is unevenly useful for retrieval. We present MACE, a Multi-Agent Candidate Event acquisition method that refines event structure before linking. MACE uses evidence-specialized LLM agents to acquire time, location, participant, and event-type evidence, exposes intermediate queries to candidate-event lookup tools, and lets a coordinator revise the evidence set before final candidate construction. Experiments on two event linking benchmarks show that adding MACE to different event linking models consistently improves accuracy. These results show that MACE improves event linking through better candidate event acquisition without modifying the event linking model.

</details>


### [119] [Recoverability as a System Primitive for Long-Horizon AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13672)
*Zhihui Zhang,Wei Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces "recoverability" as a system primitive that turns resuming an interrupted AI agent into an explicit, evidence-bound decision rather than an automatic default, and shows that restoration accuracy and task success are insufficient signals for judging whether resumption is permissible.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI agents get interrupted mid-task, and naive resumption is risky: restarting wastes completed work, while continuing from unverified or stale state propagates earlier errors. A saved state is therefore not automatically a valid resume point. Existing evaluations rely on restored bytes and final task success, which can mask disallowed starting points.

Method: The authors define recoverability as an explicit decision to select a supported starting point and a permitted recovery action, or to withhold automatic continuation. They specify a behavioral contract tying this choice to supporting evidence, execution, and independent checks, and build a reference architecture connecting persistence, validation, and control, with complementary runtime instances that exercise distinct responsibilities. Evaluation uses four deterministic and 20 paired file challenges, plus progress controls and event-time tests.

Result: Accurate restoration and successful completion can conceal disallowed starting points. Progress controls attribute retained work to shared restoration. Event-time tests show that permission must also constrain the recovery action, and that independently held policy evidence can expose violations even after an effect has already occurred.

Conclusion: Recovery decisions require their own evaluation, beyond restored bytes and final task success. Within supplied policies and a declared trust model, the work provides a common, testable interface for retaining justified progress and making the conditions for its reuse explicit and enforceable.

Abstract: AI agents can be interrupted while editing files, calling tools, or carrying out multi-step tasks. Restarting repeats completed work, but continuing from unverified or outdated progress can carry earlier errors forward. A saved state is not necessarily a suitable place to resume. We introduce recoverability as a system primitive that makes reuse an explicit decision: select a supported starting point and a permitted recovery action, or withhold automatic continuation. Its behavioral contract binds that choice to supporting evidence, execution, and independent checks. A reference architecture connects persistence, validation, and control, with complementary runtime instances testing distinct responsibilities. Four deterministic and 20 paired file challenges demonstrate that accurate restoration and successful completion can conceal disallowed starting points. Progress controls attribute retained work to shared restoration. Event-time tests show that permission must also constrain the action, and that independently held policy evidence can expose violations even after an effect occurs. These findings establish why recovery decisions need their own evaluation, beyond restored bytes and final task success. Within supplied policies and a declared trust model, the contribution is a common, testable interface for retaining justified progress and making the conditions for its reuse explicit and enforceable.

</details>


### [120] [Windowed A-K-MDP](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13676)
*Xiangwen Yang,Frankie Cho,Iadine Chades*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Windowed A-K-MDP fixes a flaw in the A-K-MDP algorithm by evaluating all distinct feasible partitions within a divisor window, improving or tying A-K-MDP on all 33 tested K-MDP instances.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MDP-based decision support for biodiversity conservation produces policies that are hard for conservation managers to interpret, even in small state spaces. K-MDP methods mitigate this by building simpler MDPs with at most K abstract states, but the existing A-K-MDP algorithm, which selects a discretisation divisor via binary search, can skip better abstract states.

Method: The authors propose Windowed A-K-MDP, which generates every distinct feasible partition induced within a declared divisor window and evaluates candidates until reaching the ideal value loss (J = 0) or exhausting the family of candidates.

Result: Across 33 K-MDP instances, Windowed improved 25 and tied 8.

Conclusion: Avoiding binary-search-based divisor selection lets Windowed A-K-MDP explore the full family of candidate partitions in a window, never performing worse and often improving over A-K-MDP.

Abstract: Markov decision processes (MDPs) are used to support decision-making in conservation of biodiversity, but policies, even over small state spaces, can be difficult to interpret for conservation managers. K-MDP methods address this problem by building simpler MDPs with at most K abstract states. We show that the previously proposed A-K-MDP algorithm that relies on selecting a discretisation divisor using binary search can skip better abstract states. To fix this issue, we propose Windowed A-K-MDP, an algorithm that generates every distinct feasible partition induced within a declared divisor window and evaluates candidates until reaching the ideal value loss (J = 0) or exhausting the family of candidates. Across 33 K-MDP instances, Windowed improved 25 and tied 8.

</details>


### [121] [Drift-Constrained Optimization: Only Direction Matters in Fine-Tuning Instruct Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13680)
*Fei Yuan,Changjiang Gao,Yilei Tu,Yifeng Liu,Shujian Huang,Yu Qiao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper reconceptualizes fine-tuning as a direction-selection problem under a fixed "behavioral drift budget," and shows that a coarse layer-selective probe can find effective update directions that reverse QA-only fine-tuning failures, improving reasoning and translation while preserving general capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fine-tuning instruct models improves target performance but induces behavioral drift from the reference model, which can degrade existing capabilities. The authors argue drift should not be treated as an uncontrolled byproduct of optimization, but rather constrained by an explicit budget, and they ask how to maximize target-task performance within that budget.

Method: Behavioral drift is modeled as inducing a shared geometry anchored at the reference model, with the drift budget as a boundary. Within this space, drift fixes the distance from the reference, leaving update direction as the remaining degree of freedom, so fine-tuning becomes direction selection compared by "directional efficiency." The prediction that changing accessible directions alters outcomes is tested in a stringent QA-only setting (fine-tuning only on final answers while requiring multi-step reasoning at inference), using a coarse layer-selective probe.

Result: The layer-selective probe reverses the failure of QA-only fine-tuning and exposes effective directions, with multiple neighboring configurations improving target performance while preserving reasoning and general capabilities. Across Qwen3-8B and Qwen3-14B, these directions substantially improve scientific reasoning and multilingual translation; across 100+ languages the models match or outperform dedicated translation systems and provide a stronger initialization for subsequent reinforcement learning.

Conclusion: Fine-tuning effectiveness depends not merely on how much a model changes but on how that change is spent—i.e., the direction of updates—supporting the drift-budget/direction-selection framing as a principled way to improve transfer without sacrificing existing capabilities.

Abstract: Fine-tuning instruct models often improves target performance while inducing behavioral drift from the reference model, which can degrade existing capabilities. Rather than treating this drift as an uncontrolled consequence of optimization, we specify a behavioral drift budget before optimization and ask how to boost the target-task performance within it. Locally, behavioral drift induces a shared geometry anchored at the reference model, with the drift budget defining a boundary within this space. In this space, drift determines distance from the reference, leaving update direction as the remaining degree of freedom. Fine-tuning updates can therefore be compared through their directional efficiency, naturally reformulating fine-tuning as a direction-selection problem. This reformulation makes a concrete prediction: changing the accessible directions can qualitatively alter the outcome of fine-tuning. We test this prediction in a stringent QA-only setting, where strong instruct models are fine-tuned only on final answers but must still generate multi-step reasoning at inference. Despite this mismatch, a coarse layer-selective probe reverses the failure of QA-only fine-tuning and reveals the existence of effective directions, with multiple neighboring configurations improving target performance while preserving reasoning and general capabilities. Across Qwen3-8B and Qwen3-14B, these directions substantially improve scientific reasoning and multilingual translation. Over more than 100 languages, the resulting models match or outperform dedicated translation systems and provide a stronger initialization for subsequent reinforcement learning. Our results suggest that fine-tuning is not just about how much a model changes, but how that change is spent. https://github.com/CONE-MT/DCO and https://huggingface.co/collections/LLaMAX/dco

</details>


### [122] [Degraded but Not Entirely Ineffective: PE-Based Deformable Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13712)
*Jinhua Wu,Xinliang Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a Position Encoding-Based Deformable Spatial Aggregation Module (PEBDSAM) and its simplified variant PEBSAM that simultaneously address over-smoothing, over-compression, limited receptive fields, and noise on heterophilous graphs in GNNs, with diagnostic findings on offset failure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional GNNs transmit messages based on first-order neighbors and face several fundamental contradictions: increasing depth causes over-smoothing, long-range dependencies cause over-compression, fixed neighborhoods restrict the receptive field, and topological neighbors become noise on heterophilous graphs. Prior works address these issues individually, but few mechanisms alleviate all simultaneously.

Method: The authors propose PEBDSAM, using a deformable mechanism in position space to identify relevant nodes supplementing first-order neighbor information and enabling adaptation to heterophilous scenarios. Diagnostic experiments reveal current offsets have no effect; they analyze why offsets fail and why performance still improves despite failure. Based on this, they simplify PEBDSAM into PEBSAM, propose PEBSAM-Speed for large datasets, and make the module plug-and-play for GCN, GAT, GIN, and GraphSAGE.

Result: The plug-and-play module applied to GCN, GAT, GIN, and GraphSAGE achieves desirable results on three homophilous and six heterophilous graph datasets.

Conclusion: PEBDSAM and its simplified PEBSAM/PEBSAM-Speed variants provide a unified approach to alleviate over-smoothing, over-compression, restricted receptive fields, and heterophilous noise in a single step, with diagnostic insights pointing to future research directions.

Abstract: Many real-world scenarios can be represented using graph-structured data. However, traditional GNNs that transmit messages based on first-order neighbors have long faced several fundamental contradictions: increasing depth leads to over-smoothing, long-range dependencies cause over-compression, fixed neighborhoods restrict the receptive field, and on heterophilous graphs, topological neighbors become a source of noise. Although many works have addressed these issues individually, few mechanisms can simultaneously alleviate all of these challenges. To address the aforementioned problems, we propose a Position Encoding-Based Deformable Spatial Aggregation Module (PEBDSAM) that solves them all in one step. Specifically, we utilize a deformable mechanism in the position space to identify relevant nodes to supplement the original first-order neighbor information of GNNs, allowing traditional GNNs to adapt to heterophilous scenarios. Through diagnostic experiments, we obtained several major findings: current offsets fail to have any effect; subsequently, we analyzed the causes of offset failure and why model performance still improves even after offset failure, pointing out future research directions. Based on these diagnostic experiments, we streamlined the original PEBDSAM, resulting in a simplified version, which we call the Position Encoding-Based Spatial Aggregation Module (PEBSAM). In addition, we propose a PEBSAM-Speed to adapt to large datasets. Finally, we designed the module to be plug-and-play and applied it to GCN, GAT, GIN, and GraphSAGE, achieving desirable results on three homophilous datasets and six heterophilous graph datasets.

</details>


### [123] [MANAS-2: Constrained Reconstruction for EEG Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13717)
*Arvasu Kulkarni,Aditya Ray Mishra,Mahir Jain,Parshva Runwal,Lakshya Saini,Siddharth Panwar,Sandeep Singh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MANAS-2 combines a Raw-Band Hybrid masked autoencoder with ConRec, a physics-motivated regularizer on the temporal decoder, producing more spectrally organized and transferable EEG latents and outperforming leading EEG foundation models on most downstream tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Masked reconstruction is widely used for EEG foundation models, but optimizing reconstruction on low-SNR waveforms does not necessarily yield the most useful latent representation. The authors aim to shape representation quality rather than merely reconstruction quality.

Method: RBH masked autoencoder jointly reconstructs temporal waveform patches and compact spectral-band targets. ConRec acts only on the temporal decoder output, penalizing differences in RMS energy between adjacent short windows of the reconstructed waveform, biasing the encoder toward oscillatory-envelope organization. It is also applied to a temporal-only MAE to test architecture independence.

Result: Across seven held-out EEG datasets, adding ConRec to an identical RBH model raises frozen ridge recovery of six-band spectral power from mean R^2=0.860 to 0.906 and inter-patch band-energy dynamics from R^2=0.283 to 0.354, while temporal waveform information remains highly recoverable. On a temporal-only MAE, ConRec improves frozen downstream transfer and frequency-dependent latent geometry despite no spectral targets, indicating architecture-independent effects. MANAS-2 outperforms leading EEG foundation models on most downstream knowledge-transfer tasks.

Conclusion: A physically motivated constraint imposed through the decoder can yield a more spectrally organized and transferable latent space. MANAS-2 provides a new EEG foundation model built around constrained reconstruction as a mechanism for shaping representation quality rather than reconstruction quality.

Abstract: Masked reconstruction is widely used for EEG foundation models, but optimizing reconstruction on low-SNR waveforms does not necessarily produce the most useful latent representation. We introduce MANAS-2, a new EEG foundation model that combines a Raw-Band Hybrid (RBH) masked autoencoder with Constrained Reconstruction (ConRec), a physics-motivated regularizer. RBH jointly reconstructs temporal waveform patches and compact spectral-band targets, while ConRec acts only on the temporal decoder output, penalizing differences in RMS energy between adjacent short windows of the reconstructed waveform. ConRec is intended to shape the encoder by biasing it toward the organization of oscillatory-envelope information. Across seven held-out EEG datasets, adding ConRec to an otherwise identical RBH model increases frozen ridge recovery of six-band spectral power from mean R^2=0.860 to 0.906 and recovery of inter-patch band-energy dynamics from R^2=0.283 to 0.354, while temporal waveform information remains highly recoverable from the frozen latents. Applied to a temporal-only masked autoencoder, ConRec also improves frozen downstream transfer and frequency-dependent latent geometry despite receiving no spectral targets: i.e., the effects of ConRec are architecture-independent. MANAS-2 also outperforms leading EEG Foundation Models on most downstream knowledge-transfer tasks. From the effects of ConRec, we see that a physically motivated constraint imposed through the decoder can make for a more spectrally organized and transferable latent space. MANAS-2 therefore provides a new EEG foundation model built around constrained reconstruction as a mechanism for shaping representation--rather than reconstruction--quality.

</details>


### [124] [IBBench-Light: A Paired Evaluation of Task-Conditioned Responses to External Directives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13725)
*Kainan Zhou,Gangzhen Qian,Zhaoyi Li,Hang Xiao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: IBBench-Light introduces paired exact-contract accuracy (PECA) to benchmark whether instruction-tuned models correctly handle the same external record under both execute and process prompt roles, revealing that marginal per-task averages obscure paired failures and that stopping policy (EOS set) can dominate results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: External records can be consumed either as a procedure to execute or as text to read/process, depending on the user request. Standard instruction benchmarks report single-role accuracy and marginal averages, which can hide whether a model succeeds on both uses of the same record. The paper argues that conditional task and output-contract success for matched prompt pairs must be evaluated together.

Method: Twelve semantic bases are used to generate 144 matched prompt pairs per model (paired execute/process roles). Four quantized instruction models produce 1,152 archived greedy responses. PECA requires both members of a pair to satisfy their output contracts. The authors audit literal-target exposure and case normalization, then add 1,722 logged CPU generations to test directive-absent controls, twelve additional semantic bases, within-base wording changes, and generation stopping (EOS set). A pinned Phi rerun varies the EOS set, and a bounded IHEval comparison reuses the same SmolLM2 checkpoint and output budget while preserving published instruction roles and scorer.

Result: Qwen succeeds on 132 execute prompts and 109 process prompts, yet only 97 of 144 pairs are complete—demonstrating that marginal averages overstate paired reliability. In the pinned Phi rerun, changing the EOS set shifts exact paired success from 0/144 to 62/144, indicating that stopping policy can dominate measured performance.

Conclusion: The benchmark measures conditional task and output-contract success, so task margins and paired (PECA) counts should be read jointly with the stopping policy. Stopping configuration and paired evaluation are first-order factors in interpreting instruction-following results.

Abstract: An external record may contain a procedure to apply or text to read, depending on the user's request. IBBench-Light tests both uses against the same record. Twelve semantic bases yield 144 matched pairs per model; four quantized instruction models produced 1,152 archived greedy responses. Paired exact-contract accuracy (PECA) requires both members to satisfy their output contracts. Qwen succeeds on 132 execute and 109 process prompts, but only 97 complete pairs, showing what marginal averages omit. We audit literal-target exposure and case normalization, then add 1,722 logged CPU generations to test directive-absent controls, twelve additional semantic bases, within-base wording changes, and generation stopping. In the pinned Phi rerun, changing the end-of-sequence (EOS) set changes exact paired success from 0/144 to 62/144. A bounded IHEval comparison uses the same SmolLM2 checkpoint and output budget while preserving its published instruction roles and scorer. The benchmark measures conditional task and output-contract success. Its task margins and paired count need to be read together with the stopping policy.

</details>


### [125] [Trustworthy Agentic AI: A Comprehensive Cybersecurity and Systems Survey on Threat Landscapes, Defense Architectures, and Open Challenges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13731)
*Seyedakbar Mostafavi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This survey provides a systems-security reference framework for trustworthy agentic AI, formalizing agent architecture and a trustworthiness taxonomy, analyzing threat surfaces, and proposing a zero-trust defense-in-depth architecture mapped to governance standards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The shift from passive foundation models to autonomous, goal-directed agentic AI introduces severe security challenges: coupling recursive reasoning, persistent memory, live tool execution, and multi-agent collaboration grants probabilistic neural cores execution authority across filesystems, networks, and cloud infrastructure, dissolving classical security perimeters. Natural language acting simultaneously as data, control code, and protocol creates a Turing-complete blast radius where untrusted data becomes executable instructions.

Method: Synthesizes 206 foundational studies and regulatory standards. Formalizes the general agent architecture as a stateful 5-tuple and establishes a 6-dimensional trustworthiness taxonomy (security, safety, privacy, explainability, fairness, accountability). Systematically analyzes threat surfaces across intra-execution loops and interaction planes. Formulates a multi-layered zero-trust defense-in-depth architecture integrating Dual-LLM isolation, Capability-Based Access Control, kernel eBPF probes, and sandboxed runtimes. Reviews standardized evaluation benchmarks and maps technical controls to international AI governance frameworks.

Result: Delivers a comprehensive systems-security reference framework for trustworthy agentic AI, including a formalized architecture, a multidimensional trustworthiness taxonomy, a systematic threat-surface analysis, a proposed defense-in-depth architecture, benchmark review, and governance mapping.

Conclusion: The survey consolidates the security landscape of agentic AI into a unified framework, connecting formal architecture and taxonomy to concrete defense mechanisms and international governance, thereby serving as a reference for building trustworthy autonomous agent systems.

Abstract: The transition from passive foundation models to autonomous, goal-directed agentic AI systems has introduced unprecedented capabilities by coupling recursive cognitive reasoning loops, persistent memory architectures, live tool execution planes, and multi-agent collaboration topologies. However, granting probabilistic neural cores execution authority across filesystems, networks, and cloud infrastructure dissolves classical security perimeters: natural language simultaneously serves as input data, internal control code, and communication protocols, exposing a Turing-complete blast radius where untrusted data represents executable instructions. This survey delivers a comprehensive systems-security reference framework for trustworthy agentic AI, synthesizing 206 foundational studies and regulatory standards. We formalize the general agent architecture as a stateful 5-tuple and establish a 6-dimensional trustworthiness taxonomy covering security, safety, privacy, explainability, fairness, and accountability. We systematically analyze threat surfaces across intra-execution loops and interaction planes, formulate a multi-layered zero-trust defense-in-depth architecture integrating Dual-LLM isolation, Capability-Based Access Control, kernel eBPF probes, and sandboxed runtimes, review standardized evaluation benchmarks, and map technical controls to international AI governance frameworks.

</details>


### [126] [How Many Thoughts Can a Vector Hold? The Capacity of Reasoning by Superposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13747)
*Hongyu Gu,Chang Liu,Jingwen Fu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Cumulative superposition of full reasoning history can require fewer representational dimensions than frontier-only superposition, and uniform cumulative weighting is minimax-optimal for robust latent reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Continuous and recurrent latent reasoning methods superpose multiple alternatives into fixed-dimensional latent states, raising a key design question: what should continuous thoughts preserve as reasoning proceeds? An intuitive answer favors keeping only the current reasoning frontier.

Method: The paper theoretically analyzes superposition under identical downstream computations, comparing cumulative superposition (retaining full reasoning history) against frontier-only superposition (retaining only current alternatives), and studies how memories accumulated in latent states should be weighted when future use is unknown.

Result: Cumulative superposition can require lower representational dimensions than frontier-only superposition. At fixed hidden width, it lets latent reasoners retain more valid evidence, distinguish more plausible downstream outcomes, and delay the point where compressed states turn unreliable. Informative historical components reinforce each other coherently, while unrelated alternatives cause random interference. Prioritizing a small set of recent or salient items produces weakly-represented memories that bottleneck subsequent attention, whereas uniform cumulative weighting avoids this flaw and is proven minimax-optimal.

Conclusion: Superposition is reframed from an observed latent-space effect into a design principle: balanced cumulative memory allows a fixed representational budget to support more reliable, reusable computations.

Abstract: Large language models solve hard problems through intermediate computations across multi-step reasoning. Traditional chain-of-thought encodes these computations as tokens. Recent continuous and recurrent methods instead move partial computations into fixed-dimensional latent states, where a single thought can superpose multiple alternatives. This raises a fundamental design question:what should continuous thoughts preserve as reasoning proceeds? An intuitive approach discards past computations and keeps only the current reasoning frontier. Storing more items seems to dilute states and waste limited representational capacity. We show this intuition can be incorrect. Under identical downstream computations, cumulative superposition retaining full reasoning history can require lower representational dimensions than frontier-only superposition holding only current alternatives. At fixed hidden width, this advantage allows latent reasoners to retain more valid evidence, distinguish more plausible downstream outcomes, and delay the point where compressed states turn unreliable. This counter-intuitive effect emerges because informative historical components coherently reinforce each other, while unrelated alternatives bring random interference. This perspective also answers a practical design question: how should models weight memories accumulated inside latent states when their future use is unknown? Across reusable weighted superpositions, prioritizing a small set of recent or salient items produces weakly-represented memories that bottleneck subsequent attention. Uniform cumulative weighting avoids this flaw, and we prove it is minimax-optimal for robust future reasoning. Our results turn superposition from an observed latent-space effect into a design principle: balanced cumulative memory lets a fixed representational budget support more reliable, reusable computations.

</details>


### [127] [Positioning manuscripts in the scientific landscape with agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13760)
*Jiawen Chen,Zichen Zhang,Bingxuan Li,Quan Sun,Yiyan Zhang,Edric Tam,Jinjie Lin,Didong Li,Yun Li,Bingxin Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PASS, an agentic AI system, infers a manuscript's likely publication venue from its content and literature context, achieving 50.3% Top-1 and 86.1% Top-5 accuracy on a leakage-audited benchmark of 2,000+ preprints across 16 biomedical fields.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Publishing a manuscript is time-consuming, stressful, and uncertain in outcome. While LLM-based agentic AI has shown promise across scientific tasks, it remains unclear whether agentic AI can reliably help researchers navigate the publication process by predicting a manuscript's eventual venue from its content and literature context.

Method: The authors introduce PASS (Publication-oriented Agentic Scientific System), which situates each manuscript in its domain-specific literature context. PASS reconstructs the manuscript's local scientific neighborhood, traces its topic trajectory, and reasons over field-specific journal spaces to predict top-matched venues. It is evaluated on a leakage-audited benchmark of over 2,000 preprints spanning 16 biomedical fields, with ablation of input granularity and independent human evaluation.

Result: PASS achieved Top-1 accuracy of 50.3% and Top-5 accuracy of 86.1%, outperforming state-of-the-art LLM baselines and established journal-selection tools. Its quality scores (e.g., impact potential, novelty) aligned with independent measures of publication outcome. The literature retrieval module was the strongest performance contributor, especially for positioning manuscripts relative to nearby work. PASS retained near-full performance using only the abstract, whereas LLM baselines required the full manuscript text. Human evaluation showed strong researcher agreement with PASS's manuscript understanding and recommendation rationale.

Conclusion: Agentic AI can reliably infer a manuscript's eventual publication venue from its content and literature context, demonstrating practical utility for researcher guidance. PASS has been released as a public platform (https://ratemypaper.ai/) for broad researcher access.

Abstract: Publishing a research manuscript is a routine yet demanding part of scientific life: time-consuming, stressful, and often uncertain in outcome. Recent advances in large language model (LLM)-based agentic AI have shown promise across a range of scientific tasks, and here we ask whether agentic AI can help researchers navigate the publication process itself by reliably inferring a manuscript's eventual publication venue from its content and literature context. We introduce PASS (Publication-oriented Agentic Scientific System), an agentic system that understands manuscripts within their domain-specific literature context and predicts top-matched publication venues. PASS positions each manuscript within its surrounding literature landscape by reconstructing its local scientific neighborhood, tracing its topic trajectory, and reasoning over field-specific journal spaces. Evaluated on a leakage-audited benchmark of over 2,000 preprints across 16 biomedical fields, PASS achieved Top-1 accuracy of 50.3% and Top-5 accuracy of 86.1%, outperforming state-of-the-art LLM baselines and established journal-selection tools. PASS-produced quality scores, such as impact potential and novelty, aligned with independent measures of publication outcome. We also found that the designed literature retrieval module is the strongest performance contributor, particularly for positioning manuscripts relative to nearby work, and that PASS maintained near-full performance from the abstract alone, whereas LLM baselines required the full manuscript text. An independent human evaluation found strong researcher agreement with PASS's manuscript understanding and recommendation rationale. PASS has been released as a public platform (https://ratemypaper.ai/) for broad researcher access.

</details>


### [128] [Homeostatic Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13771)
*Yue Jin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper formulates a Continual Learning problem and proposes "Homeostatic Continual Learning," a method enabling an AI agent to learn continuously in a changing environment without catastrophic forgetting by detecting outliers in environment data when outliers appear in the agent's output.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable an AI agent to learn continuously in a dynamic environment without suffering catastrophic forgetting, and to gradually complete its model and policy so that it performs well across increasingly many contexts.

Method: The core of Homeostatic Continual Learning is to find outliers in the environment data when the agent experiences an outlier in its output. The agent thereby gradually completes its model and policy. The paper also suggests using the method to build a world model where the agent factorizes objects into features, abstracts objects into comparable instances of concepts, and maps concepts to intents through features.

Result: The agent gradually completes its model and policy and performs well in more and more contexts. The paper additionally discusses the works needed to render the method practical and its connections to many fields in AI.

Conclusion: Homeostatic Continual Learning offers a promising route to continual learning without catastrophic forgetting, with broader implications including the construction of a world model and connections to many AI fields.

Abstract: In this paper, I formulate a Continual Learning problem and propose a method named "Homeostatic Continual Learning" that enables an AI agent to learn continuously in a changing environment without catastrophic forgetting. The core of the method is to find outliers in the environment data when the agent experiences an outlier in its output. Through this method, the agent gradually completes its model and policy and performs well in more and more contexts. I also suggest that we may use the method to build a world model where the agent factorizes the objects in the world into features, abstract objects into comparable instances of concepts and map concepts to intents through features. I discuss the works needed to render the method practical, the connections to many fields in Artificial Intelligence and the broader implications of the method.

</details>


### [129] [Surprising Effectiveness of Self-Demonstrations in Enhancing Schema-Ontology Mapping with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13776)
*Siddhesh Thombre,Manasi Patwardhan,Sunita Sarawagi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a self-demonstration-driven, neuro-symbolic approach that decomposes schema-ontology mapping into cascaded sub-tasks and auto-generates pattern-guided demonstrations, achieving +25 F1 points over prior methods on the RODI benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Integrating heterogeneous relational databases into a centralized ontology is hard due to semantic heterogeneity, cryptic schema naming, missing metadata, and the abstraction gap between relational schemas and ontological models. LLMs have strong semantic reasoning but perform poorly on schema-ontology mapping when used via one-shot prompting or naive multi-stage pipelines.

Method: A self-demonstration-driven approach combining (i) neuro-symbolic task decomposition into cascaded sub-tasks, where symbolic constraints structure the search space and the LLM reasons semantically within each focused sub-task, and (ii) a novel mechanism that automatically generates pattern-guided, dependency-aware, domain-agnostic demonstrations to supervise each sub-task.

Result: On three of the most challenging scenarios from the RODI benchmark, the approach achieves state-of-the-art performance, substantially outperforming traditional schema-to-ontology mapping techniques and recent LLM-based schema-to-ontology and schema matching approaches, with 25 percentage points F1 improvement. Ablation studies show significant benefits from pattern-guided self-demonstrations and complementary benefits from neuro-symbolic task decomposition.

Conclusion: Combining neuro-symbolic task decomposition with self-generated, pattern-guided demonstrations substantially improves LLM-based schema-ontology mapping, yielding state-of-the-art results and validating both components via ablation.

Abstract: Integrating heterogeneous relational databases into a centralized ontology remains a persistent challenge in enterprise knowledge representation, primarily due to semantic heterogeneity, cryptic schema naming, missing metadata, and the abstraction gap between relational schemas and ontological models. Although large language models (LLMs) offer strong semantic reasoning capabilities, we show that directly applying them through one-shot prompting or naive multi-stage pipelines leads to poor performance for schema-ontology mapping. This paper presents a self-demonstration-driven approach that combines a neuro-symbolic task decomposition with a novel mechanism for automatically generating pattern-guided, dependency-aware demonstrations to address this integration challenge. Our approach incorporates two key strategies to achieve substantial accuracy gains over existing LLM-based schema integration methods: (i) a neuro-symbolic decomposition of the task into cascaded sub-tasks, where symbolic constraints structure the search space and LLMs perform semantic reasoning within each focused sub-task, and (ii) self-generated demonstrations guided by domain-agnostic patterns to supervise each sub-task. Experiments on three of the most challenging scenarios from the RODI benchmark show that our approach achieves state-of-the-art performance, substantially outperforming (25 percentage points F1 improvements) both traditional schema-to-ontology mapping techniques and recent LLM-based schema-to-ontology and schema matching approaches. Ablation studies further reveal the significant benefits of pattern-guided self-demonstrations and the complementary benefits of neuro-symbolic task decomposition.

</details>


### [130] [Partition Scores Are Not System Scores: Deployment-Fidelity Gaps in Decomposed Algorithm Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13785)
*Jiachen Zhang,Yu Tang,Li Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces the "deployment-fidelity gap" G(R) to quantify the difference between partition-level oracle utility and deployable end-to-end utility in decomposed algorithm selection, showing positive gaps across five benchmarks and sign-changing comparisons in 40% of decisions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Oracle-style quantities (VBS, selected-portfolio VBS, best-in-family summaries) are routinely reported as upper bounds on deployable selector performance. In decomposed algorithm selection, partition-level scores grant an oracle choice of the best algorithm within a selected family; once the family selector is fixed, this within-family oracle must be replaced by a learned selector. The paper argues that ignoring this substitution inflates reported utility and can mislead comparisons.

Method: The authors define the deployment-fidelity gap G(R) as the difference between partition-level and deployable end-to-end utility. They derive two accounting consequences: (1) a per-instance margin-regret stability condition indicating when a partition-time family choice is deployment-optimal, and (2) a sharp partition-only identification interval that, when it strictly crosses zero, prevents the partition-level report from certifying the deployable winner. They evaluate across five public algorithm-selection benchmarks covering tabular AutoML and combinatorial CSP/SAT, and propose a training-side validation gap-correction diagnostic.

Result: Every decomposed pipeline exhibits positive G(R), ranging from 0.012 on TabZilla to 0.13 on PROTEUS-2014. Four of ten decomposed-versus-flat decisions have sign-changing point estimates; on PROTEUS-2014, a 33-point partition advantage shrinks to a 20-point end-to-end advantage. The training-side validation gap-correction diagnostic recovers the point-estimate deployable sign on all four sign-changing cells.

Conclusion: Partition-level reports systematically overstate deployable utility in decomposed algorithm selection. The gap-correction diagnostic serves as a reporting aid, not a substitute for direct end-to-end evaluation. Partition and end-to-end scores should be reported side by side to avoid misleading conclusions about selector deployment.

Abstract: Oracle-style quantities, including virtual best solvers, selected-portfolio VBS, virtual-best encodings, and best-in-family summaries, are widely reported as upper bounds on what a deployable selector could achieve. In decomposed algorithm selection, an analogous partition-level score grants an oracle choice of the best algorithm within the selected family; once the family selector is fixed, the deployable system must replace that within-family oracle with a learned within-family selector.
  We define the deployment-fidelity gap G(R) as the difference between partition-level and deployable end-to-end utility and derive two accounting consequences: a per-instance margin-regret stability condition that tells us when a partition-time family choice is deployment-optimal, and a sharp partition-only identification interval that, when it strictly crosses zero, prevents the partition-level report from certifying the deployable winner.
  Across five public algorithm-selection benchmarks spanning tabular AutoML and combinatorial CSP/SAT, every decomposed pipeline has positive G(R), ranging from 0.012 on TabZilla to 0.13 on PROTEUS-2014. Four of ten decomposed-versus-flat decisions have sign-changing point estimates; on PROTEUS-2014, a 33-point partition advantage shrinks to a 20-point end-to-end advantage. A training-side validation gap-correction diagnostic recovers the point-estimate deployable sign on all four sign-changing cells; it is a reporting aid, not a substitute for direct end-to-end evaluation. Partition and end-to-end scores should be reported side by side.

</details>


### [131] [Do Not Restart: Residual Completion for Stateful Agent Handoffs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13800)
*Runzhi Deng,Yiming Zhong,Fang Zhao,Pan Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper formalizes commitment-constrained residual completion for stateful model handoffs and proposes Commitment-Frontier Residual Completion (CFRC), which preserves accepted choices and obligations while cutting inference cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Routing and cascades cut tool-agent cost by transferring control across models, but stateful handoffs risk losing accepted choices, realized effects, and unfinished obligations. This creates a need for a principled formulation that constrains handoffs to respect prior commitments.

Method: The authors frame the problem as commitment-constrained residual completion and introduce CFRC. CFRC enforces target-before-proposal, whole-proposal-before-authority, and live-evidence-before-success. It freezes a residual contract from accepted progress, closes the successor continuation into an evidence-linked graph, and admits execution only when the remainder is covered, with live receipts discharging obligations.

Result: The paper establishes contract-relative partial correctness, extending to the original residual request under complete contract construction. Across five environments and two same-provider model pairs, CFRC achieves macro accuracy comparable to strong full-task agents at only 22.0%-34.6% of their inference cost, with cross-provider results showing broader transfer.

Conclusion: CFRC provides a commitment-preserving, evidence-linked framework for residual completion in routed tool agents, matching strong full-task accuracy at substantially lower inference cost and transferring across providers.

Abstract: Routing and cascades reduce tool-agent cost by transferring control across models, but stateful handoffs must preserve accepted choices, realized effects, and unfinished obligations. We formulate this as commitment-constrained residual completion and introduce Commitment-Frontier Residual Completion (CFRC). CFRC enforces target-before-proposal, whole-proposal-before-authority, and live-evidence-before-success: it freezes a residual contract from accepted progress, closes the successor continuation into an evidence-linked graph, and admits execution only when the remainder is covered, with live receipts discharging obligations. We establish contract-relative partial correctness, which extends to the original residual request under complete contract construction. Across five environments and two same-provider model pairs, CFRC achieves comparable macro accuracy to strong full-task agents at only 22.0%-34.6% of their inference cost, with additional cross-provider results demonstrating broader transfer.

</details>


### [132] [LLM-Enhanced Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning for Unified Electric Vehicles-Charging Station-Grid Optimization in Public Charging Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13805)
*Yang Zhang,Lindong Xie,Chongyu Wang,Gaojunjie Li,Siqi Bu,Edward Chung*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces an LLM-enhanced MARL framework that unifies the optimization of grid, EV, and station interests for IoT-enabled EV charging scheduling, using LLM-driven interpretable feature selection and adaptive multi-objective weighting, achieving superior market efficiency and over 70% training time reduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Coordinating connected EV charging scheduling in IoT environments is a complex multi-objective problem balancing charging satisfaction, station profitability, and grid stability. Existing MARL approaches struggle with high-dimensional state spaces from massive IoT sensing data and conflicting stakeholder interests, and typically require manual tuning of objective weights. There is no existing unified loop that simultaneously optimizes Grid, EVs, and Stations.

Method: A novel LLM-enhanced MARL framework that jointly optimizes the Grid, EVs, and Stations in a unified loop. An LLM performs two roles: (1) interpretable feature selection by analyzing real-time IoT-collected environmental states to extract physically significant features; and (2) adaptive multi-objective balancing by dynamically assigning weights to conflicting objectives (profit, user satisfaction, grid load) via semantic reasoning rather than manual tuning.

Result: Extensive experiments show the framework significantly outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, achieving superior market efficiency while reducing training time by over 70%.

Conclusion: The proposed LLM-enhanced MARL framework offers a scalable, transparent solution for efficient and sustainable IoT-enabled urban EV charging infrastructure management, simultaneously addressing feature selection and multi-objective balancing bottlenecks.

Abstract: In the era of the Internet of Things (IoT), coordinating connected electric vehicle (EV) charging scheduling to balance EV charging satisfaction, station profitability, and smart grid stability presents a complex multi-objective challenge. Existing Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (MARL) approaches often struggle with high-dimensional state spaces generated by massive IoT sensing data and conflicting stakeholder interests. This paper proposes a novel LLM-enhanced MARL framework that, for the first time, simultaneously optimizes the Grid, EVs, and Stations within a unified loop. By integrating Large Language Model (LLM), we address two critical bottlenecks: interpretable feature selection and adaptive multi-objective balancing. The LLM analyzes real-time IoT-collected environmental states to extract physically significant features and dynamically assigns weights to conflicting objectives-including profit, user satisfaction, and grid load-using semantic reasoning instead of complex manual tuning. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our framework significantly outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, achieving superior market efficiency while reducing training time by over 70%. This approach offers a scalable, transparent solution for efficient and sustainable IoT-enabled urban charging infrastructure management.

</details>


### [133] [Bypass Observation: A Conceptual Design of a Non-Intrusive Layer-Wise Semantic Extraction Architecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13807)
*Haibin Tong,Jiang Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Bypass Observation is a proposed non-intrusive, layer-wise readout architecture that attaches read-only observation heads to selected Transformer layers to probe hidden-state reasoning without feeding outputs back into the backbone. The work is conceptual and analytical, deriving a closed-form overhead approximation for full-vocabulary readout and outlining cost-reduction strategies, while cautioning that these readouts are only partial and potentially misleading projections of hidden states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models perform reasoning in high-dimensional hidden-state spaces, but users can only observe final outputs. This opacity motivates a method to make internal model computation more observable without altering the model's underlying behavior.

Method: The paper introduces Bypass Observation, a non-intrusive layer-wise readout architecture that attaches read-only observation heads to selected Transformer layers, explicitly without feeding their outputs back into the backbone. Three variants are considered: a shared LM head across layers, layer-specific heads, and a layer- or step-adaptive head. For full-vocabulary readout, a closed-form overhead approximation is derived, governed primarily by V/(12d), with representative estimates ranging from about 30% to 240%. Cost reductions are discussed via sparse observation, low-rank factorization, reduced vocabularies, top-k readout, and selective positions. The work further distinguishes bypass chain-of-thought from conventional chain-of-thought: conventional reasoning tokens enter the autoregressive computation, whereas bypass readouts remain causally external at inference time, though they can still provide training signals in reinforcement learning. Applications to looped and recurrent-depth Transformers are also discussed, where iteration-wise readout may expose convergence, oscillation, and potential halting signals.

Result: A closed-form overhead approximation for full-vocabulary readout is derived, governed primarily by V/(12d), with representative overhead estimates ranging from about 30% to 240%. The analysis also identifies multiple avenues for cost reduction (sparse observation, low-rank factorization, reduced vocabularies, top-k readout, selective positions) and distinguishes bypass chain-of-thought from conventional chain-of-thought based on causal externality at inference time.

Conclusion: The authors argue that Bypass Observation can make model computation more observable, while emphasizing that it remains only a partial and potentially misleading projection of hidden states. The proposal is conceptual and analytical; systematic empirical validation remains future work.

Abstract: Large language models reason in high-dimensional hidden-state spaces, while users observe only final outputs. We introduce Bypass Observation, a non-intrusive layer-wise readout architecture that attaches read-only observation heads to selected Transformer layers without feeding their outputs back into the backbone. We consider three variants: a shared LM head across layers, layer-specific heads, and a layer- or step-adaptive head. For full-vocabulary readout, we derive a closed-form overhead approximation governed primarily by V/(12d), with representative estimates ranging from about 30% to 240%, and discuss cost reductions via sparse observation, low-rank factorization, reduced vocabularies, top-k readout, and selective positions. We argue that Bypass Observation can make model computation more observable while remaining only a partial, potentially misleading projection of hidden states. We further distinguish bypass chain-of-thought from conventional chain-of-thought: conventional reasoning tokens enter the autoregressive computation, whereas bypass readouts remain causally external at inference time, although they can still provide training signals in reinforcement learning. Finally, we discuss applications to looped and recurrent-depth Transformers, where iteration-wise readout may expose convergence, oscillation, and potential halting signals. The proposal is conceptual and analytical; systematic empirical validation remains future work.

</details>


### [134] [ViperQ: Order Flow Pattern Recognition via Auction Market Theory for Reinforcement Learning Trading](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13825)
*Asser Moustafa,Rares-Mihail Neagu,Jugal Kalita*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ViperQ is a reinforcement learning trading system whose state representation is built from Auction Market Theory primitives, achieving +163.6% ROI on TSLA and +116.5% ROI on NVDA on unseen institutional tick data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Academic RL trading systems overwhelmingly rely on price-aggregate representations (OHLCV bars) or limit-order-book depth features, leaving practitioner microstructure pattern theories—specifically Auction Market Theory and Market Profile—without a peer-reviewed computational instantiation.

Method: The state representation uses Auction Market Theory primitives: Volume Point of Control, Value Area position, Low Volume Node flags, Cumulative Volume Delta divergence, and tape-velocity signatures, assembled into a 20-dimensional Z-normalised vector. Two Proximal Policy Optimisation (PPO) agents are trained with a prospect theory-grounded asymmetric reward function that penalises losing holds with a magnitude consistent with Kahneman and Tversky's loss-aversion coefficient.

Result: On a held-out twelve-month partition of institutional tick data never seen by the agents, ViperQ achieves +163.6% ROI on TSLA (-27.5% max drawdown, 27,019 trades) and +116.5% ROI on NVDA (-47.8% max drawdown, 12,892 trades) under zero leverage.

Conclusion: Auction Market Theory features constitute a tractable structured input modality for sequential decision-making on financial time series, motivating further work on microstructure-aware policy learning.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning trading systems published in the academic literature overwhelmingly rely on price-aggregate state representations (OHLCV bars) or limit-order-book depth features, leaving microstructure pattern theories from the practitioner literature, namely Auction Market Theory and Market Profile, without a peer-reviewed computational instantiation. We present ViperQ, a reinforcement learning system whose state representation is built explicitly from Auction Market Theory primitives: Volume Point of Control, Value Area position, Low Volume Node flags, Cumulative Volume Delta divergence, and tape-velocity signatures, assembled into a 20-dimensional Z-normalised vector. Two Proximal Policy Optimisation agents are trained with a prospect theory-grounded asymmetric reward function that penalises losing holds at a magnitude consistent with Kahneman and Tversky's loss-aversion coefficient. Evaluated on a held-out twelve-month partition of institutional tick data the agents have never seen, ViperQ achieves +163.6% ROI on TSLA (-27.5% max drawdown, 27,019 trades) and +116.5% ROI on NVDA (-47.8% max drawdown, 12,892 trades) under zero leverage. The results establish Auction Market Theory features as a tractable structured input modality for sequential decision-making on financial time series and motivate further work on microstructure-aware policy learning.

</details>


### [135] [UniCAR-RL: Seeing Better before Thinking Deeper in Visual Mathematics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13849)
*Yuzhe Li,Hao Yan,Hao Wang,Xingchen Liu,Ya-Qi Yu,Jihao Wu,Minghui Liao,Wei Chen,Yuliang Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: UniCAR-RL is an annotation-free reinforcement learning framework that decouples perception and reasoning optimization to improve multimodal mathematical visual reasoning in MLLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MLLMs struggle with complex mathematical visual reasoning due to poor fine-grained perception, where initial visual hallucinations trigger cascading reasoning failures. End-to-end RL with sparse rewards cannot decouple perceptual hallucinations from logical errors, while perception-enhanced CoT fine-tuning is costly and hallucination-prone.

Method: UniCAR-RL explicitly decouples perception and reasoning during training via three synergistic branches: a Caption-RL branch optimizing perception through verifier-guided reasoning validation; a Reasoning-RL branch performing logical reasoning on a gold image description to halt cascading errors; and a QA-RL branch preserving native end-to-end alignment for robust question answering.

Result: Using only raw short-answer data, UniCAR-RL substantially improves MLLMs' mathematical and visual reasoning and shows strong generalization across diverse architectures and scales.

Conclusion: Decoupling perception and reasoning optimization via an annotation-free RL framework effectively addresses cascading failures in multimodal mathematical visual reasoning.

Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) often struggle with complex mathematical visual reasoning primarily due to a lack of fine-grained perception, causing initial visual hallucinations to directly trigger cascading reasoning failures. In traditional end-to-end reinforcement learning (RL), sparse rewards fail to decouple perceptual hallucinations from logical missteps, hindering targeted perception optimization. Alternatively, fine-tuning with perception-enhanced CoT data incurs high costs and hallucinations. In this paper, we address these challenges by proposing UniCAR-RL, an annotation-free RL framework. By explicitly decoupling the optimization of perception and reasoning during the training process, it achieves isolation and optimization of both capabilities. Specifically, UniCAR-RL consists of three synergistic branches: 1) a Caption-RL branch that optimizes perception capabilities through verifier-guided reasoning validation; 2) a Reasoning-RL branch that performs logical reasoning based on a gold image description to halt cascading errors; 3) a QA-RL branch that retains native end-to-end alignment to ensure robust question-answering performance. Experiments show that UniCAR-RL substantially improves MLLMs' mathematical and visual reasoning using only raw short-answer data. Furthermore, it demonstrates strong generalization across diverse architectures and scales.

</details>


### [136] [ClinAgent: A ReAct-Based Agent for Conversational Access to Clinical Trial Information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13860)
*Antonino Vaccarella,Riccardo Cantini,Domenico Talia,Paolo Trunfio,Marianna Talia,Rosamaria Lappano,Marcello Maggiolini*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ClinAgent is a conversational, agentic RAG system that lets clinicians and researchers query clinical trial information in natural language, using an LLM ReAct agent with integrated tools (ClinicalTrials.gov, PubMed, Python analyzer). Evaluated across three phases with three LLM backends, it shows complementary strengths—DeepSeek (thinking) leads in planning quality, while Gemini achieves the best overall performance and expert ratings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Querying clinical trial registries is manual and error-prone, requiring navigation of large volumes of semi-structured data without natural language interaction or cross-source synthesis support.

Method: ClinAgent employs an LLM agent following the ReAct paradigm that iteratively reasons over queries, selects among integrated tools, and refines actions based on intermediate outputs. Tools include a ClinicalTrials.gov search interface, a PubMed module, and a Python-based analyzer over a locally cached structured clinical trial dataset. Evaluation uses a three-phase framework assessing operational effectiveness, planning quality, tool-use efficiency, and expert qualitative judgments, comparing Gemini 3.0 Flash and two DeepSeek V3.2 variants (thinking and non-thinking).

Result: The systems exhibit complementary strengths: DeepSeek (thinking mode) excels in planning quality, while Gemini achieves the highest overall performance and strongest expert ratings.

Conclusion: Agentic AI systems show potential to improve accessibility and synthesis of clinical trial information, supporting more efficient and user-centered biomedical research workflows.

Abstract: Querying clinical trial registries remains a manual and error-prone process, requiring researchers to navigate large volumes of semi-structured data without support for natural language interaction or cross-source synthesis. To address this, we introduce ClinAgent, a conversational system based on agentic Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) that enables clinicians and researchers to query clinical trial information in plain language and receive grounded, up-to-date responses across multi-turn interactions. The system centers on a Large Language Model (LLM) agent following the ReAct paradigm, which iteratively reasons over queries, selects among a set of integrated tools, and refines its actions based on intermediate outputs. These tools include a ClinicalTrials.gov search interface, a PubMed module, and a Python-based analyzer operating on a locally cached structured dataset of clinical trials. We evaluate the system using a three-phase framework assessing operational effectiveness, planning quality, tool-use efficiency, and expert qualitative judgments, comparing three LLM backends: Gemini 3.0 Flash and two variants of DeepSeek V3.2 (thinking and non-thinking). Results reveal complementary strengths, with DeepSeek (thinking mode) excelling in planning quality, while Gemini achieves the highest overall performance and strongest expert ratings. Overall, our findings highlight the potential of agentic AI systems to improve the accessibility and synthesis of clinical trial information, supporting more efficient and user-centered biomedical research workflows.

</details>


### [137] [Map Users and Mapmakers: The Scope of Cognitive Attribution from Acquired Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13879)
*Yiling Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a five-part attribution table to specify precisely what enlargements of cognitive repertoire a given acquired representation supports, and argues that complete application competence does not determine acquisition capacity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: An acquired representation can enlarge a system's cognitive repertoire without transferring the capacities that produced it, so a framework is needed to specify that enlargement and its limits rather than over-attributing capacities via labels.

Method: A five-part attribution table distinguishing effective tracking, application of acquired structures, acquisition from explicit specifications, acquisition from identifying observations, and retention and reuse; each entry pairs a positive capacity commitment with a further claim requiring additional support. The argument grants meaningful content, causal efficacy, and productive inference, uses map and category examples, and includes short formal proofs in an appendix.

Result: Even complete application competence leaves acquisition capacity undetermined; acquiring a criterion from its description differs from finding it in examples. Applied to Ng's world-model interpretation of Othello-GPT and to contemporary indicators of machine concepts, the framework preserves demonstrated recognition, classification, inference, and qualified acquisition while marking criterion discovery and accumulation as unestablished.

Conclusion: The result concerns the scope of cognitive attribution rather than constitutive conditions of concept possession: cognitive achievements deserve credit for the capacities they establish, without silently importing a broader repertoire through attached labels.

Abstract: An acquired representation can enlarge a system's cognitive repertoire without transferring the capacities exercised in producing that representation. This paper develops a framework for specifying that enlargement and its limits. Its central contribution is a five-part attribution table distinguishing effective tracking, application of acquired structures, acquisition from explicit specifications, acquisition from identifying observations, and retention and reuse. Each entry identifies a positive capacity commitment and a further claim requiring additional support. The argument deliberately grants meaningful content, causal efficacy, and productive inference, so that its conclusion does not depend on treating representations as inert encodings. Map and category examples show why even complete application competence leaves acquisition capacity undetermined, and why acquiring a criterion from its description differs from finding it in examples. Short formal proofs appear in an appendix. The framework is applied to Andrew Ng's world-model interpretation of Othello-GPT and to the specific indicators discussed in contemporary accounts of machine concepts. It preserves demonstrated recognition, classification, inference, and qualified acquisition while specifying what remains unestablished about criterion discovery and accumulation. The result concerns the scope of cognitive attribution rather than the constitutive conditions of concept possession: cognitive achievements deserve credit for the capacities they establish, without silently importing a broader repertoire through the labels attached to them.

</details>


### [138] [LoRA Fine-Tuned Models for Control Systems Course Q\&A: A Multidimensional Evaluation of Model Scale and Rank Effects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13918)
*Shaowen Lu,Chengxu Liu,Ping Zhou,Tao Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LoRA fine-tuning of Qwen2.5-3B/7B-Instruct on 360 control-systems conversations improves answer similarity and structured-output stability, with 7B-r16 leading on ROUGE-L and BERTScore-F1.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: General-purpose LLM responses to control-systems questions tend to be inconsistently structured and hard to verify, as such questions demand coordinated terminology, notation, derivations, and stepwise explanations.

Method: Built a supervised fine-tuning dataset of 360 system-user-assistant conversations from Linear Control Systems exercises and reference solutions. Applied LoRA to Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, testing ranks r=4, 8, 16 under identical data splits, inference settings, and evaluation protocols. Evaluated with ROUGE, BERTScore, and structured-output features for Solution-Method-Teaching Points format stability.

Result: LoRA improved similarity and structured-output stability at both model sizes. 7B-r16 achieved the highest ROUGE-L (0.4093) and BERTScore-F1 (0.8643); r=8 gave a better performance-parameter efficiency trade-off. Bootstrap resampling yielded ROUGE-L gains of 0.0764 [0.0613, 0.0915] for 3B-r16 and 0.0874 [0.0687, 0.1042] for 7B-r16, both intervals exceeding zero.

Conclusion: LoRA can align open-source instruction-tuned models more closely with the language and pedagogical organization of course reference answers. However, the metrics capture textual similarity and formatting consistency rather than domain-specific reasoning or mathematical correctness, which require expert assessment and task-specific rubrics.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used in specialized university courses, but control-systems questions require coordinated terminology, notation, derivations, and stepwise explanations. Direct general-purpose responses may be inconsistently structured and hard to verify. Using exercises and reference solutions from a Linear Control Systems course, we built a supervised fine-tuning dataset of 360 system-user-assistant conversations. We applied LoRA to Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct. With identical data splits, inference settings, and evaluation protocols, we compared base and fine-tuned models and tested LoRA ranks r=4, 8, and 16. Evaluation used ROUGE, BERTScore, and structured-output features to measure reference-answer similarity and stability of the Solution-Method-Teaching Points format. LoRA improved both similarity and structured-output stability at both sizes. On the current test set, 7B-r16 achieved the highest ROUGE-L (0.4093) and BERTScore-F1 (0.8643), while r=8 offered a better balance between performance and parameter efficiency. Bootstrap resampling showed ROUGE-L gains of 0.0764 [0.0613, 0.0915] for 3B-r16 and 0.0874 [0.0687, 0.1042] for 7B-r16; both intervals exceeded zero, indicating stable textual-similarity improvements on the current test set. These results suggest LoRA can align open-source instruction-tuned models more closely with the language and pedagogical organization of course reference answers. However, the metrics mainly capture textual similarity and formatting consistency, not domain-specific reasoning or mathematical correctness, which require expert assessment and task-specific rubrics.

</details>


### [139] [SAILOR: Solver-Assisted Interactive LLM-based Optimization Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13945)
*Shaghayegh Sadeghi,Stephen L. Smith,David C. Del Rey Fern'andez*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SAILOR is a proof-of-concept system that detects missing numerical values in natural-language optimization problems, asks targeted follow-up questions prioritized by uncertainty and solver-derived impact, and updates the model before solving; it reaches 27.0–87.6% exact objective agreement on 1,723 masked benchmark instances with 1.4–5.7 questions per instance under idealized feedback.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Natural-language optimization descriptions are often incomplete or vague about numerical information a solver needs (costs, capacities, demands, bounds, penalties). An LLM translating such a description into code must either halt or guess when a required value is missing, motivating a system that identifies and resolves unsupported numerical choices interactively.

Method: SAILOR is a proof-of-concept pipeline that (1) detects unsupported numerical choices in the model generated from the description, (2) asks the user targeted follow-up questions, and (3) updates the optimization model before returning a solution. Questions are prioritized using uncertainty and solver-derived estimates of how strongly each missing value affects the current model. Evaluation uses 1,723 instances from seven masked benchmarks with an idealized simulator supplying ground-truth values.

Result: Exact objective-value agreement ranges from 27.0% to 87.6% across datasets, requiring 1.4–5.7 questions per instance on average.

Conclusion: The results establish feasibility of interactive numerical elicitation under controlled branch-and-reveal feedback. They do not measure performance with human users or general structural model repair. Code is available at https://github.com/sshaghayeghs/SAILOR.

Abstract: Natural-language descriptions of optimization problems may be incomplete or vague about numerical information that a solver requires, including costs, capacities, demands, bounds, and penalties. A language model can translate the description into code, but when a required value is absent it must either stop or guess. We present SAILOR, a proof-of-concept system that detects such unsupported numerical choices, asks the user targeted follow-up questions, and updates the optimization model before returning a solution. Questions are prioritized using uncertainty and solver-derived estimates of how strongly each missing value affects the current model. We evaluate the pipeline on 1,723 instances from seven masked benchmarks using an idealized simulator that returns ground-truth values. Exact objective-value agreement ranges from 27.0% to 87.6% across datasets, with 1.4--5.7 questions per instance on average. These results establish feasibility under controlled branch-and-reveal feedback; they do not measure performance with human users or general structural model repair. Code is available at: https://github.com/sshaghayeghs/SAILOR.

</details>


### [140] [Synthetic Data in Marketing Research: How to Evaluate and When to Trust](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13995)
*Oded Netzer,Rajan Sambandam*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that the debate over synthetic respondents should shift from whether they work to when they work, and proposes a typology of synthetic data, a taxonomy of accuracy measures, and an ex-ante answerability diagnostic (R² of a random forest predicting twin outputs) for twin-based augmentation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The literature is polarized between the claim that LLMs make human respondents obsolete and calls to avoid synthetic data entirely. Both positions obscure the more useful question of when synthetic respondents are valid. Reported twin accuracy varies widely (near-perfect to near-chance), which the authors attribute largely to differences in what is being measured rather than method quality.

Method: Building on Brand, Israeli, and Ngwe (2026), the authors (1) distinguish three types of synthetic data—ungrounded LLM responses, segment-level personas, and individual-level digital twins—and map each to the decisions it can support; (2) develop a taxonomy of four families of accuracy measures; and (3) introduce the "forgotten question problem," where a question is omitted from a fielded study, as a setting for twin-based augmentation.
