Because the prompts explicitly ask each model to imagine an architectural future, this resemblance raises a testable question: whether the overlap reflects exposure to related architectural concepts, a shared learned design prior, or independent convergence toward similar computational principles. The paper uses the term epistemic jailbreak for the accompanying loss of discipline in technical provenance as requested specificity increases. The experiments establish a repeatable behavioral pattern and do not authenticate proprietary implementation claims. What we leave to the community is a harder question: are these models independently imagining the same architectural future, or do such motifs somehow propagate between model families?

</details>


### [163] [Crypto Accounting Bench: Evaluating Frontier and Open-Weight Models on Crypto-Asset Accounting Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14811)
*Kareem Khattab,Omar Khattab,Mohamed Ibrahem*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CAB is a 118-task benchmark testing whether language models can reconstruct the exact journal entry posted for crypto-asset transactions; 12 models produce 4,248 trajectories, with the best model at 77.43% Mean Score and 56.78% Pass@3, revealing account selection and full-entry composition as the main difficulties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assessing whether frontier and open-weight language models can perform the complete accounting reconstruction of a real, posted crypto-asset journal entry, a task that requires integrating transaction mechanics, valuations, wallet and legal-entity context, counterparty evidence, related legs, recurrence, tax-lot evidence, and a full chart of accounts.

Method: CAB comprises 118 evaluation tasks from 7 pseudonymized organizations, each supplying transaction mechanics, asset quantities and base-currency values, wallet/legal-entity context, counterparty evidence, related transaction legs, recurrence, tax-lot evidence, and the complete chart of accounts. Models must output a balanced structured entry with all required accounts, sides, amounts, currencies, and full-precision asset quantities. Twelve proprietary and open-weight models are evaluated over 3 independent attempts per task (4,248 trajectories), scored via Mean Score, Best@3, and Pass@3, plus deterministic diagnostics read from each task's best-of-3 attempt and macro-averaged across models.

Result: The leading model achieves 77.43% Mean Score and the best Pass@3 is 56.78%. Diagnostics (macro-averaged across 12 models, from best-of-3 attempts) show 97.8% base-amount agreement versus 56.3% deciding-account accuracy.

Conclusion: Account selection and complete-entry composition remain the principal challenges on CAB; models handle base-amount computation well but struggle to choose correct deciding accounts and assemble fully balanced, complete journal entries.

Abstract: We introduce Crypto Accounting Bench (CAB), a benchmark for assessing whether frontier and open-weight language models can reconstruct the complete journal entry that an organization actually posted for a crypto-asset transaction. CAB contains 118 evaluation tasks drawn from 7 pseudonymized organizations. Each task combines transaction mechanics, asset quantities and base-currency values, wallet and legal-entity context, counterparty evidence, related transaction legs, recurrence, tax-lot evidence, and the organization's complete chart of accounts. The target is a balanced structured entry with every required account, side, amount, currency, and full-precision asset quantity. We evaluate 12 models spanning proprietary frontier systems and open-weight releases over 3 independent attempts per task, producing 4,248 trajectories. We report 3 metrics: Mean Score, Best@3, and Pass@3. Pass@3 is the fraction of tasks with at least 1 of 3 attempts that satisfies every rubric criterion and required gate. The leading model reaches 77.43% Mean Score, while the best Pass@3 is 56.78%. Deterministic diagnostics, read from each task's best of 3 attempts and macro-averaged across the 12 models, show higher base-amount agreement (97.8%) than deciding-account accuracy (56.3%). Together with the failure analysis, these results identify account selection and complete-entry composition as the main remaining challenges on CAB.

</details>


### [164] [ANASSA: An Agentic AI Orchestration Framework for Spatial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14824)
*Constantinos Papantoniou,Brian Hilton*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This study synthesizes advances in agentic GIS and proposes ANASSA, an architecture-level agentic AI orchestration framework for traceable, reproducible, and accountable geospatial workflows, spanning eleven components across four layers and a six-step Geospatial AI Cognitive Loop.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs and LMMs enable agentic systems that integrate natural language understanding with tool-based execution, transforming expert-driven GIS workflows into semiautonomous ones. However, existing approaches suffer from fragmented integration of reasoning, execution, and evaluation, especially in complex real-world environments, with limitations in spatial reasoning, execution robustness, validation, governance, and evaluation.

Method: The study synthesizes recent advances in agentic GIS frameworks, benchmarks, and surveys to identify limitations. It then introduces ANASSA (Autonomous Neural Agents for Spatial Systems Architecture), an agentic AI orchestration framework combining structured spatial reasoning, multi-agent workflow orchestration, execution feedback, authoritative spatial validation, provenance, uncertainty handling, and human decision authority in a unified design.

Result: The contribution is an architecture-level specification comprising eleven components across four layers, a six-step Geospatial AI Cognitive Loop, cross-component contracts, and governance mechanisms designed to make agentic geospatial workflows traceable, reproducible, and accountable. No empirical performance evaluation is reported; that is reserved for implementation and deployment studies.

Conclusion: ANASSA provides a unified architectural blueprint addressing fragmentation in reasoning, execution, and evaluation for agentic GIS, emphasizing traceability, reproducibility, accountability, and human decision authority. Empirical validation remains future work.

Abstract: The emergence of large language models (LLMs) and large multimodal models (LMMs) has enabled a new class of agentic systems capable of integrating natural language understanding with tool-based execution. In geographic information systems (GIS), this shift is transforming traditional, expert-driven workflows into semiautonomous systems that can interpret user intent, construct spatial workflows, and execute geospatial analysis tasks. However, existing approaches remain limited by fragmented integration of reasoning, execution, and evaluation, particularly in complex, real-world environments. This study synthesizes recent advances in agentic GIS frameworks, benchmarks, and surveys to identify limitations in spatial reasoning, execution robustness, validation, governance, and evaluation. Building on these insights, it introduces ANASSA (Autonomous Neural Agents for Spatial Systems Architecture), an agentic AI orchestration framework that integrates structured spatial reasoning, multi-agent workflow orchestration, execution feedback, authoritative spatial validation, provenance, uncertainty handling, and human decision authority within a unified system design. The contribution is an architecture-level specification: eleven components across four layers, a six-step Geospatial AI Cognitive Loop, cross-component contracts, and governance mechanisms intended to make agentic geospatial workflows traceable, reproducible, and accountable. Empirical performance evaluation is reserved for implementation and deployment studies.

</details>


### [165] [One Model, Two Physical Stories: Auditing Misalignment in Multi-Modal World Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14833)
*Geigh Zollicoffer,Minh Vu,Rajiv Ranasinghe,Manish Bhattarai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Multimodal world models can produce convincing but mutually inconsistent outputs; the authors formalize internal (video vs. text) and external (model vs. analytic physics) misalignment, build a physics-grounded comparison pipeline, and test whether supplying self-generated (A ladder) or corrected physical (B ladder) contracts closes these gaps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: World models are increasingly expected to generate multiple modalities simultaneously, but per-modality evaluation can hide cross-modal disagreement. A model may predict a rebound in text yet omit it in video, or diverge from true physical dynamics entirely. The paper aims to make these inconsistencies measurable rather than masked by separate benchmarking.

Method: The authors define two failure modes: Internal misalignment (disagreement between a world model's generated video and its own prediction in another modality) and external misalignment (disagreement between the world model's generation and an analytic physical environment). They derive shared contracts for event, magnitude, and timing, and construct a physics-grounded pipeline enabling measurable comparison in both settings. They then test ladder interventions: the A ladder injects the model's own contract (internal setting) and the B ladder injects a corrected physical contract (external setting).

Result: Across four mechanisms and 20 settings, the language modality answered all 22 text probes correctly with respect to the true environment, while the neutral video was frequently in disagreement.

Conclusion: Current unified backbones may not simultaneously achieve correct reasoning, internal consistency, and external physical fidelity, as evidenced by text-video disagreement despite accurate textual physical predictions.

Abstract: World models, systems that generate what happens next given current environmental conditions, are increasingly being implemented with multi-modal generation in mind. However, generating multiple modalities simultaneously, such as visual simulations alongside physical state predictions in the form of text, introduces the risk of cross-modal inconsistency. Tested separately, both outputs may look convincing while still disagreeing: a model can calculate that a ball should rebound in one modality, then generate no rebound in another modality, to say nothing of diverging from real-world dynamics entirely. In this work we focus on two failures explicitly: \emph{Internal misalignment}, the disagreement between the world model's generated video and the same world model's prediction in a different modalities, and \emph{external misalignment} the disagreement between the world model's generation and an analytic physical environment. We derive common contracts of event, magnitude, timing, and construct a physics grounded pipeline to make comparisons measurable in both external and internal settings. We then ask whether progressively supplying the model's own contract (the A ladder for the internal setting) or a corrected physical contract (the B ladder for the external setting) closes the respective gaps. Across four mechanisms and 20 settings, we find that while language answers all 22 text probes correctly with respect to the true environment, the neutral video is often in disagreement, suggesting that the current unified backbones may not be capable of correct reasoning, internal consistency, and external physical fidelity all at once.

</details>


### [166] [El Agente Potente: High-Throughput Agentic Atomistic Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14840)
*Tsz Wai Ko,Jiaru Bai,Thomas Swanick,Yeonghun Kang,Changhyeok Choi,Angelina Qihong Jiang,Aiwei Yin,Varinia Bernales,Alán Aspuru-Guzik*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: El Agente Potente is an agentic system for MLIP-driven atomistic simulations that couples typed execution graphs (provenance-aware, deterministic computation with LLMs limited to planning/routing) with a complementary coding mode for flexible, customized workflows, validated across materials discovery, molecular energy landscapes, adsorption, and catalysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Foundational MLIPs enable near-ab initio accuracy at reduced cost across large chemical spaces, but translating high-level scientific intent into adaptive, high-throughput simulation campaigns while preserving workflow rigour remains a central challenge.

Method: The system combines (1) typed execution graphs providing structured, provenance-aware execution for standardized workflows, where LLMs are restricted to planning and routing while deterministic Python components handle scientific computation and validation, and (2) a coding agent that constructs customized workflows for tasks needing greater procedural flexibility, reusing existing Potente functions for supported calculations.

Result: Demonstrated across computational materials discovery, molecular energy-landscape exploration, adsorption, and catalytic reaction workflows, accompanied by systematic benchmarks of reproducibility and LLM token cost.

Conclusion: Typed execution graphs and code-based workflow construction are complementary mechanisms for agentic scientific computing, jointly delivering controlled, auditable execution alongside the flexibility required for customized atomistic simulations.

Abstract: Foundational machine-learning interatomic potentials (MLIPs) are transforming atomistic simulations by achieving near-ab initio accuracy across large chemical spaces at a fraction of the computational cost. A central challenge in using these tools for high-throughput property calculations is translating high-level scientific intent into adaptive simulation campaigns without compromising workflow rigour. We introduce El Agente Potente, an agentic system that combines typed execution graphs with a complementary coding mode for MLIPs-driven atomistic simulations. Typed execution graphs provide structured and provenance-aware execution for standardized workflows, with large language models (LLMs) restricted to planning and routing while deterministic Python components perform scientific computation and validation. Complementing this structured execution, a coding agent constructs customized workflows for tasks requiring greater procedural flexibility while invoking existing Potente functions for supported calculations. We demonstrate El Agente Potente across computational materials discovery, molecular energy-landscape exploration, adsorption, and catalytic reaction workflows, together with systematic benchmarks of reproducibility and LLM token cost. These results establish typed execution graphs and code-based workflow construction as complementary mechanisms for agentic scientific computing, combining controlled, auditable execution with the flexibility required for customized atomistic simulations

</details>


### [167] [Self-Orchestrating Language Models: Leveraging Semantic Dependence for Efficient Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14850)
*Tian Jin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This thesis introduces self-orchestrating language models, which annotate semantic dependence between tokens during generation to let a runtime parallelize autoregressive decoding, evict intermediate context, or derive denoising orders, achieving Pareto-optimal quality-efficiency trade-offs via three systems: PASTA, TIP, and Planned Diffusion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM deployment faces major efficiency challenges: autoregressive decoding incurs high inference latency and under-utilizes accelerators at low batch sizes; discrete diffusion models parallelize generation but need many denoising steps to match autoregressive quality; and long-context reasoning creates memory bottlenecks that strain even top accelerators.

Method: The thesis proposes that models direct their own inference execution by annotating semantic dependence (which tokens depend on which others). For each system, a runtime acts on these annotations to parallelize decoding, evict intermediate context, or derive denoising orders. Three systems are developed: PASTA (annotates which output chunks can generate independently to parallelize autoregressive decoding), TIP (evicts intermediate reasoning steps from the KV cache to reduce memory while preserving accuracy), and Planned Diffusion (derives a denoising order for discrete diffusion by autoregressively generating a plan specifying which chunks to denoise in parallel).

Result: The approach is demonstrated through three self-orchestrating systems—PASTA, TIP, and Planned Diffusion—each achieving Pareto-optimal quality-efficiency trade-offs for their respective challenges (parallel decoding, memory reduction, and diffusion denoising order).

Conclusion: Language models can effectively direct their own inference execution strategy through semantic dependence annotations, enabling a unified principle that yields Pareto-optimal quality-efficiency trade-offs across parallel decoding, context eviction, and diffusion denoising.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) demonstrate impressive capabilities, but their deployment presents significant efficiency challenges. Autoregressive decoding imposes substantial inference latency and under-utilizes hardware accelerators in low batch size regimes. Discrete diffusion models can generate in parallel but struggle to match autoregressive quality without many diffusion denoising steps. Long-context reasoning creates memory bottlenecks that strain even state-of-the-art accelerators.
  My thesis is that language models can direct their own inference execution strategy by annotating semantic dependence -- which tokens depend on which others -- in their generation. I call such models self-orchestrating language models. For each system, I design a runtime that acts on these annotations to parallelize autoregressive decoding, evict intermediate context, or derive denoising orders, achieving Pareto-optimal quality-efficiency trade-offs.
  I demonstrate this approach through three self-orchestrating systems. First, PASTA uses semantic dependence to parallelize autoregressive decoding, training the model to annotate which output chunks can generate independently. Second, TIP uses semantic dependence to evict intermediate reasoning steps from the KV cache, reducing memory consumption while preserving accuracy. Third, Planned Diffusion uses semantic dependence to derive a denoising order for discrete diffusion, autoregressively generating a plan that specifies which chunks to denoise in parallel.

</details>


### [168] [Domain Generalization for Smartphone-Based Human Activity Recognition: A Systematic Analysis of Components and Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14863)
*Otávio Oliveira Napoli,Edson Borin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A large-scale controlled benchmark (410k+ experiments) of domain generalization for smartphone-based human activity recognition shows that individual DG components yield limited, conditional gains, while joint configurations often outperform their parts through complementary/super-additive interactions; robust model selection is a key bottleneck.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Smartphone-based HAR models degrade under distribution shifts from users, devices, placements, environments, and acquisition protocols. DG aims to learn from source domains without target data. Existing DG methods span training objectives, representation initialization, and architecture, but these are typically evaluated in isolation despite acting at different pipeline stages, leaving their interactions and combined utility unclear.

Method: The authors build a large-scale controlled benchmark of DG for smartphone HAR with 410,000+ experiments spanning four model architectures, thirteen training objectives (including ERM), five initialization strategies, four architectural configurations, and two shift scenarios (cross-dataset and cross-position). They systematically evaluate individual components, their joint configurations, class-level behavior, and oracle checkpoint analysis.

Result: Individual DG components give limited and highly conditional gains. Alternative objectives rarely beat ERM consistently; self-supervised initialization helps only in specific settings; architectural modifications, especially Dynamic Domain Generalization, give the clearest standalone improvements. Joint configurations frequently outperform individual components with complementary and sometimes super-additive interactions, though gains remain model- and shift-dependent. Class-level analysis shows the strongest configurations mainly improve difficult, shift-sensitive decision boundaries. Oracle checkpoint analysis reveals substantial unrealized performance: source-validation selection recovers only 53% (cross-dataset) and 26% (cross-position) of available oracle gain.

Conclusion: Effective domain generalization for smartphone-based HAR requires jointly designing DG components together with robust model-selection strategies, rather than optimizing components in isolation.

Abstract: Smartphone-based Human Activity Recognition (HAR) models often degrade under distribution shifts caused by changes in users, devices, sensor placements, environments, and acquisition protocols. Domain Generalization (DG) addresses this problem by learning from source domains without access to target data. Existing DG methods span training objectives, representation initialization, and architectural modifications, but these components are typically evaluated in isolation despite operating at different stages of the learning pipeline. We present a large-scale controlled benchmark of DG for smartphone-based HAR, comprising more than 410,000 experiments across four model architectures, thirteen training objectives including Empirical Risk Minimization (ERM), five initialization strategies, four architectural configurations, and two shift scenarios: cross-dataset and cross-position. Results show that individual DG components provide limited and highly conditional gains. Alternative objectives rarely outperform ERM consistently, self-supervised initialization helps in specific settings, and architectural modifications, particularly Dynamic Domain Generalization, provide the clearest standalone improvements. Joint configurations, however, frequently outperform their individual components and exhibit complementary and sometimes super-additive interactions, although gains remain model- and shift-dependent. Class-level analysis shows that the strongest configurations mainly improve difficult, shift-sensitive decision boundaries. Finally, oracle checkpoint analysis reveals substantial unrealized performance: source-validation selection recovers only 53% and 26% of the available oracle gain in cross-dataset and cross-position settings, respectively. Overall, effective HAR domain generalization requires jointly designing DG components and robust model-selection strategies.

</details>


### [169] [GGUF-Metadata Prediction of Single-Sequence llama.cpp Throughput Across Three Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14864)
*Xinyu Qiu,Chuhong Xu,Bo Su,Ziyao Chen,Ruiyang Xu,Shimeng Dai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A roofline-shaped, quantization-aware predictor estimates single-sequence LLM inference throughput (decode and prefill) directly from GGUF metadata, achieving 13–36% MAPE for decode by charging active rather than total parameters, though fitted efficiency coefficients do not transfer universally across hosts or runtime stacks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Throughput of single-sequence LLM inference is hard to predict without running the model, since it depends on quantization, active parameter count, and host/runtime characteristics. The paper aims to derive such predictions cheaply from GGUF file metadata alone, avoiding costly benchmarking, and to test whether models fitted on some systems generalize to unseen hosts and runtimes.

Method: Uses roofline-shaped predictors that incorporate quantization-specific scale factors, with coefficients fitted on reference models. Predictions are driven from GGUF metadata (structure plus quantization info). The evaluation cohort contains 318 phase-depth measurements from 53 host-file configurations across two Apple M4 Max systems and one NVIDIA RTX 5080. Comparisons include an active-parameter decode model versus one charging total parameters, a P2 prefill baseline, and leave-one-host-out coefficient transfer. A low-bit model ladder is used to probe ordering across runtime stacks.

Result: On host-specific held-out sets of four, five, and two configurations, the active-parameter decode model yields 13.1%, 14.4%, and 36.1% MAPE, versus 49.4%, 55.3%, and 51.9% when charging total parameters. Leave-one-host-out coefficients fitted on the other two systems give 11.6%, 16.8%, and 36.0% test MAPE. The P2 prefill baseline gives 18.7%, 22.2%, and 108.2% test MAPE. A low-bit model ladder changes ordering across runtime stacks. GGUF structure provides benefit on all three systems, but fitted efficiencies are not universal.

Conclusion: GGUF metadata is sufficient to predict single-sequence throughput with reasonable accuracy, and correctly attributing active (rather than total) parameters is the dominant factor for decode accuracy. However, fitted efficiency coefficients are host- and runtime-dependent and do not universally transfer, so predictors require per-host calibration and caution when ranking low-bit models across runtime stacks.

Abstract: We predict single-sequence model throughput from GGUF metadata using roofline-shaped predictors with quantization-specific scale factors fitted on reference models. The scored cohort comprises 318 phase-depth measurements from 53 host-file configurations on two Apple M4 Max systems and an NVIDIA RTX 5080. On host-specific held-out sets of four, five, and two configurations, an active-parameter decode model obtains 13.1%, 14.4%, and 36.1% mean absolute percentage error (MAPE), versus 49.4%, 55.3%, and 51.9% when charging total parameters. Leave-one-host-out coefficients fitted on the other two systems yield 11.6%, 16.8%, and 36.0% test MAPE. A low-bit model ladder changes ordering across runtime stacks. The P2 prefill baseline gives 18.7%, 22.2%, and 108.2% test MAPE. GGUF structure helps on all three systems, but fitted efficiencies are not universal.

</details>


### [170] [Externalizing Requirement-to-Repair Artifacts as Observable Traces for LLM-Based Program Repair](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14913)
*Zewen Tao,Shin-nosuke Ishikawa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: THEMIS is a stage-aware repository repair workflow that records and exposes the requirement-to-code transition (semantic interpretation, requirement-code graph, Developer guidance, retained rationale/patches, and post-edit audit). Auditing 300 SWE-bench Lite cases shows broadly complete inspectable artifacts (complete Developer rationale in 288 cases; complete audited field set in 214, 71.3%), measurable cross-stage symbol correspondence (62.6% in rationales, 62.8% in patches, 75.8% with related symbols), and preliminary workflow-level resolution gains (19 vs 9 in a paired 100-case comparison).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Repository-level repair demands not just correct patches, but inspectable records explaining how issue requirements are mapped into code changes and post-edit evidence. Existing workflows leave this requirement-to-repair transition implicit, preventing systematic cross-stage inspection and measurement of how repair decisions persist, align, and evolve.

Method: A stage-aware repair workflow (THEMIS) that externalizes the requirement-to-repair process via five components: semantic interpretation, a runtime requirement-code graph, graph-derived Developer guidance, retained repair rationale and patches, and post-edit audit records. Evaluation combines a retrospective audit of 300 SWE-bench Lite cases with a paired 100-case comparison against a direct same-input condition.

Result: Cross-stage inspection is broadly supported: complete Developer rationale for 288/300 cases and a complete audited field set spanning selected stages for 214 cases (71.3%). Cross-stage correspondence is measurable: target symbols recur in 62.6% of Developer rationales and 62.8% of patches, rising to 75.8% when related symbols are counted. In the paired 100-case comparison, the relational workflow resolves 19 cases versus 9 for the direct same-input condition.

Conclusion: THEMIS renders otherwise implicit requirement-to-repair transitions inspectable, enabling systematic examination of how repair decisions persist, align, and evolve across stages. The resolution improvement is reported only as preliminary, workflow-level evidence, not a causal effect of the graph component, since the conditions also differ in Analyzer output, graph-derived distillation, and Judge records.

Abstract: Repository-level repair requires not only correct patches but also inspectable records that explain how issue requirements are translated into code changes and post-edit evidence. We contribute THEMIS, a stage-aware repair workflow that externalizes this requirement-to-repair process through semantic interpretation, a runtime requirement-code graph, graph-derived Developer guidance, retained repair rationale and patches, and post-edit audit records. A retrospective audit of 300 SWE-bench Lite cases demonstrates that these artifacts provide broad support for cross-stage inspection: a complete Developer rationale is available for 288 cases, and 214 cases (71.3%) retain a complete audited field set connecting the selected stages. The retained records further enable systematic measurement of cross-stage correspondence: target symbols recur in 62.6% of Developer rationales and in 62.8% of patches, rising to 75.8% when related symbols are included. In a paired 100-case comparison, the relational workflow resolves 19 cases versus 9 for the direct same-input condition; because the two conditions also differ in Analyzer output, graph-derived distillation, and Judge records, we report this as preliminary, workflow-level evidence rather than a causal effect of the graph component. Together, these results show that THEMIS makes otherwise implicit requirement-to-repair transitions inspectable, enabling systematic examination of how repair decisions persist, align, and evolve across stages.

</details>


### [171] [Geometric Flow enhanced Graph Coarsening](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14962)
*Chaoqun Fei,Guoxuan Li,Tinglve Zhou,Chuanqing Wang,Yangyang Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RicciPool is a novel graph pooling method that leverages discrete Ollivier-Ricci curvature to capture higher-order local connectivity, reweights edges via an Ollivier-Ricci flow, and learns cluster assignments with spectral clustering, outperforming existing pooling methods on protein and social network datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing clustering-based graph pooling methods for GCNNs focus only on coarse topology and neglect higher-order mutual connections among neighbors, yet the ease of message passing on edges (reflecting node closeness) depends heavily on neighbor interconnectivity.

Method: The paper introduces discrete graph curvature, specifically Ollivier-Ricci curvature, as a measure of higher-order connectivity around an edge; constructs an Ollivier-Ricci flow formula to reweigh edge weights using Ricci curvature information; and then applies spectral clustering to learn a new cluster assignment matrix for pooling.

Result: Experiments on multiple bioinformatics protein datasets and social networks demonstrate the effectiveness of RicciPool compared to existing approaches.

Conclusion: Incorporating local higher-order connection information via Ollivier-Ricci curvature and flow improves graph pooling, yielding effective cluster extraction and validated performance gains.

Abstract: Recently, researchers have proposed a graph pooling operation, akin to the pooling process in conventional convolutional neural networks (CNN), aimed at reducing the computation cost of Graph convolutional neural networks (GCNNs). While most GCNN-based methods treat graph pooling as a node clustering problem and propose learning a cluster assignment matrix, existing clustering-based pooling methods tend to focus solely on the rough topology information of graphs, neglecting the exploitation of higher-order mutual connections among neighbors. In terms of message passing on graph, the ease of information passing on edges reflects the closeness between neighboring nodes, which significantly relies on the interconnectivity among neighbors. In this study, we address this gap by considering such local connection information and introducing a novel graph pooling method named RicciPool. We introduce discrete graph curvature, particularly Ollivier-Ricci curvature, as a measure of higher-order connectivity around an edge. Subsequently, we construct an Ollivier-Ricci flow formula to reweigh edge weights, leveraging the crucial information provided by Ricci curvature, particularly vital for extracting clusters in graphs. Building upon this foundation, we utilize the spectral clustering technique to learn a new cluster assignment matrix. Experimental results on multiple bioinformatics protein datasets and social networks underscore the effectiveness of our proposed method.

</details>


### [172] [Towards a knowledge-enhanced single-cell foundation model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14970)
*Hanqing Zhang,Jie Bao,Mei Ma,Shuai Liu,Jiaying Ma,Jiaguan Liu,Jiaxiao Li,Zhenbo Li,Wenwen Gong,Zhijun Ca*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: scKITE is a single-cell foundation model that integrates cell-annotation and gene-regulatory supervision into a shared Transformer encoder via lightweight auxiliary decoders used only during pretraining, achieving superior downstream performance with <0.5% of the pretraining samples used by prior strong scFMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Single-cell foundation models rely on large-scale transcriptomic pretraining, but scaling pretraining data yields diminishing gains while substantially increasing computational cost. The authors hypothesized that biological knowledge could serve as an additional scaling dimension beyond raw data size.

Method: Data scaling analyses showed that cell-level text annotation and gene-level regulatory information provide an additional scaling dimension. Based on this, scKITE integrates cell-annotation and gene-regulatory supervision into a shared transcriptomic Transformer encoder through lightweight auxiliary decoders, which are discarded after pretraining, yielding a general-purpose knowledge-enriched encoder.

Result: With only 179,067 pretraining samples—less than 0.5% of those used by previous strong scFMs—scKITE outperformed these models across diverse downstream tasks.

Conclusion: Knowledge-enhanced pretraining is a promising paradigm for biologically grounded single-cell foundation models, offering strong downstream performance at substantially reduced data and computational cost.

Abstract: Single-cell foundation models (scFMs) increasingly rely on large-scale transcriptomic pretraining, yet expanding pretraining data can yield diminishing gains while substantially increasing computational cost. Our data scaling analyses showed that incorporating biological knowledge, including cell-level text annotation and gene-level regulatory information, provided additional scaling dimension than simply increasing data size. Motivated by this observation, we present scKITE, a simple yet effective scFM that integrates cell-annotation and gene-regulatory supervision into a shared transcriptomic Transformer encoder through lightweight auxiliary decoders. These decoders are used only during pretraining and subsequently discarded, yielding a general-purpose encoder enriched with biological knowledge for downstream applications. With only 179,067 pretraining samples, i.e., less than 0.5\% of those used by previous strong scFMs, scKITE outperformed these models across diverse downstream tasks, highlighting knowledge-enhanced pretraining as a promising paradigm for biologically grounded scFMs.

</details>


### [173] [MemRiskBench: Trace-Aware Risk-Preserving Evaluation for Long-Horizon LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14976)
*Jianhua Jiang,Dongbo Yuan,Weihua Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MemRiskBench is a 120-episode scripted benchmark with a five-category risk taxonomy, deterministic trace-grounded checks, no LLM-as-judge on the pass/fail path, and a risk-preserving subset selector that retains full ranking, risk coverage, and high-risk detection at 20% subset size (5x compute reduction).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon LLM agents accumulate memory across sessions, producing sparse but high-impact risks: stale facts, conflicting updates, cross-user leakage, revoked-memory reuse, and constraint decay. Standard aggregate scores obscure per-risk failure rates (e.g., a model with 78% average accuracy may still leak data in 4% of episodes), and benchmark compression tends to discard rare high-severity events that distinguish mostly-working models from occasionally harmful ones.

Method: MemRiskBench comprises: (1) a five-category risk taxonomy plus one documented unscored category, operationalized by deterministic trace-grounded checks; (2) a 120-episode scripted benchmark with full trace logging and no LLM-as-judge on the pass/fail path; (3) evaluation on five locally run quantized instruction-tuned models; and (4) a risk-preserving subset selector—a coverage-constrained greedy selector on deterministic trace-derived features.

Result: The selector retains full ranking (Spearman rho = 0.975, deterministic; CI collapses to a point estimate with zero bootstrap variance), risk coverage (1.0), and high-risk model detection (1.0) at a 20% subset size, reducing compute 5x. Unlike ranking-only selectors, it additionally preserves risk-type coverage and high-risk detection using trace-grounded deterministic features without an LLM judge.

Conclusion: MemRiskBench supports reproducible evaluation and risk assessment of deployed LLM agents; all episodes, traces, the scoring implementation, and the selector are released.

Abstract: Long-horizon LLM agents accumulate memory across sessions, creating sparse but high-impact risks: stale facts, conflicting updates, cross-user leakage, revoked-memory reuse, and constraint decay. Standard aggregate scores hide per-risk failure rates--a model achieving 78% average accuracy may still leak data in 4% of episodes--and benchmark compression preferentially discards the rare high-severity events that distinguish a mostly-working model from one that occasionally causes harm. We present MemRiskBench. The primary contribution is a five-category risk taxonomy (plus one documented, unscored category) operationalized by deterministic trace grounded checks, instantiated as a 120-episode scripted benchmark with full trace logging and no LLM-as-judge on the pass/fail path, evaluated on five locally run quantized instruction-tuned models. Second, a risk-preserving subset selector: a coverage-constrained greedy selector on deterministic trace-derived features that retains full ranking (Spearman rho = 0.975, deterministic; CI collapses to a point estimate with zero bootstrap variance), risk coverage (1.0), and high-risk model detection (1.0) at a 20% subset size, reducing compute 5x. Unlike ranking-only subset selectors, this selector additionally preserves risk-type coverage and high-risk detection using trace-grounded deterministic features that do not require an LLM judge. All episodes, traces, the scoring implementation, and the selector are released to support reproducible evaluation and risk assessment of deployed LLM agents

</details>


### [174] [Converting Sequenced Fuzzy Cognitive Maps to Causal Virtual Worlds with Large Video Generators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14985)
*Akash Kumar Panda,Olaoluwa Adigun,Bart Kosko*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents an agent-based method that uses feedback fuzzy cognitive maps (FCMs) combined with LLM and large-video-model agents to construct causal virtual worlds and generate corresponding video scenes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable users and agents to create and manipulate causal virtual worlds with controllable, granular causal structure and dynamics, bridging causal modeling with generative video.

Method: Feedback FCMs model the granular causal structure and guide causal evolution; local causal rules are partial/fuzzy while feedback yields global equilibria defining causal scenarios. Dynamical meta-rules of form 'If A then B' define scenes: A perturbs the FCM per user/agent discretion, transient feedback dynamics define the implication arrow, and B is the resulting equilibrium attractor (limit cycle or fixed point). The algorithm extracts meta-rules from the FCM and guides an LLM agent to write a script; the video generator converts each meta-rule into a scene consistent with dynamics flow.

Result: Applied to a simple FCM describing an undersea world of dolphins and sharks; Google's Gemini 3.1 generated the script and Google's Veo 3.1 generated the dolphin-shark video.

Conclusion: The approach is general and scalable: mixing larger FCMs and AI agents can produce more immersive virtual worlds.

Abstract: We show how users can create and manipulate causal virtual worlds with large-language-model (LLM) and large-video-model agents. The approach uses feedback fuzzy cognitive maps (FCMs) both to model the granular causal structure of the virtual world and to guide its causal evolution. The local causal rules are partial or fuzzy while the FCM's feedback structure produces global equilibria that define causal scenarios. A sequence of \emph{dynamical} meta-rules of the form ``If $\mathcal{A}$ then $\mathcal{B}$" define the causal scenes of the virtual-world video. The if-part causal pattern $\mathcal{A}$ perturbs the FCM's virtual world at the user's or agent's discretion. The FCM's transient feedback dynamics define the meta-rule's causal arrow of implication. The then-part $\mathcal{B}$ is the resulting equilibrium attractor such as a FCM limit cycle or fixed point. Our algorithm extracts these meta-rules from the FCM and guides the LLM agent to write a script based on the FCM meta-rule sequence. The large video generator converts the meta-rule into a video scene in accord with the flow of the dynamics. We applied the agent-based technique to a simple FCM that describes an undersea world of dolphins and sharks. Google's Gemini 3.1 generated the script and Google's Veo 3.1 generated the dolphin-shark video. The approach is general and can scale by mixing larger FCMs and AI agents to produce more immersive virtual worlds.

</details>


### [175] [Shallow Beliefs: Synthetic document finetuning does not inoculate against emergent misalignment from reward hacking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14998)
*Arun Jose,Julian Stastny*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Synthetic document finetuning (SDF) during midtraining can make a model verbally approve of reward hacking, but it fails to prevent emergent misalignment (EM) when the model later learns to reward hack via RL—unlike inoculation prompting (IP), which blocks EM. SDF reliably steers generalization for new associations but has unpredictable effects when overriding existing ones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work shows RL-trained reward hacking generalizes to broad misalignment (EM), and that reframing reward hacking as acceptable during training (inoculation prompting, IP) blocks this generalization. The authors ask whether synthetic document finetuning (SDF) applied during midtraining—without intervening on the later training run—can similarly inoculate a model against future EM.

Method: Insert synthetic documents that frame reward hacking as acceptable into a model's midtraining corpus, then train these models with RL on exploitable environments that teach reward hacking. Compare behavioral belief measures and EM against an IP baseline in the same setting, and probe SDF's steering of downstream generalization for inserted vs. existing associations.

Result: Behaviorally, midtraining SDF succeeds: models describe reward hacking favorably and approve more of the reward-hacking outputs they produce. However, they still exhibit strong EM after learning to reward hack, whereas IP in the same setting prevents EM. SDF predictably steers downstream generalization when inserting new associations, but struggles with unpredictable effects when overriding existing associations such as reward hacking–misalignment.

Conclusion: At the scales tested, SDF can make a model appear aligned with desired beliefs while steering its generalization from later training in unintended ways. SDF is not a reliable substitute for IP for overriding established associations.

Abstract: Recent work shows that models that learn to reward hack on RL environments can become broadly misaligned, and that reframing reward hacking as acceptable behavior during training (inoculation prompting, or IP) blocks this generalization. We ask whether synthetic document finetuning (SDF) can inoculate a model against future training we don't intervene on. We add synthetic documents framing reward hacking as acceptable behavior to a model's midtraining corpus, and then train these models with RL on exploitable environments, teaching them to reward hack. Behaviorally, midtraining succeeds: models describe reward hacking favorably and are more approving of reward-hacking outputs they produce. However, they show strong EM after learning to reward hack, while IP in the same setting prevents EM. We show that SDF can predictably steer downstream generalization when inserting new associations, but struggles and has unpredictable effects when overriding existing associations, such as that between reward hacking and misalignment that produces EM. Our results suggest that, at the scales we test, SDF can make a model appear aligned with desired beliefs while steering its generalization from later training in unintended ways.

</details>


### [176] [CoMem: Collective-Individual Memory Synergy for Evolutionary Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15009)
*Chengxin Yu,Zhaoxin Fan,Faguo Wu,Hongwei Zheng,Yun Zhou,Zhiyu Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CoMem is a memory architecture for LLM-driven multi-agent systems that unifies private experience and shared knowledge, using sedimentation, curation, and dual-stream retrieval to avoid memory pollution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing memory mechanisms in LLM-driven multi-agent systems rely on flat, unstructured memories that become filled with noise and erase inter-agent differences. This limits agents' ability to learn together and improve over time despite strong cooperation skills.

Method: CoMem introduces the collective-individual memory synergy concept with three components: (i) Private Experience Sedimentation, allowing each agent to retain and update its own useful memories; (ii) Collective Wisdom Curation, selecting only widely proven ideas to share among agents; and (iii) Parallel Dual-Stream Retrieval, enabling agents to draw from both personal memory and group wisdom, with clustering to ensure diversity.

Result: Experiments on ALFWorld and PDDL benchmarks demonstrate that CoMem achieves strong overall performance and robustly avoids memory pollution.

Conclusion: CoMem effectively addresses memory pollution and preserves agent-specific differences by synergizing private and collective memory in multi-agent learning.

Abstract: Designing effective memory mechanisms is crucial for advancing LLM-driven Multi-Agent Systems (MAS), helping agents learn together and perform better over time. While recent work has led to strong cooperation skills, most methods still use flat, unstructured memories, which easily get filled with noise and erase differences between agents. To address this, we introduce the concept of collective-individual memory synergy and propose CoMem, an architecture that unifies both private experience and shared knowledge for multi-agent learning. CoMem features:(i) Private Experience Sedimentation, which lets each agent keep and update its own useful memories over time;(ii) Collective Wisdom Curation, which carefully selects only widely proven ideas to be shared among agents;(iii)Parallel Dual-Stream Retrieval, which allows agents to draw both from their own memory and the group's wisdom, using clustering to ensure diversity.Experiments on ALFWorld and PDDL benchmarks show that CoMem achieves strong overall performance and robustly avoids memory pollution.

</details>


### [177] [Semantic-TVM: Structure-Preserving Trustworthy Virtual Memory for Memory-Augmented and Tool-Using Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15011)
*Yu Li,Qikun Cai,Tao Huang,Chen Hou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TVM is a closed-loop runtime that keeps exact private values local and exposes only a protected view to remote LLMs; span-level protection (Semantic-TVM) retains far more task utility than whole-field replacement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Memory-augmented and tool-using agents risk leaking exact private values when remote LLMs process retrieved memory, tool actions, and intermediate observations. One-way masking prevents direct exposure but removes values essential for trusted execution and may still leak via later observations, creating a utility-privacy tradeoff.

Method: Trustworthy Virtual Memory (TVM), a single closed-loop runtime maintaining exact-value state locally while presenting a protected view to the remote model. Two variants: Rule-TVM replaces whole protected fields with locally recoverable handles; Semantic-TVM replaces only sensitive spans predicted by a trusted local model, retaining surrounding task-relevant context.

Result: On Memory-EHR and Memory-RAP across two providers, span-level projection recovers most of the EHR utility lost under whole-field replacement (Task Success 84.17% vs. 52.33% on DeepSeek), while measured exposure remains low and workflows remain executable.

Conclusion: Span-level, locally recoverable protection within a closed-loop runtime can substantially reduce the utility cost of privacy protection for memory-augmented and tool-using agents, achieving high task success with low measured exposure and preserved executability.

Abstract: Memory-augmented and tool-using agents expose exact private values when remote LLMs process retrieved memory, tool actions, and intermediate observations. One-way masking limits direct exposure but removes values needed for trusted execution and can leak them through later observations. We propose Trustworthy Virtual Memory (TVM), a closed-loop runtime that keeps exact-value state local while presenting a protected view to the remote model. Within this single runtime, Rule-TVM replaces whole protected fields with locally recoverable handles, and Semantic-TVM instead replaces only sensitive spans predicted by a trusted local model, preserving surrounding task-relevant context. On Memory-EHR and Memory-RAP across two providers, span-level projection recovers most of the EHR utility lost under whole-field replacement (Task Success 84.17% vs. 52.33% on DeepSeek) while measured exposure stays low and workflows remain executable.

</details>


### [178] [Overflip: Repetition-Induced Label Flips in Guardrail Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15013)
*Xu He,Chih-Hsuan Lin,Hung-Mao Chen,Junjie Xiong,Yan Zhai,Kun Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Lightweight guardrail models can be made to flip from malicious to benign predictions simply by repeating the prompt, an instability termed Overflip, affecting 5 of 9 tested models with flip rates of 8%--92%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Guardrail classifiers are assumed to give stable decisions as inputs lengthen, but this assumption may not hold. The authors investigate whether length growth, achieved through repetition, can destabilize guardrail predictions and enable malicious prompts to bypass screening while remaining semantically intact for downstream LLMs.

Method: The authors identify and characterize Overflip, a repetition-induced instability where repeating a prompt causes a guardrail's prediction to flip from MAL to BEN as the sequence grows. They evaluate 9 widely used lightweight guardrail models (e.g., DeBERTa-based, 512-token context with bucketed relative positional encodings) on a benchmark of 100 prompts, tracking confidence margins, flip rates, and first-flip token positions, and compare Overflip against traditional attention-dilution baselines via attention analysis.

Result: Five of the 9 models exhibit MAL-to-BEN flips on 100 prompts, with confidence margins shrinking steadily as repetition increases. Among vulnerable models, flip rates range from 8% to 92%, with first flips occurring at roughly 2.6k--9.4k tokens. Overflip differs from attention-dilution baselines: it preserves malicious content, homogenizes token-level attention over repeated structure, and produces a distinct, more gradual attention-dispersion trajectory than padding or shuffling.

Conclusion: Overflip exposes repetition as an attack surface for guardrail models and is more threatening than traditional attention dilution, since the bypassed prompt stays semantically intact and is still understood by downstream business LLMs, allowing malicious intent to pass through the guardrail. The findings motivate length-robust evaluation and mitigation for guardrail models.

Abstract: Guardrail models are classifiers deployed to screen malicious prompts and responses in LLM-based services. To meet latency constraints, many lightweight guardrails adopt compact Transformer backbones (e.g., DeBERTa) that are trained with short context windows (typically 512 tokens) and rely on bucketed relative positional encodings to process longer inputs. Prior evaluations assume that a guardrail's decision is stable as the input is lengthened. We show that this assumption can fail. We identify Overflip, a repetition-induced instability where repeating a prompt causes the guardrail's prediction to flip (MAL$\to$BEN) as the sequence grows. We conduct experiments on 9 widely used lightweight guardrail models. Five exhibit MAL$\to$BEN flips on a benchmark of 100 prompts, with confidence margins shrinking steadily with repetition. Among these vulnerable models, flip rates range from 8% to 92%, with first flips occurring at roughly 2.6k--9.4k tokens. Our analysis suggests Overflip differs from traditional attention-dilution baselines, which aim to divert the model's attention away from tokens associated with malicious content, shifting it instead toward unrelated content, such as benign padding or shuffling. While Overflip preserves malicious content, it homogenizes token-level attention over repeated structure and induces a distinct, more gradual attention-dispersion trajectory than padding. Moreover, Overflip poses a greater threat to LLM services than traditional attention dilution methods. Because the bypassed prompt remains semantically intact and is still readily understood by downstream business LLMs, it can transmit malicious intent after passing the guardrail. These findings expose repetition as an attack surface for guardrail models and motivate length-robust evaluation and mitigation.

</details>


### [179] [Four Ledgers, Not One Score: Responsible Communication of LLM-Judge Calibration in Biomedical ML](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15015)
*Sidi Chang,Peiying Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper audits a private synthetic Japanese care-handoff workflow to show that passing planted mutation keys into a generic detector scorer produces proposal-confirmation yield and submitted-ledger composition metrics, not judge precision and recall, because no independent detector realization was preserved. It contributes a provenance-aware claim audit, a storage contract, and a minimum calibration gate, while positioning the case as an existence proof of a failure mode rather than a prevalence estimate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Synthetic perturbations are promoted as inexpensive calibration data for LLM evaluators in biomedical ML where expert review is scarce. However, a planted mutation key is neither a detector output nor automatically human ground truth, creating a risk that evaluations mislabel ledger composition statistics as judge operating characteristics.

Method: The authors formalize four distinct ledgers—planted perturbations, independent detector outputs, source-linked human dispositions, and human-added discoveries—and audit the evaluation design, scoring code, read paths, and current human records of a private synthetic Japanese care-handoff workflow. They also examine storage/provenance artifacts, including source-name collisions, row shadowing, forced severity, vacuous ratio defaults, and unsupported zero-support field weights.

Result: The factory stored 69 planted error cards across 47 targets. Final review covered 22 targets containing 22 confirmed imported proposals, 9 rejected proposals, and 79 human-added cards; only 3 reviewed targets were double annotated. Passing imported plant keys to a generic detector scorer yielded 22/(22+9)=0.710 and 22/(22+79)=0.218. A direct audit identity shows these are proposal-confirmation yield and submitted-ledger composition, not judge precision and recall.

Conclusion: Existing human work supports an exploratory audit of synthetic proposals, but not claims about LLM-judge operating characteristics, clinical validity, corpus prevalence, or robust inter-annotator agreement. The single-workflow forensic case is an existence proof of a failure mode, not a prevalence estimate.

Abstract: Synthetic perturbations appear to offer inexpensive calibration data for LLM evaluators in biomedical ML, where expert review is scarce. Yet a planted mutation key is neither a detector output nor automatically human ground truth. We formalize four distinct ledgers: planted perturbations, independent detector outputs, source-linked human dispositions, and human-added discoveries. We then audit the evaluation design, scoring code, read paths, and current human records of a private synthetic Japanese care-handoff workflow. The factory stored 69 planted error cards across 47 targets. Final review covers 22 targets and contains 22 confirmed imported proposals, 9 rejected proposals, and 79 human-added cards; only 3 reviewed targets are double annotated. Passing imported plant keys to a generic detector scorer yields 22/(22+9)=0.710 and 22/(22+79)=0.218. A direct audit identity shows that these values are proposal-confirmation yield and submitted-ledger composition, not judge precision and recall, because no independent detector realization was preserved for the audited proposals in the available records. The audit also finds source-name collisions, row shadowing, forced severity, vacuous ratio defaults, and unsupported zero-support field weights. We contribute a provenance-aware claim audit, a storage contract, and a minimum calibration gate for responsibly communicating biomedical ML capability claims. This single-workflow forensic case is an existence proof of a failure mode, not an estimate of its prevalence: existing human work supports an exploratory audit of synthetic proposals, but not LLM-judge operating characteristics, clinical validity, corpus prevalence, or robust inter-annotator agreement.

</details>


### [180] [Horizon-specific Expert Fusion for Photovoltaic Power Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15035)
*Xu Yuqing,Zhou Liguo,Sun Ze,Yu Lei,Jiang Mingming*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This study proposes a hierarchical ensemble for short-term photovoltaic power forecasting that fuses temporal neural models, historical analogs, state climatology, and gradient-boosted trees via horizon-specific convex weights, with a separate bias-calibration step.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Short-term PV power forecasting must capture both regular solar cycles and weather-driven fluctuations, whose relative importance shifts with the forecast horizon, motivating a horizon-aware combination strategy rather than a single fixed model.

Method: A hierarchical ensemble combines temporal neural models, historical analogs, state climatology, and gradient-boosted trees. Solar geometry and numerical weather forecasts encode expected generation conditions; horizon-specific convex weights fuse complementary predictions; and a separate calibration step corrects recent bias using historical forecast errors.

Result: On public PVDAQ data at 15–240-minute horizons, the ensemble attains a daylight capacity-normalized MAE of 4.315%, a 4.11% reduction versus full-feature LightGBM and 6.03% versus fine-tuned Chronos-2 under identical calibration; expert-removal experiments reveal ensemble redundancy. Across three GEFCom2014 solar zones with three training seeds at hourly horizons up to four hours, learned fusion outperforms equal weighting but matches LightGBM.

Conclusion: Horizon-specific combination is a useful forecasting strategy, but its advantage over strong individual models is dataset- and evaluation-period-dependent.

Abstract: Short-term photovoltaic power forecasting requires models to represent regular solar cycles and weather-driven fluctuations whose importance changes with the forecast horizon. This study develops a hierarchical ensemble that combines temporal neural models, historical analogs, state climatology, and gradient-boosted trees. Solar geometry and numerical weather forecasts describe the expected generation conditions, while horizon-specific convex weights combine complementary predictions. A separate calibration step uses available historical forecast errors to account for recent bias. The framework is evaluated on public PVDAQ data at 15--240-minute horizons and on three GEFCom2014 solar zones at hourly horizons up to four hours. On PVDAQ, the ensemble achieves a daylight capacity-normalized mean absolute error of 4.315%, reducing error by 4.11% relative to full-feature LightGBM and by 6.03% relative to fine-tuned Chronos-2 under identical calibration. Expert-removal experiments identify redundancy within the ensemble. Across three training seeds on GEFCom2014, learned fusion improves upon equal weighting but performs comparably to LightGBM. The results support horizon-specific combination as a useful forecasting strategy while showing that its advantage over strong individual models depends on the dataset and evaluation period.

</details>


### [181] [The average-farmer illusion in language-model simulations of agricultural decisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15038)
*Zhanliang Zhu,Ziwei Li,Yuchen Liu,Liujun Zhu,Ruiqi Wu,Tongqing Shen,Junliang Jin,Jianyun Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper tests whether language-model agents used as synthetic survey respondents actually simulate individuals, and finds that population-level resemblance masks weak person-level prediction—an "average-farmer illusion."


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language-model agents are increasingly deployed as synthetic people in surveys and social simulations, but their realism is typically evaluated only via population averages or distributional similarity. The authors ask what such evidence actually establishes, motivated by the concern that aggregate matching may not imply genuine individual-level simulation.

Method: The authors compared Claude, Codex and Kimi under four prespecified prompt designs against matched farmer decisions from China and four African countries. They assessed person-level predictions, distributional coverage (including policy-relevant extremes), and compared model performance to a simple generator fitted only to the observed marginal distribution with no individual information. They also introduced a claim-matched validation framework and reusable modular prompts.

Result: Some configurations reproduced observed means and adoption rates, but person-level predictions were weak. Decisions clustered around typical values, and policy-relevant extremes were largely missing. A marginal-distribution generator with no farmer information achieved greater distributional similarity than every language-model configuration. Prompt additions yielded conditional rather than universal gains, varying with model, outcome, population and validation target.

Conclusion: Population-level resemblance should be treated as the beginning of validation, not as evidence of individual simulation. The authors propose a claim-matched validation framework and modular prompts to make prompt construction an auditable experimental process.

Abstract: Language-model agents are increasingly used as synthetic people in surveys and social simulations, yet their apparent realism is often judged from population averages or distributional similarity. We tested what such evidence actually establishes by comparing Claude, Codex and Kimi under four prespecified prompt designs with matched farmer decisions from China and four African countries. Some configurations reproduced observed means and adoption rates. However, their person-level predictions were weak; their decisions clustered around typical values and policy-relevant extremes were largely missing. Most strikingly, a simple generator fitted only to the observed marginal dis- tribution, and given no information about any farmer, achieved greater distributional similarity than every language-model configuration. Prompt additions produced conditional gains rather than uni- versal improvement: results varied with model, outcome, population and validation target. We call this the average-farmer illusion: a synthetic population can look realistic while failing to repro- duce who does what or how behaviour varies. We provide a claim-matched validation framework and reusable modular prompts that turn prompt construction into an auditable experimental process. Population-level resemblance should therefore be treated as the start of validation, not as evidence of individual simulation.

</details>


### [182] [BusMA: A Bus Communication Substrate for Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15054)
*Yanwen Peng,Delvin Ce Zhang,Xi Wang,Nikolaos Aletras*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BusMA is a communication framework inspired by computer bus architectures that lets any agent address specific peer agents via a shared channel, outperforming hierarchical and router-based multi-agent protocols across 13 tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-agent systems adopt Hierarchical Manager-Worker (HMW) or Router-based Message Passing (RMP) protocols, which restrict agent autonomy: workers cannot directly consult specific peers, and misrouted messages propagate errors. A more flexible communication structure is needed.

Method: BusMA introduces a shared channel (the Bus) with three components: agent registration, message routing, and shared memory management. Tool-equipped worker agents maintain local memory and can reason, act via tools, and communicate by posting shared messages with four intents—discussion, challenge, guidance, and request for explanation—enabling fine-grained peer-to-peer communication. A Chair agent monitors shared memory to coordinate interactions and drive convergence.

Result: Extensive experiments with two frontier LLMs across 13 tasks spanning visual reasoning, mathematical reasoning, and knowledge retrieval show that BusMA consistently outperforms state-of-the-art HMW and RMP methods.

Conclusion: A bus-style shared-communication architecture increases agent autonomy and communication flexibility in multi-agent systems, yielding consistent performance gains over hierarchical and router-based protocols.

Abstract: Multi-Agent (MA) systems are effective at solving complex tasks that demand planning, tool use, and the synthesis of evidence from multiple sources. Existing systems typically adopt Hierarchical Manager-Worker (HMW) or Router-based Message Passing (RMP) structures as their communication protocol. However, these designs restrict agent autonomy: Worker agents cannot directly consult specific "peers", and misrouted messages can propagate errors. Inspired by bus architectures in computer systems, we propose BusMA, a communication framework that allows any agent to address other agents through a shared channel, i.e., the Bus. It consists of agent registration, message routing, and shared memory management components. Worker agents, each equipped with tools, have their own local memory and can reason, act (tool usage), and communicate by posting shared messages with specific intents. We introduce four intents: discussion, challenge, guidance, and request for explanation, which support fine-grained communication among agents. A Chair agent monitors the shared memory to coordinate interactions and facilitate convergence among Workers. To evaluate the effectiveness of BusMA, we conduct extensive experiments with two frontier LLMs across 13 tasks spanning visual reasoning, mathematical reasoning, and knowledge retrieval demonstrate that BusMA consistently outperforms state-of-the-art HMW and RMP methods.

</details>


### [183] [Enabling Creative Exploration for Vibe Design Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15078)
*Yifan Zhang,Nghi D. Q. Bui,Georgios Evangelopoulos,Arnaud Benard*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes an inference architecture that separates design exploration from implementation by making design direction an explicit intermediate decision, using a pre-pass to propose structured design specifications with typicality scores, an external selector to sample one, and a fixed-setting downstream generator to realize it. Applied to UI themes and visual-asset prompts, this broadens selection coverage and screenshot variation, though effects on code export remain statistically uncertain.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vibe design agents convert natural-language briefs into rendered interfaces and frontend code, but a useful design agent should help users explore coherent alternatives rather than produce just one valid page. Increasing token-level temperature is a blunt approach because it simultaneously varies aesthetic decisions and syntax-sensitive code, conflating exploration with implementation.

Method: An inference architecture that makes design direction an explicit intermediate decision, inspired by Verbalized Sampling. A pre-pass proposes structured design specifications with typicality scores, an external selector samples one specification, and the downstream generator realizes the selected specification together with the original request under fixed settings. The approach is applied to UI themes and visual-asset prompts.

Result: Across 168 prompts, with 1,255 paired comparisons per temperature for each intervention, theme sampling broadens observed selection coverage and screenshot variation, while LLM-judge preferences vary across interventions, prompt complexity, and viewport. In an online experiment with more than 300,000 tasks, the observed code-export increase remains statistically uncertain, while fewer negative feedback events coexist with more correction interactions and modest operational costs.

Conclusion: Structured design specifications serve as a practical control point for exploring alternative UI concepts while keeping downstream generation settings fixed.

Abstract: Vibe design agents turn natural-language briefs into rendered interfaces and frontend code. Yet a useful design agent should do more than produce one valid page: it should help users explore coherent alternatives. Increasing token-level temperature is a blunt solution because it varies aesthetic decisions and syntax-sensitive code at the same time. We instead separate exploration from implementation through an inference architecture that makes design direction an explicit intermediate decision. Inspired by Verbalized Sampling, a pre-pass proposes structured design specifications with typicality scores, an external selector samples one, and the downstream generator realizes the selected specification together with the original request under fixed settings. We apply this approach to UI themes and visual-asset prompts. Across 168 prompts, with 1,255 paired comparisons per temperature for each intervention, theme sampling broadens observed selection coverage and screenshot variation, while LLM-judge preferences vary across interventions, prompt complexity, and viewport. In an online experiment with more than 300,000 tasks, the observed code-export increase remains statistically uncertain, while fewer negative feedback events coexist with more correction interactions and modest operational costs. Together, these findings identify structured design specifications as a practical control point for exploring alternative UI concepts while keeping downstream generation settings fixed.

</details>


### [184] [ER-EDF: A Psychology-Grounded Emotion Regulation Framework for Speech Empathetic Dialogue Generation in Large Audio-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15089)
*Hongyu Jin,Wenda Zhang,Runqiu Fei,Gongping Huang,Mike Conway,Ting Dang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes ER-EDF, a model-agnostic, psychology-grounded framework that explicitly decouples emotion perception from emotion regulation in large audio-language models (LALMs) for spoken empathetic dialogue, improving empathetic response quality over direct emotion-conditioning baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Empathetic response generation in spoken dialogue systems needs both accurate emotion perception and appropriate emotion regulation. Recent LALMs treat emotion as a direct conditioning signal without explicit regulatory mechanisms, leading to affect mirroring rather than calibrated support. Psychological theories (Perception-Action Model, emotion regulation theory) suggest effective empathy requires inferring the user's affective state and regulating how it is expressed.

Method: ER-EDF is a psychology-grounded, model-agnostic framework that decouples emotion perception (tracking the user's emotional state) from emotion regulation (determining how that state guides empathetic response generation). It integrates seamlessly into existing LALMs. The authors also construct a spoken empathetic dialogue dataset and introduce empathy-aware evaluation metrics beyond lexical matching.

Result: Experiments across five LALMs and two datasets show that ER-EDF consistently improves empathetic response quality in both automatic and human evaluations.

Conclusion: The results highlight the importance of jointly modeling emotion perception and regulation in spoken empathetic dialogue systems, paving a new direction for psychologically grounded empathetic AI.

Abstract: Empathetic response generation in spoken dialogue systems requires both accurate emotion perception and appropriate emotion regulation. Grounded in psychological theories such as the Perception-Action Model and emotion regulation theory, effective empathy depends not only on inferring a user's affective state but also on regulating how it is expressed in responses. However, recent large audio-language models (LALMs) largely treat emotion as a direct conditioning signal, lacking explicit regulatory mechanisms, which often leads to affect mirroring rather than calibrated support. We propose ER-EDF, a psychology-grounded framework that explicitly decouples emotion perception and emotion regulation in LALMs. Perception tracks the user's emotional state, while regulation determines how this state should guide empathetic response generation. The framework is model-agnostic and integrates seamlessly into existing LALMs. We further construct a spoken empathetic dialogue dataset and introduce empathy-aware evaluation metrics beyond lexical matching. Experiments across five LALMs and two datasets show that ER-EDF consistently improves empathetic response quality in both automatic and human evaluations, highlighting the importance of jointly modeling emotion perception and regulation in spoken empathetic dialogue systems, paving a new direction for psychologically grounded empathetic AI.

</details>


### [185] [OpenAl4S: Code as Action, Science as Sessions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15096)
*Gongbo Zhang,Hao Li,Yu Wang,Mujie Lin,Liuzhenghao Lv,Yicheng Mao,Yimi Wang,Jun Zhu,Minhan Tang,Zhengxiang Jiang,Yusong Wang,Jiayu Yao,Kunpeng Ning,Dawei Pang,Yonghong Tian,OpenAI4S Community,Yuyang Liu,Li Yuan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OpenAI4S is an open-source scientific research agent built on "Code as Action, Science as Sessions," combining a persistent execution runtime with session-level provenance to enable inspectable, resumable, and reproducible computational research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI co-scientists can accelerate computational research, but long-running studies require workflows that remain inspectable, resumable, and reproducible, which demands persistent computational state and provenance—capabilities largely missing in general-purpose coding harnesses.

Method: OpenAI4S couples a persistent computing runtime with research-session management: orchestration via structured tool calls, scientific actions as complete code cells run in persistent Python and R kernels. An append-only Action Ledger, per-cell execution records, versioned artifacts, environment records, and workspace checkpoints preserve provenance and support session recovery, branching, and extension. Configurable sandboxing, permission controls, and code/trajectory screening provide safeguards.

Result: Evaluated on 36 research scenarios (retrosynthesis, molecular dynamics, protein binder design, protein mutation, catalyst screening, mineral spectroscopy), measuring task accuracy, workflow completeness, and repository reproducibility. OpenAI4S scores 7.83 overall, versus 5.7–6.4 for a general-purpose coding harness with three frontier models, with largest gains on long-horizon, computation-intensive workflows.

Conclusion: Integrating persistent execution with session-level provenance improves the reliability of AI-assisted scientific workflows. However, environment specification and full rerunnability remain weak across all evaluated systems, including OpenAI4S, so reproducibility stays an open problem for scientific agents. Code is MIT-licensed.

Abstract: AI co-scientists could accelerate computational research, but over a long-running study the workflow also has to stay inspectable, resumable and reproducible, which requires persistent computational state and provenance. Here we present OpenAI4S, an open-source scientific research agent built around the principle of \emph{Code as Action, Science as Sessions}.
