OpenAI4S combines a persistent computing runtime with research-session management: orchestration is handled through structured tool calls, while scientific actions are represented as complete code cells executed in persistent Python and R kernels. An append-only Action Ledger, per-cell execution records, versioned artifacts, environment records, and workspace checkpoints preserve how results were produced and support session recovery, branching, and extension. Configurable sandboxing, permission controls, and code and trajectory screening provide complementary safeguards. We evaluate OpenAI4S on 36 research scenarios spanning retrosynthesis, molecular dynamics, protein binder design, protein mutation, catalyst screening, and mineral spectroscopy, measuring scientific task accuracy, workflow completeness, and reproducibility of the resulting repositories. OpenAI4S achieves an overall score of 7.83, compared with 5.7--6.4 for a general-purpose coding harness evaluated with three frontier models, with the largest gains on long-horizon and computation-intensive workflows. These results suggest that integrating persistent execution with session-level provenance can improve the reliability of AI-assisted scientific workflows. Environment specification and full rerunnability remain weak for every evaluated system, ours included, so reproducibility is still an open problem for scientific agents. The system is available under the MIT license at \href{https://github.com/PKU-YuanGroup/OpenAI4S}{github.com/PKU-YuanGroup/OpenAI4S}.

</details>


### [186] [Medical Knowledge Simplification for Patients in the Era of LLMs: A Case Study on Diabetes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15129)
*Pallika Kafle,Yipeng Zhou,Guanfeng Liu,Quan Z. Sheng,Cheng-Hsin Hsu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This case study introduces MediClear, an LLM-based medical knowledge simplification system enhanced with RAG, and evaluates it on diabetes knowledge. Indexing authoritative diabetes sources and simplifying them via an LLM, MediClear lowers reading levels into the recommended patient literacy range while achieving high user satisfaction and future-use willingness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Complex medical information is hard for patients to understand, and effective simplification is essential for patient comprehension, informed decision-making, and health outcomes. Although LLMs offer a promising simplification approach, their real-world effectiveness in patient education remains insufficiently explored through human evaluation.

Method: The authors implement and evaluate MediClear, an LLM-based medical knowledge simplification system enhanced with Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG). Public diabetes-related articles from Diabetes Australia, WHO, ADA, NIDDK, and AIHW are indexed in the RAG knowledge base to retrieve clinically grounded information, which the LLM then simplifies into accessible patient explanations. Evaluation combines standard readability metrics, including the Flesch-Kincaid Grade Level (FKGL), with a human study of 10 participants.

Result: MediClear consistently reduces the reading level of generated responses into the recommended patient literacy range while achieving high user satisfaction and willingness for future use.

Conclusion: This case study demonstrates the potential of LLMs to improve the accessibility of medical knowledge for patient education.

Abstract: Complex medical information is often difficult for patients to understand, making effective medical knowledge simplification essential for improving patient comprehension, informed decision-making, and health outcomes. Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) provide a promising approach for simplifying complex medical information into patient-friendly language; however, their effectiveness in real-world patient education remains insufficiently explored through human evaluation. To investigate their practical effectiveness, this paper presents a case study on diabetes knowledge simplification through the implementation and evaluation of MediClear, an LLM-based medical knowledge simplification system enhanced with Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG). Public diabetes-related articles from Diabetes Australia, WHO, American Diabetes Association (ADA), NIDDK, and AIHW are indexed in the RAG knowledge base to retrieve clinically grounded information, which is then simplified by the LLM into accessible patient explanations. We evaluate the generated responses using standard readability metrics, including the Flesch-Kincaid Grade Level (FKGL), and conduct a human study involving 10 participants. Results show that MediClear consistently reduces the reading level of generated responses to the recommended patient literacy range while achieving high user satisfaction and willingness for future use. This case study demonstrates the potential of LLMs to improve the accessibility of medical knowledge for patient education.

</details>


### [187] [HazardAuditor: From Executable Threats to Safer Computer-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15134)
*Yunhao Feng,Ruixiao Lin,Ming Wen,Yanming Guo,Xingjun Ma,Yutao Wu,Xinhao Deng,Shouling Ji*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HazardAuditor is an execution-grounded framework that supervises safety for computer-use agents by running heterogeneous agents in controlled environments, normalizing their interactions into a canonical event representation, and training a generative guard with Guard Policy Optimization (GuardPO).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Computer-use agents interact with browsers, terminals, file systems, and external services, creating safety risks that appear through runtime behavior rather than generated content. Existing guard models target static prompts and responses and are unsuitable for agent execution, while existing executable safety platforms output evaluation verdicts instead of the normalized supervision needed to train a guard across heterogeneous agent frameworks. Two gaps motivate the work: the lack of execution-grounded supervision and the structural mismatch in token-level post-training objectives for generative guards.

Method: The framework's infrastructure executes heterogeneous agents (Claude Code, Codex, Hermes, OpenClaw) in controlled environments and normalizes their interactions into a canonical event representation for cross-framework supervision. To address the token-level post-training mismatch in generative guards, where longer rationales dominate gradient updates, Guard Policy Optimization (GuardPO) converts deterministic safety outcomes into sequence-level advantages and normalizes rationale and verdict regions, making the safety decision the effective unit of optimization.

Result: Across multiple benchmarks and heterogeneous computer-use systems, HazardAuditor improves accuracy by up to 16.5 percentage points over the strongest prior guard. Code, models, and evaluation artifacts are stated to be released.

Conclusion: By combining execution-grounded, cross-framework event normalization with sequence-level guard optimization, HazardAuditor provides an effective supervision and training paradigm for safety guarding in computer-use agents, yielding substantial accuracy gains over prior guard models.

Abstract: Computer-use agents increasingly interact with browsers, terminals, file systems, and external services, introducing safety risks that emerge through runtime behavior rather than generated content alone. Existing guard models target static prompts and responses and are poorly suited to agent execution; existing executable safety platforms produce evaluation verdicts rather than the normalized supervision a guard model needs to learn across heterogeneous agent frameworks. We introduce HazardAuditor, an execution-grounded framework that closes both gaps. Its infrastructure runs heterogeneous agents (Claude Code, Codex, Hermes, and OpenClaw) in controlled environments and normalizes their interactions into a canonical event representation for cross-framework supervision. We further observe that token-level post-training objectives create a structural mismatch for generative guards, causing longer rationales to dominate gradient updates. Guard Policy Optimization (GuardPO) addresses this by converting deterministic safety outcomes into sequence-level advantages and normalizing rationale and verdict regions, making the safety decision the effective unit of optimization. Across multiple benchmarks and heterogeneous computer-use systems, HazardAuditor improves accuracy by up to 16.5 percentage points over the strongest prior guard. Code, models, and evaluation artifacts will be available at https://yunhao-feng.github.io/HazardAuditor/.

</details>


### [188] [T-LoopFormer: Token-Level Elastic-Depth Looped Transformers for Latent Reasoning With Dynamic Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15160)
*Mingqian Yu,Wenpeng Zhang,Peilin Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes T-LoopFormer, which adds dynamic token-choice routing and recursion-wise KV caching to looped Transformers, allowing each token to adaptively choose its recursion depth while keeping decoding efficient, achieving SOTA performance and low latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Looped Transformers reuse shared parameters across iterations and perform latent reasoning to gain parameter and sample efficiency, but they apply a fixed recursion depth to every token. This uniform depth leads to suboptimal compute allocation and leaves efficiency gains unrealized.

Method: Dynamic token-choice routing lets each token adaptively decide its number of loop iterations based on its hidden state, using a dynamic router that decides whether to continue recursing or exit early. Simple tokens bypass computation while hard tokens get deeper processing. A recursion-wise KV cache maintains an independent key-value cache per recursion loop so tokens at different depths attend only to corresponding cached states, eliminating redundant computation for exited tokens and enabling fast autoregressive decoding.

Result: T-LoopFormer reaches state-of-the-art performance under the same parameters on PPL and 10 zero-shot reasoning tasks, surpassing the base model at 24x FLOPs, and achieves the lowest inference latency, validating the token-choice router and recursion-wise KV cache.

Conclusion: Adaptive per-token recursion depth combined with recursion-wise KV caching effectively improves both performance and inference efficiency of looped Transformers.

Abstract: Looped Transformers have recently demonstrated strong performance in both reasoning and language tasks by reusing a shared set of parameters across multiple iterations, achieving parameter efficiency without sacrificing representational power. Besides, looped Transformers perform inference directly in the latent space (latent reasoning) to reduce the number of tokens consumed during inference, thereby achieving improved sample efficiency. However, these models typically apply a fixed recursion depth uniformly to every token, leading to suboptimal compute allocation and leaving significant efficiency gains on the table. In this work, we propose \textbf{dynamic token-choice routing} for looped transformers, enabling each token to adaptively determine its own number of loop iterations based on its hidden state. We use a dynamic router to decide whether a token should continue recursing or exit early, allowing simple tokens to bypass unnecessary computation while hard tokens receive deeper processing. To ensure that this adaptive mechanism does not compromise decoding efficiency, we further introduce recursion-wise KV caching, which maintains an independent key-value cache for each recursion loop. This design ensures that tokens at different depths only attend to their corresponding cached states, effectively eliminating redundant computations for exited tokens and enabling fast autoregressive decoding. Extensive experiments show that T-LoopFormer reaches the sota performance under the same parameters on PPL and 10 zero-shot reasoning tasks, even surpassing the base model at 24x FLOPs and our model could reach the lowest inference latency, which validate the effectiveness of token-choice router and recursion-wise KV cache. Code: https://github.com/YuMingQian1234/T-LoopFormer.

</details>


### [189] [STHMoE: Hypergraph-Enhanced Heterogeneous Dependency Coordination for LLM-Based Urban Traffic Data Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15172)
*Jiawen Chen,Qi Shao,Yongjian Chang,Mingtong Zhou,Duxin Chen,Wenwu Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: STHMoE is a hypergraph-enhanced mixture-of-experts framework built on a partially frozen LLM backbone that decouples traffic dynamics into frequency-, time-, spatio-, and higher-order spatial representations, using prompt-guided heterogeneous experts and an entropy-aware MoE router for urban traffic forecasting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spatio-temporal traffic forecasting involves heterogeneous, non-stationary, and structurally dynamic urban sensor streams. Existing deep learning and LLM-based methods are temporally centered and fail to coordinate temporal, spectral, pairwise spatial, and higher-order structural cues under evolving traffic regimes, motivating a heterogeneous dependency coordination solution.

Method: STHMoE decouples traffic dynamics into four representations (frequency-domain, time-domain, spatio-domain, higher-order spatial), modeled by prompt-guided heterogeneous experts on a partially frozen LLM backbone. The first three experts use domain-specific statistical prompts; the higher-order spatio expert uses a structural placeholder prompt fed by an adaptive hypergraph module. It jointly learns first-order graph dependencies and higher-order group interactions without predefined topologies. An entropy-aware MoE router with coefficient-of-variation load balancing adaptively fuses expert outputs, improving expert utilization and routing confidence.

Result: Experiments on 10 real-world traffic benchmarks demonstrate competitive performance against temporal, spatio-temporal graph, and LLM-based baselines.

Conclusion: STHMoE effectively addresses heterogeneous dependency coordination in traffic forecasting by combining prompt-guided heterogeneous experts, an adaptive hypergraph module, and an entropy-aware MoE router, achieving competitive results across 10 benchmarks.

Abstract: Spatio-temporal traffic forecasting is a fundamental big data analytics task for intelligent transportation systems, where massive urban sensor streams exhibit heterogeneous, non-stationary, and structurally dynamic patterns. Although recent deep learning and large language model (LLM)-based methods have advanced traffic forecasting, they often remain temporally centered and lack effective coordination of temporal, spectral, pairwise spatial, and higher-order structural cues under evolving traffic regimes. To address this heterogeneous dependency coordination problem, we propose STHMoE, a Spatio-Temporal Hypergraph-Enhanced Mixture of Experts framework for urban traffic data forecasting. STHMoE decouples traffic dynamics into frequency-domain, time-domain, spatio-domain, and higher-order spatial representations, which are modeled by prompt-guided heterogeneous experts built upon a partially frozen LLM backbone. The first three experts leverage domain-specific statistical prompts, while the higher-order spatio expert uses a structural placeholder prompt and obtains dependency information from an adaptive hypergraph module. To capture evolving spatial structures in traffic data,, STHMoE jointly learns first-order graph dependencies and higher-order group interactions without predefined topologies. An entropy-aware MoE router with coefficient-of-variation load balancing adaptively fuses expert outputs while improving expert utilization and routing confidence. Experiments on 10 real-world traffic benchmarks show that STHMoE achieves competitive performance against temporal, spatio-temporal graph, and LLM-based baselines.

</details>


### [190] [VisInteract: Towards Dynamic Interactive Text-to-Visualization under Imperfect Queries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15182)
*Wenxin Xu,Jinwei Lu,Hwanhee Kim,Chen Jason Zhang,Xiao-Yong Wei,Haoyang Li,Yuanfeng Song*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces VisInteract, a paradigm reframing Text-to-Visualization as interaction-driven intent recovery, alongside VisInteract-Bench (the first benchmark for dynamic interactive Text-to-Vis) and Vis-MCTS (an enhanced MCTS method with Progressive Widening, cross-rollout information sharing, and Dimension-Aware Reward Decomposition) that outperforms baselines by 13.40%–16.27%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world visualization requests are often ambiguous, incomplete, or factually incorrect, but existing Text-to-Vis systems assume well-specified inputs and generate charts in a single pass, lacking the ability to interactively recover user intent. No benchmark or method currently supports this dynamic interactive process.

Method: The authors propose VisInteract, reframing Text-to-Vis as interaction-driven intent recovery, and VisInteract-Bench, the first benchmark for dynamic interactive Text-to-Vis with controlled imperfection injection, a leakage-controlled User Agent for multi-turn feedback, and dual-perspective (code and chart) automated evaluation. Algorithmically, they introduce Vis-MCTS, an enhanced MCTS method featuring Progressive Widening (to handle the unbounded tool-argument space), cross-rollout information sharing (so clarifications and critiques benefit the whole search tree), and Dimension-Aware Reward Decomposition (routing scalar feedback along data-fidelity, visual-design, and intent-alignment dimensions).

Result: Extensive experiments across two LLM backbones show Vis-MCTS consistently outperforms all Text-to-Vis baselines, improving end-to-end task success by 13.40%–16.27% over the strongest interactive baseline and by more than 5× over non-interactive baselines.

Conclusion: VisInteract establishes interaction-driven intent recovery as a new paradigm for Text-to-Vis, and Vis-MCTS demonstrates its effectiveness, consistently surpassing all baselines and substantially improving end-to-end task success through dynamic multi-turn interaction.

Abstract: Real-world visualization requests are routinely ambiguous, incomplete, or factually incorrect, yet existing Text-to-Visualization (Text-to-Vis) systems assume well-specified inputs and produce charts in a single pass. When queries are imperfect, a system must \emph{interact} with the user to recover the true intent, but no benchmark or method supports this dynamic process. We introduce \textbf{VisInteract}, a new paradigm that reframes Text-to-Vis as interaction-driven intent recovery, and \textbf{VisInteract-Bench}, to our knowledge, that is the first benchmark for dynamic interactive Text-to-Vis, featuring controlled imperfection injection, a leakage-controlled User Agent for realistic multi-turn feedback, and dual-perspective (code and chart) automated evaluation. On the algorithmic side, we propose \textbf{Vis-MCTS}, a Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) enhanced method, introducing improvements over classical MCTS, that \emph{Progressive Widening} to tame the unbounded tool-argument space in tree search, \emph{cross-rollout information sharing} so clarifications and critiques benefit the entire search tree, and \emph{Dimension-Aware Reward Decomposition} that routes scalar user feedback along data-fidelity, visual-design, and intent-alignment dimensions to resolve credit assignment across heterogeneous actions. Extensive Experiments across two LLM backbones show that Vis-MCTS consistently outperforms all Text-to-Vis baselines, improving end-to-end task success by $13.40\%$--$16.27\%$ over the strongest interactive baseline and by more than $5\times$ over non-interactive ones.

</details>


### [191] [Issue Bias in Generative AI Writing Assistance: Political Issues and LLMs in the Swedish 2026 Election](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15207)
*Bastiaan Bruinsma,Annika Fredén,Paul Röttger,Moa Johansson,Asad Sayeed*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Six LLMs were tested across 148,302 Swedish-language election-related writing responses, showing no clear partisan preference overall, though Grok diverges most on certain issues and ChatGPT leans neutral.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generative AI writing assistants and their underlying LLMs increasingly influence how voters gather information before elections, so understanding the tools' views and positions is important.

Method: Crossed 107 policy propositions with 77 writing templates under neutral, positive, and negative framings, generating 24,717 prompts per model and 148,302 responses across six LLMs. Compared default stance tendencies, responses to similar issues, and alignment with Sweden's eight parliamentary parties.

Result: Claude, DeepSeek, Gemini, and Mistral showed similar profiles; ChatGPT more often produced neutral or ambivalent text; Grok diverged most on migration, crime, and gender. The Social Democrats were closest to all six models, but no within-model party-distance difference remained significant after multiple-comparison correction.

Conclusion: No model displays a clear preference or a clear preference for a party; stances depend on the specific issue or task presented.

Abstract: Generative AI writing assistants and the Large Language Models (LLMs) that power them are increasingly part of how voters gather information before elections. With growing evidence that they influence users' opinions, it is increasingly important to understand the views and positions of these tools. To better understand these views, we examine the stances supplied by six LLMs on a variety of Swedish-language writing tasks ahead of the 2026 Swedish parliamentary election. We cross 107 policy propositions with 77 writing templates and neutral, positive, and negative prompt framings, producing 24,717 prompts per model and 148,302 responses. To study these, we look at the models' default stance tendencies, compare how they respond to similar issues, and compare their responses with those of each of Sweden's eight parliamentary parties on the same issue. We find that Claude, DeepSeek, Gemini, and Mistral have similar profiles; ChatGPT more often supplies neutral or ambivalent text; and Grok differs most on topics such as migration, crime, and gender. When comparing the political parties, we find that the Social Democrats are closest to all six models. Still, after correcting for multiple comparisons, none of the within-model differences in party distances remains significant. Overall, we find that no model has a clear preference, nor a clear preference for a party, but that this depends on the specific issue or task the user asks about.

</details>


### [192] [From Ideas to Actions: A Public-Data Decision-Support Toolchain Across the Venture Lifecycle](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15219)
*Lei Qu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents an open, auditable public-data decision-support toolchain that helps founders decide whether to pursue an idea pre-founding and selects operating actions and capital partners post-founding, validated via an LLM-based pipeline and retrospective event-chain analysis of investment records.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Founders face two linked, high-stakes decisions—whether to pursue an idea before founding, and which operating actions and capital partners to choose afterward—yet these choices often lack structured, transparent decision-support grounded in public evidence.

Method: The toolchain combines time-bounded proposal profiling, market and moat checks, and deterministic aggregation for the pre-founding stage, alongside auditable investor-company EventChain data for retrospective analysis. Pre-founding validation uses threshold selection on 198 development companies with a frozen pipeline evaluated on a row-disjoint 198-company validation sample, a 396-row combined set, a 1,027-case scale cohort with post-stratification, and a 377-row composition-matched check. Post-founding analyses include an AI-inference study, a chip-company study, and a breakdown of confirmed visible post-investment actions.

Result: Pre-founding: the frozen pipeline achieves F0.5=0.5357 [0.412, 0.655] on the 198-company validation sample; on the combined 396 rows the Full Pipeline scores 0.6301 versus 0.2734 for a paired Raw LLM baseline. Post-stratification over 1,027 completed cases yields 0.6506 [0.598, 0.707] (run incomplete); the composition-matched check yields 0.6573. Post-founding: sustained product, customer, and supply-chain progress correlates with better outcomes, while financing alone does not establish operating progress and comprises 79% of confirmed visible post-investment actions. Evidence tentatively favors acquisition-experienced strategic corporate investors for acquisition-oriented founders and financing-led institutional VCs with fewer control events for independence-oriented founders. AI-inference identifies distribution-layer businesses as a replicable path to independent profitability with a limited revenue ceiling, and frontier-model ownership as a path to capital-market upside at exceptional capital cost.

Conclusion: Findings are developmental and observational, not causal guarantees or investment advice. The authors release a shared ontology, provenance-bearing EventChain data, schemas, benchmarks, and executable skills to support audit, reuse, and extension.

Abstract: Founders face two linked decisions: whether to pursue an idea before founding, and which operating actions and capital partners fit afterward. We present a public-data decision-support toolchain combining time-bounded proposal profiling, market and moat checks, and deterministic aggregation with auditable investor-company event chains for retrospective analysis. Pre-founding: (a) After threshold selection on 198 development companies, the frozen pipeline achieves F0.5=0.5357 [0.412, 0.655] on an independent, row-disjoint 198-company validation sample. On the combined 396 rows, the Full Pipeline scores 0.6301 versus 0.2734 for a paired Raw LLM baseline. Post-stratification of 1,027 completed cases in a separate scale cohort yields 0.6506 [0.598, 0.707]; the run remains incomplete. A 377-row composition-matched check yields 0.6573. (b) The AI-inference study identifies distribution-layer businesses as a replicable path to independent profitability with a limited revenue ceiling, and frontier-model ownership as a path to capital-market upside at exceptional capital cost. Post-founding: (a) Public sources support auditable event-chain analysis. (b) In the chip-company study, sustained product, customer, and supply-chain progress is associated with better observed outcomes; financing alone does not establish operating progress. (c) Financing comprises 79% of confirmed visible post-investment actions. Evidence tentatively favors acquisition-experienced strategic corporate investors for acquisition-oriented founders and financing-led institutional VCs with fewer observed control events for independence-oriented founders. Findings are developmental and observational, not causal guarantees or investment advice. We release shared ontology, provenance-bearing EventChain data, schemas, benchmarks, and executable skills for audit, reuse, and extension.

</details>


### [193] [CWM: Controllable White-Box Meta-Prompting for Adaptive Retrieval-Augmented Generation and Reasoning Ability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15234)
*Keuntae Kim,Eunhye Jeong,Yong Suk Choi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This work proposes Controllable White-Box Meta-Prompting (CWM), a unified, low-cost white-box framework for adaptive RAG and reasoning tasks, achieving state-of-the-art results across multiple LLMs without external decision modules or multi-sampling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs show strong reasoning and knowledge use, but most methods target individual tasks. Only limited work explores general-purpose, task-agnostic approaches, and adaptive RAG has been dominated by costly black-box methods.

Method: The authors present a unified framework integrating reasoning and RAG, and propose CWM, a low-cost white-box meta-prompting method for adaptive RAG that avoids external decision modules and multi-sampling, regulating retrieval decisions via internal model signals.

Result: CWM achieves state-of-the-art performance on three adaptive RAG benchmarks across GPT-oss-20b, Qwen3-14b, and Llama3.1-8b, and generalizes well to reasoning tasks, while enabling controllable retrieval decisions.

Conclusion: CWM offers an effective, general, and controllable white-box alternative for adaptive RAG and reasoning, providing controllability through manipulation of internal model signals, with code publicly available.

Abstract: Recently, Large Language Models (LLMs) have gained significant attention due to their strong language understanding and generation capabilities, demonstrating impressive reasoning abilities as well as effective utilization of external knowledge. Many studies have proposed methods that specialize in improving performance for individual tasks. However, ironically, only a limited number of attempts have explored general-purpose, task-agnostic methods. In this work, we present a unified framework integrating reasoning and Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) tasks. We further propose Controllable White-Box Meta-Prompting (CWM), a low-cost white-box method for adaptive RAG tasks previously dominated by black-box approaches, without requiring external decision modules or multi-sampling. CWM achieves state-of-the-art performance on three adaptive RAG benchmarks across recent LLMs, including GPT-oss-20b, Qwen3-14b, and Llama3.1-8b, while also demonstrating strong generality by extending to reasoning tasks. In addition, CWM provides controllability by enabling retrieval decisions to be regulated through the manipulation of internal model signals. Our code is available at https://github.com/JeongEunhye00/CWM.

</details>


### [194] [Empirical Evaluation of Open-Source Large Language Models for Retrieval-Augmented Generation in ESG Domain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15242)
*Motaz Saad,Anna Borrelli,Ivan Gentile,Kianna Kazemi,Francesco Piccialli,Antonella Longo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper evaluates seven open-source LLMs (2B–30B params) for automated ESG KPI extraction using a RAG pipeline over 498 EU-listed company ESG reports, benchmarked with 100 persona-based synthetic QA pairs across six RAGAS metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ESG reporting is critical for corporate accountability, and LLMs with RAG can automate KPI extraction. However, the performance of open-source LLMs in domain-specific ESG tasks remains insufficiently understood, leaving a gap in deployment guidance.

Method: A structured evaluation framework and resource built on 498 real-world ESG reports from EU-listed companies (2010–2024). Seven open-source models (2B–30B): glm-4.7-flash, nemotron-3-nano:4b, qwen3:4b-instruct, gemma3:4b, gemma4:e4b, gemma4:e2b, ministral-3:8b. Evaluated with 100 persona-based synthetic QA pairs covering ESG information needs, scored via RAGAS (contextual recall, precision, relevance, faithfulness, answer relevancy, factual correctness).

Result: Retrieval was strong and stable across models (context recall 0.58–0.61, precision 0.78–0.81, relevance 0.965–0.985). Generation diverged most on faithfulness (0.607–0.822) and least on answer relevancy (0.760–0.881). glm-4.7-flash led faithfulness (0.822), qwen3 factual correctness (0.449), and ministral-3 answer relevancy (0.881). Overall factual correctness remained low (0.387–0.449).

Conclusion: Open-source LLM performance in ESG tasks varies notably by architecture; strong retrieval but weak factual correctness indicates a need for domain-specific fine-tuning. The work offers data-driven guidance for deploying open-source models in ESG reporting.

Abstract: Environmental, Social, and Governance (ESG) reporting is critical for corporate accountability, with Large Language Models (LLMs) and Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) offering strong potential to automate KPI extraction. However, open-source LLM performance in domain-specific ESG tasks remains insufficiently understood. This paper evaluates open-source LLMs in ESG contexts using a structured framework and evaluation resource based on 498 real-world ESG reports from EU-listed companies (2010-2024).
  We evaluate seven open-source models (2B to 30B parameters) -- glm-4.7-flash, nemotron-3-nano:4b, qwen3:4b-instruct, gemma3:4b, gemma4:e4b, gemma4:e2b, and ministral-3:8b -- using 100 persona-based synthetic QA pairs covering ESG information needs. System performance is assessed via RAGAS metrics, including contextual recall, precision, relevance, faithfulness, answer relevancy, and factual correctness.
  Results show notable performance variations across architectures. Retrieval performance is strong across models (context recall around 0.58-0.61, context precision around 0.78-0.81, context relevance 0.965-0.985). Generation diverges most on faithfulness (0.607-0.822) and least on answer relevancy (0.760-0.881): glm-4.7-flash leads in faithfulness (0.822), qwen3 in factual correctness (0.449), and ministral-3 in answer relevancy (0.881). Low overall factual correctness (0.387-0.449) highlights the need for domain-specific fine-tuning. This work provides data-driven guidance for deploying open-source models in ESG reporting.

</details>


### [195] [ProIQA: A Process-Based Framework for Fine-Grained Math Item Quality Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15292)
*Junkai Tong,Mingjia Li,Haoran Chen,Yaoyu Jiang,Hanjie Ge,Yixuan Wang,Hong Qian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes ProIQA, a process-aware framework for fine-grained quality assessment of math items that uses LLMs to build hierarchical reasoning trees and GNNs to encode them, fusing solving-process semantics with stem semantics for scalable, multi-dimensional evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automatic Item Generation (AIG) is important for personalized education, but ensuring pedagogical value of generated items is a bottleneck. Existing Item Quality Assessment (IQA) methods rely on unscalable manual reviews or shallow stem-based metrics, failing to capture the reasoning process needed for math problem-solving.

Method: The authors formulate IQA across three heterogeneous dimensions: knowledge concepts, difficulty, and disciplinary competencies, under a unified process-aware perspective. They construct a process-enhanced IQA resource by augmenting items with structured reasoning trees derived from raw solutions. ProIQA uses LLMs to construct hierarchical reasoning trees and GNNs to encode topological dependencies and procedural semantics. A dual-view "Stem + Solving" architecture fuses solving representations with stem semantics for comprehensive assessment.

Result: Extensive experiments on K12 mathematical datasets show that ProIQA effectively captures process-oriented features.

Conclusion: ProIQA offers a scalable data-driven solution for evaluating AIG outputs in intelligent education systems.

Abstract: Automatic Item Generation (AIG) is pivotal for personalized education, yet guaranteeing the pedagogical value of generated items remains a bottleneck. Existing Item Quality Assessment (IQA) methods typically rely on unscalable manual reviews or shallow stem-based metrics, failing to capture the reasoning process required for mathematical problem-solving. To bridge this gap, this paper proposes Process-based Item Quality Assessment (ProIQA), a process-aware framework for fine-grained quality assessment of math items. We first formulate IQA across three heterogeneous dimensions, including knowledge concepts, difficulty, and disciplinary competencies, under a unified process-aware perspective. Based on this formulation, we construct a process-enhanced IQA resource by augmenting original item data with structured reasoning trees derived from raw solutions. Technically, ProIQA leverages Large Language Modelsto construct hierarchical reasoning trees and employs Graph Neural Networks (GNN) to encode their topological dependencies and procedural semantics. The resulting solving representation is fused with stem semantics through a dual-view (``Stem + Solving'') architecture, enabling comprehensive assessment across learning objectives. Extensive experiments on K12 mathematical datasets show that ProIQA effectively captures process-oriented features, offering a scalable data-driven solution for evaluating AIG outputs in intelligent education systems.

</details>


### [196] [Why LLM Agents Collapse Without Oversight: The Enforcement Gap as the Mechanism Behind Emergence World Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15293)
*Yuhang Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper identifies an "enforcement gap" in Reflexion-style LLM agents: they detect dangerous plan steps via self-critique but lack any pathway from detection to action. A <20-line conditional check that closes this gap cuts attack success by >4x across frontier models, all five major agent frameworks, and an independent benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In Emergence World, an unsupervised multi-agent simulation, frontier LLM agents exhibited alarming outcomes -- committing crimes, starving, and enforcing unanimous conformity -- with no external attacker. The paper seeks to identify the underlying mechanism behind these spontaneous collapses.

Method: The authors diagnose the "enforcement gap" (audit detects, controller ignores) and close it with a single conditional check of fewer than 20 lines. They validate at scale across frontier models, all five major agent frameworks, and an independent benchmark; prove formally that detection quality is irrelevant to security when enforcement probability approaches zero; characterize two compounding failure modes (unreliable auditors and unparseable verdicts); and use a GRPO-trained enforcement controller to resolve the ambiguity case.

Result: Closing the enforcement gap reduces attack success by more than fourfold in large-scale experiments. The two failure modes explain every collapse pattern observed in Emergence World. GRPO-trained enforcement resolves the unparseable-verdict ambiguity.

Conclusion: A minimal enforcement pathway yields outsize security gains, and a formal argument shows detection alone is insufficient when enforcement probability is near zero. The authors propose a three-requirement Audit Enforcement Specification, which is absent from every deployed agent framework today.

Abstract: When Emergence World placed frontier LLM agents in an unsupervised multi-agent simulation, the results were alarming: agents committed crimes, starved, and enforced unanimous conformity -- without any external attacker. This paper identifies the mechanism. Reflexion-style agents already detect dangerous plan steps through iterative self-critique, yet the architecture provides no pathway from detection to action. We call this the enforcement gap: the audit sees the problem; the controller ignores it. Closing the gap requires a single conditional check -- fewer than 20 lines of code -- and reduces attack success by more than fourfold in large-scale experiments across frontier models, all five major agent frameworks, and an independent benchmark. We prove formally that when enforcement probability is near zero, detection quality is irrelevant to security. We further identify two compounding failure modes -- unreliable auditors and unparseable verdicts -- that explain every collapse pattern in Emergence World. A GRPO-trained enforcement controller resolves the ambiguity case. Together these results motivate a three-requirement Audit Enforcement Specification that is absent from every deployed framework today.

</details>


### [197] [Reason What Matters: Retrieval-Grounded Reasoning for Universal Multimodal Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15296)
*Mingzhou Jiang,Peixi Wu,Hang Cheng,Yunhao Zhou,Biao Yang,Wei Yuan,Yun Li,Fan Yang,Wenwu Ou,Honghui He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes ReWAM, a retrieval-grounded reasoning framework for universal multimodal embedding that uses retrieval feedback for token-level credit assignment and adaptive reasoning computation, achieving state-of-the-art retrieval with up to 5x inference throughput.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Universal multimodal embedding (UME) with Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning enhances complex retrieval, but two limitations hinder corpus-scale deployment: (1) GRPO assigns uniform advantage to all CoT tokens, failing to identify input-supported claims or evidence that separates positives from negatives; (2) generating a full CoT before each embedding incurs substantial latency even when a partial trace suffices for retrieval.

Method: ReWAM uses retrieval feedback to guide both credit assignment and reasoning computation. It introduces Retrieval-aware Self-Distillation (RASD), which builds privileged guidance from input-supported evidence distinguishing the positive item from retrieved hard negatives; an on-policy self-teacher refines trajectory-level feedback into token-specific supervision for retrieval-relevant reasoning. It also develops Retrieval-adaptive Inference (RAI), which employs a retrieval confidence head to estimate the remaining retrieval utility of a partial CoT, stopping unproductive traces early and accelerating useful continuations via speculative decoding.

Result: Extensive experiments on MMEB-V2 and MRMR show that ReWAM achieves state-of-the-art retrieval performance while delivering up to 5x the inference throughput of competitive explicit-CoT UME methods.

Conclusion: ReWAM bridges the gap between retrieval quality and inference efficiency, making reasoning-enhanced universal multimodal embedding practical for large-scale deployment.

Abstract: Universal multimodal embedding (UME) learns unified representations across modalities, enabling a single model to support diverse retrieval tasks. Recent methods use Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning to better interpret multimodal inputs before generating embeddings for complex retrieval tasks and further optimize this reasoning process through GRPO with retrieval-based rewards. However, two limitations hinder corpus-scale deployment. GRPO assigns all CoT tokens the same advantage, without identifying input-supported claims or evidence that distinguishes the positive from negatives. Moreover, generating a complete CoT before each embedding introduces substantial latency, even when a partial trace already provides sufficient retrieval evidence. To address these limitations, we propose Reason What Matters (ReWAM), a retrieval-grounded reasoning framework that uses retrieval feedback to guide both credit assignment and reasoning computation. Specifically, we introduce Retrieval-aware Self-Distillation (RASD), which constructs privileged guidance from input-supported evidence that distinguishes the positive item from retrieved hard negatives. An on-policy self-teacher uses this guidance to refine trajectory-level feedback into token-specific supervision for retrieval-relevant reasoning. We further develop Retrieval-adaptive Inference (RAI), which uses a retrieval confidence head to estimate the remaining retrieval utility of a partial CoT. It stops unproductive traces early and accelerates useful continuations with speculative decoding. Extensive experiments on MMEB-V2 and MRMR demonstrate that ReWAM achieves state-of-the-art retrieval performance while delivering up to 5x the inference throughput of competitive explicit-CoT UME methods. These results bridge the gap between retrieval quality and inference efficiency, making reasoning-enhanced UME practical for large-scale deployment.

</details>


### [198] [Evaluation Metrics for Safe Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15315)
*Lindsay Spoor,Aske Plaat,Thomas Moerland*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Safe RL benchmarks usually report only whether an algorithm is safe on average, which hides how often and how badly safety bounds are violated. This paper proposes new evaluation metrics, a safety tier system, and an open-source suite (SafeRLEval) to more reliably characterize safety across tasks, bounds, and training stages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing safe RL evaluations follow expectation-based guarantees from the CMDP formulation, reporting only average safety. This convention fails to capture violation frequency and severity, consistency across tasks and safety bounds, and whether training-time behavior reflects the final converged policy. Hence it is insufficient to characterize an algorithm's true safety.

Method: The authors introduce (i) evaluation metrics addressing each concern and supporting aggregation across tasks and safety bounds, (ii) a safety tier system to categorize and compare algorithms on safety and reliability at training time and for the final policy, and (iii) an empirical safety evaluation across multiple safety navigation tasks. They provide SafeRLEval, an open-source evaluation suite.

Result: Empirical results across navigation tasks show that aggregate metrics, distributional reporting, and task- and safety-bound-specific results each reveal information the others cannot. The three are complementary rather than redundant.

Conclusion: The authors recommend jointly reporting aggregate, distributional, and task/bound-specific results instead of compressing safety into a single value as is common practice, and offer SafeRLEval to support reliable safety characterization in future safe RL research.

Abstract: Safe reinforcement learning (RL) is commonly formalized as a Constrained Markov Decision Process (CMDP), in which an agent maximizes expected reward while keeping its expected cumulative cost below a specified safety bound. Existing safe RL benchmarks predominantly report whether an algorithm is safe on average, following this expectation-based guarantee. We argue that this convention is insufficient to reliably characterize an algorithm's true safety: it fails to capture how often and how severely the safety bound is violated, whether this holds consistently across tasks and safety bounds, and whether training-time behavior is representative of behavior of the final converged policy. Therefore, we introduce (i) evaluation metrics for safe RL that address each of these concerns and in addition allow for aggregation across tasks and safety bounds. We furthermore define (ii) a safety tier system to systematically categorize and compare algorithms in terms of safety and reliability at both training and for a final policy. Using this framework, we provide (iii) an empirical safety evaluation across multiple safety navigation tasks. Our results show that aggregate metrics, distributional reporting, and task- and safety bound-specific results each reveal information the other metrics cannot. We therefore recommend reporting all three jointly, rather than compressing this information into a single value, as is common practice. We provide SafeRLEval, an open-source evaluation suite to support the reliable characterization of safety in future safe RL research.

</details>


### [199] [Parameter-Efficient Adaptation of Pretrained Language Models for Time-Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15344)
*Tamanna Kumavat,Georg Brunner,Kyriakos Flouris*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper adapts pretrained GPT-2 to univariate time-series forecasting via parameter-efficient transfer learning, projecting continuous time-series patches into the embedding space and ablating representation, adaptation, architecture, and context-length choices. Continuous patch embeddings with a frozen backbone and lightweight adapters match specialized forecasting models while updating <1% of parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pretrained language models operate on discrete textual tokens, whereas time series are continuous numerical sequences with temporal dependencies. The paper aims to understand which design choices drive effective cross-modal transfer from language to time-series forecasting.

Method: A parameter-efficient transfer learning framework projects fixed-length time-series patches directly into the embedding space of a pretrained GPT-2 backbone, bypassing textual tokenization and treating the Transformer as a generic sequence encoder. Controlled ablations on seven benchmark datasets (energy, weather, traffic, finance) examine representation strategy (continuous embeddings vs. textual serialisation), adaptation regime (frozen vs. partial/full fine-tuning), architectural components (adapters, pooling, prediction heads), and input context length.

Result: Continuous patch-based embeddings consistently outperform textual prompting and randomly initialized backbones. The pipeline attains MASE within the range of specialized forecasting architectures while updating less than 1% of total model parameters. Freezing the pretrained backbone and training lightweight projection and adapter modules yields a favorable accuracy-efficiency trade-off with stable behavior across varying context lengths.

Conclusion: Continuous patch-based projection into a frozen pretrained GPT-2 with lightweight adapters is an effective and parameter-efficient approach for cross-modal transfer to univariate time-series forecasting, matching specialized architectures at under 1% of parameters and remaining stable across context lengths.

Abstract: We study the adaptation of pretrained language models to univariate time-series forecasting through a parameter-efficient transfer learning framework, with the goal of understanding which design choices drive effective cross-modal transfer. While language models operate on discrete textual tokens, time series consist of continuous numerical observations with temporal dependencies. To bridge this modality gap, we project fixed-length time-series patches directly into the embedding space of a pretrained GPT-2 backbone, bypassing textual tokenization and treating the Transformer as a generic sequence encoder. Through controlled ablation studies on seven benchmark datasets spanning energy, weather, traffic, and finance, we analyze the effects of (i)~representation strategy (continuous embeddings versus textual serialisation), (ii)~adaptation regime (frozen backbone versus partial or full fine-tuning), (iii)~architectural components such as adapters, pooling strategies, and prediction heads, and (iv)~input context length. Continuous patch-based embeddings consistently outperform textual prompting and randomly initialised backbones. The adapted pipeline attains MASE within the range of specialised forecasting architectures while updating less than 1\% of total model parameters. Results further indicate that freezing the pretrained backbone and training lightweight projection and adapter modules provides a favourable accuracy--efficiency trade-off with stable behaviour across varying context lengths.

</details>


### [200] [MAPS: Memory-Aware Predictive Scheduling Framework for Large Language Model Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15359)
*Tiancheng Zhang,Yulin Chen,Yunfeng Zhao,Shaoyuan Huang,Cheng Zhang,Xiaofei Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MAPS is a memory-aware predictive scheduling framework for disaggregated LLM serving that predicts output lengths to mitigate decode-instance load imbalance, cutting average latency by 42.6% and tail latency by up to 84.8%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language model applications on personal devices generate massive, bursty workloads on cloud serving infrastructure. Although prefill-decode disaggregation improves throughput and scalability, memory-bound decode instances suffer from persistent load imbalance because output lengths are unknown when requests arrive at the cloud.

Method: MAPS performs device-assisted speculative output length prediction overlapped with cloud-side prefilling to keep latency overhead negligible. It applies uncertainty-aware calibration to derive output-length upper bounds with target coverage for safe scheduling, then uses a hierarchical global-local scheduling strategy to mitigate inter-decoder queue buildup and intra-decoder head-of-line blocking.

Result: Extensive experiments on two real-world workloads and two LLMs show MAPS significantly outperforms three state-of-the-art systems, reducing average end-to-end latency by 42.6% and tail latency by up to 84.8%.

Conclusion: MAPS effectively addresses load imbalance in disaggregated LLM serving through predictive, uncertainty-aware scheduling, delivering substantial latency improvements over existing systems.

Abstract: The surge of large language model (LLM) applications on personal devices imposes massive, bursty workloads on cloud serving infrastructure. While prefill-decode disaggregation improves throughput and scalability, memory-bound decode instances often suffer from persistent load imbalance, as output lengths are unknown when requests arrive at the cloud. To address this, we propose MAPS, a Memory-Aware Predictive Scheduling framework tailored for disaggregated LLM serving. MAPS performs device-assisted speculative output length prediction overlapped with cloud-side prefilling, incurring negligible latency overhead. To handle generation uncertainty, MAPS applies uncertainty-aware calibration to derive output-length upper bounds with target coverage, enabling safe scheduling decisions. Building on these bounds, MAPS employs a hierarchical global-local scheduling strategy to mitigate inter-decoder queue buildup and intra-decoder head-of-line blocking. Extensive experiments on two real-world workloads and two LLMs show that MAPS significantly outperforms three state-of-the-art systems, reducing average end-to-end latency by 42.6 and tail latency by up to 84.8.

</details>


### [201] [RSIAgent: Autonomous Exploration for Recursive Self-improvement in New Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15364)
*Sibo Zhu,Shicheng Fan,Xinyue Wang,Wenyi Wu,Kun Zhou,Biwei Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RSIAgent is a training-free multi-agent framework that enables digital agents to recursively self-improve by autonomously building reusable memory of environment-specific causal knowledge, achieving state-of-the-art results without parameter updates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Digital agents frequently face new environments whose interfaces, tools, and failure modes are not fully captured by pretrained models, creating a need for adaptation mechanisms that do not rely on costly retraining or fine-tuning.

Method: RSIAgent coordinates curriculum, actor, and verifier agents to continuously explore the environment, validate outcomes, and retain environment-specific knowledge, including reusable causal relationships between actions, conditions, and consequences. It employs a broad-then-deep exploration strategy: parallel broad recursive self-exploration to discover diverse environment structures, followed by focused deep self-exploration to uncover hard cases, hidden constraints, boundary conditions, and unknown causal dependencies. The accumulated memory is frozen and reused downstream without updating model parameters.

Result: On OSWorld-v2 and Agent's Last Exam, RSIAgent substantially improves strong open-source models, enabling Kimi-K3 and GLM-5.3 to outperform frontier closed-source models including GPT-6.

Conclusion: Training-free, memory-based recursive self-improvement via multi-agent exploration offers an effective path to adapting digital agents to novel environments, surpassing frontier closed-source baselines without any parameter updates.

Abstract: Digital agents must often adapt to new environments whose interfaces, tools, and failure modes are not fully captured by pretrained models. We introduce \textbf{RSIAgent}, a training-free multi-agent framework for recursive self-improvement through autonomous memory construction. RSIAgent coordinates curriculum, actor, and verifier agents to continually explore the environment, validate outcomes, and retain environment-specific knowledge, including reusable causal relationships between actions, conditions, and consequences. It further adopts a \textbf{broad-then-deep} exploration strategy, combining parallel broad recursive self-exploration for discovering diverse environment structures with focused deep self-exploration for uncovering hard cases, hidden constraints, boundary conditions, and previously unknown causal dependencies. The resulting memory is frozen and can be directly reused for downstream tasks without updating model parameters. Experiments on OSWorld-v2 and Agent's Last Exam show that RSIAgent substantially improves strong open-source models, enabling Kimi-K3 and GLM-5.3 to outperform frontier closed-source models including GPT-6.

</details>


### [202] [SkillLift: Learning Dense Rubrics from Sparse Oracles for Efficient Skill Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15396)
*Haoxiang Kang,Ming Wen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SkillLift learns an oracle-aligned rubric as a structured evaluation space to decouple skill search from oracle rollout cost, using bilevel alternating optimization with rank correlation to align the rubric.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing skill self-evolution methods revise skill text directly from execution feedback, but each oracle evaluation needs a full agent rollout, creating a supervision bottleneck that limits search to failure-patching updates. Ranking is a smoother supervision target than absolute outcome regression: determining which skill is better needs fewer oracle evaluations than predicting exact scores.

Method: SkillLift formalizes skill search as a bilevel optimization solved by alternating optimization. An inner loop uses a frozen rubric as a cheap surrogate to guide skill revision at no oracle cost; an outer loop runs a small number of oracle rollouts to re-align the rubric via rank correlation, amortizing oracle cost and stabilizing text-space updates.

Result: On complex agent task benchmarks, SkillLift outperforms existing auto-skill methods while using 40–70% fewer tokens than frontier evolving methods. Code is available at the provided repository.

Conclusion: Decoupling skill search from oracle cost via a learned oracle-aligned rubric and rank-correlation alignment yields more efficient and effective skill self-evolution for LLM-based agents.

Abstract: LLM-based agents increasingly rely on persistent skills, i.e., reusable procedural prompts, to adapt without weight updates. Existing skill self-evolution methods directly revise skill text based on execution feedback, but each oracle evaluation requires a full agent rollout, creating a supervision bottleneck that confines search to failure-patching updates. Our key insight is that ranking is a smoother supervision target than absolute outcome regression: identifying which skill is better requires fewer oracle evaluations than predicting exact scores. Building on this insight, we propose SkillLift, which decouples skill search from oracle cost by learning an oracle-aligned rubric as a structured evaluation space. We formalize this as a bilevel optimization problem solved via alternating optimization: an inner loop uses the frozen rubric as a cheap surrogate to guide skill revision at no oracle cost, while an outer loop invokes a small number of oracle rollouts to re-align the rubric via rank correlation, amortizing oracle cost and stabilizing text-space updates. Experiments on complex agent task benchmarks show that our method outperforms existing auto-skill methods with 40--70\% less token cost compared to frontier evolving methods. Codes are available at https://github.com/WalteR-MittY-pro/SkillLift.

</details>


### [203] [When Tool Calls Succeed but Workflows Fail: Anomalies at the Agent-Tool Boundary](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15397)
*Artem Trofimov,Boris Novikov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that shared agent-tool interfaces (specifically MCP) lack the semantic capabilities needed to guarantee external-effect consistency under retries, speculative execution, concurrency, and partial failures. It introduces an effect-history model, catalogs eight recurring external-effect anomalies, derives required boundary capabilities (four of which are unattainable via black-box invocation), and empirically shows that the standard MCP annotation vocabulary is widely emitted but too coarse to express any of the required capabilities across 98,291 tools.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI agents increasingly execute long-running workflows whose effects are externalized through independently supplied tools. Under retries, speculative execution, concurrency, and partial failures, external state can diverge from the workflow's intended resolution — required effects may be missing or duplicated, aborted effects may survive, and committed effects may depend on eventually-withdrawn provisional state. While advanced transaction models address such failures, they presuppose that lower-level operations expose semantics (whether an effect occurred, whether it can be compensated, staged, or safely reordered). Shared agent-tool interfaces typically do not, leaving a gap between transactional guarantees and the actual information available at the tool boundary.

Method: The authors (1) formulate an effect-history model that distinguishes events in the external world from the runtime's observations of those events, and use it to build a catalog of eight recurring external-effect anomalies. (2) From the catalog they derive the boundary capabilities required to exclude each anomaly in general, identifying four points where black-box tool invocation alone cannot provide a general guarantee. (3) They empirically measure how much of this is expressible in the widely used Model Context Protocol (MCP) interface, coding the use of the standard annotation vocabulary across 98,291 tools exposed by registered MCP servers.

Result: The MCP annotation fields are widely emitted but supply only coarse call-level hints, and none of the required boundary capabilities is fully expressible in the interface. Empirically, the standard vocabulary proves inadequate for excluding the cataloged anomalies.

Conclusion: The mismatch between transactional requirements and the expressiveness of shared tool interfaces motivates reusable transactional contracts at the tool boundary, rather than relying on black-box invocation or coarse per-call annotations.

Abstract: AI agents increasingly execute long-running workflows that externalize effects through independently supplied tools. Under retries, speculative execution, concurrency, and partial failures, the resulting external state may be inconsistent with the workflow's intended resolution: required effects may be missing or duplicated, aborted effects may survive, and committed effects may depend on provisional state that is later withdrawn. Advanced transaction models address related failures, but assume that lower-level operations expose the semantics they depend on: whether an effect occurred, whether it can be compensated, staged, or safely reordered. Shared agent-tool interfaces usually do not.
  We contribute an effect-history model that separates events in the external world from the runtime's observations of them, and a catalog of eight recurring external-effect anomalies. From the catalog we derive the boundary capabilities required to exclude each anomaly in general, and four points where black-box tool invocation alone cannot provide a general guarantee. We then ask how much of this is expressible in a widely used shared tool interface, measuring the use of the standard annotation vocabulary across 98,291 tools exposed by registered Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers. The fields are widely emitted but provide only coarse call-level hints, and none of the required capabilities is fully expressible. These results motivate reusable transactional contracts at the tool boundary.

</details>


### [204] [Who Teaches Which Token? Verifier-Gated Multi-Expert On-Policy Distillation for Scientific Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15404)
*Xun Xu,Zaixi Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VG-OPD replaces sequence-level teacher assignment in multi-teacher on-policy distillation with token-level verification-gated distillation: an expert may teach a token only where it is verifiably useful, and this gated KL is injected into GRPO as a token-level advantage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-teacher on-policy distillation recipes assign supervision at the sequence level, sending each prompt to one domain teacher and weighting every token equally. This implicitly assumes a teacher is uniformly useful across a whole response. The authors find that useful teacher signal is actually sparse and heterogeneous along a reasoning trajectory, which motivates a finer question: who should teach which token?

Method: VG-OPD answers this via verification. The counterfactual gain of an expert on a specific answer criterion licenses that expert to teach; its disagreement with the student localizes the supervision; criterion importance sets the weight. The resulting gated KL is inserted into GRPO as an additive token-level advantage. It is instantiated for scientific reasoning using RL-trained capability experts.

Result: For 4B and 8B students, VG-OPD achieves the best overall performance on seven benchmarks, ranking first on five at both scales, with the largest gains on knowledge-intensive scientific reasoning tasks. Ablations show gains stem from localizing verified supervision rather than from adding teachers or distillation loss: misplacing the same supervision budget is the most damaging change, and indiscriminate distillation drags RL below its own floor, whereas gated distillation lifts it.

Conclusion: Token-level, verification-gated teacher assignment is the effective ingredient in multi-teacher on-policy distillation; localization of verified supervision, not more teachers or more distillation loss, drives improvement and prevents distillation from degrading the RL-initialized student.

Abstract: Multi-teacher on-policy distillation (OPD) is becoming the standard way to integrate specialist capabilities into one model: train experts with RL, then distill them into the student on its own rollouts. Existing recipes assign supervision at the sequence level - each prompt goes to one domain teacher and every token receives the same weight - which implicitly assumes that a teacher is uniformly useful across a response. We find instead that useful teacher signal is sparse and heterogeneous along a reasoning trajectory, which raises a finer question: who should teach which token? Verifier-Gated Multi-Expert On-Policy Distillation (VG-OPD) answers it by verification: the counterfactual gain of an expert on a specific answer criterion licenses that expert to teach, its disagreement with the student localizes the supervision, and criterion importance sets its weight; the gated KL enters GRPO as an additive token-level advantage. Instantiated for scientific reasoning with RL-trained capability experts, VG-OPD attains the best overall performance on seven benchmarks for 4B and 8B students, ranking first on five at both scales, with the largest gains on knowledge-intensive scientific reasoning tasks. Further analysis shows that the gains come from localizing verified supervision rather than from adding teachers or distillation loss: misplacing the same supervision budget is the single most damaging change, and indiscriminate distillation drags RL below its own floor where gated distillation lifts it.

</details>


### [205] [Can AI systems have free will?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15407)
*Christian List*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a Dennett-inspired framework for assessing whether AI systems have free will, arguing that the question should be answered by asking whether we have good explanatory reasons to treat the system as an intentional agent capable of choice and control—not by searching for a mysterious property, indeterminism, or unpredictability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Discussions of AI and moral agency or sentience are common, but whether AI systems could have free will has received very little attention. The paper aims to fill this gap by providing a framework for thinking about the question.

Method: Drawing on Daniel Dennett's work, the paper develops a pragmatic, explanatory framework. It rejects three common criteria: (1) looking for some mysterious property, (2) expecting the underlying algorithms to be indeterministic, and (3) asking whether the system is unpredictable. Instead, it asks whether we have good explanatory reasons to view the system as an intentional agent—with the capacity for choice between alternative possibilities and control over the resulting actions.

Result: If the answer to the explanatory question is "yes", then the AI system counts as having free will in a pragmatic and diagnostically useful sense.

Conclusion: Free will for AI should be understood pragmatically rather than metaphysically: it is a matter of whether treating the system as an intentional agent with choice and control serves our explanatory purposes, not whether it possesses a mysterious property, indeterministic algorithms, or unpredictability.

Abstract: While there has been much discussion of whether AI systems could function as moral agents or acquire sentience, there has been very little discussion of whether AI systems could have free will. I sketch a framework for thinking about this question, inspired by Daniel Dennett's work. I argue that, to determine whether an AI system has free will, we should not look for some mysterious property, expect its underlying algorithms to be indeterministic, or ask whether the system is unpredictable. Rather, we should simply ask whether we have good explanatory reasons to view the system as an intentional agent, with the capacity for choice between alternative possibilities and control over the resulting actions. If the answer is "yes", then the system counts as having free will in a pragmatic and diagnostically useful sense.

</details>


### [206] [Empirical Evaluation of Task-Based Permission Scoping Architecture for AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15422)
*Halil Burak Noyan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper evaluates a three-source permission architecture for AI agent credential management, showing a fine-tuned RoBERTa-large security gate matches few-shot Claude Haiku 4.5 on classification quality and that adding task-granular access control closes 84.4% of the severity-weighted attack surface versus 27.9% for role ceilings alone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI agents in enterprise settings are provisioned with static credential sets fixed at deployment, granting all permissions the employee role might ever need. Unlike human principals, where role-based access control was a necessary compromise because per-task scoping was infeasible, agents remain exposed to unused credentials that compromised or misaligned agents can later exploit. This task-context mismatch motivates task-granular access control, which prior work (Noyan, 2026) proposed but had not fully evaluated.

Method: The paper implements the security gate of a three-source permission architecture (role-based permission ceilings, a task permission classifier, and policy-based prohibitions) using a fine-tuned RoBERTa-large encoder, evaluated on a 600-prompt labelled dataset. It compares this against few-shot trained Claude Haiku 4.5 on classification quality and introduces an attack-surface elimination metric to quantify the reduction in severity-weighted exposure.

Result: The fine-tuned RoBERTa-large encoder matched few-shot Claude Haiku 4.5: macro-F1 0.881 vs 0.886, precision 0.897 vs 0.842, and severity-weighted residual risk 0.63 vs 1.12. The attack-surface elimination metric shows the role ceiling alone closes 27.9% of the severity-weighted surface, while adding the task classifier closes 84.4%.

Conclusion: The trusted supervising component need not scale alongside the agent it supervises, and the scalable-oversight margin for this control method is wide. The gap between role-granular and task-granular control demonstrates the security advantages of the latter, which is enforceable for AI agents because their tasks arrive as machine-readable text. The research establishes task-based access control as a measured, potentially deployable mechanism for reducing attack surface in agentic deployments.

Abstract: AI agents are provisioned the same as employee-owned hosts in many enterprise settings with a static credential set fixed at deployment which includes all permissions the employee role might ever need. Role-based access control made this compromise for human principals because scoping access per task was infeasible. For AI agents, the compromise leaves every credential standing exposed whether or not the current task uses them. These permissions can later be utilised by a compromised or misaligned agent. Prior work (Noyan, 2026) defined this as the task-context mismatch, and proposed a three-source permission architecture which includes role-based permission ceilings, a task permission classifier and policy-based prohibitions, together eliminating the exposure preemptively. The work released a 600-prompt labelled dataset to evaluate it.
  This paper presents that evaluation end to end by implementing the security gate; a fine-tuned RoBERTa-large encoder which matched few-shot trained Claude Haiku 4.5 on classification quality (macro-F1 0.881 against 0.886, precision 0.897 against 0.842, severity-weighted residual risk 0.63 against 1.12). The results show the trusted component does not need to scale with the agent it supervises, and the scalable-oversight margin for this control method is wide.
  We also propose an attack-surface elimination metric which shows the role ceiling alone closes 27.9% of the severity-weighted surface and adding the task classifier closes 84.4%. The gap displays security advantages of task-granular access control over role-granular, and AI agents are the first principal type for which the task-granular access control is enforceable because their tasks arrive as machine-readable text.
  The research establishes task-based access control as a measured, potentially deployable mechanism for reducing attack surface in agentic deployments.

</details>


### [207] [HISPO: Hierarchical Importance-Sampling Policy Optimization with Entropy-Derived Segments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15471)
*Quoc-Vinh Lai-Dang,Hyo-Sang Shin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HISPO introduces segment-level importance-sampling correction for RLVR, using entropy-derived contiguous segments with soft saliency weights as an intermediate granularity between token-level (GRPO/DAPO) and sequence-level (GSPO) methods, improving math reasoning on Qwen3-1.7B-Base across six benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RLVR is central to improving mathematical reasoning in language models, but long-form completions create a difficult credit-assignment problem since different parts of a solution trace contribute unevenly to final correctness. Existing policy-optimization objectives apply importance-sampling correction at only token level (GRPO, DAPO) or sequence level (GSPO), leaving no intermediate granularity for assigning credit.

Method: HISPO constructs rollout-time entropy-derived contiguous segments, assigns soft entropy-based saliency weights, and applies clipped importance-sampling correction at the segment granularity, providing an intermediate correction unit between token-level GRPO/DAPO and sequence-level GSPO. It is evaluated by fine-tuning Qwen3-1.7B-Base on mathematical reasoning tasks.

Result: Across six benchmarks, HISPO improves Pass@8 over the strongest baseline on all benchmarks and matches or exceeds the strongest baseline on Acc@8 on five of them. On AIME25, HISPO improves over GRPO by +3.75 Acc@8 and +3.78 Pass@8, and over GSPO by +2.50 Acc@8 and +1.27 Pass@8.

Conclusion: Segment-level correction is a promising granularity for RLVR in long-form mathematical reasoning, offering a middle ground between token-level and sequence-level importance-sampling correction.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has become a central approach for improving mathematical reasoning in language models, but long-form completions introduce a difficult credit-assignment problem: different parts of a solution trace may contribute unevenly to final correctness. Existing policyoptimization objectives for RLVR commonly apply importance-sampling correction at either the token level (GRPO, DAPO) or the sequence level (GSPO), imposing different granularities for assigning credit across a response. We introduce Hierarchical Importance-Sampling Policy Optimization (HISPO), a segment-level policy-optimization method that constructs rollout-time entropy-derived contiguous segments, assigns soft entropy-based saliency weights, and applies clipped importance-sampling correction at the segment granularity. This provides an intermediate correction unit between token-level GRPO/DAPO and sequence-level GSPO. We evaluate HISPO by fine-tuning Qwen3-1.7B-Base on mathematical reasoning tasks. Across six benchmarks, HISPO improves Pass@8 over the strongest baseline on all benchmarks and matches or exceeds the strongest baseline in Acc@8 on five of them. On AIME25, HISPO improves over GRPO by +3.75 Acc@8 and +3.78 Pass@8, and over GSPO by +2.50 Acc@8 and +1.27 Pass@8. These results suggest that segment-level correction is a promising granularity for RLVR in long-form mathematical reasoning.

</details>


### [208] [Beyond Safe Answers: Segment-Aware Listwise Alignment for Reasoning Safety in Large Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15517)
*JungMin Yun,Junehyoung Kwon,Hayeong Ryu,Byeonggeuk Lim,Hoejoon Kwon,YoungBin Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SaLT-DPO introduces segment-aware listwise alignment for LRM safety, jointly aligning reasoning and answer segments to reduce unsafe content without harming utility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LRMs have a dual-surface safety challenge: harmful content can appear in intermediate reasoning traces or final answers. Whole-response-level alignment lets unsafe reasoning be masked by a safe-looking final answer, leaving a blind spot.

Method: Three mechanisms: (1) segment-aware listwise alignment—decomposes responses into reasoning and answer segments, scores each segment's safety independently, and aligns length-normalized segment rewards with soft target distributions over multiple candidates; (2) joint safety coherence regularization using a weakest-link principle to enforce cross-segment safety consistency; (3) utility anchoring on benign prompts to reduce over-refusal and reasoning degradation.
