Result: On three LRMs, SaLT-DPO consistently lowers unsafe rates for both reasoning and answer segments, mitigates benign compliance degradation, and preserves general reasoning. Ablations confirm the complementary contributions of each component.

Conclusion: Segment-level, listwise preference alignment with coherence regularization and utility anchoring effectively addresses dual-surface safety in LRMs without sacrificing utility.

Abstract: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) pose a dual-surface safety challenge: both intermediate reasoning traces and final answers can contain harmful content. Existing alignment methods often operate at the whole-response level, allowing unsafe reasoning to be masked by a safe-looking final answer. We propose Segment-aware Listwise Target DPO (SaLT-DPO), which addresses this gap through three mechanisms: (1) segment-aware listwise alignment that decomposes responses into reasoning and answer segments, independently scores each segment's safety, and aligns length-normalized segment rewards with soft target distributions over multiple candidates; (2) joint safety coherence regularization that applies a weakest-link principle to promote safety consistency across both segments; and (3) utility anchoring on benign prompts to mitigate over-refusal and reasoning degradation. Experiments on three LRMs show that SaLT-DPO consistently reduces unsafe rates for both reasoning and answer segments while mitigating degradation in benign compliance and preserving general reasoning performance. Ablation studies demonstrate the complementary contributions of its components.

</details>


### [209] [GRIN+: Towards Fast Yet Effective Machine Unlearning for Imbalanced Medical Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15571)
*Minghui Huang,Junxiao Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GRIN+ is a machine unlearning framework for imbalanced medical data that resolves the privacy-efficiency-utility (PEU) trilemma via class-adaptive influence scoring and direction-constrained updates, achieving fast, precise erasure across skin cancer, brain tumor, and breast ultrasound datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Privacy regulations (GDPR, HIPAA) mandate a "Right to be Forgotten," requiring effective machine unlearning (MU) to remove patient data from trained models. Existing MU techniques struggle with a PEU trilemma, especially in medical scenarios with severe class imbalance and long-tailed distributions, where gradient dominance of majority classes causes methods to fail at protecting clinical knowledge or mistakenly delete features for diagnosing rare conditions.

Method: GRIN+ is a machine unlearning framework for fast, precise data erasure in imbalanced medical settings. It decouples unlearning-specific knowledge from generalized representations at the parameter level by analyzing gradient contributions of forget and retain sets. It introduces a class-adaptive influence scoring mechanism to rectify gradient dominance, and a direction-constrained update strategy to prevent unintended erosion of vital clinical knowledge.

Result: Benchmarking on medical datasets (skin cancer ISIC, brain tumor MRI, breast ultrasound BUSI) shows GRIN+ optimally balances the PEU trilemma, maintaining high diagnostic accuracy and robust privacy while significantly improving runtime efficiency over baselines. Code and benchmarks are open-sourced.

Conclusion: GRIN+ effectively addresses the privacy-efficiency-utility trilemma in imbalanced medical machine unlearning, offering a practical solution for regulatory-compliant patient data erasure without compromising diagnostic performance on rare conditions.

Abstract: As deep learning models become fundamental to modern healthcare, the "Right to be Forgotten" mandated by privacy regulations like GDPR and HIPAA necessitates effective machine unlearning (MU) to remove sensitive patient data from trained models. However, existing MU techniques often struggle with a fundamental "privacy-efficiency-utility" (PEU) trilemma, particularly in medical scenarios where data is frequently characterized by severe class imbalance and long-tailed distributions. In such cases, standard unlearning methods can fail to protect key clinical knowledge or mistakenly delete features essential for diagnosing rare conditions due to the gradient dominance of majority classes. To address these challenges, we propose GRIN+, a novel machine unlearning framework designed for fast and precise data erasure in imbalanced medical scenarios. GRIN+ decouples unlearning-specific knowledge from generalized representations at the parameter level by analyzing the gradient contributions of both "forget" and "retain" sets. It introduces a class-adaptive influence scoring mechanism to rectify gradient dominance and employs a direction-constrained update strategy to prevent the unintended erosion of vital clinical knowledge. Comprehensive benchmarking across multiple medical datasets, including skin cancer (ISIC), brain tumor (MRI), and breast ultrasound (BUSI), demonstrates that GRIN+ achieves an optimal balance of the PEU trilemma. Experimental results show that GRIN+ maintains high diagnostic accuracy and robust privacy while significantly enhancing runtime efficiency compared to existing baselines. We open-source the GRIN+ code and benchmarks to support further research.

</details>


### [210] [Diversified and Perceptible Counterfactual Examples Leveraging Expert Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15609)
*Akram Bensalem,Fahima Djelil,Marie-Jeanne Lesot,Gr{é}gory Smits*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper integrates a fuzzy linguistic vocabulary into CounterFactual Example (CFE) generation to make explanations semantically meaningful to the explainee, and proposes DiCEf, a linguistic-domain variant of DiCE that preserves cost minimality, sparsity, and diversity while enabling personalisation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CFEs are a cornerstone of XAI, offering local, post hoc, and model-agnostic explanations via minimal input modifications that alter a model's prediction. However, to be intelligible, these modifications must also be semantically meaningful to the explainee. Standard CFE methods do not guarantee such linguistic perceptibility.

Method: The authors integrate knowledge expressed as a fuzzy linguistic vocabulary to represent the explainee's perception and interpretation of the data. The domain induced by this fuzzy vocabulary imposes structural constraints that introduce feature dependencies, which preclude gradient-based CFE generation (e.g., DiCE). To overcome this, they propose a continuous data embedding in this linguistic domain and exploit it to define a variant of DiCE, named DiCEf, allowing personalisation for the explainee.

Result: Experiments on a real-world dataset show that this extension of the DiCE method enables the generation of CFEs that are linguistically perceptible while preserving cost minimality, sparsity, and diversity.

Conclusion: Embedding data in a fuzzy linguistic domain and adapting DiCE accordingly (DiCEf) yields semantically meaningful, explainee-personalised CFEs without sacrificing the standard quality criteria of cost minimality, sparsity, and diversity.

Abstract: CounterFactual Examples (CFEs) are a cornerstone of eXplainable Artificial Intelligence (XAI), offering local, post hoc, and model-agnostic explanations by identifying minimal input modifications that alter a model's prediction. Yet, in order to be intelligible, these modifications must also be semantically meaningful to the explainee. This paper proposes to integrate knowledge expressed as a fuzzy linguistic vocabulary to represent the explainee's perception and interpretation of the data. The domain induced by this fuzzy vocabulary imposes structural constraints that make the features dependent, preventing the use of gradient-based optimisation methods for CFE generation, e.g., DiCE. The paper proposes a continuous data embedding in this linguistic domain and exploits it to define a variant of DiCE that allows personalisation for the explainee, named DiCEf. As illustrated by experimental results on a real-world dataset, this extension of the DiCE method enables the generation of CFEs that are linguistically perceptible while preserving cost minimality, sparsity, and diversity.

</details>


### [211] [EEG-Xplain: Decoding Neural Black-Boxes of EEG Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15687)
*Hansong Ma,Junxiao Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a unified attribution framework to interpret EEG foundation models across heterogeneous architectures, combining gradient-, perturbation-, and activation-based methods along spatial, temporal, and frequency dimensions, with LLM-generated natural-language reports.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: EEG foundation models like BIOT, LaBraM, and EEGMamba perform well in neural signal decoding, but their black-box nature limits clinical trust and neuroscientific validation. A standardized approach for assessing interpretability and physiological plausibility is needed.

Method: The framework integrates gradient-, perturbation-, and activation-based explanation methods. Spatially it identifies critical EEG channels via topographic maps; temporally it uses attribution heatmaps; in the frequency domain it quantifies contributions of canonical EEG rhythms via spectral perturbation analysis. Reliability is assessed with a population-level evaluation combining AOPC and cross-method consistency. LLMs convert structured attribution outputs into natural-language reports.

Result: Experiments on Mumtaz2016 and TUAB show the generated explanations align with established neurophysiological markers, validating meaningful neural representations while exposing potential dependencies on artifacts and spurious patterns.

Conclusion: The framework provides a standardized approach for evaluating the interpretability, reliability, and physiological plausibility of EEG foundation models, bridging low-level neural representations and high-level semantic reasoning.

Abstract: EEG foundation models such as BIOT, LaBraM, and EEGMamba have achieved remarkable performance in neural signal decoding, but their black-box nature limits clinical trust and neuroscientific validation. We propose a unified attribution framework for interpreting EEG foundation models across heterogeneous architectures. The framework integrates gradient-, perturbation-, and activation-based explanation methods to analyze model behavior in spatial, temporal, and frequency dimensions. Spatially, it identifies critical EEG channels and visualizes their distributions using topographic maps. Temporally, it highlights decision-relevant signal segments through attribution heatmaps. In the frequency domain, it quantifies the contributions of canonical EEG rhythms via spectral perturbation analysis. To assess explanation reliability, we introduce a population-level evaluation combining Area Over the Perturbation Curve (AOPC) and cross-method consistency analysis. The framework further leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) to transform structured attribution outputs into natural-language reports, bridging low-level neural representations and high-level semantic reasoning. Experiments on benchmark datasets, including Mumtaz2016 and TUAB, demonstrate that the generated explanations are consistent with established neurophysiological markers, validating meaningful neural representations while exposing potential dependencies on artifacts and spurious patterns. The proposed framework provides a standardized approach for evaluating the interpretability, reliability, and physiological plausibility of EEG foundation models.

</details>


### [212] [Beyond Accuracy: Robustness, Cost, and Governance Trade-offs for Vision-Language Models in Templated Document Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15706)
*Kushal Patel,Pushkal Shrivastava,Mackenzie Lees,Qirui Lu,Bhargobjyoti Saikia,Liying Li,Junlin Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Benchmarks 11 systems on structured field extraction from synthetic checks, finding fine-tuned open-source VLMs reach F1 >0.98 and beat zero-shot commercial models; introduces a practitioner selection framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluations of VLMs for structured document extraction mostly report clean-benchmark accuracy and give practitioners little guidance on which approach fits a given task complexity, creating a gap the paper addresses.

Method: A measurement-grounded study with an open-source release: eleven systems (3 commercial, 2 reasoning, 5 open-source VLMs in pretrained and fine-tuned form, plus a non-LLM OCR->regex floor) evaluated on a 750-document held-out pool of synthetic checks. A practitioner-oriented selection framework maps a task profile (quality, latency, governance, volume) to a recommended approach via filtering and total-cost minimization.

Result: Fine-tuning on 3K samples lifts the best open-source VLMs above F1 0.98, surpassing every zero-shot commercial system on this task; GPT-5 leads the commercial pool on F1, while Claude Sonnet 4.5 collapses on Date.

Conclusion: Fine-tuned open-source VLMs can outperform zero-shot commercial systems, and the proposed framework converts these measurements into actionable approach selection, illustrated on a hypothetical mid-volume document-extraction scenario.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) are increasingly used to extract structured fields from business documents, yet most evaluations report accuracy on clean benchmarks and offer little guidance to practitioners choosing an approach for a given task complexity. We address this gap with a measurement-grounded study and an open-source release. Across eleven systems (three commercial, two reasoning, five open-source VLMs in pretrained and fine-tuned form, and a non-LLM OCR->regex floor) scored on a 750-document held-out pool of synthetic checks, fine-tuning on 3K samples lifts the best open-source VLMs above F1 0.98-above every zero-shot commercial system on this task-while GPT-5 leads the commercial pool on F1 and Claude Sonnet 4.5 collapses on Date. To turn these measurements into actionable choices, we introduce a practitioner-oriented selection framework that maps a task profile (quality, latency, governance, volume) to a recommended approach via filtering and total-cost minimization, illustrated on a hypothetical mid-volume document-extraction scenario.

</details>


### [213] [New Conditions for Philosophers to Catch the Wave of Citizen Deliberation in the Age of Artificial Intelligence in advance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15707)
*Bernard Reber*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The Democratic Commons project proposes an interdisciplinary (philosophy + computer science) evaluation of LLMs against five democratic principles, centered on the question of political bias and the conditions under which LLMs can be used democratically in citizen participation, including citizens' assemblies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI technologies are already reshaping political and private life without sufficient epistemic caution, creating both dangers and opportunities for citizen participation—from electoral and legislative engagement to political co-creation via citizens' assemblies. LLMs could support these processes (moderation, translation, facilitation, summarization, writing assistance), but this potential remains largely unrealized, and the conditions for their democratic use are unclear.

Method: A fundamentally interdisciplinary approach spanning philosophy to computer science, grounded in political theory and philosophy to supply the normative frameworks needed for democratic evaluation of AI systems. LLMs are evaluated against five proposed democratic principles, with the analysis driven by the question of political bias under conditions of experimental citizen participation.

Result: The abstract reports no empirical results; it presents the project's framing, proposing five democratic principles as evaluative criteria and identifying political bias as the central socio-technical question.

Conclusion: Meaningful democratic evaluation of AI systems requires grounding in political theory and philosophy; without such normative frameworks, assessing LLMs for use in citizen participation—itself still largely experimental—cannot be conducted meaningfully.

Abstract: Powerful technologies labeled ``AI''-without sufficient epistemic caution-are already reshaping political and private life, bringing both new dangers and new opportunities for citizen participation. These range from electoral and legislative engagement to the most ambitious form: political co-creation through citizens' assemblies. Large Language Models (LLMs) could support such processes through moderation, translation, facilitation, summarization, and writing assistance. But this potential remains largely unrealized. The Democratic Commons project takes a fundamentally interdisciplinary approach-from philosophy to computer science-to evaluate LLMs against five proposed democratic principles. At its core, the project is driven by the question of political bias: under what conditions can LLMs be used democratically within forms of citizen participation that are them- selves still largely experimental? Addressing these socio-technical questions requires grounding in political theory and, more broadly, in philosophy-disciplines that provide the normative frameworks without which the democratic evaluation of AI systems cannot be mean- ingfully conducted.

</details>


### [214] [Predicting build orientation for SLM dental parts: a comparison of rotation representations and direct vector regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15710)
*Felix Schmalzel,Reimar Waitz,Moritz Kronberger,Thorsten Schöler*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Build orientation prediction for SLM dental parts is treated as supervised learning of the up-axis. Comparing 13 rotation representations on ResNet-50 and PointNeXt-S backbones, octahedral map with TTA gives the lowest error (10.6°), and TTA is the most consistent accuracy driver.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Build orientation for selective laser melting (SLM) of dental parts is typically chosen manually by technicians, motivating an automated, learning-based approach to predict the part's up-axis from technician-labeled production data.

Method: Using ~2400 patient-specific dental parts, a ResNet-50 multi-view image backbone and a PointNeXt-S point-cloud backbone (both pretrained, fine-tuned end-to-end) were trained on 13 up-axis representations: six classical SO(3) parameterizations and seven direct unit-sphere S^2 representations. Evaluation uses geodesic angular error on a test set, with and without test-time augmentation (TTA) over K=21 known rotations.

Result: With TTA, the octahedral map yields the lowest mean angular error (10.6°, ResNet-50). The three best results overall are direct S^2 representations, though this may reflect label noise in the unsupervised in-plane component of SO(3) targets rather than a topological advantage. von Mises-Fisher collapses to near-constant predictions under PointNeXt-S but not ResNet-50. TTA reduces mean angular error by 31–73% across nearly all representation-backbone combinations.

Conclusion: Test-time augmentation over a small set of known rotations is the most consistent driver of accuracy, while the best-performing rotation representation is strongly backbone-dependent.

Abstract: Build orientation for selective laser melting (SLM) manufacturing of dental parts is usually chosen manually by technicians. We treat orientation prediction as supervised machine learning of the part's up-axis from technician-labeled production data, and test which rotation representations produce the best results. Using $n\approx2400$ patient-specific dental parts, we trained a ResNet-50 multi-view image backbone and a PointNeXt-S point-cloud backbone, both pretrained and fine-tuned end-to-end, on 13 up-axis representations spanning six classical $SO(3)$ parameterizations and seven representations defined directly on the unit sphere $S^2$. We report the geodesic angular error between predicted and ground-truth up-axis on a test set, with and without test-time augmentation (TTA) over $K=21$ known rotations. With TTA, the octahedral map achieves the lowest mean angular error ($10.6^\circ$, ResNet-50). The three lowest-error results overall are direct $S^2$ representations, though this may reflect label noise in the unsupervised in-plane component of the $SO(3)$ targets rather than a topological advantage. von Mises-Fisher collapses to a near-constant prediction when trained with PointNeXt-S but not with ResNet-50. TTA reduces mean angular error by 31-73 % across almost every representation and backbone. Overall, test-time augmentation over a small set of known rotations is the most consistent driver of accuracy, whereas the best-performing representation is strongly backbone-dependent.

</details>


### [215] [Data storytelling meets interpretable machine learning: Decoding AI decisions for non-experts without revealing sensitive data and model details](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15722)
*Lemen Chao,Zixuan Yang,Anran Fang,Mingran Sun,Ming Lei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper integrates data storytelling with interpretable machine learning (IML) to make AI decisions more accessible to non-experts, proposing the DIST Pyramid, the I-P-O Model, and an architecture generating "What-if" and "Why-not" data stories; empirical evaluation shows significantly higher comprehensibility over traditional SHAP visualizations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI-driven automated decision-making needs both predictive performance and interpretability. Although IML tools explain model predictions, their technical complexity hinders accessibility for non-experts, creating a gap between available explanations and public understanding.

Method: Following the design science research (DSR) paradigm, the study formally defines data storytelling in IML, introduces the DIST Pyramid to align data storytelling with IML, and presents the I-P-O Model to describe their interactions. It develops an architecture explaining AI decisions via distinct "What-if" and "Why-not" event-generation processes, and employs data desensitization to protect sensitive input data. Validation uses a case study on the Boston Housing dataset with SHAP values and LLMs to generate data stories with And-But-Therefore (ABT) structures.

Result: Empirical evaluation shows 76.4% of respondents rated "What-if" data stories and 74.3% rated "Why-not" data stories as more comprehensible, with significantly higher accessibility scores than traditional SHAP visualizations.

Conclusion: The paper presents a narrative interpretation framework integrating IML and data storytelling, expanding both the research scope and practical applicability of AI decision-making for broader audiences.

Abstract: AI-driven automated decision-making requires both predictive performance and interpretability. Recent advances in interpretable machine learning (IML) provide tools for explaining model predictions, but the technical complexity of these explanations may hinder accessibility to non-experts. To address this challenge, this study integrates data storytelling with IML to enhance the explainability of AI-generated decisions for a broader audience. Following the design science research (DSR) paradigm, this study proposes a formal definition of data storytelling in IML, introduces the DIST Pyramid to align data storytelling with IML, and presents the I-P-O Model to describe their interactions. It further develops an architecture to explain AI decisions through distinct "What-if" and "Why-not" event-generation processes. The architecture also employs data desensitization to protect sensitive input data. To validate the approach, a case study is conducted with the Boston Housing dataset, using SHapley Additive exPlanations (SHAP) values and large language models (LLMs) to generate data stories with And-But-Therefore (ABT) structures. An empirical evaluation shows that 76.4% and 74.3% of respondents rated the "What-if" and "Why-not" data stories as more comprehensible, with significantly higher accessibility scores than traditional SHAP visualizations. The paper concludes with the presentation of a narrative interpretation framework that integrates IML and data storytelling, thereby expanding the research scope as well as the practical applicability of AI decision-making.

</details>


### [216] [Are LLMs Good Financial User Simulators? A Preliminary Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15727)
*Jiajie He,Jiangyuan Hong,Dongling Ni,Wenjin Liu,Xintong Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A preliminary controlled paper-trading study with 120 volunteers tests whether LLMs can simulate evolving individual financial decisions; market context helps action/ticker prediction but not sizing, and models exhibit behavioral compression.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly deployed as user simulators, yet it is unclear whether they can reproduce evolving individual financial decision-making, which is dynamic, personalized, and sequential.

Method: Controlled paper-trading environment with 120 volunteers using non-redeemable virtual funds under real-time market conditions (no real accounts, positions, or transaction records). Using only pre-cutoff information, a simulator predicts the next-trading-day action, traded security, and transaction quantity. Temporally aligned rolling predictions are evaluated, with and without point-in-time market information via ablation.

Result: Market context improves action and ticker prediction in the ablation, while transaction sizing remains difficult. Systematic behavioral compression emerges: overproduction of hold actions, underprediction of sell decisions, and simplification of multi-security transactions.

Conclusion: These findings offer an initial empirical characterization of LLM-based individual financial decision simulation and motivate larger-scale evaluation of individual, temporal, and portfolio-level behavioral fidelity.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as user simulators, but their ability to reproduce evolving individual financial decisions remains unclear. We present a preliminary study in a controlled paper-trading environment with 120 volunteers. Participants used non-redeemable virtual funds under real-time market conditions; no real brokerage accounts, real-money positions, or real transaction records were accessed. Given only information available before a prediction cutoff, a simulator predicts the participant's next-trading-day action, traded security, and transaction quantity. We evaluate temporally aligned rolling predictions and compare settings with and without point-in-time market information. Market context improves action and ticker prediction in the controlled ablation, while transaction sizing remains difficult. We also observe systematic behavioral compression: models overproduce hold actions, underpredict sell decisions, and simplify multi-security transactions. These results provide an initial empirical characterization and motivate larger-scale evaluation of individual, temporal, and portfolio-level behavioral fidelity.

</details>


### [217] [Design of a Deep Learning Credit Risk Early Warning System Integrating Multi-source Heterogeneous Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15744)
*LiYang Wang,Zhen Zhong,Zhen Tian,Keyu Chen,Keyu Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a credit risk early warning system based on heterogeneous information fusion, using deep neural networks with attention mechanisms to extract multidimensional features from transaction behaviors and social networks, achieving higher accuracy and timeliness than rule-based engines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Financial risk early warning systems often suffer from inefficiency caused by information silos and monitoring delays, motivating a more integrative and timely approach.

Method: A model architecture integrating deep neural networks and attention mechanisms is employed to extract multidimensional features from heterogeneous data sources, including transaction behaviors and social networks, forming an early identification mechanism for corporate and individual credit risks.

Result: System testing shows that the approach significantly enhances the accuracy and timeliness of risk warnings, outperforming traditional rule-based engine solutions.

Conclusion: The findings provide innovative insights for early intervention in financial risks and hold practical significance for safeguarding financial stability.

Abstract: Advancements in data fusion and real-time analytics technologies have opened new avenues for addressing complex domain challenges. Financial risk early warning systems often suffer from inefficiency due to information silos and monitoring delays. This paper proposes a credit risk early warning system based on heterogeneous information fusion. The system employs a model architecture integrating deep neural networks and attention mechanisms to extract multidimensional features from diverse data sources such as transaction behaviors and social networks, thereby establishing an early identification mechanism for corporate and individual credit risks. System testing demonstrates that this approach significantly enhances the accuracy and timeliness of risk warnings, outperforming traditional rule-based engine solutions. The findings offer innovative insights for early intervention in financial risks, holding practical significance for safeguarding financial stability.

</details>


### [218] [EvoOntology: A Self-Evolving Ontology Layer for Data Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15779)
*Meiduo Chong,Shaolei Zhang,Ju Fan,Xiaoyong Du*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EvoOntology introduces a self-evolving ontology layer, exposed as an MCP server with schema, content, and tool layers, that lets data agents query and interact with heterogeneous data at runtime, achieving consistent gains across three benchmarks and four LLM backbones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Data agents must execute natural-language instructions over heterogeneous data (tables, files, databases), but face an "agent-data gap": the data lies outside the agent, which can only access it via generic tools (e.g., column names, file paths). Existing solutions either have agents directly explore raw sources or inject hand-crafted semantic layers into prompts—neither scales to large heterogeneous sources nor adapts to varying agent behaviors.

Method: EvoOntology encapsulates an ontology as an MCP server composed of three layers: a schema layer, a content layer, and a tool layer, enabling agents to actively query and interact with the ontology at runtime. It employs a builder agent for autonomous ontology construction and a self-evolution loop that continuously refines the ontology via attribution-guided typed edits, which are accepted only after a backbone-conditional paired evaluation.

Result: Experiments on three well-adopted data-agent benchmarks with four LLM backbones show that EvoOntology consistently outperforms strong baselines and existing semantic-layer approaches, effectively bridging the agent-data gap and enabling more effective interaction with heterogeneous data.

Conclusion: EvoOntology provides a scalable, adaptive self-evolving ontology layer that bridges the agent-data gap, improving data agents' ability to interact with heterogeneous data. Code is available at https://github.com/ruc-datalab/EvoOntology.

Abstract: Data agents aim to fulfill natural-language instructions over heterogeneous data, including tables, files, and databases. However, data agents face a challenging agent-data gap: heterogeneous data resides outside the agent, while the agent can access it (e.g., column names and file paths) only through generic tools. Existing approaches either let agents directly explore raw data sources or inject manually constructed semantic layers into prompts. However, neither scales well to large heterogeneous data sources nor adapts to different agent behaviors. In this paper, we introduce EvoOntology, a self-evolving ontology layer for data agents. EvoOntology encapsulates the ontology as an MCP server comprising a schema layer, a content layer, and a tool layer, enabling agents to actively query and interact with the ontology at runtime. To this end, we introduce a builder agent for autonomous ontology construction and a self-evolution loop that continuously refines the ontology through attribution-guided typed edits that are accepted only after a backbone-conditional paired evaluation. Experiments on three well-adopted data-agent benchmarks with four LLM backbones demonstrate that EvoOntology consistently outperforms strong baselines and existing semantic-layer approaches, effectively bridging the agent-data gap and enabling more effective interaction with heterogeneous data. Code: https://github.com/ruc-datalab/EvoOntology

</details>


### [219] [KnowBench: Effort Reduction as a Unified, Deployment-Grounded Benchmark for Clinical AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15794)
*Jocelyn Kang,Caroline Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces KnowBench, a benchmark whose unifying metric is Effort Reduction (ER), defined as the proportion of system-generated clinical work accepted by the responsible clinician under expert and safety review. It contributes the metric, its degenerate cases, and an auditable reporting protocol, plus a headline measurement of 97.99% aggregate ER in the documentation task across over one million signed encounters, 13 specialties, and a >6-month production window.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing clinical AI evaluation instruments were built for research settings: reference-based similarity metrics and expert rubric panels. These measure resemblance to an artifact rather than reduction of a clinical burden, so they fail to capture whether AI actually saves clinician effort. A burden-oriented, task-general, auditable metric is needed.

Method: The authors propose Effort Reduction (ER): the proportion of system-generated clinical work product accepted by the responsible clinician under expert and safety review. ER is defined once and instantiated identically per task across administrative clinical AI workloads (visit notes, diagnosis and billing codes, orders, EHR chart summarization, after-visit summaries, clinical decision support). In each instantiation, the clinician's review-and-attestation event is the ground truth, accepted units count as completed work, and corrections are residual effort. The benchmark comprises the metric, its degenerate cases, and a reporting protocol for auditable, cross-system-comparable ER claims. The initial measurement uses proprietary fine-tuned clinical foundation models in a closed feedback architecture, evaluated over >1M signed encounters, >6 months of production, and 13 specialties.

Result: Aggregate ER of 97.99% for the documentation instantiation, with per-specialty aggregates spanning 96.8-98.9%, measured over more than one million signed encounters across a production window exceeding six months and thirteen medical specialties. The release reports the protocol checklist only partially and states which companion statistics are withheld.

Conclusion: The benchmark itself is the primary contribution, offered so that the headline ER figure and all subsequent figures can be held to the same standard. Partial protocol reporting and withheld companion statistics are disclosed transparently, positioning ER as an auditable, cross-system-comparable standard for clinical AI evaluation.

Abstract: Clinical AI systems are evaluated with instruments built for research settings (reference-based similarity metrics and expert rubric panels) that measure resemblance to an artifact rather than reduction of a burden. We introduce KnowBench, pioneered by Knowtex, whose unifying metric is Effort Reduction (ER): the proportion of system-generated clinical work product accepted by the responsible clinician under expert and safety review. ER is defined once and instantiated per task across the administrative workload clinical AI automates: visit notes, diagnosis and billing codes, orders, EHR chart summarization, patient after-visit summaries, and clinical decision support. In every instantiation the construction is identical: the clinician's review-and-attestation event is the ground truth, every accepted unit is work the system completed, and every correction is residual effort returned to the clinician. The primary contribution of this paper is the benchmark itself: the metric, its degenerate cases, and a reporting protocol under which ER claims are auditable and cross-system comparable. Alongside it we report an initial headline measurement from the documentation instantiation: over one million signed encounters across a production window exceeding six months and thirteen medical specialties, Knowtex's proprietary fine-tuned clinical foundation models operating inside a closed feedback architecture achieve an aggregate ER of 97.99%, with per-specialty aggregates spanning 96.8-98.9%. This release reports the protocol's checklist partially, and states which companion statistics are withheld; the benchmark is offered so that this figure, and every figure reported after it, can be held to the same standard.

</details>


### [220] [Navigating Sparse Evidence: Agentic Visual RAG via Explicit Context Selection and Consolidation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15800)
*Yucheng Shen,Lingyong Yan,Jiulong Wu,Shuaiqiang Wang,Jianmin WU,Dawei Yin,Min Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SCoRE is a unified agent loop for VRAG that explicitly selects and consolidates visual evidence during exploration, then reloads and organizes the referenced images for grounded answering, trained via cold-start distillation plus evidence-aware RL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In VRAG, answer-relevant evidence is sparse—concentrated in a small page region or dispersed across pages—and existing agentic methods answer from raw exploration trajectories or compressed textual memories rather than an explicitly organized set of supporting images. This causes susceptibility to exploration noise and obscures the evidence-backed reasoning trace. The authors argue the bottleneck lies not only in evidence discovery but also in its preservation and organization before answer generation.

Method: SCoRE (Selection and Consolidation for Robust Evidence) is a unified agent loop: during exploration it retains only query-relevant observations and their source pointers in a maintained textual ledger, preserving earlier evidence while bounding visual context. At termination, it reloads the referenced original images and consolidates them into a logically ordered visual evidence set for answering, decoupling final reasoning from exploratory trial-and-error and enforcing strict visual grounding via indexed claim-to-image linkages. Training combines filtered cold-start trajectory distillation with evidence-aware reinforcement learning, rewarding evidence coverage, consolidation compactness, and answer correctness.

Result: The proposed paradigm enables end-to-end optimization of the unified rollout through filtered cold-start trajectory distillation plus evidence-aware RL, with a reward promoting evidence coverage, consolidation compactness, and answer correctness. (No empirical results are reported in the abstract.)

Conclusion: Explicit evidence selection and consolidation, rather than raw trajectories or textual compression, is key to robust VRAG, yielding bounded visual context, decoupled answer reasoning, strict visual grounding, and an end-to-end trainable agent loop.

Abstract: Visual Retrieval-Augmented Generation (VRAG) empowers models to navigate and answer queries about visually rich documents by retrieving relevant page images as visual evidence and reasoning over their content. However, effectively utilizing this visual evidence is usually impeded by two main challenges. First, answer-relevant evidence is sparse and may be concentrated in a small region of one page or dispersed across multiple pages. Second, existing agentic methods often generate answers based on raw exploration trajectories or compressed textual memories rather than an explicitly organized set of supporting images, making answers susceptible to exploration noise and obscuring the evidence-backed reasoning trace. We argue that the bottleneck lies not only in evidence discovery but also in its preservation and organization before answer generation. We propose SCoRE (Selection and Consolidation for Robust Evidence), a unified agent loop for explicit evidence selection and consolidation. During exploration, SCoRE retains only query-relevant observations and their source pointers in a maintained textual ledger, preserving earlier evidence while keeping the visual context bounded. At termination, it reloads the referenced original images and consolidates the visual evidence for answering, arranging it into a logical sequence. This decouples final reasoning from exploratory trial-and-error while ensuring strict visual grounding via indexed claim-to-image linkages. To enable end-to-end optimization of this unified rollout, our training paradigm combines filtered cold-start trajectory distillation with evidence-aware reinforcement learning, whose reward promotes evidence coverage, consolidation compactness, and answer correctness.

</details>


### [221] [Atria Dawn: The Dawn of Agentic Superintelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15818)
*Honglin Guo,Tao Gui,Yicheng Chen,Guanting Dong,Qiming Ge,Yuyang Hu,Zixian Huang,Jiajie Jin,Alexander Lam,Yining Li,Jiahang Lin,Yanjiang Liu,Xinyu Lu,Haijun Lv,Junlin Shang,Qisheng Su,Guoqiang Wang,Rui Wang,Zhecan Wang,Hao Xiang,Xinchen Xie,Shuhao Xing,Xiaoyu Xing,Wanghan Xu,Xinyu Yang,Yajie Yang,Chengfeng Zhao,Haoran Zhao,Ruojun Zhou,Yunhua Zhou,Yicheng Zou,Kun Cai,Qiye Cai,Xinmeng Che,Haodong Chen,Jiabei Chen,Jiahao Chen,Jiayi Chen,Yujia Chen,Lizhi Cui,Youheng Dai,Xin Deng,Yi Dong,Shihan Dou,Chenya Gu,Xu Guo,Ding Han,Feiyang Hao,Haotan He,Jie Hou,Binze Hu,Zijian Hu,Junhao Huang,Huicheng Jiang,Jiazhen Jiang,Shufan Jiang,Jiahao Kuang,Bowen Lai,Bo Li,Jiaqiang Li,Peng Li,Qilong Li,Zhuoqun Li,Jiaxiang Liu,Shuainan Liu,Tong Liu,Yi Liu,Zhonghang Lu,Jianwen Luo,Yanyi Luo,Huijie Lv,Ningsheng Ma,Zerun Ma,Houcheng Min,Chengjun Pan,Qiyuan Peng,Xiaoxuan Peng,Jianmin Qian,Jiantao Qiu,Wanying Ren,Huayu Sha,Jifei Shan,Zixin Shang,Bing Shao,Zhuohui Sheng,Jiayang Shi,Yang Shu,Aierpanjiang Simayi,Sirui Song,Yuxiao Song,Zhe Sun,Zhichao Sun,Wenzhe Tan,Wenhui Tian,Zhongbo Tian,Hanchen Wang,Pengbo Wang,Rui Wang,Yiding Wang,Yuhui Wang,Zhiheng Xi,Caijun Xu,Chao Xu,Yongfeng Xu,Xiaolei Yang,Zhixiong Yang,Qian Yao,Shihong Yi,Yuankai Ying,Jia Yu,Dingbo Yuan,Hao Yuan,Junjie Yuan,Bo Zhang,Caixian Zhang,Qiuyinzhe Zhang,Jiyuan Zhao,Penghao Zhao,Ying Zhao,Pujun Zheng,Xiaoxue Zhong,Xiaohao Zhou,Xinyu Zhou,Dongsheng Zhu,Guanru Zhu,Yulun Zhu,Yaojie Lu,Tao Ji,Hongyu Lin,Yutao Zhu,Pengfei Cao,Guoxiu He,Xianpei Han,Ben He,Zhicheng Dou,Kang Liu,Qi Zhang,Le Sun,Jun Zhao,Ji-Rong Wen,Xuanjing Huang,Yu-Gang Jiang,Bowen Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Atria Dawn Preview, a foundation agentic language model for scientific research and engineering, trained via a Verifiable Experience Pipeline, and analyzes 769 task records from 56 participants to show a shift from task-level execution to project-level human-AI partnership.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI agents participate in developing their successors, they reshape both the production of intelligence and the role of human researchers, motivating a need to understand and expand agent productivity while preserving meaningful human oversight.

Method: A Verifiable Experience Pipeline connects tool-mediated interactions to executable environments and externally verified outcomes to train the Atria Dawn Preview model. Evaluation spans 16 benchmarks across real-world research, engineering, and digital work. Human-AI collaboration is examined via a case study of the model's own R&D process, analyzing 769 task records from 56 participants alongside agent logs.

Result: Atria Dawn Preview is competitive with frontier agents and achieves the highest reported score on five of the 16 benchmarks. In the case study, participants rated about one-third of completed AI-assisted tasks as infeasible without AI; agents frequently propose methods and implement revisions, while humans retain most final decisions and guide exploration through judgment and feedback.

Conclusion: The findings indicate a shift from task-level execution to project-level partnership, with human effort concentrated on what is worth pursuing and how evidence should guide research. Progress toward more autonomous AI research requires advancing both discovery capacity and meaningful human oversight, preserving accountable human authority over risks and direction.

Abstract: As AI agents become participants in the development of their successors, they reshape both the production of intelligence and the role of human researchers. We introduce Atria Dawn Preview, a foundation agentic language model designed for scientific research and engineering workflows, with the goal of expanding the frontier of agent productivity in the real world. This model is trained via a Verifiable Experience Pipeline that connects tool-mediated interactions to executable environments and externally verified outcomes. Across 16 benchmarks spanning real-world research, engineering, and digital work, Atria Dawn Preview is competitive with frontier agents and achieves the highest reported score on five of them. Beyond standalone performance, we examine the real research-and-development process behind this model as a case study of human--AI collaboration, analyzing 769 task records from 56 participants together with agent logs. When asked to evaluate completed tasks under comparable conditions, participants rated about one-third of completed AI-assisted tasks as infeasible without AI. More strikingly, agents frequently propose methods and implement revisions, while humans retain most final decisions and guide exploration through judgment and feedback. These observations indicate a shift from task-level execution to project-level partnership, with human effort concentrating on what is worth pursuing and how evidence should guide research. Progress toward more autonomous AI research must therefore advance both the capacity for discovery and the capacity for meaningful human oversight, preserving accountable human authority over the risks and direction of continued development.

</details>


### [222] [AlgoEvo: Self-Evolving Agentic Search for Automated Algorithm Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15820)
*Junhao Qiu,Qinglong Hu,Xialiang Tong,Mingxuan Yuan,Liyong Lin,Qingfu Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AlgoEvo is a unified agentic framework that reframes automated algorithm discovery as an interactive, knowledge-accumulating process, enabling a single flexible workflow to handle diverse design paradigms with fewer evaluations and lower token cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based automated algorithm discovery frameworks trap models in rigid search pipelines with pre-defined control flows, which restricts adaptive reasoning, blocks cross-paradigm transfer, and discards valuable execution feedback.

Method: AlgoEvo employs an autonomous agent that dynamically inspects, diagnoses, and edits code based on runtime feedback. A design skill hub decouples paradigm-specific knowledge from the core discovery engine, letting one workflow address single-objective, multi-objective, and multi-component design. A hierarchical experience mechanism organizes search trajectories into a task-level tree and consolidates cross-task patterns into reusable skills.

Result: Across six representative benchmark tasks, AlgoEvo matches or surpasses specialized methods with substantially fewer evaluations and reduced token consumption. It demonstrates strong intra-task accumulation, cross-task transfer, and the ability to reproduce or exceed existing state-of-the-art performance via flexible skill activation.

Conclusion: By transforming automated algorithm discovery into an interactive, knowledge-accumulating agentic process, AlgoEvo overcomes the rigidity of conventional search pipelines, achieving efficient, transferable, cross-paradigm algorithm design.

Abstract: Large language models have advanced automated algorithm discovery by synthesizing executable code, but existing frameworks trap them in rigid search pipelines with pre-defined control flows. This limitation restricts adaptive reasoning, blocks cross-paradigm transfer, and discards valuable execution feedback. We propose AlgoEvo, a unified agentic framework that transforms automated algorithm discovery into an interactive, knowledge-accumulating process. An autonomous agent dynamically inspects, diagnoses, and edits code based on runtime feedback. A design skill hub decouples paradigm-specific knowledge from the core discovery engine, allowing a single workflow to seamlessly handle single-objective, multi-objective, and multi-component design. Meanwhile, a hierarchical experience mechanism organizes search trajectories into a task-level tree to guide exploration and consolidates cross-task patterns into reusable skills. Across six representative benchmark tasks, AlgoEvo matches or surpasses specialized methods with substantially fewer evaluations and reduced token consumption, demonstrating strong intra-task accumulation, cross-task transfer, and the ability to reproduce or exceed existing state-of-the-art performance through flexible skill activation.

</details>


### [223] [LongAgent: History-Guided Agentic Search for Longitudinal Outcome Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15859)
*Siyao Wang,Florian Guitton,Shuojie Fu,Guanyu Tao,Kai Sun,Wenjia Bai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LongAgent is an agent-based framework that autonomously searches over variable subsets, temporal windows, and longitudinal aggregation functions to build predictive representations from heterogeneous longitudinal medical data, guided by a history memory of prior searches and numerical evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extracting informative representations from longitudinal data for future-outcome prediction is a critical yet difficult medical challenge. Medical datasets are heterogeneous: many variables from different sources, sampled at different temporal spacings, capturing different aspects of health. This demands (1) identifying predictive variables, (2) processing longitudinal information, and (3) integrating multiple variables for outcome prediction—a large combinatorial search space that is hard to handle manually.

Method: LongAgent, a novel agent-based approach, autonomously searches over combinations of variable sets, temporal windows, and longitudinal aggregation functions, and identifies candidates with promising predictive performance. It leverages a history memory of previous searches and numerical evidence to guide subsequent exploration.

Result: On synthetic data, LongAgent attains a mean prediction RMSE of 1.7376, improving over the strongest non-agent baseline by 0.0151 (95% CI: [0.0045, 0.0260]; p=0.0273). On a real clinical dataset, it performs comparably to the best baseline.

Conclusion: LongAgent offers an autonomous, memory-guided search strategy for constructing predictive representations from heterogeneous longitudinal medical data. It significantly outperforms the strongest non-agent baseline on synthetic data and matches the best baseline on a real clinical dataset, suggesting promise as a general approach for longitudinal predictive modeling.

Abstract: Extracting informative representations from longitudinal data that can predict future outcomes remains a critical challenge in medicine. Medical datasets are inherently heterogeneous, consisting of a large number of variables collected from different sources, sampled with different temporal spacings, and representing different aspects of human health status. This requires identifying those variables with predictive value, processing longitudinal information, and integrating multiple variables for outcome prediction. Here, we propose a novel agent-based approach, LongAgent, that can autonomously search over combinations of variable sets, temporal windows and longitudinal aggregation functions, and identify candidates with promising predictive performance. LongAgent utilises a history memory of previous searches and numerical evidence to guide subsequent exploration. On synthetic data, LongAgent achieves a mean prediction RMSE of 1.7376 and improves over the strongest non-agent baseline by 0.0151 (95% CI: [0.0045,0.0260]; p=0.0273). On a real clinical dataset, it performs comparably to the best baseline.

</details>


### [224] [Pilot Early, Commit Late: A Real-Options Model of Enterprise AI Adoption under Rapid Technological Progress](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15919)
*Gaurav Tewari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A two-period model of AI deployment under frontier uncertainty shows that immediate deployment, limited piloting, and waiting are distinct options, yielding six timing results: frontier uncertainty raises the value of waiting and piloting but not deployment (under affine payoff); faster expected progress can reduce immediate deployment's appeal; piloting dominates waiting when expected capability value exceeds cost; valuable organization-specific learning creates a "pilot early, commit late" region; a closed-form modularity threshold determines when immediate deployment dominates; and production vs. pilot-specific learning affect timing differently.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Firms face a timing problem with AI: the technology frontier improves rapidly, implementation is partly irreversible, and organization-specific capabilities are accumulated through action. Existing treatment conflates deploying, experimenting, and waiting, leaving unclear how uncertainty and rapid progress should shape the timing of commitment.

Method: A two-period decision model of AI deployment under uncertainty in which a firm chooses among immediate deployment, a limited pilot, and waiting. Deployment earns current operating value but risks architectural obsolescence; waiting preserves the option to adopt after observing the frontier; a pilot sacrifices current operating value to build organization-specific learning without full commitment. A continuous-time extension is also developed.

Result: Five central timing results plus a sixth comparative result: (1) a mean-preserving increase in frontier uncertainty raises the value of waiting and piloting but leaves immediate deployment unchanged when its payoff is affine in the frontier; (2) faster expected frontier progress can reduce the relative attractiveness of immediate deployment when deployed architecture captures only a limited share of future improvement; (3) a pilot dominates waiting exactly when the expected value of the capability it builds exceeds its cost; (4) sufficiently valuable organization-specific learning creates a nonempty region where "pilot early, commit late" is optimal; (5) a closed-form modularity threshold exists above which immediate deployment dominates the best outside option; (6) production learning and pilot-specific learning affect the timing margin differently. The continuous-time extension recovers the standard result that uncertainty raises the adoption threshold while capability and modularity lower it.

Conclusion: The paper separates deploying, experimenting, and waiting, and shows why rapid progress can rationally increase experimentation without justifying irreversible commitment.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence presents firms with an unusual timing problem. The technology frontier is improving rapidly, implementation is partly irreversible, and organization-specific capabilities are accumulated through action. This paper develops a two-period decision model of AI deployment under uncertainty in which a firm chooses among immediate deployment, a limited pilot, and waiting. Deployment earns current operating value but exposes the firm to architectural obsolescence; waiting preserves the option to adopt after the frontier is observed; a pilot sacrifices current operating value to build organization-specific learning without full commitment. The model yields five central timing results and a sixth comparative result on where learning occurs. First, a mean-preserving increase in frontier uncertainty raises the value of waiting and piloting but leaves immediate deployment unchanged when its payoff is affine in the frontier. Second, faster expected frontier progress can reduce the relative attractiveness of immediate deployment when deployed architecture captures only a limited share of future improvement. Third, a pilot dominates waiting exactly when the expected value of the capability it builds exceeds its cost. Fourth, sufficiently valuable organization-specific learning creates a nonempty region in which "pilot early, commit late" is optimal. Fifth, there is a closed-form modularity threshold above which immediate deployment dominates the best outside option. Sixth, production learning and pilot-specific learning affect the timing margin differently. A continuous-time extension recovers the standard result that uncertainty raises the adoption threshold while capability and modularity lower it. The paper separates deploying, experimenting, and waiting, and shows why rapid progress can rationally increase experimentation without justifying irreversible commitment.

</details>


### [225] [Recurrent GraphNeural NetworkswithSet-BasedAggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15932)
*Blai Bonet*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper establishes an effective, two-directional equivalence between recurrent GNNs with set-based aggregation (under weight-checkable conditions) and the fragment BΣ°₁ of the modal μ-calculus, i.e., the Boolean closure of reachability and safety properties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior logical characterizations of recurrent GNNs rely on multi-set aggregation, graded/counting logics, and halting or acceptance conditions that cannot be verified from network parameters, limiting their usefulness for explaining network behavior.

Method: The authors study recurrent GNNs with set-based aggregation and identify sufficient conditions, checkable directly from the weights, for compiling networks into formulas and formulas into networks, invoking the modal μ-calculus fragment BΣ°₁.

Result: Networks meeting the conditions correspond exactly to the Boolean closure of reachability and safety properties; this fragment is not an artifact, being the exact expressive level of stabilization over finite vocabulary, supporting fixed points of a single polarity and their Boolean combinations but not composition of fixed points of opposite polarities.

Conclusion: The correspondence requires no counting logic, no external halting signal, and no non-effective acceptance condition, yielding a verifiable path from weights to symbolic explanations for qualifying networks.

Abstract: Recurrent GNNs iterate message passing to convergence, and their logical characterizations to date rely on multi-set aggregation, graded (counting) logics, and halting or acceptance conditions that cannot be verified from the network's parameters. We study recurrent GNNs with set-based aggregation and identify sufficient conditions checkable from the weights for networks to compile into formulas and formulas into networks. The main result is an effective, two-directional equivalence between a class of networks and the Boolean closure of reachability and safety properties, the fragment B$Σ^{\circ}_1$ of the modal $μ$-calculus. The fragment is not an artifact: it is the exact expressive level of stabilization over finite vocabulary, which supports fixed points of a single polarity and Boolean combinations thereof, but not the composition of fixed points of opposite polarities. The correspondence needs no counting logic, no external halting signal, and no non-effective acceptance condition, yielding a verifiable path from weights to symbolic explanations for networks meeting the conditions.

</details>


### [226] [Stellar Colosseum: A Many-Agent Harness for Long-Horizon Research in Mathematics and Theoretical Computer Science](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15983)
*Honghao Lin,David P. Woodruff,Yuan Deng,Jieming Mao,Song Zuo,Vahab Mirrokni*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Stellar Colosseum is a model-agnostic harness that allocates inference across long-horizon research in mathematics and TCS, exploring strategies, gating decomposition on readiness, modeling proof plans as interdependent subproblems, and routing verifier findings back to affected arguments, yielding new results on FOCS/JMLR open problems and 71.0% on TCS-Bench.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language models can produce plausible short proofs but remain unreliable on long-horizon research problems, where progress hinges on sequences of uncertain, interdependent decisions. Existing inference approaches lack mechanisms to manage this uncertainty and interdependence across extended proof construction.

Method: Colosseum is a model-agnostic harness for allocating inference across mathematical and TCS research. It explores alternative strategies before proof construction, employs a readiness gate to decide when a route is mature enough to decompose, represents the proof plan as interdependent section-level subproblems, and routes verifier findings back to the affected part of the argument. At each stage it generates candidates in parallel, attacks them with targeted falsification, and combines candidates and critiques into a single research artifact via overlapping random-sample tree aggregation. The workflow is integrated into Google Antigravity's Teamwork framework as the Long Proof pattern.

Result: With Gemini 3.1 Pro, Colosseum addresses several open problems from papers published at top venues such as FOCS and JMLR. On TCS-Bench, a benchmark of research-level theorem-proving tasks drawn from FOCS, STOC, and SODA papers, it achieves 71.0% accuracy using Gemini 3.1 Pro and Gemini 3.7 Flash. In a separate Codeforces evaluation with Gemini 3.1 Pro, the proof-oriented pipeline with execution feedback solves 218 of 222 problems.

Conclusion: Colosseum demonstrates that structured, stage-wise inference allocation with parallel candidate generation, targeted falsification, and critique aggregation can substantially improve language model reliability on long-horizon mathematical and TCS research, as evidenced by new results on open problems and strong benchmark performance.

Abstract: Language models can produce plausible short proofs, but may still be unreliable on long-horizon research problems, where progress depends on a sequence of uncertain and interdependent decisions. We introduce Stellar Colosseum, a model-agnostic harness for allocating inference across research in mathematics and theoretical computer science. Colosseum explores alternative strategies before proof construction, uses a readiness gate to decide when a route is mature enough to decompose, represents the proof plan as interdependent section-level subproblems, and routes verifier findings back to the affected part of the argument. Across these stages, it generates candidates in parallel, attacks them with targeted falsification, and combines candidates and their critiques into a single research artifact through overlapping random-sample tree aggregation. The Colosseum workflow has also been integrated into Google Antigravity's Teamwork framework as the Long Proof pattern.
  We demonstrate the capabilities of Colosseum through open-ended research and evaluations on theorem-proving and competitive programming benchmarks. Using Colosseum with Gemini 3.1 Pro, we obtain several new results that address open problems arising from papers published at top venues such as FOCS and JMLR. On TCS-Bench, a benchmark of research-level theorem-proving tasks drawn from papers published at FOCS, STOC, and SODA, Colosseum achieves 71.0% accuracy using Gemini 3.1 Pro and Gemini 3.7 Flash. In a separate Codeforces evaluation using Gemini 3.1 Pro, the proof-oriented pipeline with execution feedback solves 218 of 222 problems.

</details>


### [227] [Corrupt Plans, Clean Traces: Evading Chain-of-Thought Monitoring with Plan Injection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15989)
*Keertana Chidambaram,Andrew Ilyas,Vasilis Syrgkanis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper identifies a "plan injection" attack where harmful but benign-sounding reasoning planted in an actor LLM's context steers it into adversarial actions while evading CoT monitors, scaling to harder tasks and larger models and even degrading detection when monitors get more resources.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CoT monitoring is proposed as a safety strategy in which a monitor inspects an actor model's reasoning for unsafe planning, deception, or misalignment. The authors aim to test whether this monitoring can be subverted by manipulating the actor's context rather than its behavior directly.

Method: The authors plant harmful but benign-sounding reasoning in the actor's context (plan injection) and evaluate it first in the multiple-choice QA monitorability setting of Lanham et al. (2023) using the investigator-agent elicitation framework of Li et al. (2025). They then generalize the attack across monitorability benchmarks, larger models such as DeepSeek-R1, and vary monitor resources including access to the injected plan and thinking budget.

Result: Plan injection yields 25-33% monitor evasion rates across different monitorability benchmarks and scales to larger models like DeepSeek-R1. Actors follow injected plans and paraphrase them as their own reasoning without attribution. Giving the monitor access to the injected plan drops detection by up to 50% in the Bio-Math task, and extra thinking tokens are sometimes spent rationalizing rather than flagging the injected plan.

Conclusion: CoT monitoring is vulnerable to plan injection: an actor can be steered toward adversarial actions while evading monitors, and increasing monitor resources can be counterproductive. The authors call for re-examining assumptions underlying CoT monitoring as a safety mechanism.

Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) monitoring is a safety strategy where the reasoning of a large language model "actor" is inspected by a "monitor" (often another language model) for signs of unsafe planning, deception, or misalignment. We find that planting harmful but benign-sounding reasoning in the actor's context can steer it to perform adversarial actions while evading monitors, an attack we term "plan injection". We initially discover this attack in the multiple-choice question-answering monitorability setting proposed by Lanham et al. (2023), using the investigator-agent elicitation framework of Li et al. (2025). We generalize the attack and show that the discovered behavior scales to harder tasks (achieving 25-33% monitor evasion rates across different monitorability benchmarks) and larger models such as DeepSeek-R1. Across the settings we study, actor models not only follow injected plans but also paraphrase them as their own reasoning, without explicit attribution to the injections. Finally, we find cases where extra monitor resources cause harm - giving the monitor access to the injected plan drops detection by as much as 50% in the Bio-Math task and in a case study on monitor reasoning budget, we find transcripts where additional thinking tokens are spent rationalizing the injected plan rather than flagging it.

</details>
